They said: 'Then we will ask much.' He said: 'God is more abundant.'"* (Aḥmad 11133; al-Ḥākim, graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Targhīb*)

The three outcomes together close the gap the verse opens: nothing sincere is wasted, and the form the answer takes is not the supplicant's to specify. The discipline that follows is the one al-Bukhārī (6340) records — *"The supplication of one of you is answered so long as he does not hasten, saying: 'I supplicated and was not answered'"* — which identifies haste not as a defect of technique but as the specific thing that nullifies the practice. The hasty man asks for the object; the instructed man asks for the good, and leaves the identification of the good to the One who can see the whole chain.

**The Practical Rule the Verse Establishes**

Read as instruction rather than description, the verse yields a simple discipline: never pray for a specific outcome in a state of anger, and never treat the refusal of a request as the refusal of care. A believer who internalizes this makes his supplication differently — asking for what is good *if* it is good, and adding, as the Prophet ﷺ taught, the condition that God's knowledge govern. The hasty man asks for the thing; the instructed man asks for the good, and leaves the identification of the good to the One who sees the whole chain.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:12

> **We made the night and the day two signs. Then We effaced the sign of the night, and made the sign of the day, giving sight, that you might seek bounty from your Lord, and that you might know the number of years and the reckoning. And We have expounded everything in detail.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Signs: The Cosmological Argument in One Sentence**

*Wa jaʿalnā al-layla wa-l-nahāra āyatayn* — "We made the night and the day two signs." The dual form *āyatayn* is precise: not signs among many, but a matched pair. The alternation is the sign; neither element alone would signify. A perpetual day or a perpetual night would produce no calendar, no rhythm, no sense of order — only a single undifferentiated condition. Meaning arises from the contrast. This is one of the Qur'an's most frequently repeated appeals. Compare 2:164, *"Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of night and day, are signs for people of understanding"*; 3:190; 6:96–97, *"He cleaves the dawn, and He made the night for repose and the sun and the moon for reckoning"*; 10:67, *"It is He who made the night for you that you might rest therein, and the day giving sight"*; 30:23, *"And of His signs are your sleep by night and day, and your seeking of His bounty."* The recurrence across the revelation shows that the Qur'an treats the alternation not as background but as *evidence* — the primary datum available to every human being in every age, requiring no laboratory and no scholarship.

**"We Effaced the Sign of the Night"**

*Fa-maḥawnā āyata al-layl* — the verb *maḥā* means to efface, to obliterate, to wipe out. The commentators offer two readings. The first, favored by al-Ṭabarī and reported from Ibn ʿAbbās, is that the "sign of the night" is the moon and the "sign of the day" is the sun; God "effaced" the moon's sign by making it dark in itself, deriving its light from another — the crescent waxing and waning, unlike the sun's constant blaze. The second reading is that the effacement refers to the darkness of night itself, which "blots out" visibility. Both readings carry the same theological freight: the moon's light is *borrowed*. It shines without being a source. This is the Qur'an's most elegant natural metaphor for the whole created order — everything luminous in the universe is reflecting a light that is not its own. The verse that follows the night/day pair elsewhere makes the point directly: *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth"* (24:35).

**"And Made the Sign of the Day, Giving Sight"**

*Wa jaʿalnā āyata al-nahāri mubṣirah* — "and We made the sign of the day *mubṣirah*," literally "seeing" or "giving sight." The active participle applied to the day is a striking personification: the day is described as itself seeing, or as causing sight. Al-Zamakhsharī explains the idiom as the day's being made such that one sees in it — the same construction as "a night sleepless" meaning a night in which one does not sleep. The function is stated plainly: light exists so that vision is possible, and vision exists so that work is possible.

**Two Purposes: Bounty and Reckoning**

The verse names two ends. The first, *li-tabtaghū faḍlan min rabbikum* — "that you might seek bounty from your Lord" — is economic. The word *faḍl* means surplus, grace, superabundance; not mere survival but increase. The day exists for the pursuit of livelihood, and the Qur'an never treats that pursuit as inferior to worship. The same phrase appears at 62:10, *"And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God."* Work is not a distraction from religion; the day was made for it. The second, *wa li-taʿlamū ʿadada al-sinīna wa-l-ḥisāb* — "that you might know the number of the years and the reckoning" — is calendrical. Compare 10:5, *"It is He who made the sun a shining glory and the moon a light, and determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning,"* and 2:189, *"They ask thee about the new moons. Say: they are appointed times for mankind and for the pilgrimage."* Time is knowable only because it is *measured*, and it is measurable only because it is *cyclical*. A universe without recurrence would be a universe without history.

**Why the Calendar Is a Religious Fact, Not a Neutral One**

The verse's insistence that reckoning is a *purpose* of creation is easily overlooked and profoundly important. Islamic worship is time-structured: the five prayers depend on the sun's position, Ramadan on the moon's sighting, the pilgrimage on a lunar month, zakāt on the completion of a year. A religion whose obligations are indexed to celestial motion requires a cosmos in which that motion is regular, knowable, and divinely intended. The verse therefore grounds the entire structure of Islamic practice in the physics of the sky. It also explains why Muslim civilization developed astronomy so intensively: the calendar was not a curiosity but an act of obedience.

**"And We Have Expounded Everything in Detail"**

*Wa kulla shayʾin faṣṣalnāhu tafṣīlā* — the verb *faṣṣala* means to set out in detail, to distinguish item from item, to articulate. The cognate adverb intensifies: fully detailed, nothing left vague. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as God's having explained things clearly in the Qur'an. The same description is applied to God's signs at 6:97–98, 7:174, 11:1, and to the Book at 7:52:

> *"a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."*

The claim is audacious and the commentators defend it carefully. "Everything" does not mean every fact about the physical universe — the Qur'an explicitly disclaims that at 6:50, *"I do not say to you that I possess the treasuries of God, nor that I know the Unseen."* It means everything a human being needs for guidance: the nature of God, the obligations of the soul, the structure of justice, the reality of the Hereafter. On those matters, the Book leaves nothing essential unexplained.

****Maḥawnā: The Effaced Sign and the Borrowed Light****

The verb *maḥā* — to efface, to wipe out — is applied by the majority of the early commentators, following Ibn ʿAbbās, to the moon, and the reading yields one of the Qur'an's most elegant natural metaphors. The sign of the night is described as *effaced* because the moon has no light of its own: it is a body that reflects, waxing and waning, sometimes absent altogether. The sign of the day, by contrast, is *mubṣirah* — sight-giving, blazing, self-evident. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the report that the effacement refers to the darkness the moon carries, and al-Zamakhsharī notes the grammatical idiom by which the day is called "seeing" when what is meant is that one sees *in* it, as one says "a sleepless night" for a night in which one does not sleep. The theological content of both readings is identical: everything luminous in the created order is reflecting a light that is not its own, which is what 24:35 asserts in a single phrase — *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth."* The verse then names two purposes, and their pairing is what makes it more than cosmology. The first is economic: *li-tabtaghū faḍlan min rabbikum*, that you may seek of your Lord's bounty — the day exists for work, and the Qur'an nowhere treats the pursuit of livelihood as inferior to worship, as 62:10 confirms: *"And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God."* The second is calendrical: *wa li-taʿlamū ʿadada al-sinīna wa-l-ḥisāb*, that you may know the number of years and the reckoning. This is not incidental detail. Islamic obligation is indexed to celestial motion — the five prayers to the sun's position, Ramadan and the pilgrimage to the moon, zakāt to the completion of a year — so a religion of this kind requires a cosmos whose motion is regular, knowable, and intended. The verse grounds the entire structure of practice in the physics of the sky, and it is why the calculation of time was understood in Muslim civilization as an act of obedience rather than a technical specialty.

**The Verse's Argumentative Function**

Coming immediately after verse 11's diagnosis of human haste, the verse performs a specific task. The hasty man cannot wait; the ordered cosmos teaches waiting. Day follows night with a regularity that no human can accelerate. The one who internalizes the rhythm of the sky learns the discipline that haste destroys — and he learns, from the same sky, that the One who measures the years is also the One who measures his life.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:13

> **And [for] every man We have fastened his omen upon his neck, and We shall bring it forth for him on the Day of Resurrection as a book he will meet wide open.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ṭāʾir: The Bird That Became Destiny**

*Wa kulla insānin alzamnāhu ṭāʾirahu fī ʿunuqihi* — "every man, We have fastened his *ṭāʾir* to his neck." The word *ṭāʾir* derives from *ṭ-y-r*, to fly; it means a bird, and by extension an omen — because the pre-Islamic Arabs, like many ancient peoples, practiced augury, reading the flight and behavior of birds as portents of fortune. Al-Rāzī explains the semantic history: an Arab undertaking a journey would startle a bird; if it flew to the right he proceeded, if to the left he turned back. The bird became, in language, the thing it signified — one's fortune, one's lot. The Qur'an takes the pagan instrument and converts it. There is no bird; there is no flight to read. What is fastened to your neck is your *own* record. The verse thus performs a piece of theological demolition and reconstruction in a single image: the omen you sought in the sky is in fact the deed you performed on the ground. Compare the Qur'an's other treatment of augury, when the people of Thamūd said to Ṣāliḥ, *"We augur evil (iṭṭayyarnā) from you and those with you"* — and he answered, *"Your augury (ṭāʾirukum) is with God"* (27:47). The same word, the same correction: your fortune is not in the bird; it is in God's knowledge and your own action.

**"Fastened to His Neck": The Anatomy of the Image**

The verb *alzamnā* means We have made it adhere, We have bound it, We have made it inseparable. And the location is the *neck*. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī explain why: the neck is where honor and shame are displayed. A necklace of status hangs there; a chain of servitude is fastened there. Whatever is on the neck is visible, cannot be set down, cannot be forgotten, and travels with the person wherever he goes. The image is exactly right for a moral record. You cannot put it down to sleep. You cannot hand it to another. It does not grow heavier as you carry it — it *is* you carrying it. Modern readers recognize the phenomenon in a different vocabulary: the debt that follows the borrower, the criminal record that follows the applicant, the reputation that precedes the arrival. The Qur'an's claim is that the moral version is more inescapable than any of these.

**Two Readings: Deeds or Decree**

The commentators divide. Many — Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī — take the *ṭāʾir* as one's deeds, which portend bliss or wretchedness exactly as the bird was thought to portend fortune. Others take it as the divine decree (*qadar*) apportioned to each individual, supported by the traditions that every human being is born with an apportioned lifespan, provision, offspring, and ultimate state. Al-Qurṭubī records a third reading: that the *ṭāʾir* is moral responsibility itself (*taklīf*) — the inescapable fact that every human being is under obligation, and cannot escape being answerable. The three readings converge on one practical conclusion, and this is why the debate never troubled the exegetes as much as it might have: whether the record on your neck is what you did, what was apportioned to you, or the fact that you are answerable — in every case, you cannot remove it, and it will be opened.

**"As a Book He Will Meet Wide Open"**

*Wa nukhriju lahu yawma al-qiyāmati kitāban yalqāhu manshūrā* — *manshūr* means spread open, unrolled, published. The participle implies that the book is already open when he encounters it; there is no unsealing, no examination, no delay. The same scene appears at 18:49:

> *"And the Book will be set down, and thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it, and they will say: 'Alas for us! What kind of Book is this that leaves out nothing small or great but has enumerated it!'"*

And at 69:19–25, where the one given his book in his right hand says *"Here, read my Book! I knew that I would meet my reckoning,"* while the one given it in his left hand says *"I wish my Book had not been given to me."* And at 84:7–10, where the one given his book in his right hand receives an easy reckoning, while the one given it behind his back cries for destruction.

**Why the Book Is Wide Open to Its Owner**

The openness of the book is theologically significant. It is not merely that God sees the contents; it is that the *owner* sees them, completely, with no capacity for the self-deception that operated in this world. Al-Rāzī offers the most penetrating account of what the book actually is: every deed is like a drop of water falling on rock — individually small, but each leaves an impression, and repeated deeds carve channels. The soul is the rock. The book is not an external ledger brought from a heavenly archive; it is the soul itself, inscribed by its own choices, made legible at last. Al-Qurṭubī preserves the formulation that captures this: *"Your tongue is its pen, your saliva is its ink, your limbs are its pages; you dictate it to your own memory."* The author of the book and its reader are the same person.

****The Neck, and the Prohibition of Reading Omens****

The verse performs a piece of theological demolition and reconstruction in a single image, and the demolition is aimed at a specific pre-Islamic practice. The *ṭāʾir* is the bird, and from it the omen, because the Arabs — like the Romans with their *auspicia* — determined the advisability of an undertaking by the flight of startled birds. The Prophet ﷺ abolished the practice explicitly. Al-Bukhārī (5757) and Muslim (2220) record:

> *"There is no ʿadwā [contagion as the pagans understood it], no ṭiyarah [augury], no hāmah, and no ṣafar."* (al-Bukhārī 5757)

And al-Bukhārī (5776) preserves the positive replacement: *"The best of it is al-faʾl — the good word one hears."* The distinction the ḥadīth draws is precise: augury reads a random external event as a verdict on one's future, and thereby transfers the sense of agency from the person to the world; *faʾl* is a hopeful word that encourages action without claiming knowledge. The verse completes the substitution by relocating the omen entirely: what is fastened to your neck is not a bird's direction of flight but your own record. The anatomy of the image is exact, and al-Rāzī explains why the neck was chosen. The neck is where both honor and shame are displayed: the necklace of rank hangs there, and the collar of servitude is fastened there. Whatever is on the neck is visible to others, inseparable from the person, and travels wherever he goes. Al-Ṭabarī adds that the neck is the point at which the head is joined to the body — the seat of direction — so that what is bound there determines where the person goes. The modern reader recognizes the phenomenon under other names: the criminal record that precedes the applicant, the debt that follows the borrower, the reputation that arrives before the person. The verse's claim is that the moral version is more inescapable than any of them, and that its publication is deferred rather than cancelled.

**The Psychological Truth Beneath the Image**

The verse describes an eschatological scene, but its accuracy about ordinary psychology is what makes it land. Every human being already carries a private, largely unread record — of the promise broken, the kindness omitted, the sentence spoken in anger. Most of life consists of not reading it. Distraction, work, noise, and self-justification function as the closed cover. The Day of Resurrection, in this verse, is not the arrival of new information; it is the removal of the cover. *Manshūrā* — spread open. The terror of the scene is not that God will accuse you, but that you will finally agree with Him.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:14

> **"Read your book! On this Day, your soul suffices as a reckoner against you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Read**

*Iqraʾ kitābaka* — "Read your book." Two words, and they contain the entire moral theory of the Qur'an. The verb *iqraʾ* is the same verb with which the revelation began: *"Recite (iqraʾ) in the name of thy Lord who created"* (96:1). The first word revealed to the Prophet ﷺ is given here to every human being on the Day of Judgment. The one who refused to read the Book of God in this world will be commanded to read the book of himself in the next. The command is addressed to the individual in the singular. There is no collective reading, no tribal record, no delegation. The Qur'an is emphatic that the reckoning is personal: *"And every one of them will come to Him on the Day of Resurrection, alone (fardan)"* (19:95).

**Why "Read" Rather Than "Be Read To"**

The choice of the active imperative is decisive. The individual is not a defendant hearing a prosecutor; he is a reader reading his own text. Al-Rāzī explains that in reading the book, the person renders judgment on himself — because the book consists solely of his own acts, and no one is better placed to assess them. The verdict, in a profound sense, is self-pronounced. This is the Qur'an's answer to the complaint that divine judgment is arbitrary: it is not. The evidence is produced by the accused, in his own hand, from his own life.

**"Your Soul Suffices as a Reckoner Against You"**

*Kafā bi-nafsika al-yawma ʿalayka ḥasībā* — *ḥasīb* is the one who reckons, computes, audits; from the same root as *ḥisāb*, reckoning, and *ḥasab*, number. The construction *kafā bi-* means "suffices" — your soul is *enough* as an auditor against you. Not merely competent; sufficient. No external witness is required. The Qur'an develops this idea in the testimony of the limbs, which is among its most vivid eschatological images. At 36:65: *"This Day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will testify to what they earned."* At 41:20–22: *"And you were not hiding yourselves lest your hearing, your sight, or your skins testify against you; but you assumed that God does not know much of what you do... And they will say to their skins, 'Why did you testify against us?' They will say, 'God, who gives speech to all things, gave us speech.'"* And at 24:24: *"On the Day when their tongues, their hands, and their feet will testify against them for what they used to do."* The logic is severe and exact: the instruments you used to commit the act will be the witnesses to it. The hand that took, the tongue that lied, the eye that lingered — each will be given speech. The person who believed his body was a neutral tool discovers it was a recording device.

**God Does Not Wrong: The Guarantee Beneath the Severity**

The self-reckoning is framed by a doctrine the Qur'an repeats so often it functions as a refrain: God does not wrong people; they wrong themselves. *"Truly God does not wrong mankind in anything, but mankind wrong themselves"* (10:44). *"And God did not wrong them, but they wronged themselves"* (16:33). *"Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment"* (17:15 — the very next verse). The sequence is deliberate: verse 14 establishes that the record is self-authored; verse 15 establishes that the consequence is self-incurred. Together they close every avenue of complaint.

**The Mercy Hidden in the Verse**

Read only as threat, the verse is unbearable. But the classical commentators note something else: if the soul suffices as a reckoner, then the soul that has repented has already begun to read. The act of reading one's book is available *now*. This is the entire practice of *muḥāsabah* — self-reckoning — which the second Caliph ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb formulated in the sentence the tradition preserves:

> *"Reckon yourselves before you are reckoned, and weigh yourselves before you are weighed; for it is lighter for you to reckon yourselves today in preparation for the great Reckoning."* (reported in the ascetic tradition; al-Tirmidhī 2459 in similar wording)

The practice is not an invention of the pious but an anticipation of the verse. The one who reads his book daily will not be surprised by it on the Day. This is why the great spiritual writers made nightly self-examination a discipline: the book is being written in real time, and the cover is only temporarily closed.

****Two Reckonings, and the One That Is Private****

The verse establishes that the decisive judgment is self-pronounced, and the Prophetic tradition describes the scene in which it happens. Al-Bukhārī (6539) and Muslim (2768) record from ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim:

> *"There is none of you but that his Lord will speak with him, with no interpreter between them. He will look to his right and see nothing but what he sent forward; he will look to his left and see nothing but what he sent forward; he will look before him and see nothing but the Fire facing him. So guard yourselves from the Fire, even with half a date."* (al-Bukhārī 6539)

The absence of an interpreter is the detail that matters most, because it is the ḥadīth's parallel to this verse's *"your soul suffices as a reckoner against you."* There is no external prosecutor whose argument the accused may contest; the conversation is between a person and the record he authored, conducted in a language that admits no ambiguity. Al-Qurṭubī's formulation of the book remains the sharpest available: *"Your tongue is its pen, your saliva is its ink, your limbs are its pages; you dictate it to your own memory."* The practical discipline that the tradition builds on this verse is *muḥāsabat al-nafs* — the daily self-reckoning — which ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb formulated in the sentence preserved by al-Tirmidhī (2459): *"Reckon yourselves before you are reckoned, and weigh yourselves before you are weighed; for it is lighter for you to reckon yourselves today in preparation for the great Reckoning."* The practice is not an ascetic invention but an anticipation of the verse, and its value is diagnostic rather than punitive. Most of ordinary life consists in not reading the record — the promise broken, the kindness omitted, the sentence spoken in anger — and the energy spent maintaining that non-reading is measurable in the avoidance of silence. The command *iqraʾ kitābaka* is therefore available now, and the person who reads his book daily will not be surprised by it on the Day.

**The Verse as an Argument for Moral Seriousness**

Stripped of its eschatological setting, the verse makes a claim about the human condition that even a secular reader can test: you already know. Beneath the justifications, beneath the narratives you tell others and yourself, there is an accurate internal account of what you did and why. The energy spent constructing the alternative narrative is measurable — in the avoidance of silence, in the reflexive self-defense, in the anger at anyone who reads too closely. The Qur'an's claim is simply that this internal knowledge is not a private opinion but the actual record, and that the Day of Resurrection is the day it is published. The command *iqraʾ kitābaka* is therefore not only future; it is the most useful sentence a person can obey today.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:15

> **Whosoever is rightly guided is only rightly guided for the sake of his own soul, and whosoever is astray is only astray to its detriment. None shall bear the burden of another. And never do We punish till We have sent a messenger.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Doctrines in a Single Verse**

This verse compresses three of the Qur'an's most fundamental principles into two sentences, and their order is not accidental. First: guidance and error are personally consequential. Second: no one carries another's load. Third: punishment presupposes a messenger. The three together form the Qur'an's complete theory of justice — responsibility is individual, consequence is proportionate, and liability requires notification.

**The Personal Return of Every Act**

*Man ihtadā fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsihi wa man ḍalla fa-innamā yaḍillu ʿalayhā* — the reflexive structure is emphatic. The one who is guided is guided *for his own soul*; the one who errs errs *against it*. The verse has already appeared in essence at 17:7 in the Israelite cycle, and it recurs at 10:108, 27:40, 31:12, 39:41, 40:28, and 45:15. Al-Ṭabarī explains the underlying point: God is not increased by obedience nor diminished by disobedience. The moral order is not a treasury from which God draws profit; it is a description of what happens to souls. The practical consequence is immense and easily missed. Because the return is personal, no proxy arrangement works. A person cannot inherit another's righteousness, cannot be carried on someone else's piety, cannot outsource his standing before God to his family, his shaykh, his nation, or his party. This is the Qur'an's most leveling doctrine: it dissolves every hierarchy of spiritual credit.

**"No Bearer of Burdens Shall Bear the Burden of Another"**

*Wa lā taziru wāziratun wizra ukhrā* — the verb *wazara* means to bear a heavy load; *wizr* is the load, and by extension the sin, and also the mountain-shelter (as in 75:11, *"No! There is no refuge (wazīr)"*). The phrase is repeated at 6:164, 35:18, 39:7, and 53:38 — always in the same wording, which indicates a settled formula. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as the abolition of a pre-Islamic Arab practice: the custom of holding a tribe or a family liable for the crime of one of its members, and of blood vengeance falling on whoever was available rather than on the perpetrator. The verse strikes at collective punishment as a principle. The commentators raise an apparent tension: does not the one who *leads others astray* carry their burden? The resolution is careful and important. The misleader carries the burden of his own misleading, not the burden of the misled person's act. At 16:25: *"That they may bear their burdens in full on the Day of Resurrection, and some of the burdens of those whom they misled without knowledge. Truly evil is what they bear."* The additional burden is the burden of *causing*, which is his own deed — not a transfer of the victim's guilt. At 37:32–33 the misled say to their misleaders, *"You used to come at us from the right hand"* — and are answered that they themselves did not believe. Both parties are answerable for their own act.

**"And Never Do We Punish Till We Have Sent a Messenger"**

*Wa mā kunnā muʿadhibīna ḥattā nabʿatha rasūlā* — and this is the verse's theological crown. It is one of the clearest statements in the Qur'an of the principle that divine punishment requires prior notification. Compare 6:131: *"That is because thy Lord would not destroy the towns for their wrongdoing while their people were heedless"* — that is, before a messenger had informed them. And 26:208–209: *"And no town did We destroy but that it had warners, as a reminder — and We are never unjust."* And 28:59:

> *"And thy Lord would not destroy the towns till He had raised in their mother-city a messenger reciting unto them Our signs."*

This is the Qur'an's answer to the most serious objection to revealed religion: how can a just God condemn those who never heard? The answer is that He does not. Liability is not retroactive and it is not inherited from Adam. It arises only when the message arrives. The classical theologians debated the status of those who lived in periods between prophets (*ahl al-fatra*) — those who received no messenger at all. The dominant Sunni position, drawing on exactly this verse, is that such people are not punished for what they could not have known. Al-Ghazālī devoted a section of his *Fayṣal al-Tafriqah* to the question and concluded that the vast majority of humankind in his own day — including those in distant lands who had received only distorted reports of Islam — fell outside the category of the condemned, because they had never received the message in a form that made its truth compelling. Ibn Taymiyyah took a similar line. The verse is the ground on which that generosity stands.

****Before the Messenger Comes: The People of the Interval****

The verse's final clause — *wa mā kunnā muʿadhibīna ḥattā nabʿatha rasūlā* — became the scriptural ground for one of the most consequential discussions in Islamic theology: the status of those who lived where no messenger reached them, the *ahl al-fatra*. The question was not academic. It arose concretely whenever Muslim jurists encountered peoples beyond the reach of the message, and al-Ghazālī addressed it at length in the *Fayṣal al-tafriqah bayna al-islām wa-l-zandaqah*, concluding that the overwhelming majority of the humans of his own century — including those in distant lands who had received only distorted and caricatured reports of Islam — fell outside the category of the condemned, because what reached them was not the message but a distortion of it that made its truth impossible to recognize. Ibn Taymiyyah argued the same conclusion from the same verse. The principle the verse establishes is that liability is not inherited and not retroactive: it arises at the moment of notification and not before. This distinguishes the Qur'anic position sharply from any system in which human beings are condemned for an ancestral fault. Compare 2:134, *"Those are a people who have passed; theirs is what they earned, and yours is what you earn, and you will not be asked concerning what they used to do"* — a verse that forecloses the transfer of guilt in both directions. The Prophetic tradition supplies the anthropological premise: al-Bukhārī (1385) records, *"Every child is born upon the fiṭrah; it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian."* The human being arrives oriented toward recognition and is deflected by circumstance; the messenger's arrival is what converts that orientation into a decision for which he is answerable. Read together, the three clauses of this verse form the Qur'an's complete theory of justice — responsibility is individual, burden is non-transferable, and liability requires notification — and each clause closes an avenue of complaint that the others leave open.

**Why the Three Belong Together**

The sequence answers a chain of objections. If consequences are personal, is that not harsh? No — because no one is burdened with another's sin, and each carries only his own. If each carries his own, is that not unfair to those born into ignorance? No — because no one is punished before a messenger comes. Every avenue of complaint is closed in order, and the closing is not by assertion but by principle. The verse is, in effect, the Qur'an's declaration that its God is not arbitrary.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:16

> **And when We desire to destroy a town, We command those who live a life of luxury within it; yet they commit iniquity therein. Thus the Word comes due against it and We annihilate it completely.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Elite Are Addressed First**

*Wa idhā aradnā an nuhlika qaryatan amarnā mutrafīhā* — "and when We intend to destroy a town, We command its *mutrafūn*." The word *mutraf* is from *t-r-f*, meaning to be given ease and abundance; the *mutraf* is the one who lives in luxury, the pampered, the soft-living elite. The Qur'an uses the term consistently as a moral category: *"And We sent no warner to a town but its mutrafūn said: 'We disbelieve in that with which you were sent'"* (34:34). And at 23:33, the chiefs of Noah's people; at 11:116, *"And thy Lord would not destroy the towns unjustly while their people were reformers"*; at 43:23:

> *"And likewise We sent no warner before thee into any town but its mutrafūn said: 'We found our fathers upon a way, and we follow in their footsteps.'"*

The pattern is consistent enough to be a law: the first to reject the prophets are the comfortable. Not because luxury is inherently evil, but because the comfortable have the most to lose from a message that demands redistribution of wealth, status, and moral authority. The Qur'an's sociology of disbelief is precise and unsentimental.

**The Difficulty of "We Command"**

*Amarnā* — "We commanded" — is one of the most discussed words in the sūrah. Three readings are preserved. The first, held by al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ and most commentators, is that God commands the *mutrafūn* to belief and obedience, and they respond with iniquity. On this reading *amarnā* is the command of the Law, and *fa-fasaqū* — "and they transgressed" — is their refusal. The command and the transgression are in the correct moral order: command first, disobedience second. The second, al-Zamakhsharī's, is that the verse means God commands them to commit iniquity — but he immediately insists this must be read metaphorically, since the Qur'an states categorically at 7:28, *"Say: God does not command indecency."* For al-Zamakhsharī the meaning is that by granting them the means, the power, and the impunity to transgress, it is *as if* He had commanded it. The third, recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, is a variant vocalization: *ammarnā* — "We made them rulers" — from *imārah*, authority. On this reading the verse says: when We intend to destroy a town, We place its luxurious class in positions of authority, and they then commit iniquity, and the sentence falls. The three readings converge on the same historical observation — that the ruin of communities begins at the top — and the exegetes' refusal to read the verse as divine authorship of sin is itself a datum worth noting: the entire classical tradition treats 7:28 as an interpretive constraint on this verse.

**Al-Rāzī on the Timing of Destruction**

Al-Rāzī's contribution is a distinction of real philosophical value. "When We desire to destroy a town" does not mean that God suddenly forms a new intention; it means that the time approaches for the destruction of a town whose fate is already known to Him. God's knowledge is eternal; the *timing* of the decree is what unfolds. The command to the *mutrafūn* at that moment is therefore genuine — they are truly commanded, and they truly disobey — even though the outcome is foreknown. This is the same reconciliation of foreknowledge and freedom the commentators applied to verse 4, and it is the standard Sunni position: foreknowledge is not causation.

**"Thus the Word Comes Due Against It"**

*Fa-ḥaqqa ʿalayhā al-qawl* — the verb *ḥaqqa* means to become true, to be established, to fall due. The "word" (*qawl*, elsewhere in the Qur'an *kalimah*) is the threatened sentence. The same formula appears at 10:96, 28:63, 32:13, 36:7, 37:3, 39:19, 40:6, 41:25, 46:18. Its recurrence establishes that divine punishment operates on a schedule with a fixed endpoint: respite is real, but it expires. The verse therefore teaches a doctrine of delay that is neither mercy nor indifference. The town is not destroyed immediately; it is given time, warned, commanded, and observed. When the word falls due, it is because the period of opportunity has been consumed, not because God lost patience.

**"And We Annihilate It Completely"**

*Fa-dammarnāhā tadmīrā* — *dammara* means to destroy utterly; the cognate adverb *tadmīrā* intensifies it to total demolition. Al-Rāzī explains the word as uprooting from the foundations. The same verb describes the fate of Pharaoh's people at 7:137 and the destroyed towns at 25:39. The Qur'an's use of total-destruction vocabulary is not decorative: it corresponds to the archaeological reality of the ruined cities the Arabs passed on their caravan routes — Ḥijr, Sodom, Maʾrib, Petra — which the Qur'an repeatedly invites them to walk through and look at. *"And how many a town have We destroyed whose people were wrongdoers, so it is fallen in upon its roofs!"* (22:45).

****Amarnā Read Against 7:28: How the Tradition Constrains a Word****

The interpretive history of *amarnā* is a case study in how the exegetical tradition disciplines a difficult word by subordinating it to a clear one. Read literally, the verse says that when God intends to destroy a town He commands its luxurious class — and they then commit iniquity, which places the command and the transgression in an unsettling order. Al-Zamakhsharī confronts the difficulty directly and resolves it by insisting on a metaphorical reading: since the Qur'an states categorically at 7:28, *"Say: God does not command indecency (al-faḥshāʾ); do you attribute to God what you do not know?"*, the command here cannot be a command to evil, and what is meant is that by granting such people the means, the power, and the impunity to transgress, it is *as if* He had commanded it. Al-Rāzī, writing from the other side of the same debate, accepts the literal command as a command to *obedience* which the *mutrafūn* refuse, and reconciles the foreknowledge by distinguishing eternal knowledge from temporal occurrence. What the episode reveals is a working rule of classical exegesis: an ambiguous verse is read in the light of an unambiguous one, and 7:28 functions as such a *muḥkam*. The third reading — the variant vocalization *ammarnā*, "We made them rulers" — is preserved by al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī precisely because it harmonizes all three concerns at once: God places the luxurious in authority, they transgress, and the sentence falls. The sociological observation is identical under every reading, and it locates the origin of communal ruin where ordinary analysis does not look. When an institution fails, the visible symptoms appear at the bottom — disorder, poverty, crime — while the cause sits at the top, among those with the most to lose from correction and the most power to prevent it. The Qur'an's *mutrafūn* are not merely the rich; they are the beneficiaries of the existing order, which is why 34:34 records their answer to every warner: *"We disbelieve in that with which you were sent."*

**The Sociological Lesson**

The verse's practical force is that it locates the origin of communal ruin precisely where ordinary analysis does not look. When a society decays, the visible symptoms are usually at the bottom — crime, disorder, poverty. The Qur'an places the cause at the top — the *mutrafūn*, those who benefit from the existing order and therefore resist the reformer. Any reader who has watched an institution fail — a company, a family, a state — recognizes the mechanism: the people with the most to lose from correction are the people with the most power to prevent it, and they use it. The destruction that follows is not a punishment of the weak for the sins of the strong; it is the collapse of a structure whose leaders refused to repair it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:17

> **How many a generation have We destroyed after Noah! Thy Lord suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants, Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Noah as the Watershed of Sacred History**

*Wa kam ahlaknā min al-qurūni min baʿdi Nūḥ* — "and how many generations have We destroyed after Noah." Noah occupies a specific structural position in Qur'anic history: he is the first messenger to a people who were destroyed for rejecting him. Compare 7:59–64, the account of Noah and the flood, and 11:25–49. The verse's phrase *min baʿdi Nūḥ* therefore means something precise: from that first destruction onward, the pattern has repeated — ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot, Midian, the towns of the prophets, Pharaoh's court. The Qur'an returns to these ruins constantly: 6:6, *"Have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them?"*; 7:4, *"And how many a town have We destroyed!"*; 19:74; 20:128; 21:11; 22:45. The function of the destroyed generations in Qur'anic rhetoric is evidential. They are not stories; they are *precedents*. A legal argument cites precedent to establish that a rule has been applied before; the Qur'an cites destroyed cities to establish that the rule it announces has been enforced before.

**Kam: The Indefinite Number as Rhetoric**

*Kam* — "how many" — is used here in its exclamatory (*khabariyyah*) sense, indicating multiplicity beyond counting. The indefinite quantity is more effective than a number would be. A specific figure invites the reader to catalogue and dismiss; an unbounded figure places him inside a pattern he cannot totalize. The same device appears at 2:243:

> *"Didst thou not consider those who went forth from their homes, being thousands, out of fear of death?"*

And at 22:45, *"How many a town have We destroyed!"*

**"Thy Lord Suffices as One Aware, Seeing"**

*Wa kafā bi-rabbika bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — *khabīr* is the one who has intimate, detailed knowledge, from *kh-b-r*, meaning to test and thereby to know something by direct acquaintance; *baṣīr* is the one who sees, from *b-ṣ-r*, denoting not merely sight but insight. The pairing is one of the Qur'an's characteristic divine name-pairs and appears at 4:58, 25:58, 67:19. The verse's closing is a reassurance directed at the Prophet ﷺ and at every believer who has watched wrongdoing go apparently unpunished. The generations were destroyed; the record was kept; nothing was missed. *Kafā* — "it suffices" — is the same verb used in verse 14: *"your soul suffices as a reckoner against you."* The sufficiency of divine awareness answers the insufficiency of human justice.

**Why This Verse Follows Verse 16**

Verse 16 states the law of communal destruction; verse 17 documents its enforcement history. The sequence is legal reasoning: principle, then precedent, then the assurance that the judge has the file. The reader who doubts whether the law will actually be applied is pointed to the archaeological record — the ruined towns the Quraysh walked past on the caravan route to Syria, the very route the Prophet ﷺ had traveled as a merchant.

****The Ruins on the Caravan Road: Evidence Rather Than Story****

The generations invoked in this verse were not abstractions to the first audience; they were landmarks. The Quraysh caravan route to Syria passed within sight of the Nabataean rock-cut tombs of al-Ḥijr, the settlement of Thamūd, and the Qur'an addresses them as sightseers rather than as students of history: 15:80, *"And the Companions of al-Ḥijr denied the messengers"*; and 27:69, *"Say: Travel in the land and see what was the end of the criminals"*; and 22:46, *"Have they not traveled in the land, so that they might have hearts by which to understand, and ears by which to hear? Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts."* The ruined cities function in Qur'anic rhetoric exactly as precedent functions in a legal argument: they establish that the rule being announced has been enforced before, and therefore can be enforced again. The word *qarn* — generation, or the people of an era — is used deliberately in the indefinite plural *kam min al-qurūn*, and al-Zamakhsharī notes that the unbounded quantity is more effective than a number would be. A specified figure invites the hearer to catalogue the cases and dismiss them as a closed set; an unnumbered multiplicity places him inside a pattern he cannot totalize. The verse's closing pair, *khabīran baṣīrā*, supplies the reassurance that the pattern requires: the One who destroyed those generations is *khabīr* — acquainted by direct testing, not merely informed — and *baṣīr*, seeing with insight rather than glancing. It is the same pairing that closed verse 12 of the sūrah and will close verse 30 and verse 96, and its recurrence marks the points at which the sūrah addresses the believer's sense that wrongdoing goes unrecorded. The destroyed generations are the answer to that sense: the file was kept, the accounting occurred, and nothing was missed.

**The Comfort and the Warning in One Sentence**

The verse cuts both ways, and the classical commentators read it in both directions. For the oppressed, "thy Lord suffices as One Aware of the sins of His servants" is comfort: the tyrant's crimes are recorded by a witness who cannot be bribed, intimidated, or deceived. For the oppressor, the same sentence is a warning of unusual precision: *bi-dhunūbi ʿibādihi* — "of the sins of His servants" — not of some servants but of all, not of the public ones but of the concealed ones. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said that the believer's fear of God is proportional to his knowledge of God, and this verse is the ground of that fear: the accounting is complete. The practical application for an ordinary life is narrower but just as real. Every person carries private wrongs that no human court will ever see — the advantage quietly taken, the reputation quietly damaged, the promise quietly broken. The verse asserts that these are not outside the record. That assertion is either terrifying or consoling, depending entirely on which side of the transaction one stands.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:18

> **Whosoever would desire the ephemeral, We hasten for him therein whatsoever We will for whomsoever We desire. Then We appointed Hell for him, wherein he shall burn, blameworthy, banished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-ʿĀjilah: The Hurried Thing**

*Man kāna yurīdu al-ʿājilah* — "whoever desires *al-ʿājilah*." The word is the active participle of *ʿ-j-l*, haste — the same root as *ʿajūl* in verse 11. *Al-ʿājilah* literally means "the hastening one," and it is the Qur'an's standing name for this world: the world of immediacy, of what arrives quickly. The pairing is fixed throughout the revelation: *al-ʿājilah* and *al-ākhirah*, the hasty and the final. See 75:20–21, *"Nay, but you love the hastening (al-ʿājilah) and neglect the Hereafter (al-ākhirah)."* And 76:27, *"Truly these love the hastening and leave behind a heavy Day."* The naming is a diagnosis, not a description. The world is not called "the near" (*al-dunyā* also carries the sense of nearness, from *d-n-w*); it is called "the hasty" — the thing that comes fast. Its defining characteristic is speed of delivery, which is precisely what makes it attractive and precisely what makes it deceptive.

**The Connection to Verse 11**

The link between verse 11 and verse 18 is the hinge of this section. Man is *ʿajūl* — hasty by nature — and therefore prays for evil as he prays for good. Here, whoever desires *al-ʿājilah* is the same hasty creature, now acting on his hastiness at the level of a whole life-orientation rather than a single request. The psychological root is identical: the vividness of the immediate overwhelms the reality of the distant.

**"We Hasten for Him Therein Whatsoever We Will for Whomsoever We Desire"**

*Nuʿajjil lahu fīhā mā nashāʾ li-man nurīd* — the verse's threefold qualification is deliberate and devastating. God hastens for the seeker of the immediate: (1) what He wills — not what the seeker asked for; (2) for whom He wills — not for every seeker; (3) in the measure He wills. The one who has oriented his life toward getting does not thereby get. Al-Rāzī draws out the irony: the very thing desired is withheld from most who desire it, and given to some who do not. The worldly reward is not a wage; it is a dispensation. This dismantles the most common error in religious reasoning — the assumption that material success indicates divine approval. The Qur'an is explicit that it does not: *"Let not man consider that when We provide for him and honor him, We are honoring him. Nay, but We are testing him"* (89:15–16). And: *"Do they think that what We provide them of wealth and sons — We hasten unto them good things? Nay, but they do not perceive"* (23:55–56).

**"Then We Appointed Hell for Him"**

*Thumma jaʿalnā lahu jahannama yaṣlāhā madhmūman madḥūrā* — the verb *jaʿalnā* (We made, We appointed) is in the past tense, though the event is future. The past tense for future divine acts is a standard Qur'anic device indicating certainty: what God has decreed is as good as done. *Yaṣlāhā* — he will roast in it, from *ṣ-l-y*, to be exposed to fire. And the two adjectives complete the humiliation: *madhmūm* — blamed, condemned, the opposite of praiseworthy; *madḥūr* — banished, driven away, expelled from mercy. The pairing of blame and banishment is exact. The seeker of the immediate sought status and pleasure; he receives blame and expulsion. The one who lived for reputation dies disgraced; the one who lived for proximity to the powerful ends driven from all proximity.

**Who Counts as a Seeker of the Ephemeral**

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī extend the category beyond obvious sinners to include those who perform apparently pious acts for worldly ends — the one who gives charity for reputation, who learns knowledge for a position, who fasts to be seen. Their deeds look like the deeds of the seekers of the Hereafter; their orientation is the opposite. This is the doctrine of *riyāʾ*, ostentation, which the Prophet ﷺ called "the lesser shirk." Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record from Jundub:

> *"Whoever acts to be heard, God will make him heard [i.e., expose him]; and whoever acts to be seen, God will make him seen."* (al-Bukhārī 6499; Muslim 2986)

The verse therefore does not distinguish between the open sinner and the hidden hypocrite by their acts but by their orientation — which is why verse 19 will make intention one of the three conditions of reward.

****Al-ʿĀjilah Is Not Evil — It Is Fast****

The Qur'an's name for this world is not a moral verdict but a description of speed, and the distinction is theologically important. *Al-ʿājilah* is the active participle of *ʿ-j-l*, haste — "the hastening one" — and it is paired throughout the revelation with *al-ākhirah*, the final. 75:20–21 states the diagnosis in two lines: *"Nay, but you love the hastening, and neglect the Hereafter."* 76:27 adds the consequence: *"Truly these love the hastening and leave behind a heavy Day."* The world is not called corrupt or vile in this vocabulary; it is called *quick*, and quickness is precisely what makes it attractive and what makes it deceptive. Al-Rāzī's reading of the verse's threefold qualification is what dismantles the inference most people draw from worldly success: God hastens for the seeker *"whatsoever We will"* — not what the seeker asked for — *"for whomsoever We desire"* — not for every seeker. The transaction is therefore not a wage. This is why the Qur'an repeatedly refuses to read prosperity as endorsement. 89:15–16: *"As for man, when his Lord tries him and honors him and favors him, he says: 'My Lord has honored me.' But when He tries him and straitens his provision, he says: 'My Lord has humiliated me.' Nay!"* — the "nay" rejecting both readings at once. And 23:55–56: *"Do they think that what We provide them of wealth and sons — We hasten unto them good things? Nay, but they do not perceive."* The ḥadīth tradition states the believer's relation to this economy in a single image, recorded by Muslim (2956): *"The world is a prison for the believer and a garden for the disbeliever"* — not because the believer suffers more, but because his horizon is elsewhere, so that what satisfies one confines the other. The severity of verse 18 lies in the closure of the transaction rather than in the punishment: you asked for the immediate, you received the immediate, and the door through which anything further would have come has been locked from the inside.

**The Logic of the Verse**

The verse is not saying that worldly success is a punishment; it says that worldly success obtained by an orientation wholly directed at this world is a *completion of the request and nothing more*. You asked for the immediate; you received the immediate; the transaction is closed. There is no further credit. The severity lies in the closure — the door through which the reward would have come has been locked from the inside.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:19

> **And whosoever desires the Hereafter, and endeavors for it earnestly, and is a believer, it is they whose efforts shall be appreciated.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Conditions, Stated in Order**

*Wa man arāda al-ākhirata wa saʿā lahā saʿyahā wa huwa muʾmin* — the verse sets three conditions for the acceptance of effort, and al-Rāzī enumerates them precisely. The first is *intention*: "whoever desires the Hereafter." The orientation must be toward the final, not the immediate. The second is *earnest striving*: "and strives for it its striving" — *saʿā lahā saʿyahā* uses the cognate accusative, an intensifying construction meaning he strives for it *the striving it deserves*, with full effort proportionate to the goal. The third is *faith*: "while he is a believer." Al-Rāzī observes a crucial feature: the verse does not require *success* or *completion*. It requires desire, earnest endeavor, and belief. A person who intends good, strives genuinely, and is prevented by death or incapacity has fulfilled the verse's conditions. This is one of the Qur'an's clearest bases for the doctrine that deeds are judged by intentions — a doctrine al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both cite here.

**The Ḥadīth of Intentions**

The Prophetic formulation is the most frequently cited sentence in Islamic literature, opening al-Bukhārī's Ṣaḥīḥ as its very first entry:

> *"Deeds are only according to intentions, and every man shall have only what he intended. Whoever's emigration was for God and His Messenger, his emigration is for God and His Messenger; and whoever's emigration was for a worldly gain he might obtain, or a woman he might marry, his emigration is for that to which he emigrated."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

Al-Zamakhsharī cites this ḥadīth directly in connection with this verse. The two Hijrah cases in the ḥadīth are outwardly identical — the same journey, the same hardship, the same destination — and they differ only in the invisible orientation. This is the exact structure of verses 18 and 19: two people performing similar acts, receiving categorically different outcomes, distinguished by nothing observable.

**"It Is They Whose Effort Shall Be Appreciated"**

*Fa-ulāʾika kāna saʿyuhum mashkūrā* — *mashkūr* means thanked, appreciated, acknowledged. The word is remarkable: God *thanks* the servant. The Qur'an uses the same language at 4:147, *"What would God do with your punishment if you are grateful and believe? And God is Ever Thankful (shākir), Knowing,"* and at 2:158, *"Truly God is Thankful (shākiran), Knowing."* That the Infinite should describe Himself as thanking the finite is one of the most striking features of Qur'anic theology. Al-Ṭabarī explains that God's "thanks" is His reward — the acknowledgment expressed as recompense. The word also recalls verse 3, where Noah is called *ʿabdan shakūrā*, a thankful servant. The root *sh-k-r* binds the passage together: the grateful servant thanks God, and God thanks the striving servant. The relationship is one of reciprocal acknowledgment, not of cold transaction.

**Why Faith Is the Third Condition and Not the First**

The order — desire, striving, belief — is unusual; one might expect belief first. Al-Rāzī's explanation is instructive: belief is stated last as the *condition of validity* that must be present alongside the other two, not as a prior stage. It functions like the qualification at the end of a legal formula: whatever the striving, and whatever the intention, the whole is accepted *if* the person is a believer. The placement emphasizes that intention and effort without faith are incomplete, exactly as faith without intention and effort are incomplete. This is why the Qur'an's formula *āmanū wa ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt* — "those who believe and do righteous deeds" — appears so persistently. Neither half suffices alone.

****Saʿyahā: The Effort the Goal Deserves****

The grammar of this verse carries its doctrine. *Wa saʿā lahā saʿyahā* uses the cognate accusative (*al-mafʿūl al-muṭlaq*), in which the verb's own verbal noun is repeated in the accusative to intensify it: he strove for it *the striving it deserves*. The construction removes any reading of the verse as satisfied by token effort. Al-Rāzī's enumeration of the three conditions — intention, earnest striving, and belief — is drawn from the sequence itself, and he notes the feature that makes the verse unusually generous: it requires neither success nor completion. The one who intends good, strives genuinely, and is prevented by death or incapacity has fulfilled every condition the verse states. The Prophetic tradition confirms this reading with a ruling of extraordinary breadth. Muslim (131) records from Ibn ʿAbbās, in the account of the Prophet ﷺ transmitting from his Lord:

> *"Truly God wrote down the good deeds and the bad deeds, then He made it clear: whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it with Himself as a full good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it with Himself as ten good deeds, up to seven hundred times, and many times over. And whoever intends a bad deed and does not do it, God writes it with Himself as a full good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it as one bad deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131)

The asymmetry in that report — the abandoned sin recorded as a good deed, the unexecuted good recorded as a full one — is the practical expression of this verse's doctrine that the accounting is of orientation and effort rather than outcome. Al-Zamakhsharī cites the ḥadīth of intentions, *"Deeds are only according to intentions"* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907), at exactly this verse, and the two ḥadīth together explain the verse's final word: *mashkūrā* — thanked. That the Infinite describes Himself as thanking the finite is among the most striking features of Qur'anic theology, stated directly at 4:147, *"What would God do with your punishment if you are grateful and believe? And God is Ever Thankful (shākir), Knowing."*

**The Practical Effect of the Verse**

The verse is unusually generous in a way that has real psychological consequence. A person who strives and fails is often paralyzed by the sense that his effort was wasted — the abandoned project, the uncompleted study, the reform that did not take. The verse states that the accounting is of the *striving*, not the *outcome*. This is not a license for half-effort; the verse demands *saʿyahā*, the striving the goal deserves. But it does mean that the sincere worker who dies mid-task has not lost his wage. The pairing with verse 18 produces the complete doctrine: orientation determines the value of the act. Two people give the same coin; one seeks the immediate and gets it, one seeks the final and is thanked. The coin is identical. The Qur'an's moral arithmetic is conducted entirely in the invisible.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:20

> **Each do We aid—both these and those—with the Gift of thy Lord; and the Gift of thy Lord is not confined.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Refuses the Easy Moral Story**

After drawing the sharpest possible contrast between the seeker of the immediate and the seeker of the final, the Qur'an immediately complicates it: *kullan numiddu hāʾulāʾi wa hāʾulāʾi min ʿaṭāʾi rabbik* — "each do We supply — these and those — from the gift of thy Lord." *Kullan* is an accusative of totality: *all* of them, without exception. Both groups receive. The *ʿaṭāʾ* — the gift, the grant, the bestowed provision — is not distributed according to spiritual merit. This is one of the Qur'an's most honest statements about the world as it actually appears. The wicked are often prosperous; the pious are often poor. Any religious system that denies this is falsified by a single observation. The Qur'an confronts it directly and explains it rather than explaining it away.

**"And the Gift of Thy Lord Is Not Confined"**

*Wa mā kāna ʿaṭāʾu rabbika maḥẓūrā* — *maḥẓūr* means withheld, restricted, fenced off, prohibited; from *ḥ-ẓ-r*, to enclose, to forbid. The gift of God is not restricted to a category of people. Compare 38:9, *"Or do they possess the treasuries of the mercy of thy Lord, the Almighty, the Giver?"* and 52:37, *"Or do they possess the treasuries of thy Lord, or are they the ones in charge?"* The rhetorical point is that no human being holds the keys; the distribution is God's prerogative, and human beings are not entitled to audit it in advance. The verse simultaneously dismantles two errors: the prosperity gospel (wealth proves God's favor) and its mirror (poverty proves God's favor). Neither is true. Wealth is a gift and a test; poverty is a condition and a test. Compare 89:15–17, which rebukes both inferences in a single breath.

**Why the Address Shifts to "Thy Lord"**

The verse shifts from third person to the second-person address to the Prophet ﷺ — *ʿaṭāʾi rabbik*, "the gift of thy Lord." Al-Rāzī notes that this personalization is characteristic of passages where the Prophet ﷺ is being consoled. He had watched the wealthy leaders of Quraysh — Abū Jahl, al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, Umayyah ibn Khalaf — flourish while his followers starved. The verse tells him: their provision comes from the same source as yours, and its quantity tells you nothing about their standing. The same consolation appears at 15:3, *"Leave them to eat and enjoy, and let hope divert them; for they will come to know,"* and at 20:131:

> *"And do not extend thine eyes toward that wherewith We have provided some classes of them — the splendor of the life of this world — that We may test them therein. The provision of thy Lord is better and more lasting."*

**The Theological Function of Undiscriminating Provision**

If provision tracked righteousness, faith would be compelled: everyone would believe, because belief would visibly pay. The Qur'an is explicit that faith under compulsion is worthless — *"There is no compulsion in religion"* (2:256), and *"If thy Lord had willed, all who are on earth would have believed together. Wouldst thou then compel mankind until they become believers?"* (10:99). For belief to be a free act, its worldly consequences must be indeterminate. The indiscriminate gift is therefore a *condition of the possibility of genuine faith* — a point of considerable philosophical subtlety that al-Rāzī develops.

****Why Indiscriminate Provision Makes Faith Possible****

The verse's refusal to distribute the *ʿaṭāʾ* according to spiritual merit is not a concession to the way things happen to be; al-Rāzī argues that it is a condition of the possibility of genuine faith. If provision tracked righteousness, belief would be compelled, because believing would visibly pay and disbelieving would visibly cost. The Qur'an forecloses compelled belief as worthless in two places: 2:256, *"There is no compulsion in religion; the right direction is distinct from error"*; and 10:99, *"If thy Lord had willed, all who are on earth would have believed together. Wouldst thou then compel mankind until they become believers?"* For faith to be a free act, its worldly consequences must be indeterminate — and the indiscriminate gift is what makes them so. The verse is therefore not an apology for injustice but a statement about the epistemic conditions under which belief can be anything other than calculation. The tradition supplies the practical companion to this doctrine in the concept of *rizq* as decreed. Ibn Mājah (2144) records, with a chain al-Albānī graded ṣaḥīḥ:

> *"There is no soul that dies until it has completed its provision. So fear God, and be measured in your seeking."* (Ibn Mājah 2144)

The ḥadīth does not license passivity — the same report commands measured seeking — but it removes provision from the category of things one can secure by anxiety or by grasping, which is the psychological burden the verse lifts. Its two practical effects are worth naming. The first is the release from interpreting other people's success as a verdict on their character, a habit that produces envy in one direction and self-righteousness in the other. The second is the release from interpreting one's own lack as a verdict on one's standing. Provision, on this account, is material for stewardship and for testing, not a report card — and the believer's task is not to audit the distribution but to use what he has been given.

**The Practical Lesson**

The verse frees the believer from two exhausting preoccupations. The first is the compulsion to interpret other people's success as a verdict on their character — a habit that produces both envy and self-righteousness. The second is the compulsion to interpret one's own lack as a verdict on one's standing. Both interpretations are refused. Provision is a gift, distributed by God, on terms He has not published; it is material for stewardship and testing, not a report card. The believer's task is not to read the distribution but to use what he has been given.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:21

> **Observe how We have favored some of them over others, and surely the Hereafter is greater in ranking and greater in favor.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Look**

*Unẓur kayfa faḍḍalnā baʿḍahum ʿalā baʿḍ* — "See how We have preferred some of them over others." The imperative *unẓur* is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to every reader: this is not a proposition to be assented to but a fact to be *observed*. The Qur'an repeatedly appeals to sight in this way — *"Say: Travel in the land and see"* (6:11) — because the inequality of provision is so plainly visible that it requires no argument, only attention.

**The Fact of Gradation**

The verse states as divine action what sociologists describe as a universal: human beings are distributed unequally in wealth, capacity, health, and circumstance. The Qur'an does not treat this as an accident or an injustice to be explained away; it treats it as *faḍl* — a bestowal by God, and therefore as a matter of design. Compare 4:32, *"And covet not that whereby God has favored some of you above others"*; 6:165, *"And He it is who appointed you vicegerents on the earth and raised some of you above others in degrees, that He might try you in what He gave you"*; and 43:32, which al-Rāzī cites here:

> *"Is it they who apportion the mercy of thy Lord? We have apportioned among them their livelihood in the life of this world, and raised some of them above others in rank, that some of them may take others into service. And the mercy of thy Lord is better than what they amass."*

The last clause of 43:32 is the interpretive key to this verse: the gradation of this world is real but *inferior in kind* to the mercy of God.

**Why Inequality Exists: The Two Qur'anic Answers**

The Qur'an gives two reasons, and both are functional rather than punitive. The first, from 43:32, is mutual dependence: *"that some may take others into service"* — a society in which everyone had identical means would have no division of labor and no occasion for the exchange of services. The second, from 6:165, is testing: *"that He might try you in what He gave you."* The one with more is tested by abundance; the one with less by want. Neither condition is a verdict; both are examinations with different question papers.

**"The Hereafter Is Greater in Ranking and Greater in Favor"**

*Wa la-l-ākhiratu akbaru darajātin wa akbaru tafḍīlā* — the two comparatives repeat the structure of the verse's first half and reverse its significance. In this world there are degrees and preferences; in the Hereafter there are *greater* degrees and *greater* preferences. Al-Rāzī explains that the separation there will be absolute — the people of the Garden and the people of the Fire — and the gradations within each will be far more pronounced than anything this world produces. The Qur'an describes the gradations of the Garden in terms that indicate real distinction: 6:132, *"And for all are degrees according to what they did"*; 20:75, *"And whoever comes to Him as a believer having done righteous deeds — for those are the highest ranks (al-darajāt al-ʿulā)"*; 57:10, *"Not equal among you are those who spent and fought before the conquest. Those are greater in rank than those who spent and fought afterward."*

****Ghibṭah and Ḥasad: The Two Ways of Looking Upward****

The command *unẓur* — see, observe — makes inequality an object of attention rather than a subject of complaint, and the tradition draws a distinction that determines what the looking produces. Al-Bukhārī (73) and Muslim (815) record:

> *"There is no envy (ḥasad) except in two: a man whom God has given wealth and enabled to spend it rightly, and a man whom God has given wisdom and he judges by it and teaches it."* (al-Bukhārī 73)

The word translated here as "envy" is *ḥasad*, but the commentators explain that what the ḥadīth commends is *ghibṭah* — the wish to possess a like good without wishing the other to lose his — and that *ḥasad* proper, which wishes the other's loss, is what the same tradition condemns. The distinction is the difference between the two possible responses to the gradation this verse commands us to observe: one produces emulation, the other produces resentment, and the outward object of attention is identical in both. The two Qur'anic explanations of why the gradation exists are functional rather than punitive, and together they answer the complaint the verse anticipates. The first, at 43:32, is mutual dependence: *"We have apportioned among them their livelihood in the life of this world, and raised some of them above others in rank, that some of them may take others into service. And the mercy of thy Lord is better than what they amass."* A society of identical means would have no division of labor and no occasion for the exchange of services. The second, at 6:165, is testing: *"And He raised some of you above others in degrees, that He might try you in what He gave you."* The abundant are tested by abundance and the constrained by want, with different question papers and the same examiner. The verse then outbids the whole arrangement: the ranking of the Hereafter is *akbar darajātin wa akbaru tafḍīlā* — greater in rank and greater in favor — and the criteria are different. Al-Bukhārī (3256) describes the result in an image meant to motivate rather than to wound: *"The people of the Garden will look up at those in the elevated chambers above them as you look at a brilliant star on the horizon."*

**The Consolation and the Warning**

The verse is addressed to those who feel the inequality of this world as a wound — and it is a genuine wound, not a fiction. The Qur'an does not minimize it; it *outbids* it. Whatever the disparity here, it will be exceeded there, and the direction of the disparity will not be the same. The poor believer in this world may occupy a station in the next that no king of this world reaches. The same verse warns the prosperous: the ranking you enjoy is the smaller one. It will be superseded, and the criteria of the greater ranking are different — not wealth but what you did with it. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (6490) and Muslim (2964):

> *"The people of the Garden will look up at those in the elevated chambers above them as you look at a brilliant star on the horizon — and Abū Bakr and ʿUmar are among them, and how excellent they are."*

The image is of permanent, visible gradation — and it is presented as a motivation, not a cause of envy.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:22

> **Do not set up another god along with God, lest you sit blameworthy, forsaken.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening of the Moral Code**

Here begins the section the commentators often call the Qur'anic Decalogue — verses 22 through 39, a sequence of commands and prohibitions that Ibn Masʿūd reportedly described as containing the most comprehensive moral instruction in the whole Qur'an. Al-Bukhārī (4626) records that ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd said:

> *"Whoever wishes to look at the will (waṣiyyah) of Muhammad ﷺ upon which is the seal of his ring, let him read these verses of Sūrah Banī Isrāʾīl: 'Do not set up another god along with God...' through '...lest thou be cast into Hell, condemned, banished.'"*

The description "the will with the seal" indicates that Ibn Masʿūd regarded this passage as the Prophet's ﷺ final testament in condensed form. That it opens with the prohibition of shirk is not arbitrary: in Islamic theology, shirk is the only sin God does not forgive without repentance — *"Truly God forgives not that partners be set up with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills"* (4:48). The moral code is built on a foundation; if the foundation fails, nothing above it holds.

**"Do Not Set Up"**

*Lā tajʿal maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhar* — the verb *jaʿala* here means to appoint, to establish, to posit. The prohibition is not against the *existence* of other gods — the Qur'an denies that categorically — but against the human act of *establishing* something as a god. Shirk is not primarily a metaphysical error about how many gods there are; it is a practical act of enthronement. Whatever a person appoints to the position God occupies — as the object of ultimate reliance, ultimate fear, or ultimate love — has been made *ilāh*, whatever he calls it. The verse appears twice in this sūrah, here and at 39, framing the entire moral code with the same prohibition. The repetition is structural: the code begins and ends with tawḥīd, so that every command in between is understood as an expression of it rather than an independent rule.

**"Lest You Sit Blameworthy, Forsaken"**

*Fa-taqʿuda madhmūman makhdhūlā* — the verb *qaʿada* literally means to sit; idiomatically in Arabic it denotes the state one is left in as a result of something, as in "he ended up sitting disgraced." *Madhmūm* — blamed, condemned. *Makhdhūl* — abandoned, forsaken, left without support; from *kh-dh-l*, the root of *khidhlān*, the withholding of help. The two adjectives describe the exact consequences of shirk in this life as well as the next. The one who appoints a created thing to God's position will find, sooner or later, that it cannot hold him. Wealth fails at bankruptcy; reputation fails at scandal; the powerful patron fails at his own fall. The idolater ends *makhdhūl* — let down by the very thing he trusted. And *madhmūm*: blamed, because having been warned, he cannot plead ignorance. Note that the same pair of adjectives — *madhmūman madḥūrā*, "blameworthy, banished" — closed verse 18. The seeker of the immediate and the setter-up of partners end in the same state, because they have committed the same act: they have enthroned something created.

****Shirk as Enthronement: The Qur'an's Own Diagnosis****

The verb *tajʿal* — to appoint, to posit — determines the meaning of the prohibition, and it relocates shirk from metaphysics to practice. The Qur'an does not deny that other gods exist in order to forbid worshipping them; it denies the category and then forbids the human act of establishing something within it. This is why the Qur'an can describe shirk as widespread among people who would deny holding it. 12:106 is the verse the classical theologians cite most often on this point: *"And most of them believe not in God except while associating partners (wa mā yuʾminu aktharuhum bi-Allāhi illā wa hum mushrikūn)."* Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss, preserved by al-Ṭabarī, is that this refers to those who say, when asked who created the heavens and the earth, "God" — and then direct their supplication elsewhere. The theological belief is intact; the practical enthronement has occurred. The tradition names the subtler forms explicitly. Aḥmad (27742) records the Prophet ﷺ addressing his Companions:

> *"Shall I not tell you of what is more to be feared for you than the False Christ (al-Masīḥ al-Dajjāl)? The lesser shirk (al-shirk al-aṣghar): that a man stands and prays, and beautifies his prayer because he sees a man looking at him."* (Aḥmad 27742; Ibn Mājah 4204, graded ḥasan)

The category is defined by the mechanism rather than by the object: whatever a person appoints to the position God occupies — as the object of ultimate reliance, ultimate fear, or ultimate regard — has been made *ilāh*, whatever he calls it. This is why the prohibition opens a moral code rather than appearing within it. Each command that follows fails at a specific point: kindness to parents fails when the parent's approval becomes ultimate; honest measure fails when wealth does; chastity fails when desire does; truthfulness fails when reputation does. In every case the commandment breaks precisely where something created has been enthroned, and the verse at the end of the code will repeat this one to close the frame.

**Why Shirk Is Placed First in a Moral Code**

A modern reader may find it strange that a list beginning with prohibitions against murder, adultery, and fraud starts with a theological statement. The classical answer is that shirk is not one item on the list but the condition that makes the list intelligible. Moral commands require a Lawgiver whose authority is ultimate. If ultimate authority is shared, divided, or transferred to a created thing, the commands become negotiable — because the one who has appointed his own god can also unappoint it when inconvenient. Tawḥīd is what makes obedience non-optional. The practical version of this is visible everywhere. The person whose ultimate concern is his career will violate moral commands when they conflict with it. The person whose ultimate concern is tribal loyalty will violate them for the tribe. The person whose ultimate concern is his own desire will violate them for the desire. Each has an *ilāh ākhar*, and each finds the code negotiable exactly at the point where his god is threatened. The prohibition is first because it is the only one that protects all the others.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:23

> **Thy Lord decrees that you worship none but Him, and be virtuous to parents. Whether one or both of them reaches old age, say not to them "Uff!" nor chide them, but speak unto them a noble word.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Thing God Joins to Himself**
