*Wa qaḍā rabbuka allā taʿbudū illā iyyāhu wa bi-l-wālidayni iḥsānā* — "And thy Lord has decreed that you worship none but Him, and that you show *iḥsān* to parents." The conjunction *wa* places the duty to parents in the same sentence as the duty to God, with no intervening matter. This pairing occurs at 2:83, 4:36, 6:151, 29:8, 31:14, and 46:15 — so consistently that the classical commentators treat it as a fixed Qur'anic formula. Al-Qurṭubī states the reasoning: God is the one who created you and provided for you in the absolute sense; parents are the means through which that creation and provision reached you. To be ungrateful to the means is to be ungrateful to the Source.

**The Meaning of Qaḍā**

*Qaḍā* carries both "decreed" and "commanded." Al-Ṭabarī takes it as commanded; al-Zamakhsharī notes that it may also carry the sense that in the ultimate order, nothing is truly worshipped but God, as at 13:15, *"And unto God prostrates whoever is in the heavens and the earth, willingly or unwillingly."* Both readings agree that the obligation is not advisory. The word implies a settled determination — the same verb used at 17:4 of the decree written in the Book.

**Iḥsān: Beyond Duty**

*Iḥsān* is from *ḥ-s-n*, goodness, beauty. Its verbal form *aḥsana* means to do good, but the Qur'anic usage carries a specific excess: *iḥsān* is goodness beyond what is required. The Qur'an defines it at 16:90: *"Truly God commands justice (ʿadl) and excellence (iḥsān), and giving to kin."* Justice is giving what is owed; *iḥsān* is giving more than is owed, and giving it beautifully. Applied to parents, it means that fulfilling the minimum — feeding them, housing them, not harming them — is not the standard. The standard is generous, beautiful, willing service.

**"Whether One or Both of Them Reaches Old Age"**

*Immā yablughanna ʿindaka al-kibara aḥaduhumā aw kilāhumā* — the specification of old age is not incidental. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī all note that the verse addresses the precise moment when the relationship inverts: the parent becomes dependent, and the child becomes the provider. The phrase *ʿindaka* — "with you," "in your care" — makes the situation concrete. This is not about parents in general; it is about *your* parent, aged, *in your house*. The verse singles out old age because it is when the duty becomes hardest. A child's love for a parent is easy while the parent is strong and providing. It becomes tested when the parent is weak, repetitive, forgetful, inconvenient, and no longer able to give anything back. The Qur'an locates the command exactly at the point of maximum difficulty, which is where moral commands matter.

**"Say Not to Them 'Uff!'"**

*Fa-lā taqul lahumā uff* — the word *uff* is an interjection of irritation, a sigh, an "ugh." Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note its root meaning: something filthy, dirt, the residue under the fingernails. Some commentators therefore read the verse as addressing specifically the disgust a child may feel when cleaning an elderly parent — the most intimate and most testing form of care. Others take it generally: any expression of annoyance, however small. The exegetes draw a powerful inference from the specific prohibition. Al-Rāzī formulates it as an argument *a fortiori* (*qiyās al-awlā*): if the smallest possible expression of irritation — a sigh, a monosyllable — is forbidden, then raising the voice, insulting, striking, and abandoning are forbidden *all the more*. The Qur'an prohibits the least so that the greater is understood to be included. This method of reasoning is standard in Islamic jurisprudence and the commentators apply it here explicitly. The converse inference is equally important: the smallest kindness counts. If an "ugh" is registered as a violation, a gentle tone is registered as a virtue. The verse establishes a moral sensitivity at the level of the sigh.

**"Nor Chide Them"**

*Wa lā tanharhumā* — the verb *nahara* means to rebuff, to drive away with a harsh word, to speak sharply. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as turning away in anger or speaking dismissively. The prohibition covers not only what is said but the *manner* of saying it. A child may technically comply with every instruction while delivering compliance in a tone that wounds; the verse closes that loophole.

**"But Speak Unto Them a Noble Word"**

*Wa qul lahumā qawlan karīmā* — *karīm* means noble, generous, honorable; it is one of the Qur'an's most positive adjectives, used of God Himself (*al-Karīm*), of the Qur'an (56:77), of provision (26:58), of reward (8:4). A *qawl karīm* is speech that honors the person addressed. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as speech combining respect, gentleness, and deference — the way one speaks to someone whose dignity one is anxious to protect. The verse moves from prohibition to command: do not sigh, do not rebuff, and *do* speak nobly. The negative alone would produce sullen compliance; the positive command produces the intended state of heart.

**The Ḥadīth Literature on Parents**

The Prophetic tradition develops this verse with unusual intensity. Al-Bukhārī (5976) and Muslim (85) record from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"A man came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said: 'O Messenger of God, who is most deserving of my good companionship?' He said: 'Your mother.' He said: 'Then who?' He said: 'Your mother.' He said: 'Then who?' He said: 'Your mother.' He said: 'Then who?' He said: 'Your father.'"*

Al-Tirmidhī (1898) records:

> *"The pleasure of the Lord is in the pleasure of the parent, and the anger of the Lord is in the anger of the parent."* (graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ; al-Zamakhsharī cites it at this verse)

And al-Bukhārī (5971) records the ranking of deeds:

> *"I asked the Prophet ﷺ: 'Which deed is most beloved to God?' He said: 'The prayer at its time.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Then goodness to parents.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Then striving in the path of God.'"*

That goodness to parents is placed above jihad — the highest physical sacrifice in the tradition — indicates the weight the revelation assigns to it.

**Why Parents and Not Children**

A reader may notice that the Qur'an legislates the child's duty to the parent far more extensively than the parent's duty to the child, and the reason is structural rather than arbitrary. Parental love is largely instinctive; filial gratitude is not. A mother's attachment to her infant requires no legislation — it is carried by biology and by the memory of carrying. A grown child's patience with a demanding elderly parent requires a command, because the natural current of life runs the other way. The Qur'an legislates where nature is weakest.

****The Limits of Obedience, and the Rank of the Duty****

The verse's command is not unconditional, and the Qur'an supplies the boundary in a passage the jurists treat as its necessary companion. 31:15: *"And if they strive with thee to make thee associate with Me that of which thou hast no knowledge, then obey them not; but keep company with them in this world kindly (maʿrūfan), and follow the way of one who turns to Me."* The structure of the ruling is precise: obedience in sin is refused, and kindness in this world is commanded — the two clauses sit in the same sentence, so that the refusal does not license coldness. The Prophetic formulation is categorical, recorded by Aḥmad (1098) and al-Nasāʾī: *"There is no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator."* Al-Qurṭubī states the resulting rule: the parent's right is to companionship, provision, and honorable speech; it does not extend to the child's conscience. The rank of the duty is fixed by its placement in the hierarchy of deeds. Al-Bukhārī (5970) records the exchange in full:

> *"I asked the Prophet ﷺ: 'Which deed is most beloved to God?' He said: 'The prayer at its time.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Then goodness (birr) to parents.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'Then jihād in the path of God.'"* (al-Bukhārī 5970)

That *birr al-wālidayn* is placed above jihād — the highest physical sacrifice in the tradition — is the measure of its weight, and the reason is structural rather than sentimental. Parental love is largely instinctive and requires no legislation; filial gratitude is not, because the natural current of life runs the other way. The Qur'an legislates where nature is weakest, which is why the command is located at the precise point of maximum difficulty: *ʿindaka*, in your care, in your house, when the parent has become dependent and can give nothing back. Al-Rāzī's argument *a fortiori* from the prohibition of *uff* closes the loophole: if a sigh is a violation, then raising the voice, striking, and abandonment are violations by a greater measure, and the verse forbids the least so that the greater is understood to be included.

**The Modern Relevance**

The verse addresses a situation that has become structurally rare and morally acute. In societies where the aged are placed in institutions, the specific test the verse describes — an aging parent *ʿindaka*, in your care, in your house — is largely outsourced. The outsourcing is not itself forbidden; the Qur'an's concern is with the state of the heart and the tone of the voice, which persist at whatever distance. The person who visits weekly and sighs has violated *uff*; the person who arranges excellent care and speaks nobly has not. The verse legislates a manner, not a living arrangement.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:24

> **Lower unto them the wing of humility out of mercy and say, "My Lord! Have mercy upon them, as they raised me when I was small."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Wing of Humility": One of the Qur'an's Great Images**

*Wa akhfiḍ lahumā janāḥa al-dhulli min al-raḥmah* — "and lower to them the wing of humility, out of mercy." The image is drawn from the bird: a bird lowers its wing over its young to shelter them, and lowers its wing to another bird as a gesture of submission and non-aggression. The Qur'an transfers the gesture to the child before the parent — the reversal is the whole point. The one who was sheltered under a wing now lowers his own. *Janāḥ* means wing; *dhull* means humility, submissiveness, the state of being lowly without being degraded. The commentators distinguish *dhull* (willing lowness before one worthy of honor) from *dhull* in the sense of humiliation imposed by another. The former is a virtue; the latter is an injury. The Qur'an uses the same word of the believers toward one another at 5:54: *"humble (adhillah) toward the believers, mighty toward the disbelievers."* Al-Qurṭubī notes the extremity of the command: the child is instructed to lower himself before his parent as the bird lowers its wing — a posture of total non-assertion. There is no room in this verse for the assertion of the adult child's superiority in education, income, or worldly success, which is precisely the temptation old age creates.

**"Out of Mercy"**

*Min al-raḥmah* — the qualifier is critical. The lowering of the wing must proceed from mercy, not from obligation, fear, or social expectation. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that mercy here is the child's tenderness toward the parent's weakness, and specifically the *memory* of the parent's tenderness toward his own weakness. The verse establishes an emotional logic: you were once as helpless as they now are, and they did not sigh. The Qur'an's ethics rarely rest on rule alone; they rest on recollected experience. The command is made bearable by the memory that makes it obvious.

**The Prescribed Prayer**

*Wa qul rabbi rḥamhumā kamā rabbayānī ṣaghīrā* — "and say: My Lord, have mercy on them as they raised me when I was small." The verse does not merely command kindness; it supplies the words. *Rabbayānī* is from *r-b-y*, to raise, nurture, cause to grow — the same root as *rabb*, Lord, and *tarbiyah*, upbringing. The child is instructed to acknowledge, in prayer, that his very formation was their work. The comparative *kamā* — "as" — is remarkable. The child asks God to have mercy on his parents *in the measure of* their nurturing of him. Al-Rāzī observes that this is not a request for equivalence — God's mercy exceeds any human act — but a request that the mercy be *patterned on* their care: as they were tender with your smallness, be tender with their weakness.

**Even If They Are Disbelievers**

Al-Zamakhsharī records that the duty extends to non-Muslim parents: the child should still pray for their guidance and treat them with kindness. The Qur'anic basis is 31:15: *"And if they strive with thee to make thee associate with Me that of which thou hast no knowledge, then obey them not; but keep company with them in this world kindly (maʿrūfan)."* The theological limit is precise: obedience in shirk is refused; kindness in this world is commanded. And 60:8: *"God forbids you not, with regard to those who fought you not for religion and drove you not from your homes, that you should be kind to them and deal justly with them. Truly God loves the just."* The case of Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr is the classical precedent: her mother, a polytheist, came to visit her in Madinah, and Asmāʾ asked the Prophet ﷺ whether she should receive her. He said, "Yes, keep good ties with your mother" (al-Bukhārī 2620; Muslim 1003).

**The Prayer for the Dead Parent**

The verse's wording is in the present tense and does not restrict itself to the parents' lifetime, which the commentators use as the basis for the well-established practice of praying for deceased parents. Muslim (2553) records:

> *"When a man dies, his deeds cease except for three: a continuing charity, a knowledge from which benefit is gained, or a righteous child who prays for him."*

The verse is the scriptural ground of that third category: the child's prayer continues to benefit the parent after death, which means the duty outlives the person to whom it is owed. This is one of the few obligations in Islamic law that the death of the obligee does not extinguish.

**Why the Verse Pairs Action With Prayer**

Kindness alone would be a behavioral command; prayer alone would be a verbal one. The verse requires both, and the pairing is psychologically astute. Behavior can be performed without feeling, and feeling can be cultivated without effect. The child who lowers the wing but resents it, and the child who prays but neglects, have each failed half the verse. Prayer does something behavior cannot: it places the parent before God, which relocates the relationship out of the economy of exchange. You are no longer repaying a debt to a person who can no longer repay you; you are asking the One who can.

****Janāḥ al-Dhull: An Image Drawn From the Bird****

The metaphor is one of the Qur'an's most carefully constructed, and its rhetorical force depends on the reversal it performs. Al-Zamakhsharī classifies it as an *istiʿārah* — a borrowed image — drawn from the bird that lowers its wing over its young to shelter them, and lowers its wing to another bird as a gesture of submission and non-aggression. The verse transfers the gesture to the child before the parent: the one who was sheltered under a wing now lowers his own. The word *dhull* is chosen with equal care, and the commentators distinguish it sharply from *dhill* in the sense of degradation. Willing lowness before one worthy of honor is a virtue; humiliation imposed by another is an injury. The Qur'an uses the same word of the believers toward one another at 5:54, *"humble (adhillah) toward the believers, mighty toward the disbelievers"* — the same posture, in that verse, toward the community. The practical target of the command is specific: the adult child's superiority in education, income, and worldly standing, which is precisely the temptation that old age creates. Al-Qurṭubī notes the extremity of the required posture — total non-assertion — and the qualifier *min al-raḥmah* supplies the interior state that makes it bearable: mercy arising from *memory*, the recollection of the parent's tenderness toward one's own helplessness. The duty outlives the parent, which is unusual in Islamic law. Muslim (1631) records:

> *"When a man dies, his deeds cease except for three: a continuing charity, a knowledge from which benefit is gained, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631)

Abū Dāwūd (5142) specifies what remains after death: prayer for them, seeking forgiveness for them, fulfilling their pledges, honoring their friends, and maintaining the ties of kinship that exist only through them. The child's prayer thus continues to benefit a person who can no longer benefit from anything else, and the obligation is one of the few in the law that the death of the obligee does not extinguish.

**The Practical Shape of the Verse**

Read as instruction, the verse prescribes three things in a specific order: a posture (lowered, non-assertive), an emotion (mercy arising from memory), and a speech-act (a specific prayer). Anyone caring for an aging parent recognizes how often the difficulty is not the labor but the posture — the resistance to being *lower* than someone you now outrank in every worldly measure. The verse addresses exactly that resistance, and it does so not by command alone but by remembrance: *kamā rabbayānī ṣaghīrā* — as they raised you when you were small. The memory, honestly held, resolves the resistance.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:25

> **Your Lord knows best that which is in your souls. If you are righteous, then verily He is Forgiving toward the penitent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why This Verse Follows the Command to Parents**

The placement is deliberate and the commentators have noted its necessity. Verses 23–24 have just commanded the most demanding form of familial devotion: the lowering of the wing, the noble word, the suppression of the sigh. Any reader who has cared for an aging parent knows what the command provokes — not only effort but *inadequacy*, and sometimes irritation that is felt and immediately regretted. Verse 25 answers before the reader despairs: your Lord knows what is in your soul. Al-Rāzī explains the connection precisely: God knows that perfect, uninterrupted gentleness toward a demanding parent is beyond most human beings, and He does not condemn the struggle. The verse converts an impossible standard into a livable one — not by lowering the standard but by revealing that the standard includes the repentance.

**"That Which Is in Your Souls"**

*Mā fī nufūsikum* — not merely your actions, but the interior: the flash of annoyance, the unspoken complaint, the resentment that surfaces and is pushed down. The Qur'an asserts God's knowledge of this interior repeatedly: 2:234, *"And God knows what is in your souls, so beware of Him"*; 2:284, *"Whether you show what is in your souls or conceal it, God will call you to account for it"*; and of the breasts, 3:154, *"And God knows well what is in the breasts"*; and of the heart, 4:63, *"God knows what is in their hearts."* The commentators distinguish two things here, and the distinction is theologically important. God knows what is in the soul — including passing thoughts and involuntary feelings. But the Qur'an does not punish the involuntary. Al-Bukhārī (5269) and Muslim (127) record:

> *"God has overlooked for my community what their souls whisper to them, so long as they do not act upon it or speak it."*

The knowledge in verse 25 is therefore not a threat of punishment for every passing irritation; it is the assurance that God sees the *effort*, the regret, and the intention, none of which are visible in the outward act.

**"If You Are Righteous, He Is Forgiving to the Penitent"**

*In takūnū ṣāliḥīna fa-innahu kāna li-l-awwābīna ghafūrā* — *awwāb* is the intensive form of one who returns; the *awwābūn* are those who turn back repeatedly. The form indicates frequency: not the one who repents once, but the one whose characteristic is returning. The verse is thus addressed specifically to the person who fails, regrets, and tries again — which is the actual condition of nearly every moral life. Al-Qurṭubī explains *ghafūr* as the one who covers; the root *gh-f-r* denotes covering and protection, as a helmet covers the head. God's forgiveness is not only the cancellation of the penalty but the covering of the fault, so that it does not disgrace the sinner before others.

****Awwāb: The One Whose Characteristic Is Returning****

The plural *al-awwābūn* is the intensive form of *āʾib*, one who returns, and the form indicates frequency: not the person who repents once but the person whose settled characteristic is turning back. Al-Qurṭubī's definition, transmitted from al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, is the shortest available: the *awwāb* is the one who does not persist. The definition is not about never falling but about not remaining down, and it makes the verse address the actual condition of nearly every moral life rather than an idealized one. The verse is placed where it is for that reason — immediately after the two most demanding verses on parents in the Qur'an — and its function is to keep an exacting standard livable by disclosing that repentance is part of the standard rather than a concession outside it. The theological precision of the verse lies in the relationship between two clauses. God knows what is in the souls — including the flash of irritation, the unspoken complaint, the resentment that surfaces and is pushed down — and yet the promise is attached to righteousness, not to the absence of interior noise. The distinction is supplied by al-Bukhārī (5269) and Muslim (127):

> *"God has overlooked for my community what their souls whisper to them, so long as they do not act upon it or speak it."* (al-Bukhārī 5269)

The knowledge in this verse is therefore not a threat of punishment for every passing thought; it is the assurance that God sees the effort, the regret, and the intention, none of which appear in the outward act. The corresponding discipline is the one the tradition calls *istiqāmah*, and its classical formulation is the ḥadīth of Muslim (38): *"Say: I believe in God — then be upright (fa-staqim)."* Uprightness in that formulation is not the absence of deviation but its continuous correction, which is the same content as *awwāb* stated as a command. The person who internalizes this does not lower the standard; he stops treating each failure as evidence that the standard was never meant for him.

**The Theological Balance**

The verse holds two things together that moral systems usually separate: an exacting standard and a wide forgiveness. It does not say "do your best and that is enough," nor does it say "the standard is perfect and failure is fatal." It says: the standard is what it is, God knows your interior completely, and if you are among those who return, He is forgiving. The combination is what makes the demanding ethics of verses 22–39 actually livable. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said of the *awwāb*: he is the one who does not persist. The definition is not about never falling but about not staying down. This is the practical doctrine of the verse, and it applies far beyond the parent-child relationship — to every obligation in the code that follows.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:26

> **Give unto the kinsman his right, and unto the indigent and the traveler, but do not squander wastefully.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Claimants Named**

*Wa āti dhā al-qurbā ḥaqqahu wa-l-miskīna wa-bna al-sabīl* — "and give the kinsman his right, and the indigent, and the wayfarer." Three categories, and the order is meaningful. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both note that charity in the Qur'an begins with those nearest and moves outward — the same principle stated at 2:215: *"They ask thee what they should spend. Say: whatever you spend of good is for parents and kinsmen, orphans, the indigent, and the wayfarer."* And 2:177 includes the same list in the definition of righteousness itself. The word *ḥaqq* — right — is decisive. The kinsman does not receive charity as a favor; he receives what is *owed*. This transforms the transaction from benevolence to justice, and it removes the giver's discretion. A favor may be withheld; a right may not.

**The Kinsman: Ṣilat al-Raḥim**

*Al-qurbā* is from *q-r-b*, nearness; *dhū al-qurbā* is the one possessed of nearness — the relative. The duty of maintaining kinship (*ṣilat al-raḥim*) is one of the most heavily emphasized obligations in Islamic ethics. Al-Bukhārī (5988) and Muslim (2556) record:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him maintain his ties of kinship."*

And al-Bukhārī (5989) records the warning attached to it:

> *"No one who severs his ties of kinship will enter the Garden."*

The word *raḥim* (womb) itself supplies the theology: the tie is named after the organ from which both parties emerged. Severing it is not a social disagreement but a denial of a biological and therefore divinely-established fact. Al-Bukhārī (4830) records the ḥadīth qudsī in which God says:

> *"I am God, and I am al-Raḥmān; I created the raḥim and derived its name from My name. Whoever maintains it, I maintain him; and whoever severs it, I sever him."*

Some commentators record the view of ʿAlī Zayn al-ʿĀbidīn that *dhā al-qurbā* refers specifically to the kin of the Prophet ﷺ, who were entitled to a share of the spoils and forbidden from receiving ordinary charity. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī report this. The majority reading, however, is general: every relative has a right.

**The Indigent: Al-Miskīn**

The *miskīn* is the one who lacks, from *s-k-n*, stillness — the one whose circumstances have brought him to a standstill. The Qur'an distinguishes *faqīr* and *miskīn* at 9:60, listing both among the recipients of zakāt. The commentators explain the difference variously, but the practical sense is clear: the *miskīn* is the one whose need has become chronic rather than temporary.

**The Wayfarer: Ibn al-Sabīl**

*Ibn al-sabīl* — literally "son of the road" — is the traveler who has run out of means, even if he is wealthy at home. Al-Ṭabarī explains the duty as providing food and lodging. The category is remarkable in a pre-modern context: a legal entitlement for the stranger, based on nothing but his having arrived. In a world without hotels, banks, or embassies, this verse established a system of hospitality as religious obligation — the reason the Muslim world developed the *khān*, the *ribāṭ*, and the *waqf* for travelers.

**"But Do Not Squander Wastefully"**

*Wa lā tubadhdhir tabdhīrā* — *badhdhara* means to scatter, to disperse recklessly. The cognate adverb intensifies. The prohibition is placed inside the command to give, which is unusual and instructive: the Qur'an legislates the *manner* of generosity, not only its existence. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the forms this takes: giving excessively beyond one's means; giving for reputation rather than need; giving to those without a claim while those with a claim go unfed. The third is the most subtle and the most common — the person who gives lavishly to strangers for the appearance of generosity while his own relatives struggle. The Qur'an's balance here is stated at 25:67: *"And those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful nor miserly, but stand between that, uprightly."* The middle is not a compromise between two vices but a distinct virtue with its own name: *qawām*, uprightness.

****The Hierarchy of Claims: Why the Kinsman Comes First****

The order of the three recipients in this verse is not incidental, and 2:215 states the principle explicitly: *"They ask thee what they should spend. Say: whatever you spend of good is for parents and kinsmen, orphans, the indigent, and the wayfarer."* Charity in the Qur'an begins with those nearest and moves outward, and the term used for the kinsman's portion is *ḥaqq* — a right — rather than *ṣadaqah*, which transforms the transaction from benevolence into justice and removes the giver's discretion. A favor may be withheld; a right may not. The jurisprudential concept is *ṣilat al-raḥim*, and its name supplies its theology: the *raḥim* is the womb, so the tie is named after the organ from which both parties emerged, and severing it is not a social disagreement but a denial of a fact. Muslim (2555) records the metaphysical weight the tradition attaches to it:

> *"God created the creation, and when He finished, the *raḥim* stood and said: 'This is the standing of one who seeks refuge in Thee from severance.' He said: 'Yes — are you not content that I maintain whoever maintains you, and sever whoever severs you?' It said: 'Indeed.' He said: 'That is yours.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4830; Muslim 2555)

The wayfarer's entitlement is the other striking feature. *Ibn al-sabīl* — literally "son of the road" — is the traveler who has run out of means even if he is wealthy at home, and 9:60 lists him among the eight categories entitled to zakāt. Al-Ṭabarī explains the duty as food and lodging, and in a world without banks, embassies, or hotels this verse established hospitality as a legal claim rather than a courtesy — the ground on which the Muslim world later built the *khān*, the *ribāṭ*, and the charitable endowments for travelers. The prohibition that closes the verse regulates the manner: al-Qurṭubī identifies the three forms of *tabdhīr* as giving beyond one's means, giving for reputation, and giving to those without a claim while those with a claim go unfed — the third being the most common and the least noticed.

**Why Generosity Requires Regulation**

An unrestricted command to give would produce three predictable failures: the depletion of the giver's household, the misdirection of resources to the unworthy, and the corruption of the act by the desire for praise. The verse prevents all three by naming the rightful recipients in order and prohibiting excess. Generosity without discipline is not virtue; it is impulse, and impulse is as likely to harm as to help. The person who gives away the family's rent to a stranger has not performed charity; he has performed injustice wearing charity's clothes.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:27

> **Truly the wasteful are the brethren of satans, and Satan is ungrateful to his Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Brethren of Satans"**

*Inna al-mubadhdhirīna kānū ikhwāna al-shayāṭīn* — the plural *shayāṭīn* is significant: not "the brethren of Satan" but "of the satans." The Qur'an uses the plural for both jinn and human agents of corruption — 6:112:

> *"And thus We made for every prophet an enemy — satans from among mankind and jinn, inspiring one another with flowery speech in delusion."*

The wasteful are thus brothered to a class, not to an individual. *Ikhwān* — brethren — indicates resemblance in character and companionship in action. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as their being friends and supporters of the satans. The word implies more than similarity; it implies affiliation. The one who squanders has joined a party.

**Why Waste Is Brotherhood With Evil**

The connection is not obvious and the commentators work to establish it. Al-Qurṭubī gives the reasoning: waste is the misuse of a trust, and every trust misused is an act of ingratitude; ingratitude is Satan's defining characteristic; therefore the waster is Satan's brother in the quality that defines him. The chain is short and each link is Qur'anic. There is a second, economic link. Resources squandered are resources not available to the *miskīn* and the *ibn al-sabīl* named in the previous verse. Waste is therefore not a private extravagance but a deprivation of the rightful claimant. The one who throws away what another needs has taken it from him.

**"And Satan Is Ungrateful to His Lord"**

*Wa kāna al-shayṭānu li-rabbihi kafūrā* — *kafūr* is the intensive form of *kāfir*, one who covers, conceals, denies; in moral usage, one profoundly ungrateful. The word is chosen with precision. Satan's original sin, in the Qur'an's account, was not atheism — he knows God, addresses Him, and asks Him for respite. His sin was *kufr al-niʿmah*: the refusal to acknowledge a favor received. He was created, honored, and admitted to the assembly of angels, and he responded to the command to prostrate by saying: *"I am better than him; Thou createdst me of fire and createdst him of clay"* (7:12). The verse therefore identifies waste as a species of ingratitude, and ingratitude as the satanic quality. The parallel with verse 3 is exact: Noah was *ʿabdan shakūrā*, a thankful servant; Satan is *li-rabbihi kafūrā*, ungrateful to his Lord. The entire sūrah is framed by this contrast, and every human being is positioned somewhere between the two adjectives.

**The Logic of Gratitude as the Root of Moral Order**

The verse makes a claim of considerable depth: that the moral life rests on the recognition of favor. If a person believes his wealth is entirely self-generated, spending it is his business and no one has a claim on it. If he believes it is a gift held in trust, spending it becomes an accountable act. The entire legislation of zakāt, of the kinsman's right, of the wayfarer's entitlement depends on the second belief. Waste is the practical expression of the first. This is why the Qur'an pairs *shukr* and *kufr* so persistently — 14:7, *"If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; and if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe"*; 76:3, *"We guided him to the way, be he grateful or ungrateful."* Gratitude is not a sentiment but an epistemological position about the source of what one has, and the practical consequences follow necessarily.

****Tabdhīr, Isrāf, Bukhl: Three Vices With Different Names****

The classical exegetes distinguish the economic vices with a precision that the English vocabulary blurs, and the distinction is preserved in a report from Ibn Masʿūd and Ibn ʿAbbās cited by al-Qurṭubī: *isrāf* is spending in what is lawful beyond the measure of need, while *tabdhīr* is spending in what is unlawful at all. The first is excess within the permitted; the second is misdirection of the resource altogether. *Bukhl* — miserliness — is the third term, and 25:67 defines the correct position between the first and the third: *"And those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful (musrifūn) nor miserly (muqtirūn), but stand between that, uprightly (qawāmā)."* The word *qawām* is from the same root as *aqwam* in verse 9, so that the economic virtue is presented as an instance of the uprightness the whole sūrah is about. The Prophetic formulation of the rule is practical rather than ascetic. Al-Bukhārī (5783) records:

> *"Eat, and drink, and give charity, and wear clothing — without extravagance (saraf) or pride (makhīlah)."* (al-Bukhārī 5783; al-Nasāʾī 2559)

The verse's pairing of waste with satanic brotherhood is the strongest language the sūrah uses about money, and al-Qurṭubī supplies the chain of reasoning: waste is the misuse of a trust; every trust misused is an act of ingratitude; ingratitude is Satan's defining characteristic, since his original sin was *kufr al-niʿmah* — the refusal to acknowledge a favor received; therefore the waster is Satan's brother in the quality that defines him. There is a second, economic link: the resources squandered are the resources unavailable to the *miskīn* and the *ibn al-sabīl* named in the preceding verse, so the person who discards what another needs has taken it from him. The contemporary form of the vice is more visible than in any previous century, since consumer economies are structurally organized around *tabdhīr* — overpackaging, planned obsolescence, the disposal of edible food — and the verse's criterion is precise enough to diagnose it: waste is not the amount spent but its proportion to need and to the claims of others.

**The Modern Application**

The verse's target is more visible today than in any previous century. Consumer economies are structurally built on *tabdhīr* — the deliberate production of waste through overpackaging, planned obsolescence, the disposal of edible food, the purchase of what will never be used. The Qur'an's category is precise enough to diagnose it: waste is not the amount spent but the *proportion to need and to the claims of others*. A wealthy person spending proportionately is not *mubadhdhir*; a poor person spending beyond his means to appear wealthy is. The criterion is internal and relational, not absolute — which is why no threshold can be legislated and why the verse appeals to the heart rather than to a figure.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:28

> **But if thou turnest away from them, seeking a mercy from thy Lord, for which thou dost hope, then speak unto them a gentle word.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About Refusing Without Wounding**

*Wa immā tuʿriḍanna ʿanhumu btighāʾa raḥmatin min rabbika tarjūhā fa-qul lahum qawlan maysūrā* — "and if thou turnest away from them, seeking a mercy from thy Lord that thou hopest for, then say to them a word that is easy." The verse legislates the situation every generous person eventually faces: the request arrives, the means are not there, and something must be said. The three elements are ordered with care. First, the turning away — *tuʿriḍ*, a physical gesture of withdrawal. Second, the interior state — *ibtighāʾ raḥmah*, the seeking of a mercy, which is to say that the refusal is not a dismissal but a postponement in hope. Third, the speech — *qawlan maysūrā*, an easy word.

**"Seeking a Mercy From Thy Lord": Poverty Re-described**

Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both note the remarkable euphemism. The verse does not say "because you have nothing." It says "seeking a mercy from thy Lord for which thou hopest." Poverty is thereby re-framed as a state of expectation rather than of lack. Al-Zamakhsharī draws out the two implications: the condition is *temporary* (one who hopes for something does not regard himself as permanently without it), and the person remains *connected* to the source of provision even while lacking it. The verse thus gives the poor man a vocabulary that preserves his dignity. He is not a man who cannot give; he is a man awaiting the means to give. This distinction matters enormously in practice, because the shame of inability is what most often produces the harshness the verse prohibits.

**The Prophet's ﷺ Silence and the Correction of It**

Al-Qurṭubī records that when the Prophet ﷺ was asked for something he did not have, he would remain silent rather than refuse — waiting for provision to arrive rather than saying no. The verse instructs him to replace silence with speech. The correction is instructive: silence, though well-intentioned, leaves the petitioner in uncertainty, and uncertainty is its own injury. The one who has asked needs to know where he stands.

**"A Word That Is Easy"**

*Qawlan maysūrā* — from *y-s-r*, ease; the same root as *yusrun* in the Prophetic maxim *"the religion is easy"* and as *yassirnā* in 54:17:

> *"And We have made the Qur'an easy to remember."*

An *easy* word is one that does not burden the hearer: it does not lecture, does not moralize, does not make him feel that his asking was an imposition. Al-Qurṭubī records a second reading of the command: that the Prophet ﷺ should speak *for* them easy words — that is, supplicate on their behalf. On this reading, the one who cannot give materially gives spiritually. The two readings are not in conflict; together they describe the full duty: explain gently, and pray for them.

**The Alternative Occasion of Revelation**

Al-Qurṭubī records a different context: that the verse was revealed after the Prophet ﷺ declined a request from people he knew would use the funds for corruption, so that his refusal was itself a seeking of mercy — the mercy of preventing harm. On this reading the verse covers the refusal that is *not* due to lack of means but to judgment about use. Both situations produce the same command: even the justified refusal requires an easy word.

**Why the Verse Belongs in the Code**

Placed between the prohibition of waste (vv. 26–27) and the prohibition of both miserliness and excess (v. 29), the verse handles the case the surrounding verses create. A person told not to squander will sometimes have to say no. The Qur'an anticipates the psychological danger: the one who learns to refuse may learn to refuse *coldly*. The verse closes that gap. Generosity is not only about what leaves the hand; it is about what enters the other person's heart.

****Qawl Maysūr: What a Refusal Owes the Refused****

The verse legislates a situation every generous person eventually faces, and it identifies the specific injury that a refusal can inflict even when the refusal is justified. Al-Qurṭubī records that when the Prophet ﷺ was asked for something he did not have, he would remain silent rather than refuse — waiting for provision rather than saying no — and that the verse instructed him to replace silence with speech. The correction is instructive: silence, however well-intentioned, leaves the petitioner suspended in uncertainty, and uncertainty is itself a harm. The person who has asked needs to know where he stands, and the verse treats that knowledge as something owed to him. The word chosen for the required speech is *maysūrā*, from *y-s-r*, ease — the same root as the Prophetic maxim *"the religion is easy (yusr)"* and as 54:17, *"And We have made the Qur'an easy to remember."* An easy word is one that does not burden its hearer: it does not lecture, moralize, or imply that the asking was an imposition. Al-Qurṭubī preserves a second reading in which the command is to speak *for* them easy words — to supplicate on their behalf — and the two readings together describe the complete duty: explain gently, and pray for the one you could not help. The reported alternative occasion sharpens the point further: al-Qurṭubī records that the verse may have been revealed after the Prophet ﷺ declined a request from people he knew would use the funds for corruption, so that the refusal was itself a seeking of mercy. The command applies equally to that case, which means the justified refusal — the one grounded in wisdom rather than in lack — carries the same obligation of gentleness as the refusal forced by poverty. The verse's placement between the prohibition of waste and the prohibition of both miserliness and excess is what gives it its place in the code. A person told not to squander will sometimes have to say no, and the Qur'an anticipates the psychological drift: the one who learns to refuse may learn to refuse coldly. Generosity is measured not only by what leaves the hand but by what enters the other person's heart, and the verse legislates the second as carefully as verses 26–27 legislate the first.

**Refusing Without Wounding Today**

The situation is constant and the failure is familiar. The colleague who asks for help you cannot give; the relative who asks to borrow; the stranger who asks on the street. The instinctive responses are either a flat refusal that humiliates, or a vague evasion that leaves hope suspended indefinitely. The verse prescribes a third: state the truth, state it easily, and keep the door's status clear. And where the inability is genuine, pray for the one refused — which costs nothing and is, in the verse's own logic, a form of giving.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:29

> **And let not thine hand be shackled to thy neck; nor let it be entirely open, lest thou shouldst sit condemned, destitute.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Bodily Images of Economic Vice**

*Wa lā tajʿal yadaka maghlūlatan ilā ʿunuqika wa lā tabsuṭhā kulla al-basṭ* — the verse draws two pictures. The first: a hand bound to the neck — the posture of the miser, unable to reach out even if he wished. The second: a hand stretched out to its full extension — the posture of the one who gives without limit. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr all read the first as the metaphor of miserliness, which the Qur'an condemns elsewhere: *"And whosoever is miserly is only miserly toward himself"* (47:38); and 3:180, *"Let not those who are miserly with what God has given them of His Bounty think that it is good for them. Nay, it is evil for them"*; and 57:24, *"those who are miserly and enjoin miserliness upon mankind."* The images are not decorative; they carry the argument. A bound hand is *useless* — the miser's wealth does nothing, for him or anyone else. An over-extended hand is *unrecoverable* — once fully open, it cannot be closed to protect what remains.

**Why Excess Is Prohibited Alongside Miserliness**

Most moral systems prohibit stinginess and praise generosity without qualification. The Qur'an prohibits both extremes, and the prohibition of excess is stated in the same breath as the prohibition of stinginess. The reason is practical: unmeasured generosity destroys the giver's capacity to give. The person who has given away his household's means has not performed charity; he has created two claimants where there was one. This is why the Qur'an's ideal is not maximum giving but *qawām* — a standing, a measured middle: *"And those who, when they spend, are neither wasteful nor miserly, but stand between that, uprightly (qawāmā)"* (25:67). The same root gives *qāʾim*, upright, and connects to *aqwam* in verse 9 — the most upright way. Economic virtue is a species of the uprightness the whole sūrah is about.

**"Lest Thou Shouldst Sit Condemned, Destitute"**

*Fa-taqʿuda malūman maḥsūrā* — *malūm* means blamed, reproached; *maḥsūr* means cut off, exhausted, depleted, prevented from moving — from *ḥ-ṣ-r*, to confine or besiege. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī all gloss *maḥsūr* as exhausted or depleted. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī distinguish the two failure modes by the source of the blame. The miser is blamed *by those he refused* — the relative, the needy, the petitioner who went away empty. The prodigal is blamed *by himself and by others* once the depletion becomes visible: he is reproached for having ruined his own household through foolishness. Both end *sitting* — a posture of immobility, unable to act, which is precisely what a hand is for. The image of *sitting* recalls verse 22's *fa-taqʿuda madhmūman makhdhūlā* and verse 18's *madhmūman madḥūrā*. The Qur'an uses this verb consistently for the terminal state of moral failure: the person who acted wrongly ends up sitting in the consequence.

**The Islamic Economic Principle Behind the Verse**

The verse is the scriptural basis for a substantial body of jurisprudence. Islamic law prohibits giving away more than one can bear when doing so harms dependents — the rights of the wife, the children, and the parents take precedence over voluntary charity. The Prophet ﷺ asked Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, who wished to bequeath all his wealth:

> *"Two-thirds?" "Too much." "Half?" "Too much." "A third?" "A third, and a third is much. Truly, leaving thy heirs wealthy is better than leaving them poor, begging from people."* (al-Bukhārī 1295; Muslim 1628)

The ḥadīth applies the verse's logic to a different domain: even posthumous generosity is limited by the claims of the household. The same principle governs the ruling that a person may not give away wealth he owes, nor charity that leaves his family destitute.

****Two Hands, Two Failures, and the Jurisprudence of the Third****

The verse draws its two pictures from the body, and the legal tradition built a substantial body of ruling on the space between them. The hand bound to the neck is not merely unwilling but *unable* — the miser's wealth does nothing, for him or for anyone else, which is why 47:38 describes miserliness as self-harm rather than self-protection: *"And whosoever is miserly is only miserly toward himself. God is the Rich, and you are the poor."* The hand extended to its full span is equally disordered, because once fully open it cannot be closed to protect what remains; and the one who has given away his household's means has not performed charity but created a second claimant where there was one. The Islamic ideal is therefore neither maximum giving nor minimum giving but *qawām* — a measured standing — stated at 25:67 and named with a word from the same root as *aqwam* in verse 9 of this sūrah. The jurisprudence is explicit and traces directly to this verse. When Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, ill and expecting to die, proposed to bequeath his wealth, the exchange recorded by al-Bukhārī (1295) and Muslim (1628) established the limit:

> *"'Two-thirds?' 'Too much.' 'Half?' 'Too much.' 'A third?' 'A third — and a third is much. Truly, leaving thy heirs wealthy is better than leaving them poor, begging from people.'"* (al-Bukhārī 1295)

The ruling generalizes: voluntary charity may not be given at the expense of dependents, and the claims of the wife, the children, and the parents take precedence over supererogatory generosity. The psychological insight beneath the two prohibitions is that both extremes arise from the same defect — an inability to relate to wealth as a measured trust. The miser over-values it and the spendthrift under-values it, and neither has achieved the relationship the verse presupposes, in which wealth is a resource with a purpose, distributed according to a hierarchy of claims that must be re-judged at every request.

**The Psychological Insight**

Both extremes arise from the same defect: an inability to relate to wealth as a *measured trust*. The miser over-values it; the spendthrift under-values it. Neither has achieved the relationship the verse presupposes, in which wealth is a resource with a purpose, distributed according to a hierarchy of claims. The middle is not a tepid compromise but the most difficult position, because it requires constant judgment — each request assessed against the actual state of the purse and the actual hierarchy of need.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:30

> **Truly thy Lord outspreads and straitens provision for whomsoever He will. Verily of His servants He is Aware, Seeing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Control of Provision**

*Inna rabbaka yabsuṭu al-rizqa li-man yashāʾu wa yaqdir* — "thy Lord outspreads provision for whomever He wills, and straitens [it]." The pair of verbs describes the full range: *yabsuṭu*, from *b-s-ṭ*, to spread out, to extend, to make ample; *yaqdir*, from *q-d-r*, to measure, to apportion, to restrict. The same pairing appears at 13:26, 28:82, 29:62, 30:37, 34:36 and 39, 39:52, and 42:12 — a fixed formula asserting that provision is under divine control. The verb *yabsuṭu* shares its root with the divine Name *al-Bāsiṭ*, the Expander, the Open-Handed — a Name attested in the ḥadīth lists of the ninety-nine though not occurring in the Qur'an as such. The quality it names is expressed in the Qur'an at 5:64:

> *"The Jews say, 'God's Hand is shackled.' Shackled are their hands, and cursed are they for what they said. Nay, but His two Hands are outspread (mabsūṭatān); He spends as He wills."*

The contrast with verse 29 is deliberate and almost punning. The verse before prohibits the human hand from being *maghlūlah* — shackled to the neck. This verse declares that God's Hands are *mabsūṭatān* — outspread. Human miserliness is measured against divine generosity and found to be its opposite.

**Why This Verse Follows the Prohibition of the Two Extremes**

The connection is not incidental. Having forbidden both miserliness and excess, the Qur'an grounds the prohibition in theology: the distribution of provision is God's act, not yours. Al-Rāzī draws out the two consequences. If God expands, you need not hoard — hoarding will not add to what has been apportioned. If God straitens, you cannot force it open — spending beyond your means will not produce provision. Both extremes are therefore not only morally wrong but *futile*, which is a stronger argument than moral prohibition alone. The Prophet ﷺ stated the underlying principle in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (3339) and Muslim (2643) from Ibn Masʿūd:

> *"Then the angel is sent to him and he breathes the spirit into him, and he is commanded with four words: to write his provision, his life span, his deeds, and whether he is wretched or happy."*

Provision (*rizq*) is among the decreed matters. This does not license passivity — the same tradition insists on action — but it removes provision from the category of things one can secure by anxiety or by grasping.

**"Verily of His Servants He Is Aware, Seeing"**

*Innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — the same pair that closed verse 17. The placement here gives it a specific force: God's awareness of His servants is the *reason* the distribution is what it is. It is not random, not a matter of luck, not the outcome of a purely mechanical competition. It proceeds from knowledge of each servant's condition. The classical commentators draw the pastoral conclusion: a person whose provision is straitened should not read it as abandonment, because the One who straitened it knows him. And a person whose provision is expanded should not read it as endorsement, for the same reason. Both readings of one's own circumstances are forbidden by the same attribute.

****The Verse as a Pun on the Miser's Hand****

Read against the verse immediately before it, this one functions as a deliberate contrast, and the wordplay is close enough that the commentators treat it as intended. Verse 29 forbids the human hand from being *maghlūlah* — shackled to the neck. Verse 30 declares that God's action is *yabsuṭu*, outspreading, from the same root as the divine Name *al-Bāsiṭ*, the Expander. And 5:64 states the corresponding denial in terms the Qur'an treats as blasphemy: *"The Jews say, 'God's Hand is shackled.' Shackled are their hands, and cursed are they for what they say. Nay, but His two Hands are outspread (mabsūṭatān); He spends as He wills."* The miser is thus measured against a generosity whose opposite is precisely what he has become, and the condemnation is theological before it is moral. The pairing of *yabsuṭu* with *yaqdir* — He expands and He straitens — appears as a fixed formula at 13:26, 28:82, 29:62, 30:37, 34:36 and 39, 39:52, and 42:12, and al-Rāzī draws from it the two consequences that dismantle both extremes of verse 29. If God expands, hoarding is futile, because hoarding does not add to what has been apportioned. If God straitens, spending beyond one's means is equally futile, because expenditure does not force provision open. Both vices are therefore not only wrong but pointless, which is a stronger argument than prohibition alone. The closing pair, *khabīran baṣīrā*, supplies the pastoral ground: the distribution proceeds from One who sees each servant's condition, so the person whose provision is straitened should not read it as abandonment, and the person whose provision is expanded should not read it as endorsement. Both readings of one's own circumstances are forbidden by the same attribute, and the Prophet ﷺ stated the underlying principle in the report of Muslim (2643), in which the angel is commanded to write four things before birth: provision, life span, deeds, and whether the person is wretched or happy.

**The Verse as Answer to the Complaint of Inequality**

Verse 21 commanded the reader to *look* at the fact of gradation. Verse 30 explains its governance. Together they answer the perennial complaint: the inequality is real, it is not deserved in any simple sense, and it is administered by One who sees. The Qur'an does not resolve the complaint by denying the inequality; it resolves it by locating the inequality within knowledge rather than chance — and by promising, in verse 21, that the greater gradation lies elsewhere.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:31

> **And slay not your children for fear of poverty. We shall provide for them and for you. Surely their slaying is a great sin.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Practice the Verse Addresses**

*Wa lā taqtulū awlādakum khashyata imlāq* — "and do not kill your children for fear of poverty." The occasion is specific and well documented. Among certain tribes of pre-Islamic Arabia, infanticide was practiced — the burying alive of infant daughters, driven by fear of poverty and of the shame associated with female children. The Qur'an returns to the practice repeatedly, and its eschatological indictment is among the most severe in the Book: 81:8–9, *"And when the buried girl (al-mawʾūdah) is asked: for what sin was she slain?"* The parallel verse at 6:151 places the same prohibition in a similar list: *"And slay not your children out of poverty — We provide for you and for them."* The two verses differ in a meaningful detail, which the commentators noticed. In 6:151 the wording is *min imlāq* — "out of poverty," i.e., from poverty already present — and the assurance is *"We provide for you and for them,"* placing the parents first. Here the wording is *khashyata imlāq* — "for fear of poverty," i.e., poverty anticipated but not yet present — and the assurance is *"We provide for them and for you,"* placing the children first. Al-Rāzī and others draw the inference: when poverty is already present, the parents' need is the pressing concern, so they are mentioned first; when poverty is merely feared, the child's provision is the concern, so the child is mentioned first. The variation is a small masterpiece of Qur'anic precision.

**The Theological Correction**

The verse does not merely prohibit; it *refutes*. The fear of poverty rests on a false belief about who provides. If you believe the child will consume your provision, you will kill the child. If you understand that the child arrives with provision apportioned by God, the fear dissolves. *Naḥnu narzuquhum wa iyyākum* — "We provide for them and for you." The subject is God, and the order of provision is His. This is why the prohibition of infanticide is inseparable from the doctrine of *rizq*. Every act of killing one's own child for economic reasons presupposes that the parent is the source of sustenance; the verse denies the premise. The Prophet ﷺ named the sin among the gravest. Al-Bukhārī (6871) and Muslim (86) record:

> *"I asked: 'O Messenger of God, which sin is greatest with God?' He said: 'That thou make a rival for God while He created thee.' I said: 'Then which?' He said: 'That thou slay thy child for fear that he will eat with thee.'"*

The two sins are joined — shirk and infanticide — because the second flows from the first: to believe that provision comes from anywhere but God is already to have set up a rival.

**"Surely Their Slaying Is a Great Sin"**

*Inna qatlahum kāna khiṭʾan kabīrā* — *khiṭʾ* means a sin, a wrong, an error of grave kind; *kabīr* means great. The verse closes with a flat declaration, unqualified by any mitigating circumstance. The Qur'an uses *kabīr* for sin in only a few places, most notably 2:219 on intoxicants and gambling — *"in them is great sin (ithmun kabīr) and some benefit for men, but their sin is greater than their benefit."* The classification places infanticide among the *kabāʾir*, the major sins, in the technical vocabulary of Islamic law.

****Two Verses, Two Orderings: The Precision of the Provision****

The commentators noted a difference between this verse and its parallel at 6:151 that is small enough to miss and exact enough to be deliberate. There the wording is *min imlāq* — "out of poverty," i.e., from poverty already present — and the assurance runs *"We provide for you and for them,"* placing the parents first. Here the wording is *khashyata imlāq* — "for fear of poverty," i.e., poverty anticipated but not yet present — and the assurance runs *"We provide for them and for you,"* placing the children first. Al-Rāzī states the inference: when poverty is already present, the parents' need is the pressing concern, so they are named first; when poverty is merely feared, the child's provision is the concern, so the child is named first. The variation is a small masterpiece of precision, and it demonstrates that the Qur'an's word order carries meaning at the level of a single sequence. The theological correction beneath the prohibition is what makes it more than a legal ruling. The fear of poverty rests on a false belief about who provides: if the parent believes the child will consume the household's sustenance, killing the child appears rational; if he understands that the child arrives with provision apportioned by God, the fear loses its premise. This is why the sin is joined to shirk in the report of al-Bukhārī (6871) and Muslim (86), in which the Prophet ﷺ ranks the gravest sins and places the slaying of one's child for fear that he will eat with him second only to making a rival for God. To believe that provision comes from anywhere but God is already to have set up a rival. The classification of the act as *khiṭʾ kabīr* — a great sin — places it among the *kabāʾir* in the technical vocabulary of the law, the same category the Qur'an assigns to intoxicants and gambling at 2:219, and the jurists extended its principle to every situation in which a human being is treated as an economic liability: the aged parent whose care is costly, the disabled child whose upbringing is expensive, the dependent relative who consumes resources.

**The Extension of the Principle**

The classical jurists extended the verse's principle well beyond the Arabian practice. It is the primary textual basis for the prohibition of abortion, debated in the schools with respect to timing and to cases of genuine necessity — a discussion the verse itself does not settle, but which the schools conduct within its frame. It is also invoked against the modern forms of the same logic: the refusal to have children for economic reasons is not, in Islamic law, the same as killing them, but the verse's diagnosis — fear of poverty as a motive for refusing life — is what the commentators apply. The deeper application is to every situation in which a human being is treated as an economic liability. The aged parent whose care is costly, the disabled child whose upbringing is expensive, the dependent relative who consumes resources — the logic the verse refutes is not confined to infants. Its premise is that a person's value is measured by his consumption, and its answer is that provision comes from God, not from the household budget.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:32

> **And approach not adultery; verily it is indecency and an evil way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Approach Not": The Grammar of Prevention**

*Wa lā taqrabū al-zinā* — "and do not *approach* unlawful sexual intercourse." The verb is not "do not commit" (*lā tafʿalū*) but "do not come near" (*lā taqrabū*). The choice is one of the Qur'an's most consequential legal-linguistic decisions, and the commentators treat it as deliberate. Al-Qurṭubī explains that prohibiting the approach prohibits the means as well as the end: the glance, the seclusion, the conversation, the arrangement of circumstances. A prohibition on the act alone could be observed while every road to it remained open; a prohibition on approach closes the roads. The same construction appears at 2:187 regarding the fast, at 2:229 regarding transgressing limits, and at 6:151 in the parallel list. It reflects the Islamic legal maxim *sadd al-dharāʾiʿ* — blocking the means — which holds that what leads to the forbidden is itself forbidden.

**Zinā: The Scope of the Term**

*Zinā* designates sexual intercourse outside a valid marriage. The Qur'an legislates it at 24:2–3 with corporal penalties, and the Prophetic tradition extends the concept to the lesser forms. Al-Bukhārī (6243) and Muslim (2657) record:

> *"The eyes commit zinā, and their zinā is the look; the ears commit zinā, and their zinā is listening; the tongue commits zinā, and its zinā is speech; the hand commits zinā, and its zinā is the touch; the foot commits zinā, and its zinā is the step; and the heart desires and wishes — and the private part confirms that or denies it."*

The ḥadīth does not equate these with the legal sin; it explains the *mechanism* by which the legal sin is approached — which is precisely what the verse's verb prohibits.

**"Verily It Is an Indecency and an Evil Way"**

*Innahu kāna fāḥishatan wa sāʾa sabīlā* — *fāḥishah* is from *f-ḥ-sh*, exceeding measure; it denotes that which is grossly excessive, shameful, obscene. The Qur'an applies the term to zinā here, to the sin of Lot's people at 7:80, and to stinginess at 2:268 — *"Satan threatens you with poverty and commands you to indecency (bi-l-faḥshāʾ)."* The word indicates a transgression that exceeds not only law but the natural measure of things. *Wa sāʾa sabīlā* — "and evil is it as a way." The noun *sabīl* means path, road. The Qur'an characteristically describes moral states as roads: the straight path (*al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*), the way of error, the way of those who earned wrath. Calling zinā an *evil way* rather than an evil act makes a claim about trajectory: it is not an isolated event but a road with a direction and a destination. The one who enters it finds that it goes somewhere, and that the somewhere is worse than the beginning.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Between the Prohibitions of Killing**

The sequence is striking: verse 31 forbids killing one's children; verse 32 forbids zinā; verse 33 forbids killing the inviolable soul. The commentators note the internal logic. Zinā destroys lineage (*nasab*), which is one of the five things Islamic law exists to protect — religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property. A child born of uncertain paternity is, in the pre-modern world, without inheritance, without protection, without standing. The destruction of lineage is a form of social killing, and it is placed between two literal killings for that reason. Al-Ghazālī and the jurists after him organized the whole of Islamic law around these five protected interests, and verses 31–34 of this sūrah cover three of them in order: life (31), lineage (32), life again (33), and property (34). The sequence is not arbitrary; it is a compressed legal theory.

****Lā Taqrabū: The Legal Principle of Blocking the Means****

The verb is not *lā tafʿalū* — do not commit — but *lā taqrabū*, do not come near, and al-Qurṭubī treats the choice as the foundation of a general jurisprudential principle: *sadd al-dharāʾiʿ*, the blocking of the means, which holds that what leads to the forbidden is itself forbidden. A prohibition on the act alone can be observed while every road to it remains open; a prohibition on approach closes the roads. The same construction appears at 2:187 concerning the fast, at 2:229 concerning the transgression of limits, and at 6:151 in the parallel list, which indicates that the device is a settled feature of Qur'anic legislation rather than a rhetorical variation. The Prophetic tradition supplies the mechanism the prohibition targets. Al-Bukhārī (6243) and Muslim (2657) record:

> *"The eyes commit zinā and their zinā is the look; the ears commit zinā and their zinā is listening; the tongue commits zinā and its zinā is speech; the hand commits zinā and its zinā is the touch; the foot commits zinā and its zinā is the step; and the heart desires and wishes — and the private part confirms that or denies it."* (al-Bukhārī 6243)

The ḥadīth does not equate these with the legal sin; it describes the sequence by which the legal sin is approached, which is exactly what the verse's verb forbids. The social logic is stated in the verse's own terms: *fāḥishah* denotes an excess beyond measure, and *sāʾa sabīlā* — an evil way — describes a trajectory rather than an event. The placement between two prohibitions of killing is the key to the classical reading: the jurists organized the whole of the law around five protected interests — religion, life, intellect, lineage, and property — and verses 31 to 34 of this sūrah cover three of them in order, with zinā destroying *nasab* (lineage). A child of uncertain paternity was, in the pre-modern world, without inheritance, protection, or standing, so the destruction of lineage functions as a form of social killing, which is why it stands between two literal ones.

**The Social Logic of the Prohibition**

The Qur'an does not argue for sexual restraint on the basis of sentiment but on the basis of consequence. Zinā is *fāḥishah* — an excess beyond measure — because it separates the sexual act from the structure of responsibility that makes its consequences bearable. The child produced has claims; the act that produced it created no one obligated to meet them. The prohibition is therefore not primarily about the body but about the *unmet claims* the act generates. The modern reader who finds the prohibition harsh should note what the verse does *not* say. It does not describe desire as evil, does not describe the body as unclean, and does not treat the sinner as beyond recovery — the Qur'an's account of repentance is open to all, and 25:68–70 lists zinā among the sins of those who, upon repenting and believing, *"God will change their evil deeds into good."* The prohibition is on the *way*, not on the person, and the way back is explicitly available.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:33

> **And slay not the soul that God has made inviolable, save by right. And whosoever is slain unjustly, We have appointed authority unto his heir. Then let him not be excessive in slaying. Verily he shall be helped.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Inviolable Soul**

*Wa lā taqtulū al-nafsa allatī ḥarrama Allāhu illā bi-l-ḥaqq* — "and do not kill the soul that God has made *ḥarām* except by right." The verb *ḥarrama* means to make forbidden, sacred, inviolable; the same root gives *ḥaram*, the sacred precinct, and *iḥrām*, the pilgrim's consecrated state. To call a soul *muḥarramah* is to place it in the category of the sacred — not because of anything it has done but because God has so designated it. The parallel is 6:151, and the general principle is stated at 5:32 in the verse that has become the Qur'an's most quoted statement on the value of life:

> *"Whoever slays a soul, not for a soul or for corruption in the land, it is as if he had slain all mankind; and whoever saves a life, it is as if he had saved all mankind."*

**"Save by Right": The Exception and Its Limits**

*Illā bi-l-ḥaqq* — the exception is narrow and legally specified. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr identify the cases: capital punishment for murder after due process, and the punishments prescribed for waging war against God and His Messenger (5:33). The exception is defined by the Law, not by private judgment — a limitation of enormous importance in a society in which blood vengeance was customary. The Prophet ﷺ specified the categories in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (6878) and Muslim (1676):

> *"The blood of a Muslim who testifies that there is no god but God and that I am the Messenger of God is not lawful except in one of three: a life for a life, a married person who commits adultery, and the one who abandons his religion and separates from the community."*

**"We Have Appointed Authority Unto His Heir"**

*Fa-qad jaʿalnā li-waliyyihi sulṭānā* — *walī* means the near relative, the protector, the one with standing; *sulṭān* means authority, warrant, power. Al-Zamakhsharī explains *walī* as the relative close enough to the deceased to claim retribution. The authority granted has three possible exercises, as Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī all state: to demand execution of the murderer in retaliation (*qiṣāṣ*), to pardon, or to accept the blood-money (*diyah*). The granting of *choice* is the crucial feature. Pre-Islamic Arabia operated on mandatory vengeance, typically extended to the tribe. The verse transfers the decision to a specific individual and makes it discretionary. Al-Bukhārī (2434) records the Prophet's ﷺ encouragement of the merciful option:

> *"Whoever has a right of retaliation and he pardons, it is an expiation for him."*

And the Qur'an itself recommends pardon at 2:178: *"But whosoever is pardoned somewhat by his brother, let the following be honorable and the payment be with kindness"* — and at 5:45, *"And whosoever judges by what God has revealed, those are the righteous,"* after stating *"a life for a life... and whosoever forgoes it in charity, it is an expiation for him."* The historical use of the verse is recorded by Ibn Kathīr: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ invoked it at the arbitration following Ṣiffīn (37/658) to argue that Muʿāwiyah, as *walī* of the slain Caliph ʿUthmān, possessed *sulṭān* — political authority. The exegetes record the argument and generally decline to endorse the extension of *sulṭān* from the right of retribution to the caliphate.

**"Let Him Not Be Excessive in Slaying"**

*Fa-lā yusrif fī al-qatl* — the prohibition of *isrāf*, excess, is the verse's most socially transformative clause. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the requirement that retaliation fall only on the actual perpetrator and not extend to his family or tribe. Al-Qurṭubī adds the further prohibition: the condemned must not be tortured, maimed, or mutilated before execution. This clause abolished the mechanism by which a single homicide produced decades of tribal war. In the pre-Islamic practice, the killing of one man could justify the killing of his kinsman of equal or greater status, escalating indefinitely. The verse terminates the chain at the individual. Compare 2:194, *"And transgress not; truly God loves not the transgressors,"* and 5:45, which sets the retaliation as an equal exchange.

**"Verily He Shall Be Helped"**

*Innahu kāna manṣūrā* — the pronoun is read by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī as referring to the heir: he is supported by the Law, by the community, and by God in his claim. Others read it as the slain person, whose cause is not lost. Either reading makes the same point: the victim is not abandoned. The verse's assurance is directed at the family of the murdered — the people most likely to take the law into their own hands precisely because they feel no one else will act.

****Sulṭān With Three Options: How a Chain of Vengeance Was Cut****

The verse's central achievement is not the authorization of retaliation but its *discretion*, and the difference abolished a mechanism that had produced generations of tribal war. Pre-Islamic Arabia operated on mandatory vengeance, extended to the kin: the killing of one man could justify the killing of his kinsman of equal or greater status, escalating indefinitely, as in the war of al-Basūs that ran for forty years over a single camel. This verse transfers the decision to a specific individual — the *walī*, the near relative with standing — and gives him three options: to demand execution in retaliation (*qiṣāṣ*), to pardon, or to accept the blood-money (*diyah*). 2:178 states the same structure and then recommends the merciful option: *"But whosoever is pardoned somewhat by his brother, let the following be honorable and the payment be with kindness."* The clause *fa-lā yusrif fī al-qatl* — let him not be excessive in slaying — is the clause that ends the chain. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the requirement that retaliation fall only on the actual perpetrator and not extend to his family or tribe; al-Qurṭubī adds the prohibition of torture, mutilation, or disfigurement before execution. The two together convert vengeance into law: the punishment is proportionate, individual, and executed by authority rather than by the aggrieved. Al-Bukhārī (2434) records the incentive attached to mercy:

> *"Whoever has a right of retaliation and he pardons, it is an expiation for him."* (al-Bukhārī 2434)

And 5:45 states the principle as a moral ranking: *"And We ordained for them therein a life for a life... and whosoever forgoes it in charity, it is an expiation for him."* The verse's assurance that the heir *kāna manṣūrā* — will be helped — is directed at the family of the murdered, the people most likely to take the law into their own hands precisely because they feel no one else will act. The historical use of the verse is worth noting for its limits: Ibn Kathīr records that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ invoked it at the arbitration after Ṣiffīn to argue that Muʿāwiyah, as *walī* of the slain ʿUthmān, possessed *sulṭān* in the sense of political authority, and the exegetes generally decline to endorse the extension of a right of retribution into a claim to the caliphate.

**The Modern Significance**

The verse establishes three principles that remain contested everywhere: that life is inviolable by default, that the exception must be legal rather than private, and that even the legal exception must not exceed the perpetrator. Any system in which punishment falls on family members, in which retaliation is unlimited, or in which killing is decided by the aggrieved party rather than by law has departed from all three. The verse is the Qur'an's compact theory of criminal justice, and its central achievement is the substitution of proportion for escalation.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:34

> **And approach not the orphan's property, save in the most virtuous manner, till he reaches maturity. And fulfill the pact; surely the pact is called to account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Again, "Approach Not"**

*Wa lā taqrabū māla al-yatīm* — the same verb used of zinā in verse 32. The prohibition is on *approach*, not merely on consumption, and the commentators apply it accordingly: the guardian may not place himself in a position where the orphan's wealth is within his casual reach. Al-Qurṭubī explains that this forbids not only theft but commingling, speculative investment without safeguards, and any arrangement in which the guardian's interest could conflict with the orphan's. The Qur'an's concern for orphans is among its most persistent themes. It appears in 2:83, 177, 215, 220; 4:2–10; 6:152; 8:41; 89:17; 93:9; and 107:2, where the one who *"repulses the orphan"* is numbered among those who deny the religion. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (5304) and Muslim (2983):

> *"I and the one who supports the orphan will be in the Garden like this" — and he indicated with his index and middle fingers, separating them slightly.*

And al-Bukhārī (6011) records the pairing of the sin:

> *"Avoid the seven destructive sins... associating partners with God, sorcery, slaying the soul God has forbidden except by right, consuming usury, consuming the orphan's property, fleeing on the day of battle, and slandering chaste, heedless, believing women."*

**"Save in the Most Virtuous Manner"**

*Illā bi-llatī hiya aḥsan* — the exception is not a permission to use but a standard for handling. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī specify the permitted cases: where the property is land or animals, the guardian may use it in a way that maintains rather than depletes it; an indigent guardian may take what is strictly necessary; and the guardian may trade the property in order to increase it. The governing principle is that every act must be *aḥsan* — better than the alternative. The Qur'an legislates the same at 6:152, *"And approach not the orphan's property save in the most virtuous manner, till he reaches his strength,"* and at 4:6, *"And test the orphans until they reach marriageable age; then if you perceive in them sound judgment, deliver to them their property, and consume it not wastefully and hastily, [anticipating] that they will grow up."* That last clause — *badāran an yakbarū* — captures the specific temptation the verse addresses: the guardian who spends the orphan's wealth quickly, *before the orphan grows up and asks for it*.

**"Till He Reaches Maturity"**

*Ḥattā yablugha ashuddah* — *ashudd* denotes the fullness of strength, physical and mental maturity.
