The Qur'an makes the transfer of property conditional on demonstrated capacity, not on age alone, which is why 4:6 requires *testing*. This is a genuinely advanced piece of legislation: the orphan's wealth is protected until the orphan is *competent*, not merely adult.

**"And Fulfill the Pact"**

*Wa awfū bi-l-ʿahd* — the command shifts abruptly from orphans to covenants, and the shift is meaningful: guardianship *is* a covenant. The guardian undertook a trust, and the verse treats the undertaking as binding. The Qur'an commands the fulfillment of pacts throughout: 2:40, *"O Children of Israel, remember My favor... and fulfill My covenant, that I may fulfill your covenant"*; 5:1, *"O you who believe, fulfill the contracts (al-ʿuqūd)"*; 16:91, *"And fulfill the covenant of God when you have made a covenant, and break not oaths after confirming them"*; 8:58; 9:4; 13:20; 23:8; 33:15; 70:32.

**"Surely the Pact Is Called to Account"**

*Inna al-ʿahda kāna masʾūlā* — the construction is grammatically striking: the *pact* is described as the thing questioned, not the person. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain it as a rhetorical intensification — the covenant itself will be brought forward and asked, as though it were a witness. The device personalizes the obligation: the promise you made is not a passing speech-act but an entity that survives you and will testify. Compare 24:24, where the tongue and hands testify, and 41:20–22, where the skin testifies. The Qur'an consistently imagines the instruments of human action becoming witnesses. The promise is added to that list — which means the casual commitment, the verbal assurance, the contract signed without reading, all acquire an eschatological weight that ordinary life does not assign them.

****Badāran an Yakbarū: The Guardian's Specific Temptation****

The Qur'an legislates the orphan's property twice, and the second formulation names the temptation so precisely that it functions as a diagnosis. 4:6: *"And test the orphans until they reach marriageable age; then if you perceive in them sound judgment, deliver to them their property, and consume it not wastefully and hastily, anticipating that they will grow up (badāran an yakbarū)."* The final clause identifies the exact pressure on a guardian: the child cannot yet ask, cannot yet account, and will one day arrive with the right to demand. The interval between the death of the father and the maturity of the son is therefore a period in which the only check on the guardian is his own conscience, which is why the prohibition here is on *approach* rather than on consumption — the guardian may not place himself where the wealth is within his casual reach. The permissions in the phrase *illā bi-llatī hiya aḥsan* are correspondingly careful. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī specify the cases: where the property is land or animals, the guardian may use it in ways that maintain rather than deplete it; an indigent guardian may take what is strictly necessary; and the guardian may trade the property in order to increase it. The governing standard is comparative — every act must be *aḥsan*, better than the alternative — and 4:2 supplies the sanction: *"Truly those who consume the property of orphans unjustly consume nothing but fire into their bellies."* The second command, on the covenant, is not a separate topic but the same one stated more generally, since guardianship *is* a covenant. The grammatical striking of the closing phrase is what gives it force: *inna al-ʿahda kāna masʾūlā* — the covenant itself is described as questioned, not the person who made it. Al-Rāzī explains the construction as a rhetorical intensification, as though the promise were a witness summoned to testify. The device belongs to a family of Qur'anic images in which the instruments of human action become witnesses — the tongue and hands at 24:24, the skin at 41:20–22 — and its application is uncomfortably current: the casual assurance, the verbal commitment, the contract signed without reading, all acquire a weight that ordinary life does not assign them.

**The Two Commands as One**

Orphan's property and covenants seem unrelated until one sees the common thread: both are *trusts held against a future claim*. The orphan's wealth is held against the day he matures; the covenant is held against the day it is called for. In both cases the holder is tempted by the fact that no one is currently watching and no one can currently enforce. The verse addresses precisely that temptation, and its answer is the same in both cases: the accounting is coming, and the covenant itself will be asked.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:35

> **And give full measure when you measure, and weigh with the straight balance. That is better and more virtuous in the end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Marketplace as a Site of Revelation**

*Wa awfū al-kayla idhā kiltum wa zinū bi-l-qisṭāsi al-mustaqīm* — "and give full measure when you measure, and weigh with the straight balance." The verse places commercial honesty inside a moral code that begins with tawḥīd and ends with the prohibition of shirk. The placement is a claim: the marketplace is not a morally neutral zone governed by its own rules. What a man does with his scales is of the same order as what he does with his prayer. The Qur'an returns to this command with remarkable persistence. Sūrah al-Muṭaffifīn — "The Defrauders" — is named for it and opens with a woe:

> *"Woe to the defrauders, who, when they take measure from mankind, take it in full; but when they measure or weigh for them, they diminish. Do they not think that they will be resurrected for a tremendous Day?"* (83:1–5)

And Shuʿayb's mission to Midian was substantially economic: *"O my people, give full measure and weight with justice, and withhold not from mankind their goods, and do not act corruptly in the land, making mischief"* (11:85). And again at 26:181–183. And 55:8–9 makes it a cosmic principle: *"And the heaven He raised, and He set the balance, that you not transgress in the balance — so establish the weight with justice and diminish not the balance."*

**Al-Qisṭās al-Mustaqīm**

*Qisṭās* is a loanword, likely from Greek, meaning balance or scale; *mustaqīm* is the straight, the upright — the same root as *ṣirāṭ mustaqīm*, the straight path of the Fātiḥah, and connected to *aqwam* in verse 9. The pairing of the *straight* balance with the *straight* path is not accidental in a sūrah that opened by describing the Qur'an as guiding to what is *most upright*. Justice in the marketplace and uprightness in the soul are the same quality expressed in different media.

**Two Forms of the Same Fraud**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain that the command covers both directions: giving short measure when selling, and demanding excess when buying. The Muṭaffifīn passage makes the asymmetry explicit — the defrauder takes in full and gives short. This is the exact structure of most economic dishonesty: the party with more information or more power adjusts the measure in his favor, and the other party cannot verify. The verse's generalization is important: it applies not only to physical scales but to every exchange in which what is given and what is received are meant to correspond. Al-Qurṭubī and later commentators extend it to wages, to contracts, to the description of goods, to the concealment of defects. The modern forms — misrepresentation in advertising, hidden fees, the undisclosed conflict of interest, the wage paid late or short — fall under the same command because they are the same act: a manipulated measure.

**"That Is Better and More Virtuous in the End"**

*dhālika khayrun wa aḥsanu taʾwīlā* — *taʾwīl* means outcome, final interpretation, where the matter eventually arrives; from *a-w-l*, to return. The phrase means: this is better, and better in its *outcome*. The verse makes a claim about the long-run consequences of honesty — that the fair dealer's position is ultimately better even in worldly terms, and certainly in the accounting. The claim is counterintuitive enough to require the argument. In the short run, the defrauder profits. The Qur'an does not deny this; it disputes its *duration*. Compare 3:185, *"And what is the life of this world but the enjoyment of delusion,"* and 30:39, *"And whatever you give of usury that it may increase in the wealth of mankind, it increases not with God."* The economics of the Qur'an run on a longer horizon than the quarter.

**Why the Verse Belongs in This Code**

Verses 31–34 covered life, lineage, and property. Verse 35 covers the mechanism by which property changes hands. A society that protects life and property but permits fraudulent exchange has protected nothing, because the fraudulent exchange accomplishes by stealth what theft accomplishes by force. The verse is thus not a commercial appendix to the moral code but its economic enforcement mechanism.

****Al-Qisṭās: A Foreign Word Placed in the Moral Code****

The word *qisṭās* is a loanword, most likely from Greek, meaning a balance or scale, and its presence in the middle of a code that opens with tawḥīd and closes with the prohibition of shirk is itself a claim. The Qur'an could have used the native *mīzān*, which it does use elsewhere — 55:7–9, *"And the heaven He raised, and He set the balance, that you not transgress in the balance — so establish the weight with justice and diminish not the balance."* The choice of a commercial foreign term anchors the command in the actual marketplace of the audience rather than in an abstract register, and it places the counting-house inside the moral code rather than beside it. The command runs in both directions, and Sūrah al-Muṭaffifīn is named for the asymmetry:

> *"Woe to the defrauders, who, when they take measure from mankind, take it in full; but when they measure or weigh for them, they diminish. Do they not think that they will be resurrected for a tremendous Day?"* (83:1–5)

The defrauder takes in full and gives short, which is the structure of most economic dishonesty: the party with more information or more power adjusts the measure in his favor, and the other party cannot verify. This is why al-Qurṭubī and the jurists after him extended the command well beyond physical scales — to wages, to the description of goods, to the concealment of defects, to every exchange in which what is given and what is received are meant to correspond. The mission of Shuʿayb to Midian was substantially economic, which indicates how central the issue is to the Qur'anic conception of a just society: 11:85, *"O my people, give full measure and weight with justice, and withhold not from mankind their goods, and do not act corruptly in the land, making mischief."* The closing phrase *aḥsanu taʾwīlā* — better in its outcome — makes a claim about the long run that is counterintuitive enough to require the argument, since in the short run the defrauder plainly profits. The Qur'an does not deny this; it disputes its duration, as at 30:39: *"And whatever you give of usury that it may increase in the wealth of mankind, it increases not with God."* The economics of the Qur'an run on a longer horizon than the quarter, and the tradition attaches to commercial honesty a rank that indicates how reliable an indicator it is: al-Tirmidhī (1209) records, *"The truthful, trustworthy merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs."*

**The Practical Weight**

The command is unusually testable. A person can deceive himself about almost any virtue; he cannot easily deceive himself about whether he gave short measure. This is why the ḥadīth tradition attaches such weight to the marketplace. Al-Bukhārī (2079) records:

> *"The truthful, trustworthy merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs."* (al-Tirmidhī 1209, graded ḥasan)

The rank is extraordinary — the honest trader placed with the prophets — and it exists because commercial honesty is performed daily, under continuous temptation, in private, and with immediate material cost. It is therefore among the most reliable indicators of character available.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:36

> **And pursue not that whereof you have no knowledge. Truly hearing, and sight, and the heart—all of these will be called to account.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Governs Speech, Inquiry, and Belief**

*Wa lā taqfu mā laysa laka bihi ʿilm* — "and do not follow what you have no knowledge of." The verb *taqfū* is from *q-f-w*, to follow on the heels, to track, to pursue — the same root as *qāfah*, a tracker who reads footprints. The image is of someone following a trail without knowing where it leads or whether it is a trail at all. Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify three applications, all of which the verb supports. The first is *speech*: do not say what you do not know. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as the prohibition of false testimony and of claiming knowledge one does not possess. The Qur'an makes the same point with devastating force in the slander of ʿĀʾishah: *"When you received it with your tongues and said with your mouths that of which you had no knowledge, and thought it a small matter — but it is great in the sight of God"* (24:15). The second is *inquiry*: do not investigate private matters in order to expose the faults of others. This connects to 49:12, *"And spy not (wa lā tajassasū), nor let some of you slander others."* The tracker's instinct — to follow another's trail to find what he has hidden — is prohibited. The third is *following*: do not follow a path or a leader without knowing whether he will guide or mislead. Al-Zamakhsharī reads the verse as a rebuke of *taqlīd*, unthinking imitation in matters of belief. The commentators note the careful distinction: in law, the non-specialist necessarily follows the rulings of the qualified; in creed, blind following is not permitted, because belief requires knowledge.

**The Debate Over Speculation**

Al-Rāzī records an argument among the jurists about whether this verse prohibits reasoning by analogy (*qiyās*) in law, since analogy involves a degree of inference beyond certain knowledge. The majority responded that many necessary determinations — the direction of the qiblah from a distant location, the issuance of a non-binding legal opinion — inherently involve estimation, and that the verse prohibits following *without* knowledge, not reasoning *toward* knowledge. The distinction is between conjecture that has been examined and conjecture that has not.

**The Three Faculties**

*Inna al-samʿa wa-l-baṣara wa-l-fuʾāda kullu ulāʾika kāna ʿanhu masʾūlā* — "truly hearing, and sight, and the heart — each of those will be questioned about it." The Qur'an names these three as the instruments of knowledge repeatedly: 16:78, *"And God brought you forth from the wombs of your mothers knowing nothing, and gave you hearing and sight and hearts, that perhaps you would be grateful"*; 23:78; 32:9; 67:23:

> *"Say: He is Who created you and made for you hearing and sight and hearts. Little thanks you give."*

The three are gifts for which gratitude is owed, and they can be withdrawn: 2:7, *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and upon their sight is a covering"*; 6:46; 45:23, *"God has sealed his heart and his hearing, and set a covering over his sight."* The faculties are not automatically reliable; they are accountable instruments that can be corrupted by misuse.

**"Each of Those Will Be Questioned"**

The pronoun in *ʿanhu masʾūlā* admits two readings, and both are recorded. The first: the person will be questioned about each faculty — how did you use your hearing, your sight, your heart? The second: the faculties themselves will be questioned, as witnesses. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both preserve the second, connecting it to the testimony of the limbs at 24:24 and 41:20–22. The second reading is the more unsettling and the more precise for the verse's purpose. If the faculties testify, then the ear that listened to slander, the eye that sought what it should not see, and the heart that entertained what it should not entertain will each give an account. The instruments are not neutral channels; they are participants.

**Why This Verse Is in the Moral Code**

Placed between the command of honest weights (v. 35) and the prohibition of arrogance (v. 37), the verse addresses the epistemic vices that sit between commercial and personal ones. False testimony destroys justice as false measure destroys trade; arrogant bearing proceeds from a heart that claims to know more than it does. The sequence treats the mind's honesty as a moral obligation of the same rank as the hand's honesty.

****Qafw and Tajassus: Two Prohibitions About Attention****

The verb *taqfū* is from *q-f-w*, to follow on the heels, to track — the same root as *qāfah*, the tracker who reads footprints in sand — and the image determines the range of the prohibition. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī identify three applications, all supported by the verb. The first is speech: do not say what you do not know, which includes false testimony and the claim to knowledge one does not possess. The second is inquiry: do not investigate private matters in order to expose the faults of others, which is the prohibition stated at 49:12, *"And spy not (wa lā tajassasū), nor let some of you slander others."* The third is following: do not follow a path or a guide without knowing whether he will guide or mislead, which al-Zamakhsharī reads as a rebuke of *taqlīd* in matters of belief. The distinction the jurists drew on the third reading is careful and still governing. In law, the non-specialist necessarily follows the rulings of the qualified; in creed, blind following is not permitted, because belief requires knowledge. Al-Rāzī records the related dispute over whether the verse forbids reasoning by analogy (*qiyās*), since analogy involves inference beyond certain knowledge, and the majority response: many necessary determinations — the direction of the qiblah from a distant location, the issuance of a non-binding legal opinion — inherently involve estimation, so the verse prohibits following *without* knowledge, not reasoning *toward* it. The line runs between examined conjecture and unexamined conjecture. The closing clause supplies the enforcement mechanism, and its two readings are both preserved. Either the person will be questioned about each faculty — how he used his hearing, his sight, and his heart — or the faculties themselves will be questioned, as witnesses, in the manner of 41:20–22, where the skin says, *"God, who gives speech to all things, gave us speech."* The second reading is the more precise for the verse's purpose, because it makes the instruments participants rather than neutral channels: the ear that listened to slander, the eye that sought what it should not see, and the heart that entertained what it should not entertain will each give an account. No verse in this sūrah addresses the modern condition more directly, since an environment in which assertion is costless and the forwarding of the unverified costs one tap is a mass implementation of *qafw*. The rule is not a prohibition of ignorance but of acting as though one knows; the person who says "I do not know" has complied.

**The Contemporary Urgency**

No verse in this sūrah is more directly addressed to the modern condition. An information environment in which assertion is costless, in which a claim travels faster than its verification, and in which the sharing of the unverified is a single tap, is a mass implementation of *qafw* — following what one has no knowledge of. The verse's prohibition is not against ignorance; it is against *acting as though one knows*. The person who says "I don't know" has complied; the person who forwards, asserts, or accuses on the basis of a headline has not. The verse supplies both the rule and its enforcement mechanism. The rule: do not pursue what you do not know. The mechanism: your hearing, your sight, and your heart will be asked. The one who scrolls, watches, and inwardly entertains is not performing a neutral act; he is using accountable instruments, and the accounting will include how they were used.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:37

> **And walk not exultantly upon the earth; surely thou shalt not penetrate the earth, nor reach the mountains in height.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prohibition of the Arrogant Gait**

*Wa lā tamshi fī al-arḍi maraḥā* — "and do not walk upon the earth *maraḥan*." The noun *maraḥ* denotes exultant, self-conceited walking — the gait of one pleased with himself. The parallel is Luqmān's counsel to his son at 31:18: *"And turn not thy cheek from mankind in disdain, nor walk upon the earth exultantly (maraḥā). Truly God loves not any arrogant boaster."* And the contrasting description of the servants of the Compassionate at 25:63: *"And the servants of the Compassionate are those who walk upon the earth gently (hawnā)."* The Qur'an legislates a *gait* — not a feeling but a manner of moving the body through public space. The choice is characteristically concrete: arrogance is most legible in how a person walks, and the command is aimed at the visible sign because the visible sign shapes the interior.

**Why God Prohibits a Way of Walking**

A reader may wonder why a revealed book concerns itself with posture. The commentators' answer is that the body is not separate from the soul; it trains it. Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur'an addresses both directions of the relationship — the interior producing the exterior (arrogance producing the arrogant walk) and the exterior reinforcing the interior (the arrogant walk deepening the arrogance). Prohibiting the gait is a practical means of prohibiting the state. This is why the Qur'an's ethics include so much physical detail: the lowering of the gaze, the moderation of the voice (31:19, *"And lower thy voice; truly the most repulsive of voices is the voice of the donkey"*), the humble walk. Each is a small, repeatable, publicly observable act whose repetition forms character.

**"Thou Shalt Not Penetrate the Earth"**

*Innaka lan takhriqa al-arḍ* — the verb *kharaqa* means to pierce, to cut through, to traverse. Al-Rāzī explains the sense: however forcefully you strike the ground with your feet, you will not break through it. The earth is not impressed by your walking. The image is comic and deflating in exactly the right measure — the arrogant walker is, from the earth's perspective, irrelevant.

**"Nor Reach the Mountains in Height"**

*Wa lan tablugha al-jibāla ṭūlā* — and you will not attain the mountains in stature. The comparison is with the fixed, massive, immovable features of the landscape. The one who walks as though he were enormous is, next to a mountain, nothing. The verse's rhetorical method is *scale*: it does not argue against arrogance, it simply places the arrogant person in a landscape where his size is obvious. This is a recurring Qur'anic technique. 40:57, *"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most mankind know not."* And 79:27–29, *"Are you more difficult to create, or the heaven? He built it, raised its height, and ordered it."* The cure for self-importance offered by the Qur'an is not a lecture on humility but a look out of the window.

**The Prophetic Example**

Al-Ṭabarī preserves in his *Taʾrīkh* the description composed by ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib of the Prophet's ﷺ manner of walking: he walked energetically, leaning forward as though descending a slope, but never proudly. The description is important because it distinguishes two things a casual observer conflates: *vigor* and *arrogance*. The prohibition is not on confident, purposeful movement but on movement that performs superiority. The Prophet ﷺ walked with urgency — the urgency of a man with work to do — and without display. Al-Tirmidhī (2458) records the sharpest Prophetic statement on the subject:

> *"Whoever has the weight of a mustard seed of pride (kibr) in his heart will not enter the Garden."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 91)

And Muslim (91) records the definition: *"Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people."* The definition is behavioral, not emotional — it identifies arrogance by what it *does*, which is why the verse can legislate a walk.

****Legislating a Gait: Why the Qur'an Addresses the Body****

That a revealed book concerns itself with how a person walks is not a detail but a method, and al-Rāzī explains the reasoning: the body is not separate from the soul but trains it. The relationship runs in both directions — arrogance produces the arrogant gait, and the arrogant gait deepens the arrogance — so prohibiting the visible sign is a practical means of prohibiting the state. This is why the Qur'an's ethics include so much physical detail: the lowering of the gaze, the moderation of the voice at 31:19 (*"And lower thy voice; truly the most repulsive of voices is the voice of the donkey"*), the humble walk of the servants of the Compassionate at 25:63 (*"who walk upon the earth gently (hawnā)"*). Each is small, repeatable, publicly observable, and formative through repetition. The noun *maraḥ* denotes exultant, self-conceited walking, and Luqmān's counsel to his son at 31:18 pairs it with a second gesture: *"And turn not thy cheek from mankind in disdain, nor walk upon the earth exultantly. Truly God loves not any arrogant boaster."* The tradition preserves the Prophet's ﷺ own manner as the counter-example, in the description composed by ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib and preserved by al-Ṭabarī in his *Taʾrīkh*: he walked energetically, leaning forward as though descending a slope, but never proudly. The description distinguishes two things a casual observer conflates — vigor and arrogance — and it clarifies the prohibition's target: not confident, purposeful movement, but movement that performs superiority. Muslim (91) supplies the definition that makes the gait legible as a moral fact:

> *"Pride (kibr) is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Muslim 91)

The definition is behavioral rather than emotional, which is why a walk can be legislated. The verse's rhetorical method for curing self-importance is not exhortation but *scale*: you will not pierce the earth, and you will not reach the mountains in height. However forcefully the arrogant walker strikes the ground, the earth is not impressed, and next to a mountain his stature is nothing. The same technique appears at 40:57 — *"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most mankind know not"* — and its function is identical: the cure offered is not a lecture on humility but a look out of the window.

**The Verse's Place in the Code**

The code ends with arrogance because arrogance is the condition that makes all the other commandments negotiable. The one who does not regard himself as bound will not honor the orphan's property, will not give full measure, will not refrain from following what he does not know. The sequence descends from the theological root (v. 22) through the family (vv. 23–25), through wealth (vv. 26–30), through life and lineage (vv. 31–33), through trust and exchange (vv. 34–35), through the mind (v. 36), and arrives at the character trait that governs them all. Verse 38 will then pronounce the whole set loathsome, and verse 39 will name it wisdom and return to tawḥīd.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:38

> **The evil of all this is loathsome unto thy Lord.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Verdict Over Eighteen Verses**

*Kullu dhālika kāna sayyiʾuhu ʿinda rabbika makrūhā* — "all of that — its evil — is, with thy Lord, hateful." The demonstrative *dhālika* gathers the entire preceding sequence. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī specify the referent as the sins warned against in verses 31–37: infanticide, adultery, murder, misappropriation of the orphan's wealth, fraudulent measure, speaking without knowledge, and arrogant bearing. Some extend it to the whole code from verse 22. The pronoun *sayyiʾuhu* — "its evil" — is grammatically notable. Al-Rāzī explains that the phrase means: all of that, insofar as it is evil, is hateful to God. The construction avoids implying that everything mentioned is evil in every respect; the *evil* of it is what is hateful.

**Makrūh: The Language of Divine Dislike**

*Makrūh* is the passive participle of *kariha*, to dislike, to find hateful. The verse attributes *dislike* to God — an affective term, and one the classical theologians handled with care. The standard Sunni reading, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and others, is that the divine attributes are real and not reducible to created affect: God's dislike is a true attribute whose *reality* is not like ours, and whose *consequence* for the creature is what matters. The verse is therefore not merely a statement of legal prohibition; it declares a divine disposition. The prohibited acts are not arbitrary taboos but things that God finds repugnant. The Qur'an uses the same vocabulary of divine love and dislike extensively: *"God loves not the transgressors"* (2:190), *"God loves not the arrogant boaster"* (4:36), *"God loves not any ungrateful sinner"* (2:276), against *"God loves the patient"* (3:146), *"God loves those who purify themselves"* (9:108). The moral universe of the Qur'an is not indifferent; it is structured by divine love and divine aversion. The Qur'an states the pairing of divine love and divine aversion in a single verse, closing a comprehensive list of commands with the disclaimer:

> *"Truly God loves not one who is a vainglorious boaster."* (4:36)

**Why a Summary Verdict Is Needed**

The code of verses 22–37 has moved through diverse domains — theology, family, wealth, life, commerce, speech, bearing. Without a unifying verdict, a reader might conclude that these are unrelated rules of varying weight. Verse 38 unifies them: they are all *makrūh* to the same Lord, in the same way. Infanticide and short measure stand under one description. This is a claim of considerable moral seriousness — it refuses to rank economic dishonesty far below violence.

****Makrūh as an Attribute: The Theology of Divine Aversion****

The verse attributes *dislike* to God, and the classical theologians handled the attribution with care precisely because it is an affective term. *Makrūh* is the passive participle of *kariha*, to find hateful, and the standard Sunni position — articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and held in different formulations by the Ashāʿirah and Māturīdiyyah — is that the divine attributes are real and not reducible to created affect: God's dislike is a true attribute whose *reality* is not like ours, and whose *consequence* for the creature is what the verse is concerned to establish. The prohibited acts are therefore not arbitrary taboos but things God finds repugnant, and the Qur'an uses this vocabulary extensively in both directions: *"God loves not the transgressors"* (2:190), *"God loves not any arrogant boaster"* (4:36), *"God loves not any ungrateful sinner"* (2:276), against *"God loves the patient"* (3:146), *"God loves those who purify themselves"* (9:108), *"God loves the doers of good"* (2:195). The unifying verdict of this verse is therefore a claim of considerable moral seriousness, and its most important effect is what it refuses to rank. Infanticide, adultery, murder, misappropriation of an orphan's wealth, fraudulent measure, speaking without knowledge, and arrogant bearing are all described as *makrūh* to the same Lord in the same way. A moral code that ended without such a verdict could be read as a list of rules of varying weight, permitting the reader to treat economic dishonesty as a minor matter beside violence. The verse forecloses that reading. The shift to the second person — *ʿinda rabbika*, with thy Lord — is directed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to every reader, and al-Rāzī notes that this personal form appears at points of consolation. The one who keeps this code will often find himself at odds with the surrounding culture, which practiced infanticide, defrauded in trade, and prized the arrogant gait; the verse assures him that the divergence is not a defect, and that what his society considers normal, his Lord considers loathsome.

**The Address to "Thy Lord"**

The shift to the second person — *ʿinda rabbika*, "with thy Lord" — is directed to the Prophet ﷺ and through him to the reader. Al-Rāzī notes that this personal form is used in passages of consolation and reassurance. The one who keeps this code will find himself often at odds with the surrounding culture — which practiced infanticide, defrauded in trade, and prized the arrogant gait. The verse assures him that the divergence is not a defect: what his society considers normal, his Lord considers loathsome.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:39

> **That is from the wisdom thy Lord has revealed unto thee. Do not set up another god along with God, lest thou be cast into Hell, condemned, banished.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Naming the Whole: Ḥikmah**

*Dhālika mimmā awḥā ilayka rabbuka min al-ḥikmah* — "that is of what thy Lord has revealed to thee of wisdom." The demonstrative now covers the entire code of verses 22–39, and it names it *ḥikmah* — wisdom. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī both note the significance of the designation: these are not merely commands but *wisdom*, meaning truths accessible to the intellect as well as to revelation. Al-Rāzī develops the point at length: the inherent rightness of these injunctions is evident to reason, which is why they are found in every religion and every creed, and why they are never abrogated. Unlike ritual details, which vary between revelations, the moral core does not change. A report preserved by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī states that all of these injunctions were found in the tablets given to Moses — the Qur'an is claiming continuity with the Torah at exactly the point where it has been discussing the Children of Israel. This is the basis for the classical doctrine that the moral law is both revealed and rational — the position of the Māturīdīs and of many Muʿtazilīs, and substantially shared by the Ashʿarīs with respect to the *content* if not the *obligation*. Ibn Taymiyyah argued the same conclusion by a different route: revelation and sound reason never conflict, because both issue from the same source.

**The Frame: Tawḥīd at Both Ends**

Al-Zamakhsharī's observation is the key to the section's architecture. Verse 22 opened with *"Do not set up another god along with God"*; verse 39 closes with the identical words. The code is enclosed by tawḥīd, because — in al-Zamakhsharī's phrase — knowledge of God's Oneness is the beginning and foundation of all wisdom. Everything between the two verses is an elaboration of what it means to have one God. The logic is not merely rhetorical. Each command in the code fails without tawḥīd for a specific reason. Kindness to parents fails when the parent's approval becomes the ultimate standard. Honest measure fails when wealth is the ultimate good. Chastity fails when desire is the ultimate authority. Truthfulness fails when reputation is the ultimate concern. Humility fails when the self is the ultimate reference. In each case, the commandment is broken precisely at the point where something created has been enthroned — which is what shirk is. The gravity of the code's foundation is stated in the Qur'an's most cited formulation of what is not forgiven:

> *"Truly God forgives not that partners be set up with Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whom He wills."* (4:48)

**"Lest Thou Be Cast Into Hell, Condemned, Banished"**

*Fa-tulqā fī jahannama malūman madḥūrā* — *tulqā* is the passive of *alqā*, to be thrown, cast, hurled. The verb conveys violence and indifference: the one who commits shirk is not escorted but thrown. *Malūm* — blamed. *Madḥūr* — banished, driven away from mercy. The pair echoes verse 18's *madhmūman madḥūrā* and verse 29's *malūman maḥsūrā*. The sūrah uses these terminal adjectives as a refrain for the state of the one who has transgressed the boundary, and the repetition is cumulative: by the time the reader reaches verse 39, the words carry the weight of everything before them.

**Why the Section Closes With a Warning Rather Than a Promise**

The code has been largely imperative and prohibitive; it closes with the consequence of violating its foundation. The choice is deliberate. A moral code that ends with encouragement may be read as advisory. A code that ends with the alternative establishes that the matter is grave. And the gravity is located precisely: not in the details but in the root. The one who keeps every rule while enthroning something created has kept nothing; the one who has tawḥīd and stumbles has a foundation to return to — which is why verse 25 already assured the *awwābūn* of forgiveness.

****Why the Code Is Called Ḥikmah and Not Only Law****

The designation of verses 22–39 as *ḥikmah* — wisdom — rather than as *aḥkām*, rulings, is a claim about their epistemic status, and the classical tradition debated its implications at length. Al-Rāzī's position is that the inherent rightness of these injunctions is evident to reason, which is why they are found in every religion and every creed and why they are never abrogated: unlike ritual details, which vary between revelations, the moral core does not change. A report preserved by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī states that all of these injunctions were found in the tablets given to Moses, and the claim is contextually pointed, since the sūrah has been discussing the Children of Israel since verse 2. This is the scriptural ground for the position that the moral law is both revealed and rational — the view of the Māturīdiyyah and of many Muʿtazilīs, and substantially shared by the Ashāʿirah with respect to the *content* of the good even where they located its *obligatoriness* in the command. Ibn Taymiyyah reached the same conclusion by another route: revelation and sound reason never conflict, because both issue from a single source. Al-Zamakhsharī's structural observation explains the frame. Verse 22 opened with *"Do not set up another god along with God"*; verse 39 closes with the identical words, because knowledge of God's Oneness is the beginning and foundation of all wisdom. Each command between them fails without tawḥīd for a specific reason — kindness to parents fails when the parent's approval becomes the ultimate standard, honest measure fails when wealth does, chastity fails when desire does, truthfulness fails when reputation does — and in every case the failure occurs precisely where something created has been enthroned. The ḥadīth tradition supplies an independent testimony to the passage's standing. Al-Tirmidhī (3145) records from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd:

> *"Whoever wishes to look at the will (waṣiyyah) of Muhammad ﷺ upon which is the seal of his ring, let him read: 'Do not set up another god along with God' — until he completes these verses."* (al-Tirmidhī 3145, graded ḥasan)

The parallel passage at 6:151–153 contains substantially the same code and is similarly described in the tradition as the testament of the revelation, which indicates that the early community recognized this material as the settled moral floor beneath which a Muslim life does not fall.

**The Code as a Whole**

Read as a unit, verses 22–39 constitute one of the most compact moral statements in the Qur'an: God alone; parents honored; wealth given, not squandered, and not hoarded; children not killed for economic fear; sexuality guarded; life inviolable except by law; the orphan's property held in trust; covenants kept; measures honest; speech limited to what is known; bearing humble. Ibn Masʿūd called it the Prophet's ﷺ sealed testament, and the description fits: it is the minimum below which a Muslim life does not fall, and it is presented not as a burden but as *ḥikmah* — wisdom, the thing the human intellect recognizes as sound when it sees it clearly.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:40

> **Did your Lord favor you with sons, while He took females from among the angels [for Himself]? Surely you speak a monstrous word!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Their Own Prejudice**

*Afa-aṣfākum rabbukum bi-l-banīna wa-ttakhadha mina al-malāʾikati ināthā* — "Has thy Lord chosen you for sons, and taken for Himself females from among the angels?" The verb *aṣfā* means to select for oneself, to give the purest and best part; the Qur'an uses it at 16:57 in the same polemical context. The argument is not a metaphysical refutation but a *reductio* aimed at the Arabs' own values, and its force depends entirely on a fact the Qur'an states elsewhere with unusual bluntness. At 16:57–59:

> *"And they ascribe unto God daughters — glory be to Him! — and for themselves what they desire. And when one of them is given the tidings of a female, his face darkens and he chokes with grief. He hides himself from the people because of the evil of what he has been told: shall he keep her in humiliation, or bury her in the dust? Surely evil is what they judge."*

The Arabs who claimed the angels were God's daughters regarded the birth of a daughter as a calamity — so much so that infanticide was practiced, which verse 31 of this very sūrah prohibits. The verse's argument is therefore devastating in its simplicity: you attribute to God the very thing you regard as a misfortune for yourselves, and you keep for yourselves the thing you regard as a blessing.

**The Qur'an's Method of Refutation**

Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note that the Qur'an frequently argues against shirk not by abstract demonstration alone but by exposing the internal incoherence of the belief. See 43:16–19, *"Or has He taken daughters from what He creates, and favored you with sons? And when one of them is given tidings of the like of what he ascribes to the Compassionate, his face darkens and he is full of grief"*; and 52:39, *"Has He daughters, and you sons?"*; and 53:21–22, *"What! For you the male, and for Him the female? That indeed were an unfair division."* The phrase at 53:22 — *tilka idhan qismatun ḍīzā*, "that indeed is an unjust division" — states the logical form explicitly. The argument is not that God cannot have children; it is that the *specific* attribution is self-contradictory given the attributors' own premises. A belief that its holders would not accept if applied to themselves is not a belief but a convenience.

**"Surely You Speak a Monstrous Word"**

*Innakum la-taqūlūna qawlan ʿaẓīmā* — *ʿaẓīm* means great, enormous, tremendous. In a negative context it carries the sense of monstrous, appalling. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the enormity lies in what the attribution implies about God: to ascribe to Him a supposed deficiency, and to do so while regarding that same thing as a disgrace for oneself, reveals a total absence of reverence. The word *qawl* — a saying, a word — is important. The condemnation falls on *speech*: not merely on a private belief but on a statement made publicly, defended, and built into a religious system. The Qur'an treats speech about God as a matter of grave consequence, which is why it prohibits saying of God what one does not know (7:33, 10:68).

**Why the Qur'an Argues This Way**

The polemical strategy is instructive for anyone who must argue about God. The Qur'an does not begin with the attributes of the divine nature in the abstract; it begins with the inconsistency in the opponent's position, using the opponent's own standards. This is a form of argument that remains available and effective: the person who says one thing about God and the opposite about himself has not stated a theology but a preference. The exposure of the preference is often more persuasive than any proof, because it is undeniable.

****Reductio ad Absurdum: The Qur'an's Argument From the Opponent's Prejudice****

The verse's method is not metaphysical demonstration but *reductio*, and its force depends entirely on a fact the Qur'an states elsewhere with unusual bluntness. 16:57–59 records both halves of the belief it is dismantling:

> *"And they ascribe unto God daughters — glory be to Him! — and for themselves what they desire. And when one of them is given the tidings of a female, his face darkens and he chokes with grief. He hides himself from the people because of the evil of what he has been told: shall he keep her in humiliation, or bury her in the dust? Surely evil is what they judge."* (16:57–59)

The men who claimed the angels were God's daughters regarded the birth of a daughter as a calamity, to the point that infanticide was practiced — the very practice verse 31 of this sūrah prohibits. The argument is therefore devastating in its simplicity: you attribute to God the thing you regard as a misfortune for yourselves, and you keep for yourselves the thing you regard as a blessing. 53:21–22 states the logical form explicitly: *"What! For you the male, and for Him the female? That indeed were an unfair division (qismatun ḍīzā)."* The significance of the method extends beyond this verse. The Qur'an frequently argues against shirk not by abstract proof alone but by exposing the internal incoherence of the belief using the believer's own standards — at 43:16–19, at 52:39, at 37:149–153. A belief whose holders would reject it if applied to themselves is not a belief but a convenience, and the exposure of the convenience is often more persuasive than any demonstration, because it cannot be denied. The word *ʿaẓīm* — enormous, monstrous — is applied to the *speech* rather than to a private thought, and the choice is deliberate: the condemnation falls on a statement made publicly, defended, and built into a religious system. The diagnostic remains available in modern form, since every generation attributes to God whatever its own culture finds acceptable and refuses what its culture finds distasteful, while insisting that the resulting picture is the truth about God rather than a mirror of the age. The test the verse supplies is simple: if your conception of God differs from your neighbor's in exactly the places where your culture differs from his, the conception may be doing more work for you than for the truth.

**The Modern Form of the Same Error**

The specific belief is gone, but the structure persists. Every generation attributes to God whatever its own culture finds acceptable, and refuses to attribute to Him whatever its own culture finds distasteful — while insisting that the resulting picture is the truth about God rather than a mirror of the age. The verse's diagnostic remains useful: if your conception of God differs from your neighbor's in exactly the places where your culture differs from his, the conception may be doing more work for you than for the truth.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:41

> **And We have indeed varied [Our signs] in this Quran, that they might reflect, though it increased them in naught but aversion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Variety as Design**

*Wa laqad ṣarrafnā fī hādhā al-qurʾāni li-yadhdhakkarū* — "and We have varied [it] in this Qur'an, that they might remember." The verb *ṣarrafa* is the intensive form of *ṣarafa*, to turn, to divert, to direct in different directions; the intensive indicates repeated, deliberate turning. The Qur'an applies the same verb to its own composition at 6:46, 6:65, 6:105, 7:58, and 46:27, and to its parables at 18:54. Al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī explain the variety: the Qur'an presents its truths sometimes as rational demonstration, sometimes in an awesome register that inspires fear, sometimes in a gentle register that attracts. The same truth appears as argument, as warning, as promise, as narrative, as image, as legal ruling. The variation is not redundancy; it is pedagogy — the recognition that different minds are reached by different approaches.

**"That They Might Remember"**

*Li-yadhdhakkarū* — from *dh-k-r*, to remember, to recall, to be reminded. The Qur'an's characteristic description of its own function is *dhikr*, remembrance: 15:9, *"We have sent down the Reminder (al-dhikr) and We are its Guardian"*; 21:50, *"And this is a blessed Reminder We have sent down."* The verb implies that the content is not wholly new to the human being — that something in him already knows it and has forgotten. Revelation reminds rather than informs, which is why the response to it feels like recognition.

**"It Increased Them in Naught but Aversion"**

*Wa mā yazīduhum illā nufūrā* — *nufūr* is from *n-f-r*, to flee in alarm, to shy away as a startled animal does. The word is stronger than mere dislike; it denotes the reflexive recoil of a frightened creature. The verse states that the same variation which was meant to produce reflection produced flight in some. The Qur'an documents this divergence repeatedly and without embarrassment. At 2:26: *"He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misleads none but the transgressors."* At 9:124–25: *"And whenever a sūrah is revealed, some of them say: 'Which of you has it increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith... But as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth."* And at 17:82 in this very sūrah:

> *"We send down of the Qur'an that which is a cure and a mercy for the believers, and it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss."*

**The Theological Debate the Verse Provoked**

Al-Rāzī records that this verse became a battleground between the Muʿtazilah and the Ashāʿirah. The Muʿtazilite argument: God varied the signs precisely *so that* different people could reflect — which indicates a universal divine will for human understanding. The Ashʿarite counter-argument: God varied them knowing that some would be increased only in aversion — which indicates that the divine will encompasses the turning away of some. The debate is genuine and unresolved within the tradition, and the verse supports both readings grammatically. The Sunni synthesis, articulated by al-Rāzī and others, distinguishes the *universal will* (what God commands and desires for all) from the *particular will* (what God brings about) — and holds that human beings are responsible for the response they make to a guidance that is genuinely offered. The verse's own wording supports this: the variation is *for the sake of* reflection; the aversion is the hearers' achievement, attributed to them in the verb's subject position (*mā yazīduhum* — "it increases *them*," with the increase happening *in them* by their own recoil).

****Ṣarrafnā and Nufūr: The Verse That Divided the Theologians****

The verb *ṣarrafa* is the intensive of *ṣarafa*, to turn in different directions, and its application to the Qur'an's own composition — at 6:46, 6:65, 6:105, 7:58, 46:27, and 18:54 for the parables — describes a deliberate design. Al-Bayḍāwī explains the variation: the same truth is presented sometimes as rational demonstration, sometimes in an awesome register that inspires fear, sometimes in a gentle register that attracts. Argument, narrative, image, law, warning, promise, dialogue, oath — every mode is employed, because different minds are reached by different approaches. The stated purpose, *li-yadhdhakkarū*, uses a verb from *dh-k-r*, to remember, which implies that the content is not wholly new to the human being but something he once knew and has forgotten; the response the revelation seeks is recognition rather than information. The verse became a battleground between the Muʿtazilah and the Ashāʿirah, and al-Rāzī preserves both arguments. The Muʿtazilite reading: God varied the signs precisely *so that* different people could reflect, which indicates a universal divine will for human understanding. The Ashʿarite counter-reading: God varied them knowing that some would be increased only in aversion, which indicates that the divine will encompasses the turning away of some. The Sunni synthesis distinguishes the universal will — what God commands and desires for all — from the particular will, what God brings about — and holds the hearer responsible for his response to a guidance genuinely offered. The verse's own grammar supports this: the variation is *for the sake of* reflection, while the aversion is attributed to the hearers as something that happens *in them*. The word *nufūr* is stronger than dislike. It is from *n-f-r*, to flee in alarm, to shy away as a startled animal does, and it denotes a reflexive recoil rather than a considered rejection. The Qur'an documents the divergence without embarrassment at 2:26 — *"He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misleads none but the transgressors"* — and at 9:124–25, where the same sūrah increases some in faith and increases others in filth upon their filth. The practical consequence for anyone responsible for conveying truth is a considerable liberation: the outcome is not wholly in the conveyor's hands, and its failure is not proof that the conveying was defective. The messenger's task is to vary the approach; the response belongs to the hearer.

**The Practical Observation**

Anyone who has taught, preached, or simply argued knows the phenomenon: the same explanation that convinces one person repels another, and often the more careful the explanation, the stronger the recoil. The verse names this and does not treat it as a failure of the message. The messenger's task is to vary the approach; the hearer's response is his own. This is a considerable liberation for anyone responsible for conveying truth — the outcome is not entirely in the conveyor's hands, and its failure is not proof that the conveying was defective.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:42

> **Say, "If there were gods with Him, as they say, they would surely seek a way unto the Possessor of the Throne."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From Order**

*Qul law kāna maʿahu ālihatun kamā yaqūlūna idhan labtaghaw ilā dhī al-ʿarshi sabīlā* — the verse presents a conditional: if there were other gods alongside Him, as they claim, they would seek a way to the Possessor of the Throne. Two readings are preserved by Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī, and both are defensible. The first, favored by al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī, is *competitive*: the rival gods would seek a path to *dethrone* Him and struggle with Him for dominance. On this reading the verse is a version of the argument from cosmic order — the universe displays a single coherent governance, which would be impossible under competing sovereignties. The second, recorded by Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī, is *submissive*: the supposed gods would themselves seek nearness to the One God, recognizing His dominance, and would therefore be worshippers rather than objects of worship. On this reading the verse answers the specific Quraysh claim recorded at 39:3: *"We worship them only that they may bring us near to God."* The response: if these were gods, would they not seek nearness for themselves rather than serving as intermediaries for you?

**The Parallel Argument at 21:22**

The Qur'an states the same reasoning more explicitly elsewhere:

> *"Were there gods other than God in them, they would surely have been corrupted. So glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above that which they ascribe."* (21:22)

And at 23:91: *"God has taken no son, nor is there any god along with Him; otherwise each god would have taken away what he created, and some of them would have risen over others. Glory be to God above what they describe."* The argument's logical form is what later Islamic theology called *dalīl al-tamānuʿ* — the argument from mutual prevention. If there were two necessary beings with will, each would will something; where their wills differed, one would be prevented; the prevented one would not be omnipotent; therefore there is one. The observable fact that the cosmos is *not* in chaos is the empirical premise.

**"The Possessor of the Throne"**

*Dhī al-ʿarsh* — the Throne is the Qur'an's designation for the summit of created existence, the boundary beyond which no creature passes. Its use here is deliberate: the argument is set at the highest point of the hierarchy. The one who possesses the Throne possesses everything below it. Any supposed god must either be below the Throne — and therefore a creature, dependent, and not divine — or contend with its Possessor — in which case the cosmos would show the marks of the contention. It shows none.

****Dalīl al-Tamānuʿ: The Argument From Mutual Prevention****

The verse states a conditional whose logic the later theologians formalized as *dalīl al-tamānuʿ*, the argument from mutual prevention, and its two classical readings correspond to the two ways a rival sovereignty could relate to the true one. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī read it as competitive: were there other gods, they would seek a path to *dethrone* the Possessor of the Throne and struggle with Him for dominance. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī preserve the submissive reading: the supposed gods would themselves seek nearness to the One God, recognizing His dominance, and would therefore be worshippers rather than objects of worship. The second reading answers the specific Quraysh claim recorded at 39:3 — *"We worship them only that they may bring us near to God"* — with a question: if these were gods, would they not seek nearness for themselves rather than serving as intermediaries for you? The formal structure appears most clearly at 23:91: *"God has taken no son, nor is there any god along with Him; otherwise each god would have taken away what he created, and some of them would have risen over others."* The premises are that beings with will contend where their wills differ; that the one whose will is prevented is not omnipotent; and that the observable cosmos displays a single coherent governance rather than the marks of contention. 21:22 supplies the empirical premise: *"Were there gods other than God in them, they would surely have been corrupted."* The argument is not that contention is inconceivable but that it is absent, and its absence requires a single sovereign. The verse's application to intermediary worship is where it retains its force. The Quraysh acknowledged God as creator and placed idols between themselves and Him; the verse closes every available position for the intermediary. Whatever it is, it is either created — in which case it needs a way to God as much as the worshipper does, and cannot confer what it lacks — or uncreated — in which case it is a rival, and the coherence of reality refutes the supposition. There is no third option, which is why the conditional can be stated so confidently. The modern form of the same arrangement is easy to identify: whatever a person treats as the necessary intermediary between himself and ultimate reality — an institution, a lineage, a leader, a ritual system — falls under the same dilemma. Al-Rāzī's conclusion is that whatever one claims as an object of worship other than God, to the extent that it exists at all, is itself dependent on the One God, and therefore cannot rightly be called a god in any true sense.

**Why the Argument Works Against Intermediary Worship**

The Quraysh did not deny God's existence; they acknowledged Him as creator and placed idols between themselves and Him. The verse's argument is aimed precisely at that arrangement. Whatever the intermediary is, it is either created — in which case it needs a way to God as much as the worshipper does, and cannot confer what it lacks — or uncreated — in which case it is a rival, and the observable order of the world refutes the supposition. There is no third option, which is why the verse can state the conditional so confidently. The argument remains available in modern form. Whatever a person treats as the necessary intermediary between himself and ultimate reality — an institution, a lineage, a leader, a ritual system — must either be dependent or independent. If dependent, it cannot be the source of access; if independent, the coherence of reality is unexplained. The verse's logic is not time-bound.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:43

> **Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above whatsoever they say.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Tanzīh as the Necessary Response**

*Subḥānahu wa taʿālā ʿammā yaqūlūna ʿuluwwan kabīrā* — "Glory be to Him, and exalted is He above what they say, greatly exalted." The two terms form the vocabulary of *tanzīh* — declaring God beyond every deficiency. *Subḥānahu* declares Him free; *taʿālā* declares Him high; and *ʿuluwwan kabīrā* — a cognate accusative of intensification — declares the elevation to be great. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note that this formula appears consistently after the mention of false deities or of children attributed to God: 6:100, 10:18, 16:1, 21:22, 23:91, 28:68, 30:40, 37:159, 39:67, 43:82, 52:43, 59:23. It functions as a liturgical correction — the revelation pausing to cleanse the record after reporting an attribution.

**Why the Qur'an Pauses to Glorify**

The practice is not merely rhetorical. Al-Rāzī observes that the repeated interjection trains the reader's response: upon encountering a false claim about God, the correct reaction is not analysis first but glorification. The mind that habitually responds to a misdescription of God with *subḥānahu* develops a reflex of reverence that protects it from casual theological error. This is why *tasbīḥ* occupies such a place in Muslim devotion. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Bukhārī (6682) and Muslim (2694):

> *"Two words are light on the tongue, heavy in the scale, and beloved to the Compassionate: Subḥāna Allāhi wa bi-ḥamdih, subḥāna Allāhi al-ʿaẓīm."*

The verse is the scriptural model of the practice, and the practice is the verse's intended effect.

****Tanzīh as a Trained Reflex****

The formula appears at this point in the Qur'an with a regularity that indicates a pedagogical intention rather than a stylistic habit. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr catalogue its occurrences after the mention of false deities or of children attributed to God: 6:100, 10:18, 16:1, 21:22, 23:91, 28:68, 30:40, 37:159 and 180, 39:67, 43:82, 52:43, 59:23. Al-Rāzī's explanation of the pattern is that the revelation trains the reader's response: upon encountering a misdescription of God, the correct reaction is not analysis first but glorification. A mind habituated to answering a false claim about God with *subḥānahu* develops a reflex of reverence that protects it from casual theological error, in the same way that a practiced musician's hand moves correctly before his thought arrives. The two terms divide the work of *tanzīh* between them. *Subḥānahu* declares God free of every deficiency; *taʿālā* declares Him high; and *ʿuluwwan kabīran* — a cognate accusative of intensification — declares the elevation to be great. The phrase became doctrinally significant in the discussion of the divine attributes, and the classical discipline for handling it is the formula attributed to Mālik ibn Anas when he was asked how God is established upon the Throne: the elevation is known, the *how* is unknowable, belief in it is obligatory, and questioning about the *how* is innovation. Ibn Taymiyyah argued from *ʿuluwwan kabīran* that transcendence is not a mere abstraction but includes God's being above His creation in a manner befitting His majesty, without resemblance to created elevation — and that the verse's own word *greatly* indicates that whatever elevation is meant, it exceeds the category. The devotional consequence is direct. Al-Bukhārī (6682) and Muslim (2694) record:

> *"Two words are light on the tongue, heavy in the scale, and beloved to the Compassionate: *Subḥāna Allāhi wa bi-ḥamdih, subḥāna Allāhi al-ʿaẓīm*."* (al-Bukhārī 6682)

The verse is the scriptural model of the practice, and the practice is the verse's intended effect: the one who says the words regularly finds, when a false claim about God reaches him, that the answer is already formed.

**The Theological Content of the Formula**

*ʿUluwwan kabīrā* — a great elevation — carries a doctrinal claim that Ibn Taymiyyah treated as foundational: that God's transcendence is not a mere abstraction but includes His being above His creation in a manner befitting His majesty, without resemblance to created elevation. The Salaf's formula on such matters was the one Mālik ibn Anas gave when asked about the verse of the Throne: the elevation is known, the *how* is unknowable, belief in it is obligatory, and questioning about the *how* is innovation. The verse's phrase — *greatly* exalted — is the Qur'an's own indication that whatever elevation is meant, it exceeds the category.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:44

> **The seven heavens, and the earth, and whosoever is in them glorify Him. And there is no thing, save that it hymns His praise, though you do not understand their praise. Truly He is Clement, Forgiving.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Universal Worship as a Fact, Not a Metaphor**

*Tusabbiḥu lahu al-samāwātu al-sabʿu wa-l-arḍu wa man fīhinna wa in min shayʾin illā yusabbiḥu bi-ḥamdih* — "the seven heavens, and the earth, and whoever is in them glorify Him; and there is not a thing but that it glorifies Him with His praise." The scope is total: *min shayʾin* — any thing whatsoever. The negation *in min* with *illā* is the strongest form of universal statement in Arabic. The Qur'an states the same doctrine repeatedly. 13:13: *"And the thunder hymns His praise, as do the angels, in awe of Him."* 22:18: *"Hast thou not considered that unto God prostrates whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on the earth — the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, and the beasts, and many among mankind?"* 24:41: *"Hast thou not considered that God is glorified by whosoever is in the heavens and the earth, and by the birds with wings outspread? Each knows its prayer and its glorification."* 16:49–50; 59:1; 62:1.

**Is the Praise Literal?**

The commentators divide, and the division is instructive. One position, held by many of the Salaf and by Ibn Kathīr, is that the praise is real, in a manner appropriate to each creature, known to God and not to us — which is exactly what the verse says: *wa lākin lā tafqahūna tasbīḥahum*, "but you do not understand their glorification." Al-Ṭabarī records the opinion that *subḥāna Allāh* is the universal prayer of all creatures. The other position, associated with the rationalist exegetes, is that the praise is the "speech of the case" (*lisān al-ḥāl*): every creature, by its existence, its order, and its dependence, testifies to its Maker, as a well-made object testifies to its maker without speaking. The verse's own wording — *you do not understand their praise* — favors the first reading, since the second would be comprehensible: the testimony of order is precisely what reflection discerns. If the praise were merely the evident fact of design, the verse would not need to say that it is beyond our understanding. The exegetes who note this argue that the praise is real but of a mode we cannot apprehend.

**The Ethical Consequence Al-Ṭabarī Draws**

Al-Ṭabarī records a practical inference of unusual tenderness: since every creature possesses spirit and glorifies God, one should never show disrespect to any animal, or indeed to any creature. The doctrine of universal praise is not a metaphysical curiosity; it re-orders the human being's relation to the natural world. If the bird in the tree is engaged in worship you cannot hear, your treatment of the bird is a treatment of a worshipper. This is the theological ground of the extensive Islamic legislation on animal welfare: the Prophet's ﷺ prohibition of shooting at animals as targets, his command to sharpen the blade before slaughtering so as to minimize suffering, and his statement recorded by Abū Dāwūd (2550):

> *"God has written excellence (iḥsān) upon everything. So when you kill, kill well; and when you slaughter, slaughter well. Let one of you sharpen his blade and spare suffering to the animal he slaughters."*

**The Argument Against Idolatry Completes**

The verse's placement after verses 40–43 is argumentative. Al-Bayḍāwī notes that it continues the reasoning of verse 42: everything in the heavens and the earth is subordinate, dependent, and turned toward the One God. To worship any of them — even as an intermediary — is to be ignorant of what they are. Compare 41:37: *"Among His signs are the night and the day, the sun and the moon. Prostrate not unto the sun nor unto the moon, but prostrate unto God Who created them."* The contingency of the whole cosmos is the proof of the Necessary One. Everything that could have not existed, and does, points beyond itself.

****Do the Mountains Actually Praise? The Debate and Its Ethics****

The verse's scope is total — *wa in min shayʾin illā yusabbiḥu bi-ḥamdih*, there is not a thing but that it glorifies Him with His praise — and the commentators divided over whether the praise is literal, with the division producing one of the tradition's more tender practical rulings. The first position, held by many of the Salaf and by Ibn Kathīr, is that the praise is real, in a manner appropriate to each creature, known to God and not to us — which is exactly what the verse states: *wa lākin lā tafqahūna tasbīḥahum*, you do not understand their glorification. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the opinion that *subḥāna Allāh* is the universal prayer of all creatures. The second position, associated with the rationalist exegetes, is that the praise is the "speech of the case" (*lisān al-ḥāl*): every creature testifies to its Maker by its existence, order, and dependence, as a well-made object testifies to its maker without speaking. The verse's own wording favors the first reading, and the commentators who press the point do so on a precise ground: if the praise were merely the evident fact of design, it would be comprehensible, since design is exactly what reflection discerns. The statement that we do not understand their praise is only necessary if the praise is of a mode beyond our apprehension. From this reading al-Ṭabarī draws an inference of unusual tenderness: since every creature possesses spirit and glorifies God, one should never show disrespect to any animal or any creature. The doctrine of universal praise is therefore not a metaphysical curiosity but a re-ordering of the human being's relation to the natural world — the bird in the tree is engaged in worship you cannot hear, and your treatment of it is the treatment of a worshipper. This is the theological ground of the extensive Islamic legislation on animal welfare, and the Prophetic formulation is explicit. Abū Dāwūd (2877) records:

> *"God has written excellence (iḥsān) upon everything. So when you kill, kill well; and when you slaughter, slaughter well. Let one of you sharpen his blade and spare suffering to the animal he slaughters."* (Muslim 1955)

The verse closes the polemic of verses 40–43 with this argument: everything in the heavens and the earth is subordinate, dependent, and turned toward the One God, so to worship any of it — even as an intermediary — is to be ignorant of what it is. The contingency of the whole cosmos is the proof of the Necessary One, which is why 41:37 concludes, *"Prostrate not unto the sun nor unto the moon, but prostrate unto God Who created them."*

**"Truly He Is Clement, Forgiving"**

*Innahu kāna ḥalīman ghafūrā* — *ḥalīm* is the one who withholds punishment despite the capacity to inflict it; al-Bayḍāwī explains it as God's not hastening punishment upon wrongdoers. *Ghafūr* is the one who covers and forgives. The pairing appears at 2:225, 2:235, 3:155, 5:101, and 35:41. The closing is remarkable in context. The entire universe is praising God; the human idolater alone is refusing. The response is not immediate destruction but forbearance and the offer of forgiveness. The verse thus ends the polemic not with a threat but with the two Names most associated with patience toward human failure — which is itself an argument about the character of the One being described.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:45

> **And when thou recitest the Quran, We place a hidden veil between thee and those who believe not in the Hereafter.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Veil Placed by God**

*Wa idhā qaraʾta al-qurʾāna jaʿalnā baynaka wa bayna alladhīna lā yuʾminūna bi-l-ākhirati ḥijāban mastūrā* — "and when thou recitest the Qur'an, We place between thee and those who do not believe in the Hereafter a veil *mastūr*." The veil is God's act, not the hearer's — a point of considerable theological weight, which the commentators handle carefully. *Ḥijāb mastūr* — the participle *mastūr* means covered, concealed, hidden. Al-Rāzī explains the doubling: a veil that is itself veiled, so that the one behind it does not know he is behind it. This is the most unsettling feature of the image. The disbeliever does not experience the barrier as a barrier; he experiences only incomprehension, or boredom, or irritation. He cannot locate the obstacle because the obstacle is invisible from his side.

**Two Readings of the Veil's Function**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ record two interpretations. The first: the veil prevents the disbelievers from comprehending the recitation and deriving benefit from it — a reading consistent with verse 46's coverings over the hearts. The second, favored by al-Rāzī: the veil protects the Prophet ﷺ from their harm, so that their hostility cannot reach him while he recites. The two are not mutually exclusive, and the exegetes often hold both. The same device appears at 41:5, where the disbelievers themselves describe it:

> *"Our hearts are under coverings from that to which thou callest us, and in our ears there is deafness, and between us and thee is a veil. So act; truly we are acting."*

**Why the Verse Specifies Disbelief in the Hereafter**

The veil is placed specifically against *"those who do not believe in the Hereafter"* — the same specification as verse 10. The reason is the same: denial of the Hereafter is not one doctrinal error among many but the removal of the disposition that makes reception possible. A person who does not believe he will be questioned has no reason to attend to a message whose entire content is about the questioning. The veil is therefore not an arbitrary obstruction but the natural form taken by an antecedent refusal.

**The Consolation for the One Who Recites**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and, through him, to everyone who conveys the message. Its practical function is to explain failure. The Prophet ﷺ recited to assemblies that produced nothing — not because the recitation was deficient but because a veil had been placed. This is one of the Qur'an's most important pastoral teachings: the conveyor is responsible for the conveying, not for the reception. The same reassurance appears at 27:80–81: *"Truly thou canst not make the dead hear, nor make the deaf hear the call when they have turned their backs. Nor canst thou guide the blind out of their error. Thou canst make none hear save those who believe in Our signs and are submissive."* And 28:56: *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills."*

****The Veil That Cannot Be Seen From Inside****

The participle *mastūr* — covered, concealed — applied to a veil produces a doubling that al-Rāzī treats as the verse's central feature: a veil that is itself veiled, so that the person behind it does not know he is behind it. This is the most unsettling aspect of the image. The disbeliever does not experience the barrier as a barrier; he experiences only incomprehension, or boredom, or irritation, and he cannot locate the obstacle because it is invisible from his side. The same device appears at 41:5, where the disbelievers themselves describe their condition — *"Our hearts are under coverings from that to which thou callest us, and in our ears there is deafness, and between us and thee is a veil"* — which the commentators note as an acknowledgment rather than a complaint: they know they are not receiving, and they prefer it that way. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ record two readings of the veil's function, and the exegetes often hold both. The first: the veil prevents the disbelievers from comprehending the recitation and deriving benefit from it, which is consistent with the coverings and deafness of the following verse. The second, favored by al-Rāzī: the veil protects the Prophet ﷺ from their harm while he recites. The specification of the audience — *those who do not believe in the Hereafter* — repeats the exact qualification of verse 10, and for the same reason: denial of the Hereafter removes the disposition that makes reception possible, since a person who does not expect to be questioned has no reason to attend to a message whose entire content is the questioning. The veil is therefore not an arbitrary obstruction but the form taken by an antecedent refusal. The verse's pastoral function is what has made it important across the tradition. The Prophet ﷺ recited to assemblies that produced nothing, and the Qur'an explains the failure without attributing it to a defect in the recitation. The same reassurance appears at 27:80–81 — *"Truly thou canst not make the dead hear, nor make the deaf hear the call when they have turned their backs"* — and at 28:56, *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills."* Anyone who has attempted to discuss faith with a person committed to disbelief recognizes the phenomenon: the words are heard but not received, and the argument is answered without being engaged. The verse names it, locates it, and thereby releases the speaker from measuring his success by the hearer's response.

**The Veil in Modern Conversation**

Anyone who has attempted to discuss faith with a person committed to disbelief recognizes the phenomenon the verse describes: the words are heard but not received; the argument is answered but not engaged; the conversation moves without ever arriving. The verse names it and locates it — not in the inadequacy of the presentation but in a condition the presenter cannot see and cannot remove. The practical conclusion is not to stop speaking but to stop measuring success by reception, and to recognize that the removal of the veil is not in the speaker's hands.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:46

> **And We have placed coverings over their hearts, such that they understand it not, and in their ears a deafness. And whenever thou dost mention thy Lord alone in the Quran, they turn their backs in aversion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Aknah and Waqr: The Anatomy of Non-Reception**

*Wa jaʿalnā ʿalā qulūbihim akinnatan an yafqahūhu wa fī ādhānihim waqrā* — "and We have placed *akinnah* over their hearts, lest they understand it, and in their ears a *waqr*." Two precise terms. *Akkinnah* is the plural of *kinān*, a covering — the same word used for a quiver that holds arrows, or a sheath. It is not a light film but an enclosure. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as a barrier that prevents *fiqh* — the deep comprehension that goes beyond hearing words to grasping their meaning. The verse distinguishes the two levels explicitly: they may hear the sounds and not attain the understanding. *Waqr* is a heaviness in the ear, a partial deafness — not total, since they do hear the recitation, but sufficient to prevent it from entering. The same pair appears at 6:25: *"And among them are some who listen to thee, but We have placed coverings over their hearts lest they understand it, and in their ears a deafness,"* and at 18:57.
