At 41:5 the disbelievers describe their own condition in the same terms, which the commentators note as an acknowledgment: they know they are not receiving, and they prefer it that way.

**The Theological Question and the Classical Answer**

That God *places* the coverings raises the question of human responsibility. The classical answer, standard from al-Ṭabarī through al-Rāzī, rests on sequence: the coverings are placed *after* the refusal, as a consequence of it, not as its cause. Compare 2:7, *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing"* — which follows verses describing those who *chose* deception; and 61:5, *"And when they turned aside, God turned aside their hearts."* And 6:110:

> *"And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time."*

The principle is that the initial turning away is the human act; the sealing is the divine confirmation of a chosen state. The person who repeatedly refuses to look eventually loses the capacity to see, and the loss is real, and it is his — though it is God who enacts it. This is the Qur'an's doctrine of the hardening of the heart, and it is one of its most serious warnings: refusal has cumulative consequences that are not fully reversible.

**"Whenever Thou Dost Mention Thy Lord Alone"**

*Wa idhā dhakarta rabbaka fī al-qurʾāni waḥdahu wallaw ʿalā adbārihim nufūrā* — al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that this refers to the recitation of verses affirming God's Oneness and refuting partners. The reaction is not indifference but *nufūr* — the same word as in verse 41, the startled recoil of an animal. The specificity is diagnostic. It was not the Qur'an's moral teaching, its narratives, or its descriptions of the Hereafter that produced the recoil; it was the *exclusive* claim. Tawḥīd is the intolerable element, because it demands the removal of every other object of devotion — including the ancestral gods that structured Quraysh's social and economic life. The verse identifies the exact point at which the message becomes unacceptable: not where it is difficult to believe, but where it is costly to accept.

****The Sequence of Sealing, and What It Warns Against****

That God *places* the coverings raises the question of responsibility, and the classical answer rests on sequence. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both argue that the sealing follows the refusal rather than causing it, and the Qur'an states the sequence explicitly at 61:5: *"And when they turned aside, God turned aside their hearts."* And at 6:110: *"And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time."* The initial turning away is the human act; the sealing is the divine confirmation of a chosen state. The person who repeatedly refuses to look eventually loses the capacity to see, and the loss is real, and it is his — though it is God who enacts it. This is among the Qur'an's most serious warnings, because it means that refusal has cumulative consequences that are not fully reversible. The two terms are precise. *Akkinnah*, the plural of *kinān*, denotes an enclosure rather than a film — al-Qurṭubī compares it to a quiver or a sheath — and it prevents *fiqh*, the deep comprehension that goes beyond hearing words to grasping their meaning. *Waqr* is a heaviness in the ear, a partial deafness rather than a total one, since they do hear the recitation. The verse therefore distinguishes two levels: the sounds arrive, the understanding does not. 2:7 states the same condition with the verb of sealing — *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and upon their sight is a covering"* — and the placement of that verse immediately after descriptions of those who *chose* deception is what fixes the order of cause and effect. The clause about mentioning the Lord *alone* identifies the precise point of rupture, and it is diagnostic. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that the reference is to verses affirming God's Oneness and refuting partners. It was not the Qur'an's moral teaching, its narratives, or its eschatology that produced the recoil; it was the exclusive claim. The historical evidence is striking: al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, one of the Quraysh's most accomplished judges of Arabic, heard the Qur'an and said of it that it was not the speech of mankind, and that it shatters what is below it and rises above it — and he still did not believe. The verse therefore supplies a test available to any reader: observe not what in the message you find hard to understand, which is an intellectual matter, but what makes you want to leave the room. That point is where the message is touching something you have enthroned.

**The Diagnostic Value of the Verse**

The verse offers a test available to any reader: observe what in the message you cannot bear. Not what you find hard to understand — that is an intellectual matter — but what makes you want to leave the room. That point is where the message is touching something you have enthroned. The disbelievers of Makkah could listen to the Qur'an's poetry with admiration; al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah famously said of it that it was not the speech of mankind. What they could not tolerate was *waḥdahu* — "alone." The verse suggests that the location of a person's aversion is a reliable map of his idols.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:47

> **We know best that which they listen for when they listen to thee, and when they converse in secret, when the wrongdoers say, "You follow naught but a man bewitched!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Knows the Purpose of the Listening**

*Naḥnu aʿlamu bimā yastamiʿūna bihi idh yastamiʿūna ilayka* — "We know best what they listen *for* when they listen to thee." The construction with *bi-mā* indicates the object or purpose of the listening: not what they hear, but what they are seeking in the hearing. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that they listened not for guidance but for material to mock. The distinction is one of the Qur'an's most penetrating observations about the psychology of hostile listening. Two people can hear identical words; one listens for truth, the other for a flaw. The second is not engaged in inquiry but in ammunition-gathering, and the verse asserts that God's knowledge extends to that interior posture — which is invisible to the speaker and often to the listener himself.

**The Secret Converse**

*Wa idh hum najwā* — "and when they are in secret conference." The noun *najwā* denotes private, whispered consultation. The Qur'an treats secret conference with consistent suspicion, because it is usually where opposition is organized. 4:114: *"There is no good in most of their secret converse, save for him who enjoins charity or kindness or reconciliation between men."* And Sūrah al-Mujādilah (58:7–10) is largely devoted to it: *"Hast thou not considered those forbidden secret converse, then returning to that which they were forbidden, and conversing in sin and enmity and disobedience to the Messenger?"* — and:

> *"Their secret converse is only from Satan, that he may grieve those who believe."*

The verse's assertion — *We know best* — is directed at the Prophet ﷺ as consolation. The conspiracies were hidden from him; they were not hidden from God. This is the recurring function of divine-knowledge statements in the Qur'an: not to inform but to reassure.

****The Variety of the Accusations as Evidence Against Them****

The charge recorded here — *rajulan masḥūrā*, a man bewitched — is one of a shifting set, and al-Rāzī turns the shifting into an argument. The Quraysh described the Prophet ﷺ variously as bewitched, as a poet (21:5, 37:36, 52:30), as possessed (15:6, 23:70, 34:8, 44:14, 68:51), as taught by a human being (16:103), and as a liar. The commentators note that these characterizations are mutually exclusive: a poet is not a madman, a madman is not composing verse of acknowledged excellence, and a man deceived by sorcery is not a deliberate deceiver. The multiplicity is the evidence. A conclusion reached by observation is stable; a charge selected to serve a purpose changes as the purpose changes, and the trial of each description in turn is the behavior of people searching for an accusation rather than reporting a finding. The accusation is also a reflex rather than an evaluation, and the Qur'an documents its recurrence across the entire prophetic history. 51:52 states the pattern: *"Likewise no messenger came to those before them but they said: 'A sorcerer, or a madman!'"* Ṣāliḥ was called bewitched at 26:153, Shuʿayb at 26:185, and Moses in this very sūrah at verse 101. The content of such an accusation therefore tells one nothing about the truth of the message, and its recurrence should alert the hearer to its character — a point with obvious application to any age in which an unwelcome messenger is met with a standard vocabulary of dismissal. The verse's first clause is the more penetrating of the two. *Naḥnu aʿlamu bimā yastamiʿūna bihi* — We know best *what they listen for* — extends divine knowledge to the purpose of the listening rather than merely its content. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī explain that they listened not for guidance but for material to mock, and the distinction is one of the Qur'an's most exact observations about hostile attention. Two people can hear identical words; one listens for truth and the other for a flaw, and the second is not engaged in inquiry but in gathering ammunition. The posture is invisible to the speaker and frequently to the listener himself, which is why the verse needs to assert it rather than demonstrate it. The secret conference (*najwā*) receives the same treatment throughout the Qur'an — 4:114 concedes an exception for those who enjoin charity, kindness, or reconciliation, which implies that most secret conference has another purpose — and 58:10 supplies the diagnosis: *"Their secret converse is only from Satan, that he may grieve those who believe."*

**"A Man Bewitched"**

*Idh yāqūlu al-ẓālimūna in tattabiʿūna illā rajulan masḥūrā* — *masḥūr* is the passive participle of *saḥara*, to enchant; it means one who has been bewitched, or one whose mind has been taken. The accusation is that the Prophet ﷺ is not a liar but a *victim* — deceived by sorcery, speaking under an influence he does not control. The commentators note that this is a milder form of the more common accusation, that the prophets are *sorcerers* — active practitioners. The Qur'an records the accusation against many prophets: 5:110, 6:7, 10:76 and 81, 11:7, 20:57, 21:3, 26:35, 34:43, 37:15, 43:30, 51:52, 54:2, 61:6. Ṣāliḥ was called bewitched at 26:153, Shuʿayb at 26:185, and Moses himself in this very sūrah at 17:101. And 51:52 records the pattern across all of them: *"Likewise no messenger came to those before them but they said: 'A sorcerer, or a madman!'"* The recurrence is the point. The accusation is not a considered evaluation but a reflex — the standard vocabulary by which a society defends itself against a messenger it cannot refute. Al-Rāzī observes that the variety of the accusations (poet, sorcerer, madman, taught by a man, bewitched) is itself evidence against them: a man cannot be all of these at once, and the shifting charges indicate that none is held sincerely.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:48

> **Look how they set forth descriptions of thee. Thus they go astray and cannot find a way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Invitation to Observe**

*Unẓur kayfa ḍarabū laka al-amthāl* — "See how they have struck examples for thee." The verb *ḍaraba al-mathal* — to strike a likeness, to coin a description — is the standard Qur'anic phrase for setting forth a comparison or characterization. The imperative *unẓur* invites the Prophet ﷺ and the reader to examine the accusations as objects: look at them, see what they are. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī identify the descriptions: bewitched (v. 47), poet (21:5, 37:36, 52:30), possessed (15:6, 23:70, 34:8, 44:14, 68:51), taught by a human being (16:103), a liar (38:4). The list is a catalogue of incompatible characterizations, and the verse asks the reader to notice the incompatibility.

**The Argument in the Invitation**

The command to look is itself the refutation. A man who is a poet is not a madman; a man who is mad is not composing verse of acknowledged excellence; a man bewitched is not a deliberate deceiver. The Quraysh tried each description in turn, which is the behavior of people searching for a charge rather than stating a finding. Al-Rāzī makes the argument explicit: the multiplicity and mutual exclusion of the descriptions demonstrate that none of them rests on observation. The Qur'an describes the same closure elsewhere, and locates its origin in the first refusal:

> *"And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time."* (6:110)

****Premature Explanation as a Closed Door****

The imperative *unẓur* — see, look — invites the Prophet ﷺ and the reader to examine the accusations as objects, and the examination is itself the refutation. Al-Rāzī makes the argument explicit: a man who is a poet is not a madman; a man who is mad is not composing verse of acknowledged excellence; a man bewitched is not a deliberate deceiver. The Quraysh tried each description in turn, which is the behavior of people searching for a charge rather than stating a finding, and the invitation to look is an invitation to notice the incompatibility. The command is rhetorical rather than evidential: no new fact is supplied, because the facts were already in the objectors' hands and the difficulty was not information. The clause *fa-ḍallū fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna sabīlā* — they have gone astray and cannot find a way — states the consequence, and al-Qurṭubī explains the mechanism: their very act of describing him wrongly is what closed the path. This is a subtle claim about error, and it is more interesting than a simple condemnation. Error is not merely a wrong belief but a *closure*. Once the mind settles on an explanation — even a poor one — it stops investigating, because the feeling of having understood substitutes for the work of understanding. The Quraysh's descriptions were not conclusions reached by inquiry but substitutes for inquiry, and having adopted them, they could not find a way out. The verb *ḍalla* means to lose the way, and *sabīl* is a road, so the image is of people who have mislaid the road and cannot locate another. The phenomenon is universally recognizable and the verse describes it with clinical accuracy. The person who has decided what something is — a political opponent, a religious claim, a colleague's motive — no longer investigates it, and the decision feels like knowledge from the inside. This is why premature explanation is more obstructive than ignorance: the ignorant person may still ask, while the person with a settled explanation has no reason to. The verse's practical instruction is therefore negative and useful: when you find that you have a complete account of something you have not examined, the completeness is itself the signal to look again. The disbelievers of Makkah had a complete account of the Prophet ﷺ within weeks of his public preaching, and the completeness is what the verse identifies as their undoing.

**"Thus They Go Astray and Cannot Find a Way"**

*Fa-ḍallū fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna sabīlā* — "so they have gone astray and cannot find a way." The verb *ḍalla* means to lose the way, to err; *sabīl* is a path. The image is of people who have lost the road and cannot locate another. Al-Qurṭubī explains that their very act of describing him wrongly is the mechanism of their straying: by fixing on a false characterization, they closed the path to recognizing the truth. This is a subtle and important claim about error. Error is not merely a wrong belief; it is a *closure*. Once the mind has settled on an explanation — even a bad one — it stops investigating. The Quraysh's descriptions were not conclusions reached by inquiry but substitutes for inquiry, and having adopted them, they could not find a way out. The verse describes the trap of premature explanation, and it applies to every person who has ever decided what something is in order to avoid having to consider it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:49

> **They say, "What! When we are bones and dust, shall we indeed be resurrected as a new creation?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Skeptic's Own Words**

*Wa qālū a-idhā kunnā ʿiẓāman wa rafātan a-innā la-mabʿūthūna khalqan jadīdā* — the verse reproduces the objection in direct speech, with its incredulity intact. The opening interrogative *a-* and the emphatic *a-innā la-* convey the tone: *what — us? after we're bones and dust? really?* *ʿIẓām* — bones, the hardest remaining part of the body after decay. *Rafāt* — that which has been crushed to powder, disintegrated dust. The two terms mark the two stages of dissolution, and the objection is that both are beyond recovery. The word *khalqan jadīdā* — a new creation — is the object of their incredulity: not merely revival but *re-creation*. The same objection recurs throughout the Qur'an: 23:35, *"Does he promise you that when you are dead and become dust and bones, you will be brought forth?"*; 23:82; 36:78, *"And he strikes for Us a likeness, and forgets his own creation, saying: 'Who will give life to bones when they are rotten?'"*; 37:16–17; 56:47; 79:10–11; and in this sūrah again at 17:98.

**Why the Qur'an Quotes the Objection at Length**

The Qur'an does not caricature its opponents' arguments; it states them accurately and answers them. This is a feature of its method worth noting. The objection from decomposition is not a frivolous one — it is the strongest empirical argument available against resurrection, and the Qur'an engages it repeatedly rather than dismissing it.

**The Structure of the Answer That Follows**

Verses 50–51 answer in a specific order, and the order is logical. First: the material difficulty is not a difficulty for God, since He could give life to stone or iron — materials that never had life. Second: the agent is identified — the One who originated you the first time. Third: the timing question is answered separately. Each response addresses a distinct component of the objection: the material, the agent, and the time. The verse's own argumentative force lies in the *asymmetry* it exposes. The objector grants that he was created once from nothing — dust, then a clot, then a child. He then denies that the same agent can reconstitute what He made. The Qur'an's response, developed in the following verses, is that re-creation is *less* demanding than origination, since the origination had no precedent and the re-creation does.

****Quoting the Objection in Full: The Qur'an's Evidential Honesty****

The verse reproduces the skeptical question in direct speech with its incredulity intact — the opening interrogative, the emphatic *a-innā la-*, the tone of *what, us?* — and this practice is a feature of the Qur'an's method rather than an incidental detail. It does not caricature its opponents' arguments; it states them accurately and answers them. The objection from decomposition is not frivolous but the strongest empirical argument available against resurrection, and the Qur'an engages it repeatedly — 23:35 and 82, 36:78, 37:16–17, 56:47, 79:10–11, and again at verse 98 of this sūrah — rather than dismissing it. The two nouns mark the two stages of dissolution with precision. *ʿIẓām*, bones, is the hardest part remaining after decay; *rafāt* is what has been crushed to powder. Together they cover the objection completely: the objector is not asking about a recently dead body but about dust. The phrase *khalqan jadīdā* — a new creation — names the specific claim he finds incredible: not mere revival but re-creation. The structure of the answer that follows is logical rather than rhetorical, and its three parts address three distinct components of the objection. Verses 50–51 concede the material premise and then exceed it: were you stone or iron — materials infinitely further from life than bones — the answer would be the same. The agent is then identified: the One who originated you the first time. Finally, the question of timing is handled separately. The verse's own argumentative force lies in the asymmetry it exposes. The objector concedes that he was created once from nothing; he then denies that the same agent can reconstitute what He made. The re-creation is therefore the *less* demanding of the two acts, not the more, since the origination had no precedent. The Qur'an locates the objection's source not in evidence but in wish, and the diagnosis is careful. 75:3–5: *"Does man think that We shall not assemble his bones? Yes indeed — We are able to reshape his very fingertips. But man desires to deny what is before him (yurīdu li-yafjura amāmah)."* The phrase identifies the motive: the denial of resurrection is a desire to continue without constraint. This is a claim about motivation rather than about argument, and it is made without denying that the objector has reasons; it says that his reasons are powered by a preference. Anyone who has watched a person resist a conclusion because of what accepting it would require recognizes the description.

**The Psychological Root of the Denial**

The Qur'an identifies the objection's source not as evidence but as wish. At 75:3–5:

> *"Does man think that We shall not assemble his bones? Yes indeed — We are able to reshape his very fingertips. But man desires to deny what is before him."*

The phrase *yurīdu li-yafjura amāmah* — "he desires to go on sinning ahead of him" — locates the motive: the denial of resurrection is a desire to continue without constraint. This is a claim about motivation rather than argument, and the Qur'an makes it carefully. It does not say the objector has no argument; it says his argument is powered by a preference. Anyone who has watched a person resist a conclusion because of what accepting it would require recognizes the diagnosis.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:50

> **Say, "Be you of stone, or of iron,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From the Harder Case**

*Qul kūnū ḥijāratan aw ḥadīdā* — "Say: Be you stone, or iron." The imperative *kūnū* is not a command to become stone but a hypothetical: *suppose you were*. The Prophet ﷺ is instructed to concede the material premise entirely and then exceed it. The choice of stone and iron is precise. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī explain the reasoning: these are the materials the Arabs regarded as most remote from life — the hardest, most inert, most resistant to change. Stone does not decay; iron does not soften. If the objector's difficulty is that bones and dust are too far gone, the answer escalates to materials infinitely further from life than bones ever were.

**Why the Escalation Works**

The argument is a standard *a fortiori*: if God can give life to what never had it, He can restore life to what once had it. Al-Zamakhsharī states the point sharply: bones were once alive by God's act; stone and iron never were. The resurrection of a human being is therefore the *easier* of the two, not the harder. The objector has misjudged the difficulty. The same argument appears at 36:79:

> *"Say: He will give them life Who originated them the first time. And He knows every creation."*

And at 30:27: *"He it is Who originates creation, then brings it back again, and it is easier for Him."* The phrase *wa huwa ahwanu ʿalayhi* — "and it is easier for Him" — states the principle directly, though the commentators note that "easier" here is relative to human estimation, not to God, for whom nothing is easier than anything.

**The Rhetorical Form**

The verse is a fragment — it breaks off mid-command, with the apodosis supplied in verse 51. This is characteristic Qur'anic style: the speaker leaves the conclusion for the hearer to complete. The effect is that the objector must himself supply the answer, which is more persuasive than being given it. The Qur'an uses the device repeatedly, and here it forces the interlocutor to finish the sentence: *if we were stone or iron... He could still bring us back.* Having said it, he has conceded.

****The Hypothetical That Makes the Opponent Finish the Sentence****

The verse is a fragment, and its incompleteness is the device. The imperative *kūnū* is not a command to become stone but a hypothetical — *suppose you were* — and the apodosis is left for verse 51 to supply. The Qur'an uses this construction repeatedly, and its effect is to require the interlocutor to complete the thought himself, which is more persuasive than being handed the conclusion. The objector must finish: *if we were stone or iron... He could still bring us back.* Having said it, he has conceded the point in his own words. The selection of the two materials is precise, and al-Zamakhsharī explains the reasoning. Stone and iron were, to the Arab imagination, the substances most remote from life: the hardest, the most inert, the most resistant to change. Stone does not decay and iron does not soften. If the objector's difficulty is that bones and dust are too far gone, the answer escalates to materials infinitely further from life than bones ever were — and the escalation cannot be evaded, because verse 51 immediately invites him to name anything harder still. Al-Zamakhsharī states the resulting inference sharply: bones were once alive by God's act; stone and iron never were. The resurrection of a human being is therefore the easier of the two, and the objector has misjudged the difficulty. The argument depends on a specific conception of divine power, and identifying it clarifies why the objection fails. If creation were the rearrangement of pre-existing material, then stone and iron would present a genuine obstacle, since nothing in them is disposed to life. Because creation is *origination* — the bestowal of existence rather than the manipulation of substance — the material is irrelevant, and the only question is whether the Maker wills. 30:27 states the principle directly: *"He it is Who originates creation, then brings it back again, and it is easier for Him (wa huwa ahwanu ʿalayhi)."* The commentators note that "easier" is relative to human estimation rather than to God, for whom nothing is easier than anything, and that the phrase is accommodated to the way a human being compares two acts. This is why the Qur'an's resurrection argument is inseparable from its creation argument. A person who cannot conceive of making something from nothing cannot conceive of resurrection, and a person who accepts the first has already accepted the second. The objector's position is therefore unstable: he knows he came into being, and he has either answered the question of how or left it open. If he has answered it, resurrection follows; if he has left it open, he has no standing to declare resurrection impossible, since he cannot account for his own existence. 52:35 states the trilemma in its cleanest form: *"Or were they created from nothing? Or are they the creators?"*

**What the Verse Assumes About God**

The argument depends on a specific conception of divine power: that God's creative act is not a manipulation of pre-existing material but a bestowal of existence. If creation were rearrangement, then stone and iron would present a genuine difficulty. Because creation is origination, the material is irrelevant — the question is not *what* is being made but *whether the Maker wills it*. This is why the Qur'an's resurrection argument is inseparable from its creation argument: the one who cannot conceive of making something from nothing cannot conceive of resurrection, and the one who accepts the first has already accepted the second.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:51

> **or some other created thing more difficult [to resurrect] to your minds." Then they will say, "Who will bring us back?" Say, "He Who originated you the first time." And they will shake their heads at thee and say, "When will it be?" Say, "It may well be nigh."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Open-Ended Escalation**

*Aw khalqan mimmā yakburu fī ṣudūrikum* — "or a created thing that is great [difficult] in your breasts." The verse does not name a third material; it invites the objector to name whatever he considers most impossible. The phrase *yakburu fī ṣudūrikum* — "grows large in your chests" — locates the impossibility in the objector's own imagination, which is a subtle move: the difficulty is not in the thing but in his conception of it. The rhetorical effect is that the argument cannot be evaded by proposing a harder case. Whatever is proposed falls under the same answer.

**The Second Question: "Who Will Bring Us Back?"**

*Fa-sayaqūlūna man yuʿīdunā* — "they will say: who will restore us?" The Qur'an reports the question in advance, which the commentators note as a sign of the Prophet's ﷺ prophethood: he described the conversation before it happened, and it happened as described. The question is not genuinely seeking an answer — the objector knows who the Muslims say will do it. It is a way of restating the incredulity: *who could possibly?* The answer is therefore not new information but a reminder of what the objector already concedes.

**The Answer: The First Origination**

*Qul alladhī faṭarakum awwala marrah* — "Say: He Who originated you the first time." The verb *faṭara* means to originate, to split open, to bring into being with an innate constitution; the same root gives *fiṭrah*, the primordial human disposition of 30:30. The choice of *faṭara* rather than *khalaqa* is significant: it emphasizes the *initial* bringing-forth, the act that had no model. The argument's force is that the objector already accepts it. He does not claim to be eternal; he knows he came into being. The question of who originated him the first time is one he has either answered (God) or left open. If he has answered it, the resurrection follows. If he has left it open, he has no standing to declare resurrection impossible, since he cannot account for his own existence. The Qur'an states the argument in its cleanest form at 52:35: *"Or were they created from nothing? Or are they the creators?"* The trilemma exhausts the possibilities, and only the first has an answer that permits resurrection.

**The Third Question: "When Will It Be?"**

*Fa-sayunghiḍūna ilayka ruʾūsahum wa yaqūlūna matā huwa* — "and they will shake their heads at thee and say: when is it?" The gesture is described with unusual vividness: *yunghiḍūna ruʾūsahum* — they will wag their heads, the universal gesture of mocking disbelief. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the shaking of the head in dismissal. The question about timing is a deflection. Having failed to refute the possibility, the objector asks for a date — a demand the prophets are consistently told to refuse. 7:187: *"They ask thee about the Hour, when it will come. Say: the knowledge of it is only with my Lord."* And 67:25–26: *"They say: when is this promise, if you are truthful? Say: the knowledge is only with God, and I am only a clear warner."*

****Two Fingers, and the Nearness That Is Personal****

The three questions in this passage — what will be raised, by whom, and when — are answered in order, and the third answer is the one that has entered Muslim consciousness most deeply. The gesture is preserved in two reports. Al-Bukhārī (6504) and Muslim (2950) record:

> *"I and the Hour have been sent like these two" — and he joined his index and middle fingers.* (al-Bukhārī 6504)

The image is of two things so close that nothing can fit between them. Al-Ṭabarī connects the verse's *ʿasā an yakūna qarībā* — it may well be nigh — to the commentators' observation that *ʿasā* spoken by God indicates something that will certainly occur rather than something merely possible, the same principle applied to *ʿasā* in verse 8 of this sūrah. The answer is therefore not a date but a warning with the force of a promise. The general rule elsewhere in the Qur'an is refusal. 7:187: *"They ask thee about the Hour, when it will come. Say: the knowledge of it is only with my Lord; none shall manifest it at its time but He."* And 67:25–26: *"They say: when is this promise, if you are truthful? Say: the knowledge is only with God, and I am only a clear warner."* The exception here is granted for a specific reason, which al-Rāzī identifies: the nearness is true for every hearer personally, whatever the cosmic schedule, because each person's Hour arrives at his death. 63:11 states the personal version of the same law: *"And God will never delay a soul when its term has come."* The answer thus defeats the deflection, since the objector who asks *when* in order to postpone has been told that the postponement is shorter than he assumes. The head-shaking described in the verse — *fa-sayunghiḍūna ilayka ruʾūsahum*, they will wag their heads at thee — is reported with unusual vividness, and al-Ṭabarī explains it as the universal gesture of mocking dismissal. The gesture completes the diagnostic begun in verse 49: having failed to refute the possibility, the objector demands a date, which is a deflection rather than an inquiry. The Qur'an identifies such demands as bad faith elsewhere, and the reasoning is that a person who makes his belief conditional on his own terms has already decided that the terms, rather than the evidence, will govern. The cosmic nearness is stated at 54:1 — *"The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has split"* — and at 21:1, *"The reckoning of mankind has drawn near, yet they turn away in heedlessness."*

**"Say: It May Well Be Nigh"**

*Qul ʿasā an yakūna qarībā* — the exception to the general refusal. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note the commentators' observation that *ʿasā* from God indicates something that will certainly occur, not something merely possible — the same principle applied to *ʿasā* in verse 8. The answer is not a date but a warning: it is nearer than you assume. The Prophet ﷺ reinforced this. Al-Bukhārī (6504) and Muslim (2950) record:

> *"I and the Hour have been sent like these two" — and he joined his index and middle fingers.*

And Muslim (2940) records: *"The Hour and I are like these two,"* with the same gesture. The image is of two things so close together that no third can fit between them. The theological reasoning behind the nearness is stated at 54:1: *"The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has split,"* and at 21:1: *"The reckoning of mankind has drawn near, yet they turn away in heedlessness."* From the perspective of the eternal, the interval between the Prophet's ﷺ mission and the Hour is short — and from the perspective of any individual, it is shorter still, since each person's Hour arrives at his death. Al-Rāzī notes this: the answer *it may well be nigh* is true for every hearer personally, whatever the cosmic schedule.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:52

> **The Day when He calls you, you will respond by praising Him, and you will think that you tarried but a short while.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universal Response to the Summons**

*Yawma yadʿūkum fa-tastajībūna bi-ḥamdih* — "the Day He calls you, and you respond with His praise." The verb *tastajībūna* is the tenth form of *j-w-b*, to respond, to answer a call. The Qur'an describes the same scene at 30:25: *"And among His signs is that the heaven and the earth stand by His command; then when He calls you with a single call from the earth, behold, you come forth."* And at 39:75:

> *"And thou wilt see the angels circling around the Throne, glorifying the praise of their Lord."*

The response is *bi-ḥamdih* — with His praise. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī identify three grounds for the praise at that moment: the recognition of God as Creator, now inescapable; the recognition of His power to resurrect, now demonstrated; and the recollection of His blessings, now fully visible. Al-Zamakhsharī adds that the praise is an expression of total obedience — the response is immediate and unhesitating, unlike the response to the same call in worldly life.

**The Praise That Comes Too Late to Help**

The commentators are careful to note the tragic dimension: the recognition and obedience of those who refused both in earthly life bring them no benefit. Compare 40:84–85: *"But when they saw Our might, they said: 'We believe in God alone, and we disbelieve in what we associated with Him.' But their faith did not avail them when they saw Our might — the wont of God which has passed among His servants."* And 32:12: *"And if thou couldst see, when the guilty hang their heads before their Lord: 'Our Lord, we have seen and heard, so return us; we will do good — truly we are certain.'"* The verse therefore contains one of the Qur'an's central warnings: faith compelled by sight is not faith. The response at the summons is universal precisely because it is no longer a choice — and the value of faith lies entirely in its being chosen when it was not compelled.

**"You Will Think That You Tarried But a Short While"**

*Wa taẓunnūna in labithtum illā qalīlā* — the verb *ẓanna* here means to reckon, to estimate. The estimate will be that the stay was *qalīl* — brief. The Qur'an specifies the estimate elsewhere with striking consistency: 18:19, *"They will say: we tarried a day or part of a day"*; 23:113, *"They will say: we tarried a day or part of a day — ask those who count"*; 10:45, *"as though they had tarried but an hour of a day"*; 30:55; 46:35, *"as though they had tarried but an hour of a day"*; 79:46, *"It will be as though they had tarried but an evening or its morning"*; 20:104.

**Why the Duration Collapses**

The commentators offer two explanations, and both are illuminating. The first is comparative: the Hereafter is eternal, and any finite duration, however long, is negligible beside it. A thousand years is nothing next to forever. The second is phenomenological: the intensity of the Day compresses the memory of everything before it, as a great event compresses the years preceding it in the recollection of anyone who has lived through one. The Qur'an makes the comparative argument explicitly at 23:115: *"Did you think that We created you in vain, and that you would not be returned to Us?"* — the question that only makes sense if the earthly interval is genuinely short relative to what follows.

****The Collapse of Duration, and the Recalibration It Demands****

The verse's final clause — *wa taẓunnūna in labithtum illā qalīlā*, you will reckon that you tarried but a little — is one of the Qur'an's most consistently repeated eschatological claims, and the consistency is itself significant. The estimate is specified across a range of verses with remarkable agreement: a day or part of a day (18:19, 23:113), an hour of a day (10:45, 30:55, 46:35), an evening or its morning (79:46). The commentators offer two explanations and both are illuminating. The first is comparative: the Hereafter is eternal, and any finite duration, however long, is negligible beside it — a thousand years is nothing next to forever. The second is phenomenological: the intensity of the Day compresses the memory of everything preceding it, as a great event compresses the years before it in the recollection of anyone who has lived through one. The Qur'an makes the comparative argument explicitly at 23:115: *"Did you think that We created you in vain, and that you would not be returned to Us?"* The question presupposes the disproportion: a life spent entirely within a brief interval, on the assumption that the interval is the whole, is the specific error the verse identifies. The universal praise with which the summoned respond — *fa-tastajībūna bi-ḥamdih* — is explained by al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī as arising from three recognitions now inescapable: God as Creator, His power to resurrect, and the blessings He bestowed. Al-Zamakhsharī adds that the response is immediate and unhesitating, unlike the response to the same call in worldly life, which is what gives the scene its tragedy: the commentators are careful to note that this recognition and obedience bring no benefit to those who refused both in earthly life, since 40:85 records the principle — *"Their faith did not avail them when they saw Our might — the wont of God which has passed among His servants."* The verse's practical force does not require accepting the eschatology to be felt. Every person who has reached the end of anything — a career, a marriage, a life — reports the same collapse: it seemed long while it was happening and short in retrospect. The Qur'an takes that universal experience to its limit and uses it to recalibrate the scale on which decisions are made. Most of what consumes a person's attention is urgent within a frame that this verse declares to be brief, and Sūrah al-ʿAṣr states the resulting loss in a single sentence: humanity is in loss except those who believe, do good, and counsel one another to truth and patience. The loss is precisely the spending of the brief interval on what does not survive it.

**The Practical Force of the Verse**

The verse is a meditation on proportion, and its application does not require accepting the eschatology to feel its truth. Every human being who has reached the end of anything — a career, a marriage, a life — reports the same collapse: it seemed long while it was happening and short in retrospect. The Qur'an takes that universal experience and extends it to its limit: the entire span of worldly life, from the vantage of what follows, will register as *qalīl*. The verse's function is therefore to recalibrate the scale on which decisions are made. Most of what consumes a person's attention is urgent within a frame that the verse declares to be brief. The one who internalizes this does not thereby neglect the world — the Qur'an commands work, trade, family, and effort — but he stops treating the brief as though it were the final. The same recalibration appears at 109:1–2 in a different register, and at 103, where the whole of humanity is described as being in loss except those who believe and do good: the loss is precisely the spending of the brief interval on what does not survive it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:53

> **Say unto My servants that they should say that which is more virtuous. Surely Satan provokes ill feeling between them. Surely Satan is a manifest enemy unto man.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command About Words, Not About Arguments**

*Wa qul li-ʿibādī yaqūlū allatī hiya aḥsan* — "and say to My servants that they should say that which is *aḥsan*." The elative *aḥsan* — the better, the best — implies comparison: among the things one could say, choose the one that is better. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as a general command to speak gently, without harshness; al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note the elided feminine noun, most commonly *kalimah*, so that the phrase is "say the word that is best." The verse's placement is significant. It follows the description of the disbelievers' coverings, deafness, and accusations (vv. 45–48) — that is, it addresses the believer's response to provocation. The Qur'an is legislating the tone of a community under sustained verbal attack.

**The Reported Occasion**

Al-Qurṭubī and others record that the verse was revealed concerning an incident in which ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was insulted by an Arab and cursed or threatened him in return; the verse instructed pardon instead. Whether or not the specific report is accepted, the verse's logic fits it exactly: the harsh reply escalates, and escalation is Satan's objective.

**Why the Better Word Is Strategically Correct**

Al-Rāzī gives the reasoning: harsh speech provokes the opponent to answer in kind, entrenching him in his opposition. The Qur'an states the same principle at 6:108: *"And revile not those whom they call upon besides God, lest they revile God in enmity without knowledge."* The prohibition on reviling false gods — even though they deserve nothing — is justified by its *consequence*: it would produce blasphemy against God. This is a consequentialist argument inside a revealed text, and it is worth noting how clearly the Qur'an makes it. The moral quality of a statement is not determined solely by its truth; its effects enter into its evaluation. A true statement delivered so as to harden the hearer against truth has failed in the very purpose for which speech exists. Al-Qurṭubī records the recommended form of reply to an insult: *"May God have mercy upon you."* The formula converts the exchange entirely — the insulter receives a prayer rather than a counter-insult, and there is nothing to answer.

**"Satan Provokes Ill Feeling Between Them"**

*Inna al-shayṭāna yanzaghu baynahum* — the verb *nazagha* means to insert, to intrude, to prod; it denotes a small, sharp intervention that sets something in motion. The Qur'an uses it at 7:200, *"And if a provocation (nazgh) from Satan provokes thee, seek refuge in God,"* and at 41:36. The image is of a wedge driven into a joint. The verse identifies Satan's method with precision: he does not usually attack faith directly; he attacks *relationships*. Compare 5:91, *"Satan only seeks to sow enmity and hatred between you through wine and gambling, and to turn you from the remembrance of God and from prayer"*; and 12:100, where Joseph says to his brothers, *"It was Satan who incited evil between me and my brothers."* The story of Joseph is the Qur'an's fullest case study of the mechanism: a family destroyed not by an external enemy but by an internal provocation that no one recognized at the time.

**"A Manifest Enemy"**

*Inna al-shayṭāna kāna li-l-insāni ʿaduwwan mubīnā* — *ʿaduww* means enemy; *mubīn* means clear, manifest, evident. The enmity is not hidden or ambiguous; it was declared at the beginning, in the refusal to prostrate and the vow that follows in verses 62–64. Compare 2:168, 2:208, 6:142, 12:5, 28:15, 35:6, 36:60, 43:62. And 35:6: *"Truly Satan is an enemy to you, so take him as an enemy."*

****Aḥsan as a Selection Rule, Not a Command to Be Nice****

The elative *aḥsan* — the better, the best — implies a set of alternatives, and that is what makes the verse an instruction rather than a sentiment. Among the true things one could say, choose the better one. Al-Zamakhsharī reads it as a general command to speak gently without harshness, and al-Qurṭubī supplies the reported occasion: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was insulted by an Arab and cursed or threatened him in return, and the verse instructed pardon instead. The elided feminine noun most commonly supplied is *kalimah*, so that the phrase reads "say the word that is best," and al-Ṭabarī records the identification of that word with the testimony of faith — a reading that makes the verse's object not merely tone but content. The reasoning al-Rāzī gives is consequentialist in a way that is worth noticing inside a revealed text. Harsh speech provokes the opponent to answer in kind and entrenches him in his opposition, so the moral quality of a statement is not determined solely by its truth; its effects enter into the evaluation. The Qur'an states the same principle at 6:108 with an argument of unusual force: *"And revile not those whom they call upon besides God, lest they revile God in enmity without knowledge."* The prohibition on reviling false gods — which deserve nothing — is justified by its consequence, which would be blasphemy against God. A true statement delivered so as to harden the hearer against truth has failed in the very purpose for which speech exists. Al-Qurṭubī preserves the recommended form of reply to an insult: *"May God have mercy upon you"* — a formula that converts the exchange entirely, since there is nothing to answer. The second half of the verse identifies the adversary's method with precision. *Yanzaghu* is from *n-z-gh*, to insert, to intrude, to prod, and it denotes a small sharp intervention that sets something in motion — a wedge driven into a joint. Al-Rāzī's observation is that Satan does not usually attack faith directly but attacks *relationships*, and the Qur'an's case studies confirm it: 5:91 describes the enmity sown through wine and gambling, and 12:100 records Joseph telling his brothers, *"It was Satan who incited evil between me and my brothers"* — a family destroyed not by an external enemy but by an internal provocation no one recognized at the time. The practical discipline is therefore testable in daily life: before speaking in conflict, the question is not "am I right?" but "is this the better word, among the true things I could say?" Often the better word is shorter, softer, or absent — which is why al-Bukhārī (6474) records the Prophet ﷺ compressing the whole matter into a single instruction: *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."*

**The Practical Discipline**

The verse yields a discipline that is testable in daily life. Before speaking in conflict, the question is not "am I right?" but "is this the better word?" — the word most likely to produce good, among all the true things available. Often the better word is shorter, softer, or absent. The Qur'an does not command the believer to abandon truth or to accept injustice; it commands him to select, among truthful options, the one with the best outcome. This is why the tradition treats restraint of the tongue as a primary virtue. Al-Bukhārī (6474) records:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent."*

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:54

> **Your Lord knows you best. If He wills, He has Mercy upon you, and if He wills, He punishes you, and We have not sent thee as a guardian over them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Knowledge Preceding Disposition**

*Rabbukum aʿlamu bikum in yashaʾ yarḥamkum aw in yashaʾ yuʿadhdhibkum* — "your Lord knows you best; if He wills, He has mercy on you, or if He wills, He punishes you." Al-Ṭabarī explains that "knows you best" means God has full knowledge of the states of human beings and arranges their affairs accordingly. The mercy and the punishment are thus not arbitrary; they proceed from knowledge. The commentators differ on the referent. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī offer: the believers, being delivered from persecution or left under it; or the disbelievers, being guided to faith or punished for rejecting it. Both readings agree on the doctrine: the disposition of affairs belongs to God, and it is grounded in His knowledge rather than in human performance.

**The Reassurance in the Verse**

For a community being tortured in Makkah — Bilāl under the stone, the family of Yāsir, Khabbāb on the coals — the verse is a statement about timing, not indifference. God knows you best; the arrangement of your affairs is not a verdict on your worth. The Qur'an repeatedly separates worldly outcome from spiritual standing, and here it does so in the most absolute terms: mercy and punishment are His to distribute, and their distribution in this world does not disclose His final judgment.

**"We Have Not Sent Thee as a Guardian Over Them"**

*Wa mā arsalnāka ʿalayhim wakīlā* — *wakīl* is the trustee, the disposer of affairs, the same word as in verse 2's *"Take no guardian apart from Me."* The Prophet ﷺ is not a *wakīl* over people: he does not answer for their choices, control their outcomes, or bear their accountability. His function is conveyance. The Qur'an states this repeatedly: 5:92, *"And obey God and obey the Messenger, and beware. But if you turn away, know that upon Our Messenger is only the clear conveyance"*; 6:66; 10:108, *"Say: O mankind, the truth has come to you from your Lord. Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment — and I am not a guardian (wakīl) over you"*; 39:41; 42:6; 88:21–22:

> *"So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator over them."*

****The Grief of the Messenger, and the Office That Could Not Carry It****

The verse addresses a specific pain in the Prophet's ﷺ life, and the Qur'an speaks to that pain directly in two other places. 18:6: *"Perhaps thou wouldst kill thyself with grief that they believe not in this discourse."* 35:8: *"Let not thy soul waste away in grief over them."* The reports of his distress at the rejection of his people are numerous, and the verse's final clause is the structural answer: *wa mā arsalnāka ʿalayhim wakīlā* — We have not sent thee as a guardian over them. The *wakīl* is the trustee who answers for the outcome, and the Prophet ﷺ is explicitly not that. His function is conveyance, and what happens after conveyance is between the hearer and God. The Qur'an repeats this delimitation with a persistence that indicates how necessary it was. 5:92: *"If you turn away, know that upon Our Messenger is only the clear conveyance."* 10:108: *"Whoever is guided is guided for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment — and I am not a guardian over you."* 88:21–22 states it most starkly: *"So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator (muṣayṭir) over them."* And 28:56 settles the matter of outcome: *"Truly thou guidest not whom thou lovest, but God guides whom He wills."* The clause about mercy and punishment — *in yashaʾ yarḥamkum aw in yashaʾ yuʿadhdhibkum* — is read by al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī in two ways, both pastoral: that God may deliver the believers from persecution or leave them under it, or that He may guide the disbelievers to faith or punish them for rejecting it. Either way the point for a community being tortured in Makkah is that the arrangement of worldly affairs is not a verdict on spiritual standing. Bilāl under the stone, the family of Yāsir, and Khabbāb on the coals were not being punished for their faith, and their oppressors were not being rewarded for their unbelief; the timing belonged to God and disclosed nothing. The discipline this verse supplies is one every teacher, parent, and preacher needs. The alternative to accepting the limit is not greater effectiveness but either coercion or despair: the person who believes himself responsible for another's response will eventually try to produce it by force, or will collapse under the failure. The verse locates the boundary precisely, and its location is what makes sustained effort possible without either distortion.

**Why This Matters to the Conveyor**

The verse frees the one who conveys from a burden he cannot carry. The Prophet ﷺ grieved over the disbelief of his people — the Qur'an addresses that grief directly at 18:6, *"Perhaps thou wouldst kill thyself with grief that they believe not in this discourse"*; and at 35:8, *"Let not thy soul waste away in grief over them."* The verse's answer is structural: your responsibility ends at delivery. What happens after delivery is between the hearer and God. This is a discipline every teacher, parent, and preacher needs. The alternative — believing oneself responsible for another's response — produces either coercion or despair, both of which the Qur'an forbids.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:55

> **And thy Lord knows best whosoever is in the heavens and the earth. And We indeed favored some of the prophets over others, and unto David We gave the Psalms.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Universal Knowledge as the Frame for a Specific Claim**

*Wa rabbuka aʿlamu bi-man fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* — the verse moves from knowledge of the believers (v. 54) to knowledge of everything in the heavens and the earth. The expansion is preparatory: the One who knows all of creation is competent to distribute excellence among its members.

**"We Favored Some of the Prophets Over Others"**

*Wa laqad faḍḍalnā baʿḍa al-nabiyyīna ʿalā baʿḍ* — the verse states a hierarchy among the prophets, and it does so in the same terms used of human beings in verse 21. Al-Ṭabarī explains that while all the prophets occupy the highest human station, God gave them gifts of varying kinds and degrees. The parallel is 2:253:

> *"Those messengers — We favored some of them above others. Among them are those to whom God spoke, and some He raised in degrees."*

The Qur'an distinguishes carefully between this hierarchy and the believer's attitude. 2:285: *"The Messenger believes in what was sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. All believe in God, His angels, His Books, and His Messengers: 'We make no distinction between any of His Messengers.'"* The commentators reconcile the two: the believer makes no distinction *in belief* — he accepts all of them as prophets — while acknowledging that God *gave* them different gifts and ranks. The highest ranks are named elsewhere: the *ulu al-ʿazm*, the messengers of steadfastness (46:35), and the two *khalīl* and *kalīm* relationships — Abraham as God's intimate friend (4:125) and Moses as the one to whom God spoke directly (4:164). The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is described at 17:1 by the title *ʿabd*, and his distinction is established by the journey and the vision, not by a competing list.

**"And Unto David We Gave the Psalms"**

*Wa ātaynā Dāwūda zabūrā* — the *Zabūr* is the Psalms. The mention of David is a specific example of the general principle of differential favor, and David is chosen because his gifts were unusually varied: kingship, prophethood, the Psalms, the softening of iron (34:10), the submission of the mountains and birds to his praise (38:18–19), the speech of birds (27:16), and the judgment he rendered with Solomon (21:78–79). Al-Qurṭubī records the description of the Psalms as a revealed book containing no religious law — only praise and glorification of God. If that characterization is accepted, it makes David's gift distinctive in kind: a scripture of worship rather than of legislation. This is consistent with the Qur'an's description of David at 34:10–11: *"And We gave David favor from Us: 'O mountains, hymn with him, and you birds!' And We made the iron soft for him — 'Make full coats of mail, and measure in the links.'"*

****Rank Among the Prophets, and the Believer's Non-Distinction****

The verse states a hierarchy and the Qur'an elsewhere forbids a distinction, and the reconciliation of the two is a settled point in the tradition. 2:253 states the ranking: *"Those messengers — We favored some of them above others. Among them are those to whom God spoke, and some He raised in degrees."* 2:285 states the non-distinction: *"The Messenger believes in what was sent down to him from his Lord, and so do the believers... We make no distinction between any of His Messengers."* Al-Ṭabarī's resolution is that the believer makes no distinction *in belief* — he accepts all of them as prophets — while acknowledging that God *gave* them different gifts and stations. The two statements concern different things: the object of faith and the distribution of favor. The highest ranks are named rather than left abstract. The *ulu al-ʿazm*, the messengers of steadfastness, are identified at 46:35; Abraham is described as God's *khalīl*, intimate friend, at 4:125; Moses as the one to whom God spoke directly at 4:164. The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is described in this sūrah by the title *ʿabd* — servant — at verse 1, and his distinction is established by the journey and the vision rather than by a competing claim of rank, which is a structurally significant choice: the sūrah that opens with the greatest honor conferred on a human being describes that honor in the language of servitude. David is selected as the example of differential favor because his gifts were unusually varied: kingship and prophethood together, the Psalms, the softening of iron (34:10), the submission of the mountains and birds to his praise (38:18–19), the speech of birds (27:16), and the judgment he rendered alongside Solomon (21:78–79). Al-Qurṭubī records the description of the *Zabūr* as a revealed book containing no religious law but only praise and glorification, which if accepted makes David's gift distinctive in kind — a scripture of worship rather than of legislation — and consistent with the Qur'an's account of him at 34:10–11, *"And We gave David favor from Us: 'O mountains, hymn with him, and you birds!'"* The verse's placement after the argument against intermediary worship is what gives it its function: genuine excellence exists, and excellence is a *gift* rather than a divinity. The one who receives a scripture remains a servant, and the angels who received the command to prostrate were themselves servants — which is the point verses 56 and 57 will press.

**Why the Verse Is Here**

The placement follows the discussion of the false deities. The Qur'an has just argued (vv. 56–57 will continue) that whatever is worshipped besides God is a servant seeking approach to Him. Verse 55 supplies the counter-example: genuine excellence exists — David was given a Book, the prophets were ranked — and excellence is a *gift*, not a divinity. The one who receives a scripture remains a servant. This is the answer to the idolater's implicit reasoning: excellence does not make its possessor an object of worship.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:56

> **Say, "Call upon those whom you claim apart from Him, but they have no power to remove affliction from you, nor to change [it]."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Test Proposed**

*Quli dʿū alladhīna zaʿamtum min dūnihi fa-lā yamlikūna kashfa al-ḍurri ʿankum wa lā taḥwīlā* — "Say: Call upon those you claim apart from Him; they have no power to remove harm from you, nor to transfer it." The verse proposes an experiment, and it is the Qur'an's most direct empirical challenge to idolatry. The verb *zaʿama* — to claim, to assert — carries in Arabic a connotation of unsupported assertion; it is the word used for a claim that may well be false. The Qur'an uses it of false claims at 6:148 and 4:117. The choice of verb is a quiet verdict on the claim before the experiment is even run.

**The Two Powers Denied**

*Kashf al-ḍurr* — the removal of harm — and *taḥwīl* — its transfer or alteration. Al-Qurṭubī explains the distinction: the idols can neither lift the affliction nor redirect it elsewhere. The two together cover every form of relief: cessation and displacement. The verse excludes both, and therefore excludes any possible assistance. The same argument runs through the Qur'an. 7:194–195: *"Truly those you call upon besides God are servants like yourselves. Call upon them, and let them answer you, if you are truthful. Have they feet with which they walk? Or hands with which they grasp? Or eyes with which they see? Or ears with which they hear?"* And 10:106: *"And call not upon, apart from God, that which neither benefits thee nor harms thee; for if thou dost, then thou art of the wrongdoers."* And 34:22:

> *"They possess not so much as an atom's weight in the heavens or the earth, nor have they any share in either, nor is there for Him any supporter from among them."*

**The Argument From Crisis**

The Qur'an's most powerful version of this argument appeals not to theory but to observed behavior. In a crisis, the idolater calls only on God. 6:40–41: *"Say: Have you considered — if the punishment of God comes upon you, or the Hour comes — will you call upon other than God, if you are truthful? Nay, but to Him you will call, and He will remove that for which you call upon Him, if He wills, and you will forget what you associated."* And 10:12: *"And when affliction touches man, he calls upon Us, whether lying on his side, or sitting, or standing; but when We remove his affliction from him, he passes on as though he had never called upon Us for an affliction that touched him."* And 29:65: *"And when they embark upon a ship, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion; but when He has brought them safely to land, behold, they associate."* The argument is phenomenological: the idolater's own behavior in extremis testifies against his theology. This sūrah will make the point explicitly at verses 67–69, using the experience of the sea.

****The Public Test, and the Argument From Crisis****

The command is *say* — announce it — and the publicity is part of the argument. The challenge is not a private refutation but a test anyone can run, so that the failure is visible to all. The verb *zaʿama* — to claim, to assert — carries in Arabic a connotation of unsupported assertion, and the Qur'an uses it of false claims at 4:117 and 6:148; the choice of verb is a quiet verdict on the claim before the experiment begins. The two powers denied cover every form of relief: *kashf al-ḍurr*, the removal of harm, and *taḥwīl*, its transfer elsewhere. A being that can do neither has nothing to offer, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the exclusion of both closes every possibility of assistance. The Qur'an's strongest version of this argument is phenomenological rather than theoretical, and it appeals to observed behavior. In a crisis the idolater calls only on God. 29:65: *"And when they embark upon a ship, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion; but when He has brought them safely to land, behold, they associate."* 10:12: *"And when affliction touches man, he calls upon Us, whether lying on his side, or sitting, or standing; but when We remove his affliction from him, he passes on as though he had never called upon Us."* 6:40–41 poses it as a direct question: *"Will you call upon other than God, if you are truthful? Nay, but to Him you will call, and He will remove that for which you call upon Him, if He wills, and you will forget what you associated."* The claim is that the idolater's own behavior in extremis testifies against his theology, and this sūrah will make the point concretely at verses 67–69 using the experience of the sea. The anthropology beneath it is the doctrine of *fiṭrah*: the human being is not ignorant of God but inconsistent, and the crisis strips away the acquired theology to expose the unacquired knowledge beneath. The one who calls on his god in a storm and receives nothing learns more from the silence than from any argument, which is why the verse proposes the test rather than supplying the conclusion. The argument retains its force in modern form, since the structure of the error is unchanged. Whatever a person treats as the source of relief in extremity — a market, a diagnosis, a patron, a technique — occupies the position that the verse reserves for God, and the test of a crisis will disclose which. The verse's demand is therefore not that the reader abandon his means but that he stop attributing to them a power they do not possess: the means are used, and the outcome is sought from the One who governs them.

**Why the Verse Demands a Public Test**

The command is *say* — announce it. The challenge is to be made openly, so that the failure is public. This is characteristic of the Qur'an's polemical method: it does not argue privately with sophisticated refutations but proposes tests that anyone can run. The one who calls on his god in a crisis and receives nothing has learned more than any argument could teach him. The verse also contains an implicit anthropology. The Qur'an assumes that human beings already know this — that the innate disposition (*fiṭrah*) recognizes the true object of reliance, which is why the crisis strips away the acquired theology. The idolater is not ignorant of God; he is inconsistent, and the crisis exposes the inconsistency.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:57

> **It is they who make supplication, seeking a means of approach to their Lord. Which of them is nearer? And they hope for His Mercy and fear His Punishment. Truly the Punishment of thy Lord is something of which to be wary.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objects of Worship Are Themselves Worshippers**

*Ulāʾika alladhīna yadʿūna yabtaghūna ilā rabbihimu al-wasīlah* — "those whom they call upon themselves seek the means of approach to their Lord." The pronoun reversal is the whole argument: the ones being called upon are themselves calling. Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the referents variously as the angels (whom the Arabs called God's daughters), the jinn (whom they took as partners — 6:100, *"They make the jinn partners with God, though He created them"*), or the righteous human beings whose tombs and memories were venerated. All three readings yield the same conclusion. The verse connects directly to verse 42: whatever is worshipped besides God seeks a way to God. A being that needs a way to God is not God; a being that supplicates is not the one supplicated. The logic is airtight and does not depend on identifying the beings.

**Al-Wasīlah: The Means of Approach**

*Wasīlah* means that by which one draws near — a means, a connection, an access. The Qur'an commands its pursuit at 5:35: *"O you who believe, fear God and seek the means of approach (al-wasīlah) to Him, and strive in His path."* The word became a technical term in later Islamic piety, and the verse is its scriptural anchor. The verse's polemical point is that *wasīlah* is real but is not what the idolaters think. The means of approach to God is not another being interposed between oneself and Him; it is what the verse describes in the following words — hope, fear, and supplication. Compare 7:56, *"And call upon Him in fear and hope"*; and 32:16:

> *"They rise from their beds, calling upon their Lord in fear and hope."*

**"Which of Them Is Nearer?"**

*Ayyuhum aqrab* — the interrogative is read as a challenge: which of these supposed intercessors is actually nearer to God, such that you have chosen him? The question cannot be answered, because the idolaters have no criterion. The verse exposes the arbitrariness of the choice: they select intermediaries without any basis for preferring one over another, which is itself evidence that the selection reflects custom rather than knowledge.

**Hope and Fear as the Structure of Faith**

*Wa yarjūna raḥmatahu wa yakhāfūna ʿadhābah* — "and they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment." The two are joined as the essential disposition of the servant, and the Qur'an joins them constantly: 7:56, 15:50–51, 32:16, 39:9, 76:9. The classical spiritual writers made the pair the two wings of the spiritual life — the bird cannot fly on one. Hope without fear produces presumption; fear without hope produces despair; both are named as sins (12:87, *"and despair not of the Spirit of God; truly none despairs of the Spirit of God but the disbelieving people"*; 15:56, *"and who despairs of the mercy of his Lord but those astray?"*). The verse's point about the supposed deities is that they too are in this condition. The angels, the jinn, the righteous dead — all hope and all fear. A being that fears cannot protect; a being that hopes cannot grant.

****Al-Wasīlah: The Means, and What the Word Does Not Include****

The word *wasīlah* means a means of approach, that by which one draws near to a thing, and its appearance here in the mouths of the very beings invoked is the verse's decisive move. Al-Ṭabarī records the occasion from Ibn ʿAbbās: the verse was revealed concerning a group of Arabs who had been accustomed to worship a group of jinn, and those jinn embraced Islam while their former worshippers, unaware of the change, continued to invoke them. The jinn are therefore described as themselves competing in drawing near to God — *ayyuhum aqrabu*, which of them is nearer — and the beings to whom worship was directed were themselves supplicants, ranked, and frightened of the very punishment they were invoked to avert. The Qur'an uses the same word of the believer's own duty at 5:35: *"O you who believe, fear God and seek the means of approach to Him (wa btaghū ilayhi al-wasīlah) and strive in His way."* That verse is the ground of the classical discussion of *tawassul*, and the distinction that governs it is worth stating precisely. Every school agrees that the means of approach include faith, righteous deeds, and supplication itself — the Qur'an praises those who *"seek the means of approach to Him through that which pleases Him"* — and that a supplicant may ask God by reference to his own faith or his love of the Prophet ﷺ. The disputed question is whether one may invoke God by the person or the rank of another, and the disagreement is over evidence rather than principle. What no school claims, and what this verse excludes, is that the invoked party has power in himself: the one nearest to God is nearest *by approach*, not by nature. The verse's structure makes the point twice. First, the invoked are described with the three verbs of dependence: they *seek* means, they *hope* for mercy, they *fear* punishment. Hope and fear are the twin dispositions of worship in the Islamic account, and their presence in the object of worship dissolves its claim. Second, the punishment feared is described as *maḥdhūrā* — a thing to be guarded against — which the commentators gloss as a real and specific terror rather than a formal one. The beings appealed to for protection are themselves in danger, which is the shortest possible refutation.

**"Truly the Punishment of Thy Lord Is Something to Be Wary Of"**

*Inna ʿadhāba rabbika kāna maḥdhūrā* — *maḥdhūr* means that which one guards against, that from which one flees. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the force of the statement: if the punishment is to be feared even by the prophets — and the Qur'an has the Prophet ﷺ say at 10:15, *"Truly I fear, if I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day"* — then it is to be feared by all. The verse thus closes the argument against intermediary worship by relocating the reader's fear to its proper object. The idolater fears his idols, or fears their displeasure, or fears the loss of their favor. The verse says: the thing worth fearing is the punishment of your Lord, and the beings you fear cannot remove it — as verse 56 has just established.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:58

> **There is no town, save that We shall destroy it before the Day of Resurrection, or punish it with a severe punishment. That is inscribed in the Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Universal Statement About Towns**

*Wa in min qaryatin illā naḥnu muhlikūhā qabla yawmi al-qiyāmati aw muʿadhdhibūhā ʿadhāban shadīdā* — the construction *in min ... illā* is the strongest universal negation in Arabic: there is not a single town except that one of two things will happen to it. The Qur'an, which so often speaks of *specific* destroyed towns, here states that *every* town has an appointed end.

**Two Fates, Distinguished**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī offer the interpretation that gives the verse its moral content: the righteous town is "destroyed" through the ordinary course of death — its people die and it empties — while the wicked town is destroyed through severe punishment, the terrifying destruction that befell the towns of the prophets. On this reading the verse is not a prediction of universal catastrophe but a statement that every community ends, and that the *manner* of its ending corresponds to its character. The alternative reading takes both branches as calamity, differing in degree. Either way the verse establishes that no settlement is permanent — which is a claim against the assumption, natural to every flourishing city, that it will outlast its inhabitants' behavior.

**The Qur'an's Precedent**

The sūrah has already established the pattern at verse 16: *"And when We desire to destroy a town, We command those who live a life of luxury within it..."* And the Qur'an's catalogue of destroyed towns is extensive: 7:4, *"How many a town have We destroyed! Our might came upon it by night, or while they napped at midday"*; 7:59–101, the accounts of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, and Shuʿayb; 21:11; 22:45, *"How many a town have We destroyed whose people were wrongdoers, so it lies fallen upon its roofs!"*; 28:59.

**"That Is Inscribed in the Book"**

*Kāna dhālika fī al-kitābi masṭūrā* — *masṭūr* means written in lines, inscribed. Al-Ṭabarī and others take *the Book* as the Preserved Tablet, of which 85:21–22 speaks: *"Nay, it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet."* The same idea appears at 7:34, *"And for every nation there is a term; when their term comes, they cannot delay it an hour, nor hasten it"*; at 15:4, *"And We destroyed no town but that it had a known decree"*; and at 18:59:

> *"And those towns — We destroyed them when they wronged, and We appointed for their destruction a term."*

The inscription of the term has two consequences. The first is that the delay is real: the town is not destroyed arbitrarily but at a set time, which means the period before it is a genuine period of opportunity. The second is that the delay is finite: the appointed hour cannot be extended by wealth, power, or negotiation.

****A Fixed Term for Every City, and the Book That Was Written****

The verse is a universal statement about civilizations, and its placement — immediately after the refutation of intermediary worship and immediately before the account of the withheld sign — is not accidental. Al-Ṭabarī explains the two alternatives as destruction before the Hereafter or a severe chastisement, and both are attested in the preceding narratives: the people of Noah were destroyed, and the people who came after them were punished with the *ʿadhāb alīm* of verse 58 without annihilation. Al-Qurṭubī reads the verse as a direct warning to Makkah, and the historical outcome confirmed it: the city that had driven out the Prophet ﷺ surrendered to him within eight years of the Hijrah. The phrase *fī al-kitābi masṭūrā* — written in a book, inscribed — connects the verse to the Qur'an's doctrine of the divine decree, and the commentators are careful about what the doctrine does and does not imply. It does not make human choice irrelevant, since every city's destruction in the Qur'an is attributed to its own wrongdoing; it means that the pattern is not arbitrary. 7:34 states it as a rule: *"And for every nation there is a term; when their term comes they cannot delay it an hour, nor can they hasten it."* And 6:6: *"Have they not seen how many generations We destroyed before them? We had established them on earth as We have not established you."* The Qur'an's treatment of the past is thus a controlled comparison: power, wealth, fortification, and numbers did not prevent destruction, and the variables that determined the outcome were moral. Al-Rāzī draws the consequence that gives the verse its modern relevance. The civilization that feels permanent is making an empirical error, and the error is corrected not by speculation but by evidence — the ruins are visible along the trade routes the Quraysh traveled twice a year. The verse's argument is therefore archaeological before it is theological: look at what remains of the peoples who were stronger than you. The *masṭūr* then adds the dimension that makes the warning personal, because a term that has been written applies to the reader as well. Every institution, and every individual within it, is inside a schedule he did not set.

**The Verse as a Meditation on Civilizations**

The verse is one of the Qur'an's few statements about the fate of human settlements as such, and it has been read across the centuries as a philosophy of history. Every city the reader knows — including the ones that seem permanent — has a term written. The ruins the Quraysh passed on their trade routes were not anomalies; they were previews. And the cities of the reader's own age are not exempt. The practical application is not fatalism but proportion. The verse does not say that nothing can be done; it says the end is fixed and the interval is for use. A community that understands this does not build as though it were eternal, nor does it neglect building; it builds within a frame.
