The same frame is applied to the individual at 63:11, *"And God will never delay a soul when its term has come"* — the personal version of the same law.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:59

> **Naught hinders Us from sending signs, save that those of old denied them. And We gave unto Thamūd the she-camel as a clear portent, but they wronged her. And We do not send down Our signs, save to inspire fear.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Miracles and Its Refusal**

*Wa mā manaʿanā an nursila bi-l-āyāti illā an kadhdhaha bihā al-awwalūn* — "and nothing prevents Us from sending signs except that the former peoples denied them." The verse is read by Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī as a response to the Quraysh's repeated demands for spectacular proof — including the reported request that the Prophet ﷺ turn the hill of Ṣafā into gold. The demands are recorded elsewhere: 6:37, *"And they say: if only a sign were sent down upon him from his Lord!"*; 10:20, *"And they say: if only a portent were sent down upon him from his Lord! Say: the Unseen belongs to God alone, so wait — I am with you among those who wait"*; 7:203; 29:50–51.

**The Reason for the Refusal**

The verse gives a reason that is not obvious and requires careful reading. God is not unable to send signs; He declines because previous peoples asked for them, received them, and then denied them — and the denial of a requested sign brings immediate destruction. 6:157: *"Or lest you say: 'If only the Book had been sent down to us, we would have been better guided than they.' Now has come to you a clear proof from your Lord, a guidance and a mercy; and who is more unjust than one who denies the signs of God and turns away from them?"* And 6:4: *"And no sign comes to them of the signs of their Lord but that they turn away from it."* The commentators draw the conclusion explicitly: the withholding of the requested signs was an act of *mercy* toward the Quraysh. Had Ṣafā been turned to gold and the Quraysh still refused — which the precedent indicates they would have — the punishment would have followed immediately, as it followed every previous people granted its requested sign. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī and others state this plainly.

**The Case of Thamūd**

*Wa ātaynā Thamūda al-nāqata mubṣiratan fa-ẓalamū bihā* — the example is precise and well known. Thamūd asked for a sign; God sent the she-camel from the rock, as their own prophets had specified (7:73; 11:64; 26:155). They hamstrung her (7:77, 11:65, 91:14), and the destruction followed within three days (11:65–67; 54:23–31). The phrase *fa-ẓalamū bihā* — "they wronged [themselves] by it" or "they wronged her" — compresses the whole sequence: a requested sign, granted, and answered with violence. The Qur'an returns to Thamūd repeatedly precisely because it is the clearest case of the pattern: request, grant, denial, destruction.

**The Exception of the Splitting of the Moon**

The commentators note that one requested sign *was* granted: the splitting of the moon. Al-Bukhārī (4864) and Muslim (2802) record from Anas and from Ibn Masʿūd:

> *"The moon was split in the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ into two halves, one half behind the mountain and one half above it. And the Messenger of God ﷺ said: 'Bear witness.'"* (al-Bukhārī 4865)

And the Qur'an refers to it at 54:1–2: *"The Hour has drawn near, and the moon has split. And whenever they see a sign, they turn away and say: 'Continuing sorcery!'"* The response was exactly as predicted — the sign was attributed to bewitchment, which is the same accusation reported in verse 47 of this sūrah. The incident thus illustrates the verse: the sign came, the denial followed, and only God's forbearance postponed the consequence.

**"We Send Not Signs Save to Inspire Fear"**

*Wa mā nursilu bi-l-āyāti illā takhwīfā* — *takhwīf* means the inspiring of fear, in the sense of admonition and warning. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain that signs are sent as warnings, not as entertainment or as proofs for the merely curious. The purpose of a sign is to produce *taqwā*, not amazement. This is why the Qur'an treats the demand for signs as evidence of bad faith rather than sincere inquiry. A person who says "I will believe if you do X" has already decided that his belief is conditional on his own terms; and if X is done and he still refuses — as the precedent shows — the refusal reveals that the demand was never about evidence. The verse's refusal is therefore not a failure of power but a refusal to participate in a negotiation that the demandant does not intend to honor.

****The Sign That Was Withheld, and the Rule That Withheld It****

The occasions recorded for this verse are among the most specific in the sūrah. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the report that the Quraysh asked the Prophet ﷺ to turn the hill of Ṣafā into gold, and that Gabriel came and offered it, and the Prophet ﷺ declined, preferring the pattern of prophetic patience. In another report they asked for a spring to gush forth, or for the sky to fall in pieces as he had claimed. The verse supplies the reason the request was not granted, and the reason is a settled divine practice: *wa mā manaʿanā an nursila bi-l-āyāti illā an kadhdhaba bihā al-awwalūn* — nothing prevented Us from sending the signs except that the former peoples denied them. The commentators explain the mechanism precisely, and it is a mechanism of mercy. A people given an unmistakable sign and then disbelieving it are destroyed; that is the established *sunnah*. 6:158 states the rule: *"On the day some of the signs of thy Lord come, the faith of one who did not believe before will not profit him."* The Quraysh's request was therefore a request for their own destruction, and its refusal was the reason they survived to hear the message for thirteen more years and, most of them, to accept it. The verse's phrase *wa ātaynā thamūda al-nāqata mubṣirah* supplies the case study: the she-camel was given, was denied, and Thāmūd was destroyed — *fa-ẓalamū bihā*, and they wronged themselves by it. The second half states the positive principle: *wa mā nursilu bi-l-āyāti illā takhwīfā* — We send the signs only as warnings. Al-Rāzī explains that the signs are given to produce reflection, not to satisfy demand, and the distinction matters because a sign demanded on the questioner's terms is not received as a sign but as a concession. 30:9 supplies the corollary: *"Then consider the end of those who did evil — they denied the signs of God and made a mockery of them."* The verse therefore explains why the Qur'an, rather than a miracle, is the central sign of this dispensation: a sign of the intellect can be received by every generation, argued about without coercion, and returned to without being exhausted — and 29:51 makes exactly this claim: *"Is it not sufficient for them that We have sent down to thee the Book, recited to them?"*

**The Modern Parallel**

The pattern is entirely current. The conditional demand — "prove it to my satisfaction and I'll consider it" — is a common way of postponing a decision one does not wish to make. The verse identifies the mechanism and declines it, offering instead what verse 82 will call a cure: a Book that addresses the heart rather than a spectacle that addresses the senses.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:60

> **And [remember] when We said unto thee, "Surely thy Lord encompasses mankind." We did not ordain the vision that We showed thee, save as a trial for mankind, and the Accursed Tree in the Quran. And We inspire fear in them, but it increases them in naught but great rebellion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thy Lord Encompasses Mankind"**

*Wa idh qulnā laka inna rabbaka aḥāṭa bi-l-nās* — *aḥāṭa* means to surround, to encircle, to comprehend. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī read it as an assurance: God encompasses mankind in power and knowledge, and nothing they do can overcome His protection of the Prophet ﷺ. Others read it as a promise of eventual victory — the conquest at Badr and beyond. The placement immediately before the mention of the vision is significant. The Prophet ﷺ was about to be commanded to announce something that would cost him followers; the assurance of encompassing power precedes the command.

**"The Vision That We Showed Thee"**

*Wa mā jaʿalnā al-ruʾyā allatī araynāka illā fitnatan li-l-nās* — the majority of commentators, including al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, identify the *ruʾyā* with the Night Journey of verse 1. The word *ruʾyā* can mean a waking vision or a dream-vision, and the exegetes discuss both; the connection to verse 1 within the same sūrah makes the identification compelling. The verse states its purpose: *illā fitnatan li-l-nās* — "except as a trial for mankind." And the trial was real. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Ṭabarī record that when the Prophet ﷺ reported the journey, some who had believed left the faith, finding it incredible; and the Quraysh used it to mock him, asking him to describe Jerusalem, which he did. Al-Bukhārī records in the long ḥadīth of the Isrāʾ that the Quraysh asked him about the caravan and about the city, and he answered. The alternative readings — the dream of the pilgrimage fulfilled at Ḥudaybiyyah, or a vision of victory at Badr or the conquest of Makkah — are recorded by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, who note the difficulty: those events are Madinan, and this sūrah is Makkan.

**The Theological Point of the Trial**

That God would ordain a vision *as a trial* is a claim of considerable importance. It means the difficulty of believing was intended. A miracle so overwhelming that belief became compulsory would not produce faith; it would produce submission. The Isrāʾ was reported rather than displayed — the Prophet ﷺ announced it, and the audience had to decide whether to trust him. The trial was therefore a trial of *trust in the messenger*, which is the actual content of faith, rather than a trial of sensory evidence. This explains why the Qur'an so consistently refuses spectacle (v. 59) while granting the Isrāʾ: the Isrāʾ was not offered as public proof but as a private experience publicly reported. Those who trusted the reporter believed; those who did not, did not. The mechanism of faith was preserved intact.

**"And the Accursed Tree in the Qur'an"**

*Wa-l-shajarata al-malʿūnata fī al-qurʾān* — the reference is to the tree of Zaqqūm, described at 37:62–66, 44:43–46, and 56:52–54. At 37:64–65: *"Truly it is a tree that springs from the bottom of Hell; its spathes are as though they were the heads of satans."* And 44:43–44:

> *"Truly the tree of Zaqqūm is the food of the sinner."*

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī explain the "curse": those who eat from it are cursed, and its fruit is poisonous; or it is remote from all good and from God's mercy. The Quraysh ridiculed the description, and their objection is recorded: fire consumes everything, including stones (2:24, *"the Fire whose fuel is men and stones"*), so how can a tree grow in it? Abū Jahl is reported to have mocked it, saying they knew *zaqqūm* as rich food they would enjoy. The verse names the tree alongside the vision as the second thing that became a trial — two reports the audience could not verify and had to accept or reject on the basis of trust.

****The Dreamed Vision and the Cursed Tree****

The verse is among the most densely debated in the sūrah, and both its halves were contested in the Prophet's ﷺ lifetime. The "vision We showed thee" is identified by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr with the visions of the Night Journey, and the Qur'an calls them *ruʾyā* — a seen thing, though the classical discussion concerns whether the journey was of body and spirit together or of spirit alone, with the majority holding the former on the strength of 53:11–18 and of the reports. The *shajarah malʿūnah*, the cursed tree, is identified with *Zaqqūm* by an overwhelming consensus of the exegetes, and 37:62–66 describes it: *"Is this a better welcome, or the tree of Zaqqūm? We have made it a trial for the wrongdoers. It is a tree that springs from the bottom of the Hellfire; its spathes are as though they were the heads of devils."* The controversy is the verse's stated subject, since the Qur'an says the tree was made a *fitnah* — a trial, a thing that separates people. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the reports of the Makkan response: Abū Jahl is said to have mocked the claim by calling for butter and dates and declaring that this was the Zaqqūm with which the Prophet threatened them, arguing that fire consumes trees and cannot nourish one. The objection is a category error — the fire of the Hereafter is not the fire of this world, and the tree described is not a tree of the botanical kind — but it functioned as a trial because it gave the hearers a reason to disbelieve that felt like a reason. The verse names this dynamic exactly: *wa nukhawwifuhum fa-mā yazīduhum illā ṭughyānan kabīrā*, We frighten them and it increases them only in great insolence. The doubling of the trial — the vision and the tree — is instructive. Both were given as evidence, and both became occasions of division, because evidence is not self-validating: it is received by a disposition, and the same datum confirms the receptive and hardens the resistant. 74:31 makes the general point about the revelation as a whole: *"God lets go astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills, and none knows the hosts of thy Lord but He — and this is nothing but a reminder to mankind."* The verse therefore supplies a criterion for reading any difficult text: the difficulty is part of the test rather than an obstacle to it, and the response it produces is what is being measured.

**"We Inspire Fear in Them, But It Increases Them in Naught but Great Rebellion"**

*Wa nukhawwifuhum fa-mā yazīduhum illā ṭughyānan kabīrā* — *ṭughyān* is exceeding bounds, tyrannical transgression; the same root gives *ṭāghūt*, the tyrant or false object of worship. The verse states that the warnings produced not caution but escalation. This is the same dynamic as verse 41 and is a recurring Qur'anic observation: 5:64, 5:68, 25:60, 35:42, 71:6 — Noah's people, warned repeatedly, *"increased in flight."* The mechanism is a familiar one: a person warned of a consequence he does not wish to accept does not usually reconsider; he escalates his denial, because accepting the warning would require changing his life. The greater the warning, the greater the denial required — hence *ṭughyānan kabīrā*.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:61

> **And when We said unto the angels, "Prostrate before Adam," they all prostrated, save Iblīs. He said, "Shall I prostrate before one whom Thou hast created of clay?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene Recalled**

*Wa idh qulnā li-l-malāʾikati usjudū li-Ādama fa-sajadū illā iblīs* — the account appears at 2:34, 7:11, 15:29–31, 18:50, 20:116, and 38:73–74. Its recurrence indicates its centrality: the Qur'an treats the refusal of Iblīs as the archetypal sin, the pattern from which all others derive. The prostration is understood by the commentators as an act of honor commanded by God, not an act of worship directed to Adam — God is the one who commanded it, and obedience to the command is the worship. Ibn Kathīr states this explicitly, noting that the angels' prostration was obedience to God and honor to Adam.

**The Superiority It Establishes**

The commentators connect the prostration to Adam's knowledge, established at 2:31–33: *"And He taught Adam the names, all of them. Then He presented them to the angels and said: 'Inform Me of the names of these, if you are truthful.' They said: 'Glory be to Thee! We have no knowledge save what Thou hast taught us.'"* The one who was taught the names is honored before those who were not. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr draw the conclusion: the spiritually perfected human being, particularly in respect of knowledge, holds a rank above the angels. This is a remarkable claim in the hierarchy of beings, and it is the ground of the Islamic understanding of the human being's dignity — which verse 70 of this sūrah will state directly.

**"Shall I Prostrate Before One Thou Hast Created of Clay?"**

*Qāla a-asjudu li-man khalaqta ṭīnā* — the interrogative is not a request for information but an expression of refusal: *am I supposed to prostrate to this?* The verse records the reasoning, and the reasoning is the point. At 7:12 and 38:76 the reasoning is made explicit: *"I am better than him; Thou createdst me of fire and createdst him of clay."* Iblīs's argument is a syllogism: fire is nobler than clay; I am of fire; he is of clay; therefore I am nobler than he, and the nobler does not bow to the less noble.

**The Flaw in the First Sin**

The classical commentators analyze the syllogism with care, because identifying its flaw identifies the structure of every sin that follows. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī note several errors at once. The first is the *premise*: that fire is nobler than clay is an assertion, not a demonstration. Clay is the source of growth, stability, and nourishment; fire is destructive and unstable. The claim rests on the felt superiority of the luminous over the dense — a preference, dressed as a fact. The second is the *standard*: Iblīs measures nobility by origin of substance, while God had measured it by the capacity for knowledge. He applies a criterion God did not adopt, and then objects to God's verdict on the basis of his own criterion. The third, and most fundamental, is the *refusal of the command*. Even if the premise were true, the prostration was commanded by God. The sin is not the reasoning but the subordination of the command to the reasoning. Al-Rāzī identifies this as the essence of *kibr* — pride: the elevation of one's own judgment above the revealed command. This is why the Prophet ﷺ defined pride as he did, in the ḥadīth recorded by Muslim (91):

> *"Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 91)

Both halves are present in Iblīs: he rejected the command and despised Adam. The definition is not a moral generality but a description of the first sin.

****Iblīs's Question, and the Fallacy Beneath It****

The command to the angels and the refusal of Iblīs are narrated in seven places in the Qur'an, and each account adds a distinct element; this one adds the question, and the question exposes the reasoning. *A-raʾaytaka hādhā alladhī karramta ʿalayya* — "Do you see this one whom Thou hast honored above me?" — is a challenge to the divine judgment rather than to the command. 38:76 supplies the reasoning behind it: *"I am better than him; Thou didst create me from fire and didst create him from clay."* The argument is one of material composition, and its structure is a syllogism with a false premise: what is nobler in substance produces what is nobler in being. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr dismantle the premise on several grounds. Fire is destructive, unstable, and rises; clay is generative, stable, and receives form. Fire consumes what it touches and clay nourishes what is planted in it. The human being's composition is precisely what allows knowledge — the receptivity that the angels' question at 2:30 anticipated — while Iblīs's composition allows only heat. And the deeper error is that the argument bypasses the divine act entirely: God did not honor Adam for his clay but for what He gave him, namely the *names*, and the angels' prostration was obedience to a command whose wisdom had been disclosed to them. Iblīs's refusal is therefore not a considered judgment but the assertion of self-sufficiency — *istikbār* — which 38:75 names directly: *"What prevented thee from prostrating when I commanded thee? He said: I am better than him."* The consequence is the delay, and the delay is a mercy whose shape is easily missed. *Ukkhirnī ilā yawmi yubʿathūn* — grant me respite until the day they are raised — is granted as *ilā yawmi al-waqti al-maʿlūm*, until the appointed time, which 15:38 specifies. Had the refusal been answered with immediate annihilation there would be no test, and without the test there would be no distinction between the sincere and the coerced. 14:22 preserves Satan's own admission that his power was never real: *"God promised you the promise of truth, and I promised you and broke my promise to you; I had no authority over you except that I called you and you answered me — so blame me not, but blame yourselves."* The verse that follows here states the same limit: *innā ʿibādī laysa laka ʿalayhim sulṭān*.

**The Analogical Reasoning Gone Wrong**

Iblīs's argument is sometimes described in the Islamic tradition as the first *qiyās* — analogical reasoning — and as an example of analogy set against text. Ibn Taymiyyah discusses the case in this light: reasoning is legitimate, but when it contradicts an explicit command, the command governs. The one who uses his reason to *escape* an obligation rather than to understand it has inverted the purpose of reason.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:62

> **He said, "Dost Thou see this, which Thou hast honored above me? If Thou dost grant me reprieve till the Day of Resurrection, I shall surely gain mastery over his progeny, all save a few."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address and Its Audacity**

*Qāla a-raʾaytaka hādhā alladhī karramta ʿalayya* — "Have You seen this one whom You have honored above me?" The construction *a-raʾaytaka* is an idiom of challenge: *tell me about this*, *do You really consider this?* The commentators note the extraordinary character of the speech: Iblīs is disputing God's judgment to God's face, and disputing it *after* being commanded. The verb *karramta* — "You have honored" — is the same root as *karramnā* in verse 70, *"We have honored the Children of Adam."* The verse thus records the origin of the dispute that verse 70 will settle: the human being's honor is a divine bestowal, and Iblīs's objection to it is the first objection to human dignity in the history of creation.

**The Request for Reprieve**

*La-in akhkhartani ilā yawmi al-qiyāmati la-aḥtanikanna dhurriyyatahu illā qalīlā* — the request for delay is granted in the parallel accounts: 7:14–15, *"He said: Thou art of those reprieved. He said: Because Thou hast led me astray, I will surely sit in ambush for them on Thy straight path"*; 15:36–38, *"He said: My Lord, reprieve me until the Day they are resurrected. He said: Thou art of those reprieved, until the Day of the time appointed"*; 38:79–81. Here the granting is implicit. The reprieve has a fixed limit — *the Day of the time appointed* — which the commentators understand as the Day of Resurrection or the first trumpet-blast. Iblīs is not immortal; he is deferred.

**"I Shall Surely Gain Mastery Over His Progeny"**

The verb *iḥtanaka* is from *ḥ-n-k*, related to the palate; it denotes gaining control over someone, as one who puts a rope in an animal's mouth and leads it. The image is of domination through the point of appetite and speech. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī explain it as leading them astray. The vow is stated at 7:16–17 in its fullest form:

> *"Because Thou hast led me astray, I will surely sit in ambush for them on Thy straight path. Then I will come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right hands and from their left hands, and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful."*

The four directions describe total encirclement — and the stated *objective* is significant: not merely sin, but ingratitude. The goal is to destroy *shukr*, which is why the sūrah opened by naming Noah a *thankful servant*.

****A Stated Success Rate: The Enemy's Own Estimate****

This verse is remarkable for its frankness. The adversary announces his plan and his expected yield in the same breath: *la-aḥtanikanna dhurriyyatahu illā qalīlā* — I will surely take hold of his offspring, except a few. He does not claim all of them. The estimate is stated by the one making the effort, and it is the most compact statement in the Qur'an of the scope of temptation. The verb is from *ḥ-n-k*, and the commentators explain it variously as to embrace, to encompass, to bring under one's control, and — in the image al-Qurṭubī prefers — to take hold of the *ḥanak*, the palate, as one who leads a horse by the bridle in its mouth. On that reading the verb describes not a blow but a hold, and the image is of a mount directed by its rider rather than of an enemy striking. Al-Rāzī's observation is that the tactic is encirclement rather than assault: the person is not attacked once but surrounded gradually, so that every direction of escape leads to the same place. This matches the fourfold strategy of the following verse, which is a program rather than a temptation. The exception — *illā qalīlā* — is the verse's hinge, and the Qur'an is consistent about the proportion. 12:103: *"And most of them, though thou art eager, are not believers."* 38:24 and 7:17 both carry the same qualification. The minority is small and it is specified elsewhere as the sincere servants: 15:40 records the exception from the other side, *"except My servants among them, the sincere ones."* The two statements together give the structure of the human condition as the Qur'an describes it: the majority is taken hold of, the minority is not, and the difference is not capacity but sincerity. The practical consequence is uncomfortable and useful. A strategy that expects to succeed against most people is designed for the ordinary rather than for the exceptional, and it will therefore work on anyone who assumes he is the exception without doing the work the exception requires. 14:22 records Satan disclaiming responsibility on the Day: *"I had no authority over you except that I called you and you answered me."* The admission identifies what actually happened — an invitation that was accepted — and locates the decision where it was made.

**"All Save a Few"**

*Illā qalīlā* — Iblīs himself concedes an exception. The parallel at 15:40 and 38:83 makes the exception explicit and identifies its cause: *"Except Thy servants among them, the sincere (al-mukhlaṣīn)."* The few are not few by chance; they are those whose sincerity places them beyond his reach, as verse 65 will state. The concession is theologically important: the adversary himself acknowledges that his power has a boundary, and that the boundary is defined by the quality of the servant rather than by his strength.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:63

> **He said, "Go! And whosoever among them should follow thee, surely Hell shall be thy recompense—an ample recompense!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Single Word of Banishment**

*Qāla idhhab* — "Go." The commentators note the terseness. After the long speech of Iblīs, the divine response is one word. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī observe that the brevity is itself the rebuke: the one who has overstepped is not argued with but dismissed. The parallel formulations are equally short: 7:13, *"Get thee down from it"*; 15:34, *"Get thee out of it"*; 38:77, *"Get thee out of it, for thou art cursed."* The dismissal is total in another sense: Iblīs is not destroyed. He is permitted to continue, which is both a mercy withheld and a mercy granted — he asked for reprieve and received it, and the reprieve is the condition of the test.

**"Whosoever Among Them Follows Thee"**

*Fa-man tabiʿaka minhum* — the verb *tabiʿa* means to follow. The verse places the decisive act with the human being: following. Iblīs does not compel; he invites, and the following is the human act for which the human is accountable. This is the Qur'an's consistent position, stated most plainly by Satan himself on the Day of Judgment at 14:22:

> *"God promised you a true promise, and I promised you and failed you. And I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me. So blame me not, but blame yourselves."*

The verse is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements on free will: the adversary disclaims authorship of human sin, and God accepts the disclaimer.

**"Hell Shall Be Thy Recompense — An Ample Recompense"**

*Fa-inna jahannama jazāʾukum jazāʾan mawfūrā* — *mawfūr* means complete, full, abundant; from *w-f-r*, to be plentiful. The word is used elsewhere of provision, as at 17:20's *ʿaṭāʾ*, and here it is turned into an irony: the followers of Iblīs will receive their fill. The pronoun is plural — *jazāʾukum*, "your recompense" — addressed to Iblīs and his followers together. Ibn Kathīr notes this: the threat is collective, as at 15:43, *"And truly Hell is the promised place for them all"*; 38:85, *"I will fill Hell with thee and with those who follow thee among them, all together"*; and 32:13. The leader and the led share the destination, which is a claim about the nature of following: to follow someone is to accept where he is going.

**The Word "Jazāʾ"**

*Jazāʾ* means recompense, requital — the same word used of reward. The Qur'an uses one term for both outcomes, which is deliberate: Hell is not an arbitrary infliction but a *settling*, the appropriate return for the choice made. The same logic runs through verse 7 (*"if you do good, you do good for your own souls; and if you do evil, it is against them"*) and verse 82 (*"it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss"*). The punishment is described as what the act *earns*, not as what God *imposes*.

****Go: A Permission That Is Not an Approval****

The imperative *idhhab* — go — has generated careful comment, because a divine command ordinarily indicates divine pleasure and this one cannot. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī resolve it by distinguishing the imperative of endorsement from the imperative of permission (*idhn*), and the distinction is a general principle of Qur'anic theology: God's granting of a request, or His permitting an act, is not His approving it. The same structure appears at 2:185 and throughout the legislation of concessions — what is permitted is not thereby commended. Al-Qurṭubī adds a second reading: the command carries humiliation in it, as when one says to an insolent person *go and do what you like*, the phrase being itself a threat. The second clause is the theological center: *wa kafā bi-jahannama jazāʾan* — Hell is sufficient as a recompense. The commentators note the word *jazāʾ*, which is a recompense and a *just* one, and its pairing with *sufficiency* produces a precise claim: the punishment corresponds to the crime and is not exceeded. 4:122 and 29:54 use the same formula of the reward of the believers, *"and it is a sufficient reward,"* which establishes that sufficiency is a term of justice rather than of intensity — each receives what corresponds, no more. The granting of the delay is then explained in the tradition by a third consideration, which al-Rāzī draws out. The delay is itself a mercy, since it makes the test possible, and the test is the only thing that distinguishes a willing servant from a compelled one. Had Iblīs been destroyed at his refusal, the human being would have had no adversary and therefore no occasion to choose God against a competing claim; there would be obedience but not love, and compliance but not sincerity. The verse's own wording supports this: Iblīs is sent rather than annihilated, and what is sent is *man istaḥwāhu*, whoever he makes to follow him, which implies a following entered into rather than imposed. 16:99–100 states the limit of the permission with equal precision: *"His authority is only over those who take him as a patron and those who associate partners with Him."* The permission has a defined range, and the range is set by the human being's own response.

**The Verse's Function in the Sequence**

Verse 62 records the vow; verse 63 records the answer. The answer does not prevent the vow's execution — Iblīs will indeed gain followers — but it fixes the outcome. The reader is thereby given the information needed to make the choice: here is the offer, here is the offerer's destination, and here is the fact that following him means going where he goes. The moral argument is complete before the tactics are described in the next verse.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:64

> **So incite whomsoever thou canst among them with thy voice, and bear down upon them with thy cavalry and thy infantry, and be their partner in wealth and children, and make them promises." Satan promises them naught but delusion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Permission, Not a Command**

*Wa-stafziz mani staṭaʿta minhum bi-ṣawtika* — "and incite [or: unsettle] whomever you can among them with your voice." Al-Zamakhsharī is emphatic that this is not a command to Iblīs but a declaration that God will not intervene in the struggle; al-Qurṭubī reads it as a challenge exposing Iblīs's weakness, since verse 65 immediately limits his reach. The form *istafziz* is the tenth form of *f-z-z*, to unsettle, to startle, to rouse; the intensive tenth form indicates the attempt to provoke a hasty, destabilized response. This connects directly to verse 11's diagnosis: man is *hasty*. Iblīs's method exploits the haste. Al-Rāzī records the Muʿtazilite explanation of why God permits this at all: the intensification of moral challenge increases the reward available for overcoming it. The test is real only if the temptation is real.

**"With Thy Voice"**

*Bi-ṣawtika* — the commentators offer a range of readings. Al-Qurṭubī and others record the view that it refers to music and singing, and the verse was cited by those who argued for prohibiting music. The exegetes note honestly that the verse does not mention music; the more probable meaning, as al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī state, is the *whisper* — the internal suggestion, the *waswās* described at 7:20, 20:120, and in the whole of Sūrah al-Nās (114:4, *"from the evil of the whisperer who withdraws"*). The Islamic tradition's actual position on music is varied, as the commentators acknowledge: some jurists prohibit all musical forms beyond Qur'anic recitation and the call to prayer, while music and musical expressions of piety have always had a place in Islamic culture — beginning with the song *Ṭalaʿa al-badru ʿalaynā*, "The Full Moon Has Risen over Us," sung at the Prophet's ﷺ arrival in Madinah and still sung today.

**"Bear Down Upon Them With Thy Cavalry and Thy Infantry"**

*Wa ajlib ʿalayhim bi-khaylika wa rajlika* — *ajlib* means to shout, to drive forward with a cry, to bring up one's forces. *Khayl* is cavalry, horsemen; *rajl* is foot soldiers. The idiom *bi-khaylika wa rajlika* was proverbial in Arabic for "with all your might," and the Qur'an uses it here as a military image. Ibn Kathīr, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read the horsemen and footmen as all those — jinn or human — who ride or walk in acts of disobedience. The image is of an army composed of the disobedient themselves, which is a precise account of how corruption spreads: not by an external force but by the accumulated momentum of those already committed. Compare 41:29 and 114:6, which identify the whisperers as being "from among jinn and mankind."

**"And Be Their Partner in Wealth and Children"**

*Wa shārikhum fī al-amwāli wa-l-awlād* — the verse identifies the two domains in which the partnership occurs, and they are the same two named at verse 6 as the blessings given to Israel, and at 3:14 as the objects of human desire, and at 18:46 as the adornment of this world. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī specify the forms of the partnership. In wealth: acquisition through usury or other illicit means, and expenditure in immoral ways. In children: children conceived through fornication and adultery; the mistreatment, misguidance, or killing of children; and pride in offspring as a mark of status. The word *sharīk* — partner — is theologically loaded. It is the word used of the partners ascribed to God in shirk. Satan becomes a *partner* not in divinity but in the two things human beings value most, and the partnership consists in his having a hand in how they are obtained and used. The person who acquires wealth through usury has made Satan a partner in his wealth; the person who raises children without guidance has made him a partner in his children.

**"And Make Them Promises"**

*Wa ʿidhum* — the promise is Satan's principal instrument. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Qurṭubī identify its content: that there will be no Resurrection and no Reckoning; or that the idols will intercede. Compare 4:120, *"He promises them and arouses desires in them, and Satan promises them naught but delusion"*; and 14:22, where Satan himself admits the failure of his promises.

****Voice, Cavalry, Infantry: Temptation as an Organized Campaign****

The four commands in this verse are the Qur'an's fullest description of the methods of temptation, and the military metaphor is deliberate: *ajlib* is a command used of driving horses and gathering troops with a shout. Temptation is not presented as a series of unrelated suggestions but as a coordinated operation with distinct arms. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr identify them: the *voice* is the call to disobedience — every summons to sin, including, on one report, singing and idle talk; the *cavalry* is the mounted force, and the commentators gloss it as the temptations that come swiftly and openly; the *foot-soldiers* are those that come on foot, slowly and persistently, and al-Ṭabarī records the specific gloss that every disobedient person on horseback or on foot is among them. The fourth arm is the most subtle and the most consequential: *wa shārikhum fī al-amwāl wa al-awlād* — share with them in wealth and children. The commentators enumerate the partnerships exhaustively. In wealth: unlawful acquisition, interest, vows made to idols, wealth spent in disobedience, wealth withheld from its due, and the division of inheritance by pre-Islamic custom. In children: children born outside the marriage bond, children raised in disbelief or in corruption, children dedicated to idols, and children used as instruments of disobedience. The verb *shārik* — be a partner — is the sharpest word in the list, because it does not describe theft but co-ownership: the adversary does not take the wealth away, he acquires a share in how it is used. This is why 63:9 warns against it in the strongest terms: *"O you who believe, let not your wealth nor your children divert you from the remembrance of God."* The promise that follows — *wa ʿidhum, wa mā yaʿiduhum al-shayṭānu illā ghurūrā* — identifies the mechanism that makes the campaign work: deception about the outcome. The temptation does not present itself as loss but as gain, and the gain is real in the short term, which is precisely what makes the deception effective. 14:22 records the confession: *"I promised you and broke my promise to you."* The verse's own summary of the whole enterprise is in its last words: the promise is *ghurūr*, an illusion that looks like a thing. The modern application requires no translation. Every system that monetizes attention operates by exactly this architecture — a voice, a mounted and a foot-borne reach, and a partnership in the household's wealth and children — and it presents itself throughout as enrichment. The verse names the pattern, and naming it is the greater part of resisting it.

**"Satan Promises Them Naught but Delusion"**

*Wa mā yaʿiduhumu al-shayṭānu illā ghurūrā* — *ghurūr* is deception, the presentation of the false as the attractive; the same root gives *al-gharrūr*, one of Satan's titles at 31:33 and 35:5, "the Deluder," and 57:14:

> *"the Deluder deceived you about God."*

The verse's closing contrast is with the Qur'an's repeated assertion that *God's promise is true*: 4:122, 10:4, 14:22, 18:21, 30:60, 35:5. Two promisers address the human being; one is truthful and one is a deceiver; and the entire moral life consists in determining which promise one is acting on. Every decision about wealth and children — the two domains the verse names — is a wager on one of the two promises.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:65

> **"As for My servants, truly thou hast no authority over them." And thy Lord suffices as a Guardian.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Limit Declared by God**

*Inna ʿibādī laysa laka ʿalayhim sulṭān* — "My servants — you have no *sulṭān* over them." *Sulṭān* is authority, warrant, dominion; the same word used at verse 33 of the heir's authority over the murderer. Iblīs has no warrant, no standing, no enforceable claim over God's servants. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī identify *My servants* as the righteous specifically, supported by the parallel verses in which Satan himself makes the exception: 15:40, *"Except Thy servants among them, the sincere"*; and 38:83, *"By Thy Might, I will surely lead them all astray, except Thy servants among them, the sincere."* The adversary's own concession establishes the boundary.

**What "No Authority" Means**

Al-Zamakhsharī explains that it means Satan cannot succeed in leading them astray — not that he does not try. He will whisper to the righteous as to everyone; the difference is that the whisper does not become a decision. Compare 15:42: *"Truly My servants — thou hast no authority over them, save those who follow thee among the perverse."* Some commentators extend *ʿibādī* to all morally responsible beings and read the verse as a statement of Satan's ultimate powerlessness over everyone, since the human being alone is responsible for his error. This reading is supported by 14:22, where Satan disclaims authority: *"I had no authority over you, save that I called you, and you responded to me."* On this reading the verse is about the *nature* of temptation: it never has authority, only invitation, and the response is always the human being's. Both readings are preserved and both are true in their own domain. The righteous are protected in fact; all human beings are free in principle.

**"And Thy Lord Suffices as a Guardian"**

*Wa kafā bi-rabbika wakīlā* — the same *wakīl* as in verses 2 and 54, and the same verb *kafā* as in verses 14 and 17. The formula — *God suffices as guardian* — recurs at 3:173, 4:81, 4:132, 4:171, 33:3, and 33:48. At 3:173 it appears in a specific historical moment: *"Those to whom the people said: 'The people have gathered against you, so fear them' — but it increased them in faith, and they said: 'God suffices us, and how excellent a Guardian (wakīl) is He!'"* The verse's function here is to complete the picture the preceding three verses have drawn. Iblīs has been reprieved, has been permitted his tactics, and has been given access to wealth and children. The reader may reasonably ask what protection remains. The answer is one sentence: God suffices as guardian. And the *condition* of that guardianship has been stated: it belongs to *My servants* — the sincere.

****The Null Hypothesis: No Authority Over the Servants****

The verse is the exception that defines the promise, and its first word does the work: *ʿibādī*, My servants. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both note that the possessive restricts the statement, since not every human being is described in the Qur'an as God's servant in this sense — the term denotes the one who serves Him sincerely. The immunity is therefore conditional, and the condition is stated in the following words: *wa kafā bi-rabbika wakīlā*, sufficient is thy Lord as a trustee. The one who takes God as his *wakīl* is protected; the one who does not is not, and the verse makes no claim beyond that. The word *sulṭān* — authority, dominion, compelling power — is what is denied, and the denial is narrow and therefore precise. Satan is not said to be absent from the believer's life, nor incapable of whispering, nor unable to suggest. What is denied is his *authority*, his power to compel. 16:99–100 states the same limit: *"There is no authority for him over those who believe and rely upon their Lord. His authority is only over those who take him as a patron."* And 15:40–42 gives the exception from the adversary's side. The Qur'an's anthropology here is coherent: temptation is real and universal, compulsion is not, and the distinction between the two is the space in which human responsibility lives. The verse closes with the sufficiency of God as *wakīl*, and the word has already appeared in this sūrah at verse 3 and verse 54, where the Prophet ﷺ is told that he is not a *wakīl* over the people. The pairing is instructive: the Prophet ﷺ does not hold that office over anyone, and God holds it fully over those who accept it. The one who has God as trustee has delegated the outcome, which is not passivity but the condition of acting without anxiety — the same disposition 65:3 describes as *tawakkul* and attaches to the promise of sufficiency: *"And whoever relies upon God, He is sufficient for him."* The psychological consequence is direct. The believer who understands this verse does not experience temptation as evidence of abandonment but as evidence of a fight that he has the standing to win. The one who believes the adversary has authority over him has already conceded the premise on which the adversary operates, and 14:22 shows the adversary disclaiming exactly that premise on the Day: *mā ana bi-muṣrikhikum wa mā antum bi-muṣrikhiyya* — I cannot help you and you cannot help me.

**The Practical Doctrine**

The verse establishes that protection from misguidance is available and that it is conditional on servitude. This is not a passive guarantee; the classical spiritual tradition treats it as the outcome of active practice. The means are specified throughout the Qur'an and Sunnah: the recitation of Sūrah al-Nās and al-Falaq, the *istiʿādhdhah* before recitation (16:98), the remembrance of God at the points of entry and exit, and above all sincerity — *ikhlāṣ* — which is the exact quality Satan himself identified as placing human beings beyond his reach. The Prophet ﷺ taught the specific supplication recorded by Abū Dāwūd (5052) and al-Tirmidhī (3401):

> *"O God, I take refuge in Thee from the withdrawal of Thy blessing, from the change of Thy protection, from the suddenness of Thy wrath, and from all that displeases Thee."*

The verse's assurance and the supplication belong together: the guardianship is promised to the servants, and the servant asks to remain one.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:66

> **Your Lord is He Who makes the ships sail upon the sea, that you might seek of His Bounty. Verily, He is Merciful unto you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ship as a Standing Sign**

*Rabbukumu alladhī yuzjī lakumu al-fulka fī al-baḥri li-tabtaghū min faḍlih* — "Your Lord is He Who drives the ships for you upon the sea, that you may seek of His bounty." The verb *yuzjī* means to drive along, to urge forward, to propel — the image is of God as the one who moves the vessel. The ship is among the Qur'an's most frequently invoked signs: 2:164, 14:32, 16:14, 30:46, 31:31, 35:12, 40:80, 42:32, 43:12, 45:12. At 55:24 the ships are described as *"like mountains upon the sea"*; at 42:32:

> *"And of His signs are the ships upon the sea, like mountains."*

**Why a Wooden Object on Water Is a Proof**

The Qur'an's reasoning is not sentimental but analytical. A ship floats because of a property of water that no human being established; it carries weight because of a relationship between density and displacement that no human being negotiated; it moves because of wind or current that no human being produces. Every element of the arrangement is given. Al-Rāzī develops this: the human contribution is the shaping of the wood; the water's buoyancy, the wind's force, and the sea's bearing capacity are not his. The Qur'an makes the same argument about the human being's own dependence at 22:65: *"Hast thou not seen that God has made subservient to you whatever is in the earth, and the ships that run upon the sea by His command?"* The word *bi-amrihi* — by His command — is the operative clause.

**"That You Might Seek of His Bounty"**

*Li-tabtaghū min faḍlihi* — the same phrase used at verse 12 of the day's purpose. The Qur'an treats trade and travel as religiously legitimate pursuits instituted by divine arrangement. The sea voyage is not a departure from the religious life; it is one of the channels through which God's *faḍl* — surplus, grace — reaches human beings. Compare 62:10: *"And when the prayer is concluded, disperse in the land and seek of the bounty of God,"* and 73:20, *"and others traveling in the land, seeking of the bounty of God."* The Qur'an never frames economic activity as a distraction from worship; it frames it as an occasion for gratitude.

**"Verily, He Is Merciful Unto You"**

*Innahu kāna bikum raḥīmā* — the verse closes with the Name of mercy, and the placement is meaningful. The sea is dangerous; the verse has just described it as a place of peril in the verses that follow. The mercy lies not in the removal of the danger but in the *possibility of the crossing* — the arrangement by which a creature made for land can traverse water and return.

****The Ship as the Paradigm Case of Dependence****

The shift from the argument about Iblīs to the image of the sea voyage is not a change of subject but a change of evidence. Al-Ṭabarī explains the connection: having established that no created being has power over the servant of God, the Qur'an demonstrates the point in the setting where the human being is most aware of his helplessness. The sea voyage was the paradigm case of uncontrollable danger for the pre-Islamic Arab, whose world was the desert and whose relationship to the sea was one of trade and dread, and the Qur'an returns to it repeatedly — 10:22, 16:14, 29:65, 31:31–32, 42:32–34. The verb *tujrī*, runs, carries the sense of being made to run: the ship is not self-propelled but driven, and the phrase *bi-l-baḥr* rather than *fī al-baḥr* indicates movement through the sea rather than mere location. The clause *bi-faḍlin minhu*, by His favor, is the theological pivot. The wind that fills the sail is not a natural constant but a favor conferred, and the verse thereby removes the possibility of taking the voyage for granted. 45:12–13 makes the same point at length, ending with the purpose: *"...that you may seek of His bounty, and that you may be grateful."* The verse's rhetorical function within the sūrah is to complete the argument begun at verses 56–57. Those verses established the point by argument: the invoked are themselves supplicants, fearful, seeking means of approach. This verse establishes it by experience: in the storm there is no one else to call. Al-Qurṭubī notes the reason the sea is chosen as the setting: it removes the intermediaries physically, since the idols are on shore and the ship is between two elements that no one controls. The result is the purest available test of a person's actual theology, and the verses that follow record the outcome — which is not what the Qur'an wishes it to be. The modern parallel is exact in structure. Every person lives inside systems he does not control and mostly does not notice, and the notice comes in the failure: the diagnosis, the market collapse, the flight through turbulence. The verse treats that moment not as an interruption of ordinary life but as a disclosure of what ordinary life actually is — *bi-faḍlin minhu*, by a favor, every hour of it.

**The Verse's Function in the Argument**

The sūrah has been arguing against intermediary worship. Verse 66 shifts to a phenomenon every Makkan knew: the caravan to Syria, the ships of the Red Sea, the trade on which Quraysh's wealth depended. The argument is: you already know who provides. Your livelihood arrives by an arrangement you did not make, through a medium you do not control, to a destination you reach only if the sea permits. The next two verses will draw the conclusion.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:67

> **And whenever affliction befalls you at sea, forgotten are those whom you would call upon, save for Him. Then when He has delivered you safely to land, you turn away. Man is ever ungrateful!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crisis as Disclosure**

*Wa idhā massakumu al-ḍurru fī al-baḥri ḍalla man tadʿūna illā iyyāh* — "and when affliction touches you at sea, *lost* is whoever you call upon, except Him." The verb *ḍalla* here means to vanish, to be lost, to slip from awareness. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain it: in the moment of danger, the false deities simply disappear from the mind — they seem suddenly useless and contemptible. This is one of the Qur'an's most powerful epistemological claims. It asserts that beneath the acquired theology there is an unacquired knowledge, and that danger strips away the acquisition. The idolater does not need to be argued out of idolatry; he needs to be frightened, and he will do it himself.

**The Parallel Passages**

The Qur'an returns to this scene repeatedly, which indicates how much weight it places on the observation. 10:22–23:

> *"He it is Who makes you travel by land and sea, until, when you are in the ships and they run with them with a fair wind and they rejoice therein — a storm wind comes upon them and the wave comes upon them from every side, and they think they are encompassed. They call upon God, sincere to Him in religion: 'If Thou deliverest us from this, we will surely be among the grateful.' But when He has delivered them, behold, they act unjustly in the earth without right."*

And 29:65; 31:32; 6:40–41.

**The Reversion**

*Fa-lammā najjākum ilā al-barri aʿraḍtum* — "but when He delivered you to land, you turned away." The verb *aʿraḍa* means to turn the face aside, to withdraw — the same verb used at verse 46 of the disbelievers turning from the mention of God. The moment of sincerity lasts exactly as long as the danger. The Qur'an's diagnosis of this reversion is not that the person is lying during the crisis but that his sincerity is *situational*. He means it while the waves are high; he does not carry it to shore. The character that survives the crisis is the character that existed before it — the crisis reveals but does not reform.

**"Man Is Ever Ungrateful"**

*Wa kāna al-insānu kafūrā* — *kafūr* is the intensive form of one who covers or denies, from *k-f-r*, the root of *kāfir*. The Qur'an's use of the same root for both disbelief and ingratitude is not a coincidence of etymology but a statement of identity: to disbelieve is to be ungrateful, because the disbeliever covers over the favors he has received. Compare 14:34, *"And He gave you of all that you asked Him; and if you counted the favor of God, you could not number it. Truly man is unjust, ungrateful (ẓallūm kafār)"*; 22:66; 25:50; 100:6, *"and truly he is severe in the love of good"*; 80:17.

**The Psychological Precision**

The verse describes a mechanism anyone can verify in himself. Gratitude is easy during deliverance and difficult during comfort. The person who has just been saved feels it vividly; the person for whom nothing has gone wrong feels nothing. Safety produces amnesia about the danger, and the amnesia extends to the deliverer. This is why the Qur'an legislates *remembrance* — dhikr, the daily prayers, the fixed times of verse 78 — as an artificial maintenance of a state that would otherwise decay.

****Distress, Invocation, Rescue, Relapse: The Anatomy in Four Steps****

The verse is a sequence rather than a description, and its four steps are stated with clinical precision. First the affliction: *massakum al-ḍarru*, the harm touches you. Second the invocation: *daʿawtumuhu ḍurraʿan*, you call upon Him humbly, the word *ḍurraʿ* denoting the abasement of one who has no alternative. Third the rescue: *kashafa ʿankum*, He removes it from you. Fourth the relapse: *aʿraḍtum*, you turn away — and the verb is specific, from the root meaning to turn the shoulder, the gesture of deliberate withdrawal rather than of forgetting. The word *ḍurraʿ* is worth pausing on, because it describes the quality of the invocation rather than its content. The one who calls *ḍurraʿan* has abandoned pretense; the intermediaries are gone and the voice is the voice of a person who has nothing left. The Qur'an treats this invocation as evidentially significant, not as hypocrisy. 29:65 states the same sequence: *"And when they embark upon a ship, they call upon God, sincere to Him in religion (mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn); but when He has brought them safely to land, behold, they associate."* The word *mukhliṣīn* — sincere, purified — is applied to people the Qur'an elsewhere condemns, which is a striking theological claim: their sincerity in the crisis is genuine, and the association that follows is also genuine, and both are true of the same people. Al-Qurṭubī draws the doctrinal inference: the invocation in distress is proof that the knowledge of God is present in the human being prior to argument, since it emerges when argument is unavailable. This is the doctrine of *fiṭrah* stated through behavior rather than asserted — 30:30's *"the nature (fiṭrah) upon which God originated mankind"* is demonstrated rather than declared. 39:8 adds the psychological detail that makes the verse most useful: *"And when affliction touches man, he calls upon Us; then when We bestow a favor from Us upon him, he says: I was given it only by knowledge (ʿalā ʿilm)."* The relapse is accompanied by a theory, and the theory attributes to his own competence what was in fact delivered to him. The verse's final clause — *wa kāna al-kafūru kafūrā* — is a rhetorical intensification, and the commentators read it as either the specific reference to the disbeliever or the general statement that the ungrateful person is intensely so. The doubling is deliberate: the *kāfir* is *kafūr*, the one who covers is one who covers much. The sequence therefore closes with a diagnosis rather than a verdict, and the diagnosis is available to any reader: watch what you say in the crisis, and then watch what you say a week later.

**The Application Beyond the Sea**

The sea is the Qur'an's chosen example because it is the situation of maximum helplessness in the pre-modern world: no technology, no rescue, no escape. The modern equivalents are numerous — the diagnosis in a doctor's office, the turbulence at thirty thousand feet, the night a child is ill. In each, the person discovers what he actually relies on. The verse's warning is not about the moment of discovery but about the shore.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:68

> **Do you feel secure that He will not cause the shore to engulf you or unleash a torrent of stones upon you? Then would you find no guardian for yourselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The False Security of the Shore**

*Afa-amintum an yakhsifa bikum jāniba al-barri* — "Do you feel secure that He will not cause a side of the land to swallow you?" The interrogative *a-fa-amintum* — "do you then feel secure?" — targets precisely the state produced by the deliverance described in verse 67. The one who has just been saved to land concludes that the land is safe. The verse says: the danger followed you. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī connect *yakhsifa* to the punishment of Qārūn at 28:81: *"So We caused the earth to swallow him and his house"*; and 34:9. The verb *khasafa* means to cause the ground to collapse and swallow. The one who escaped the sea may be swallowed by the earth.

**"Or Unleash a Torrent of Stones Upon You"**

*Aw yursila ʿalaykum ḥāṣiban* — *ḥāṣib* is a stoning wind, a storm that hurls stones; al-Ṭabarī notes it may also mean a violent destructive wind on land. The Qur'an uses it of the punishment of Lot's people at 11:82, 15:74, and 54:34, and the commentators connect it to the stones of Sijjīl sent against the People of the Elephant at 105:1–4:

> *"And He sent upon them birds in flocks, hurling against them stones of baked clay."*

**"Then You Would Find No Guardian for Yourselves"**

*Thumma lā tajidū lakum wakīlā* — the *wakīl* again: the one to whom affairs are entrusted. The verse's argument is that in the moment of such a punishment, the person who has taken guardians apart from God will discover that none of them functions. Compare 6:94, *"And you have come to Us alone, as We created you the first time, and you have left behind what We provided for you."*

**The Rhetorical Method**

The verse is a rhetorical question, and it does not predict that these punishments will occur. It challenges the assumption of security — which is the actual error. The Qur'an's repeated warnings against false security are among its most consistent themes: 7:97–99, *"Do the people of the towns feel secure that Our might will not come upon them by night while they sleep? ... None feels secure from the plan of God except the losing people"*; 12:107; 16:45–47; 67:16–17.

****Four Fears on Land: The Argument That Safety Is Not a Place****

The verse answers the implicit objection in the previous one, and the objection is natural: the lesson of the sea does not apply to me, because I live on land. The four questions close that escape, and they are ordered from the ground upward. *Yakhsifa bikum al-arḍ*, He causes the earth to swallow you — the threat is beneath the feet. *Yursila ʿalaykum ḥāṣibā*, He sends upon you a *ḥāṣib*, a storm that pelts with stones or gravel — the threat is from above. The two directions exhaust the physical environment, and the pairing is the Qur'an's characteristic completeness. The word *ḥāṣib* is specific rather than general, and al-Ṭabarī glosses it as a wind that carries gravel, which is the punishment sent on the people of Lot at 11:82 and 15:74. The reference matters, because the threat is then not hypothetical: it has happened, to a named people, in recorded history, and the same word is used at 51:32 and 67:17. The verse's question is therefore not "could this happen?" but "has it not happened, and what makes you exempt?" — the argument the sūrah made at verse 58 from the ruins on the caravan road. The two closing clauses are the more penetrating part. *Thumma lā tajidū lakum wakīlā* — then you will find no trustee — and at verse 69, *thumma lā tajidū lakum ʿalaynā bihi tabīʿā*, you will find no one to pursue Us for it. Al-Rāzī explains the function of the *wakīl* here: it is the one to whom a person in danger turns for protection and management of his affair. The verse removes not merely the safety but the recourse. On land the human being assumes he has options — a wall, a shelter, a neighbor, an authority — and the verse strips them in a single clause. The psychological force of the passage lies in its refusal to let the setting do the work. The man who has learned the lesson of the sea and returned to the land has learned nothing if he concludes that the lesson belonged to the sea. 67:16–17 makes the same argument with the same two directions: *"Do you feel secure that He Who is in the heaven will not cause the earth to swallow you? Or do you feel secure that He Who is in the heaven will not send upon you a storm?"* The point is not that disaster is imminent but that security is not a property of geography, and the person who has confused the two has located his trust in the wrong place.

**The Logical Principle**

The argument is one of *consistency*: if God's power operated at sea, it operates on land. The person who acknowledges the danger at one location and denies it at another has not reasoned; he has relocated. The verse exposes the relocation. The practical application is to the general human tendency to treat safety as a *place* rather than a *condition*. People feel secure in their homes, in their countries, in their routines — and the feeling is not based on any analysis of what could happen there. It is based on the absence of recent harm. The verse identifies this as unfounded and asks the reader to substitute reliance on God for reliance on location.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:69

> **Or do you feel secure that He will not cause you to return to it another time, and unleash upon you a tempestuous wind, and drown you for your having been ungrateful? Then you would find no avenger therein against Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Voyage**

*Am amintum an yuʿīdakum fīhi tāratan ukhrā* — "or do you feel secure that He will not return you to it another time?" The verse anticipates the obvious: the traveler will sail again. Trade requires it; livelihood depends on it. And the deliverance of the previous voyage does not guarantee the next. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī read the verse as a statement of continued liability: the person delivered once remains exposed, and his exposure is now aggravated by ingratitude.

**"And Drown You for Your Having Been Ungrateful"**

*Fa-yughriqakum bimā kafartum* — the drowning is explicitly attributed to the ingratitude, not to the weather. The *bi-mā* construction gives the reason. This is the Qur'an's moral reading of events: the physical cause is real, but the verse locates the operative cause in the moral condition. The two levels of causation are not in competition in the Qur'anic worldview. The wind is the wind; the reason it drowns *this* person at *this* time is stated as the ingratitude. Compare 71:25:

> *"Because of their wrongs they were drowned and made to enter a Fire."*

**"Then You Would Find No Avenger Against Us"**

*Thumma lā tajidū lakum ʿalaynā bihi tabīʿā* — *tabīʿ* is one who pursues a claim, a pursuer of vengeance or restitution; the same root as verse 33's context of retribution. The verse states that there will be no one to pursue the claim against God. This is a claim about the finality of divine action. In human affairs, every injury has a claimant — the family, the tribe, the court. The verse asserts that where God acts, there is no such claimant, because there is no party with standing. Compare 4:123, *"And God is not unjust to the servants,"* and 10:44, *"Truly God does not wrong mankind in anything, but mankind wrong themselves."* The absence of an avenger is not because the action is unjust but because the justice is not in question.

****The Second Pair, and the Question of Redress****

The verse repeats the structure of verse 68 at sea, and the repetition is the argument: the two environments are governed by the same authority, and safety is not a matter of location. Al-Ṭabarī reads the pair of verses as answering the same implicit claim from two directions — the man on land who says the sea lesson does not apply to him, and the man at sea who assumes the danger has passed. The verb *yuʿīdakum fīhi tārah ukhrā*, He returns you to it another time, is significant: the danger is not a single event but a recurring condition, and the human being returns to it voluntarily, for trade and travel. The verb *qāṣifan*, used of the wind, denotes a wind that breaks and destroys what it strikes, and al-Qurṭubī contrasts it with the favorable wind of verse 66 — the same element that carried the ship now breaks it. The verse thereby completes the point made at verse 66: the favor was not a natural constant, and its withdrawal is equally within the same governance. The final clause is the most striking in the passage: *thumma lā tajidū lakum ʿalaynā bihi tabīʿā*. The word *tabīʿ* is from the root meaning to follow, and it denotes one who pursues a claim — an avenger, or in the language of law, a claimant who follows up a debt. The verse is therefore stating, in the most concrete legal image available, that there is no recourse: no one will take up your case against God, no one will pursue the matter on your behalf. Al-Rāzī explains that this is the removal of the last comfort, since a person in extremity may give up on safety and still hold on to the expectation of vindication. The theological content of that clause is the sovereignty the entire sūrah has been establishing. If there is no *wakīl* and no *tabīʿ*, then there is no court above this one and no party with standing to appeal — which is precisely what *tawḥīd* means in practice rather than in formula. 40:16 states the same reality from the other side: *"...and the judgment today belongs to God, the Exalted, the Great."* The pastoral reading of the pair of verses is what has made them matter to readers. They are not a threat of arbitrary destruction; they are a correction of a specific illusion — that safety is somewhere. The man who stops looking for it in geography begins to look for it in the One who governs the geography, and 65:3 gives the answer that the search is meant to reach: *"And whoever relies upon God, He is sufficient for him."*

**The Two Verses as a Unit**

Verses 68 and 69 cover the two domains — land and sea — and the two forms of punishment — engulfment and drowning. The coverage is total, which is the point: there is no location in which the person is beyond the reach of the One he has declined to thank. The argument of verses 66–69 is complete: God provides the means of your livelihood (66), you recognize it only in crisis (67), and your security is nowhere (68–69). The natural conclusion, which the verse leaves the reader to draw, is that the acknowledgment which the crisis produces should be the permanent state.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:70

> **We have indeed honored the Children of Adam, and We carry them over land and sea, and provide them with good things, and We have favored them above many We have created.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of Human Dignity**

*Wa laqad karramnā banī Ādam* — "and We have certainly honored the Children of Adam." The emphatic *la-qad* and the verb *karrama* — to honor, to ennoble, to make noble — make this the Qur'an's most direct statement of human dignity, and it is the verse cited throughout the Islamic tradition whenever the intrinsic worth of the human being is affirmed. The honor is bestowed on *banī Ādam* — the children of Adam, the species as such. It is not conditioned on faith, on righteousness, on ethnicity, or on capacity. The disbeliever and the believer are equally *Children of Adam*. The classical jurists drew extensive legal consequences from this: the prohibition of mutilation even of an enemy's body, the requirement of burial, the prohibition of selling a free person, the rules protecting the dignity of the accused.

**What the Honor Consists In**

The commentators enumerate the components. Al-Ṭabarī and others cite the authority given to human beings over other creatures and the subjection of creation to them: 14:33, *"And He made subservient to you the sun and the moon, constant in their courses, and made subservient to you the night and the day"*; 16:12; 45:13. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī add the use of hands and arms in a manner no other animal employs; al-Rāzī adds intellect, discernment, and speech; others add the capacity to know God. The Qur'an's own account of Adam's distinction is threefold and precise. He was created with God's two Hands: 38:75, *"What prevented thee from prostrating before what I created with My two Hands?"* He was given of God's Spirit: 15:29, 32:9, 38:72, *"and breathed into him of My Spirit."* And he was taught the names: 2:31, *"And He taught Adam the names, all of them."* The three — direct creation, the breathing of spirit, and knowledge — constitute the Qur'anic anthropology.

**"And We Carry Them Over Land and Sea"**

*Wa ḥamalnāhum fī al-barri wa-l-baḥr* — the same verb *ḥamala* used of the passengers of Noah's Ark in verse 3. The connection is deliberate: the carriage that saved Noah's progeny is the same carriage that operates daily for all of Adam's children. Over land by riding animals — 16:8, *"And horses, mules, and donkeys, that you may ride them, and as an adornment"*; 40:79 — and over sea by the ships of verse 66.

**"And Provide Them With Good Things"**

*Wa razaqnāhum mina al-ṭayyibāt* — *ṭayyibāt* means the good, pure, wholesome things. The Qur'an uses the term for lawful and pleasant provision: 2:168, *"O mankind, eat of what is in the earth, lawful and good (ḥalālan ṭayyibā)"*; 7:157, where the Prophet ﷺ is described as:

> *"making lawful for them the good things (al-ṭayyibāt) and forbidding them the foul."*

The verse thus includes in the honor the quality of the provision: not mere survival but the wholesome and the pleasant.

**"And We Favored Them Above Many We Have Created"**

*Wa faḍḍalnāhum ʿalā kathīrin mimman khalaqnā tafḍīlā* — the phrase *above many* has been read in two ways. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record the view that it means above all creation, including the angels, on the basis of the commanded prostration (2:34; 7:11; 17:61; 15:29; 18:50; 20:116; 38:73–74). Others take *many* literally, excluding some categories. The commentators also distinguish the two verbs: *karramnā* — We honored — may refer to worldly blessings, while *faḍḍalnā* — We favored — refers to spiritual rank or the blessings of the Hereafter.

****Karramnā: A Dignity That Is Given, Not Achieved****

This verse is the Qur'an's most explicit statement of human dignity, and its grammar is what gives it force. The particle *laqad* combines the oath-particle with the perfect, producing an emphatic assertion: *wa laqad karramnā banī Ādam* — and We have indeed honored the children of Adam. The verb *karrama* is from the intensive form of the root *k-r-m*, and it denotes honoring in a way that confers status rather than merely praising. The honor is stated as a completed divine act, not as a quality the human being earns, and the consequences of that grammatical fact run through the whole of Islamic ethics. The four gifts enumerated are specific. Carriage on land and sea — *ḥamalnāhum fī al-barr wa al-baḥr*, We carried them, the same verb of being made to travel used of the ship at verse 66, which ties the honor to the argument just made: the danger of the sea is the same governance as the honor of the voyage. Provision of good things — *wa razaqnāhum min al-ṭayyibāt*, of the pure and wholesome, not merely of the available. And preferment — *wa faḍḍalnāhum ʿalā kathīrin mimman khalaqnā tafḍīlā*, We preferred them over much of what We created, with a marked preference, the cognate accusative *tafḍīlā* intensifying the verb as *ʿuluwwan kabīrā* did at verse 43. The word *kathīr* — much, many — rather than *jamīʿ* or *kull* has been the subject of extensive comment. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī discuss the two readings it permits: that the human being is preferred over much of creation but not over all, the angels being excepted in one view; or that the preference is comprehensive and the word *kathīr* is used in a general sense. The disagreement matters less than what both readings share, which is that the preference is a divine act with a stated ground — and the Qur'an states that ground at 2:30–33, where Adam is given the *names* and the angels are asked to produce them and cannot. The honor is therefore rooted in the capacity for knowledge.

The commentators enumerate its visible forms. Al-Qurṭubī lists the upright posture, which frees the hands and lifts the face; speech and its articulation; writing, by which knowledge crosses generations; the hand capable of craft; and reason itself. 95:4 states the same doctrine in a single phrase: *"We created man in the best of forms (fī aḥsani taqwīm)."* And 2:31 supplies the ground: *"And He taught Adam the names, all of them."* The ethical consequences have been drawn from this verse for fourteen centuries, and they are worth stating because the verse is often quoted without them. Dignity is not conditional on virtue, usefulness, belief, or capacity, since the honor was conferred on *banī Ādam* — the children of Adam — as a class, before any of them had done anything. This is the scriptural ground on which Islamic jurisprudence built its protections for the inviolability of life, the prohibition of mutilation even of an enemy's corpse, the requirement to bury the dead with honor, and the prohibition of selling a free human being. The one who is honored by God cannot be treated as an instrument by man. The verse's placement is the final point, and it is deliberate. It follows the argument that no created being has authority over the servant of God, and it precedes the account of the gathering on the Day of Resurrection at verse 71. The dignity conferred is real and it is not the end of the matter: the honored being is still summoned, still questioned, and still answerable. The Qur'an's anthropology holds both without collapsing either — 95:4–6 states them in two consecutive verses, the best of forms, and then the return to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do righteous deeds.

**The Verse as the Answer to Iblīs**

The verse's placement immediately after the Iblīs narrative (vv. 61–65) is decisive. Iblīs objected: *shall I prostrate to one You created of clay?* — a refusal of human dignity based on origin. Verse 70 is the divine reply, and it does not argue about clay. It states the honor as a fact of bestowal: *We have honored them.* The dignity is not derived from the material; it is conferred by the Conferrer. This has a practical consequence that the tradition has drawn repeatedly. Since human dignity is bestowed rather than earned, it cannot be forfeited by failure. The sinner remains a child of Adam; the disbeliever remains honored in his person. The honor that is given cannot be taken back by the recipient's conduct — only its *fruit* in the Hereafter varies, which is what the following verses address.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:71

> **On the Day We shall call every people by their imam, whosoever is given his book in his right hand, it is they who shall read their book, and they shall not be wronged so much as the thread of a date stone.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-Imām: That Which Is Followed**

*Yawma nadʿū kulla unāsin bi-imāmihim* — "the Day We call every people by their *imām*." The word *imām* derives from *a-m-m*, to intend, to head toward; an *imām* is that which stands in front and is followed. The term is value-neutral in Arabic, and the Qur'an uses it of both the righteous and the wicked. Of the righteous: 2:124, *"And [remember] when his Lord tried Abraham with words, and he fulfilled them. He said: 'I will make thee an imām for mankind'"*; and 21:73, *"And We made them imāms guiding by Our command."* Of the wicked: 28:41:

> *"And We made them imāms calling to the Fire, and on the Day of Resurrection they will not be helped."*

**The Five Readings**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record a range of interpretations. The *prophet*: each community will be called by the name of its prophet — the community of Moses, the community of Jesus, the community of Muhammad. This is supported by 4:41, *"And how will it be when We bring a witness from every nation, and bring thee as a witness against these?"* The *revelation*: each community by its Book — the people of the Torah, the people of the Gospel, the people of the Qur'an. Compare 45:28–29. The *religion or way followed*: each group by the path it took. The *book of deeds*: al-Qurṭubī notes that *imām* is used at 36:12 of the record — *"and everything We have enumerated in a clear imām"* — and that the verse's subsequent mention of receiving the book supports this reading. The book is an *imām* because it is consulted as an authoritative register. The *spiritual leaders or schools followed*: al-Qurṭubī extends it to the scholars and schools of thought a person adhered to. Al-Rāzī records an inward reading of real interest: that the *imām* is the defining trait of the person — generosity, the desire for knowledge, courage, or hatred, desire, anger — the quality that led him and therefore names him. On this reading, each person is called by the thing he actually followed, which is often not what he claimed to follow.

**The Shīʿite Reading**
