Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record the Shīʿite interpretation, citing the eighth Imām ʿAlī al-Riḍā: *"On the Day of Judgment He will call all people by the imām of their time, the Book of their Lord, and the sunnah of their prophet."* And from Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq, that everyone will be called by the Imām of his time, and the Imāms will be called by the Prophet ﷺ. The Twelver tradition holds that the twelfth Imām, in occultation since 329/941, continues to guide and is the *ṣāḥib al-zamān*, the master of the age.

**The Right Hand and the Left**

*Fa-man ūtiya kitābahu bi-yamīnihi fa-ulāʾika yaqraʾūna kitābahum* — the book in the right hand indicates divine contentment; in the left, divine wrath. The scene is developed at 69:19–25 and 84:7–10. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the contrast: those given the book in the right hand read it willingly and with joy, while those given it in the left are so overwhelmed by fear, shame, and regret that they cannot read it. The reading here connects to verse 14: *"Read your book! On this Day, your soul suffices as a reckoner against you."* The same command, now differentiated by the outcome: the one whose record is good reads it gladly; the one whose record is evil cannot bear to.

****Bi-Imāmihim: One Word, Four Readings, and What Each Implies****

The word *imām* in this verse has generated more exegetical discussion than any other in the sūrah, and the plurality of readings is instructive rather than confusing, because each is attested and each produces a coherent account of the Day. Al-Ṭabarī catalogues them. The first, from Ibn ʿAbbās and a number of the Salaf: *imām* means the prophet — every community will be called by its prophet, who will testify to what he conveyed. The second: the scripture — the people of the Torah called by the Torah, the people of the Gospel by the Gospel, the people of the Qur'an by the Qur'an. The third: the book of deeds, which the following verse appears to support with *fa-man ūtiya kitābahu bi-yamīnih*, whoever is given his book in his right hand. The fourth: the leader whom the people followed, whether prophet or false guide — 25:41 records the mockery of the false leader, and 2:166–167 describes the followers disowning the followed. The Qur'an itself uses *imām* in each of these senses, which is why the exegetes could sustain all four. 11:17 describes the Qur'an as preceded by the Book of Moses *imāman wa raḥmah* — a guide and a mercy. 15:79 uses it of a road, *ʿalā imāmin mubīn*. 36:12 uses it of the record in which everything is inscribed, *fī imāmin mubīn*. 2:124 uses it of a human leader, *"I will make thee an imām for mankind."* The word therefore spans the meanings of guide, model, record, and road, and the verse exploits the range. Al-Qurṭubī adds the Shīʿite reading — that the *imām* is the imām of the age, whose obedience or neglect determines the community's standing — and records the report attributed to ʿAlī that the verse refers to the imāms of every age. The exegetes treat this as a possible specification rather than a contradiction, since it falls under the fourth reading. The reports that the communities will be called by their mothers' names rather than their fathers' appear in the collections in connection with this verse, and al-Qurṭubī preserves the reasoning: the mother's name is certain. The verse's function is to establish that the summons is not anonymous. The individual is not called as a member of an undifferentiated mass but by reference to what he followed, what he was given, and what he was sent. Al-Rāzī draws the consequence: the calling by the *imām* means the standard is fixed in advance and cannot be renegotiated, which is why the following verse describes the immediate consequence — reading one's own record — with such precision. The verse that closes the passage, 71's own final clause, states the outcome in two lines: whoever is given his book in his right hand will read it with pleasure and will not be wronged the width of a date-stone's thread.

**"They Shall Not Be Wronged the Thread of a Date Stone"**

*Wa lā yuẓlamūna fatīlā* — *fatīl* is the thread in the groove of a date stone, the thin filament; by extension, the smallest imaginable quantity. The Qur'an uses the same idiom at 4:49, *"Do they not see that God does not wrong anyone so much as an atom's weight (mithqāla dharrah)?"*; and 4:77, 124; 19:60; 21:47. The verse's assurance is that the reckoning is exact. Not one filament of good will be lost, and not one filament of evil will be added. This is the Qur'an's answer to the fear that the accounting might be unfair — the fear that keeps many people from examining their record at all. The precision is the comfort: the one who has done little good will receive that little in full, and the one who fears he has been overcharged has a guarantee against it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:72

> **And whosoever was blind in this [life] will be blind in the Hereafter, and further astray from the way.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Continuity of the Condition**

*Wa man kāna fī hādhihi aʿmā fa-huwa fī al-ākhirati aʿmā* — the verse states a continuity between the two states, and the pronoun *hādhihi* — "this" — is read by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī as referring to this world (*al-dunyā*). The one blind here will be blind there. The claim is not that blindness is *punished* by more blindness as an external penalty, but that the condition *persists* — that it is the same condition, continuing. This is consistent with the sūrah's repeated theme: the book on the neck (v. 13), the soul as reckoner (v. 14), the self-authored record. Nothing external is imposed; the interior state is made permanent.

**The Blindness Is of the Heart**

The Qur'an is explicit that the blindness in question is not physical. 22:46: *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but the hearts within the breasts go blind."* Compare 2:18, *"Deaf, dumb, blind — so they will not return"*; 2:171; 5:71; 6:50. And the Prophet's physical blindness is nowhere treated in the Qur'an as a spiritual deficiency — 80:1–10, the opening of Sūrah ʿAbasa, is a rebuke to the Prophet ﷺ for turning from a blind man who came seeking guidance, and it establishes the opposite: physical blindness is no barrier and no disgrace.

**"And Further Astray From the Way"**

*Wa aḍallu sabīlā* — the comparative is significant. The blindness of the Hereafter is not merely equal to that of this world but *worse*, and the straying is *further*. Al-Rāzī explains: in this world, the blind person retains some capacity to recover — the message continues to arrive, the reminder continues to sound, the opportunity to turn remains. In the Hereafter, none of that remains. The blindness is now without remedy, and the straying is without return. Compare 20:124–26:

> *"And whoever turns away from My remembrance, his shall be a constricted life, and We shall raise him on the Day of Resurrection blind. He will say: 'My Lord, why hast Thou raised me blind, when I was seeing?' He will say: 'Thus — Our signs came to thee and thou didst forget them; and likewise today thou art forgotten.'"*

The exchange in that passage makes the mechanism explicit: the blindness of the Hereafter is the *forgetting* of the signs made permanent.

**The Counter-Image**

The Qur'an supplies the opposite at 50:22: *"Thou wast heedless of this; now We have removed thy covering from thee, and thy sight today is piercing (ḥadīd)."* The covering removed, the sight sharp — for the one who will benefit from seeing. The same removal occurs for the one who will not, and it is worse for him, because he sees what he refused to see while there was time to act.

****Blind Here, Blind There: The Continuity Rather Than the Reversal****

The verse is one of the Qur'an's clearest statements that the Hereafter is not a reversal of the earthly condition but its completion. *Wa man kāna fī hādhihī aʿmā fa-huwa fī al-ākhirati aʿmā wa aḍallu sabīlā* — whoever is blind in this life is blind in the Hereafter, and further astray from the way. The comparative *aḍallu sabīlā* is the crucial word: the blindness is not merely continued but intensified, and the intensification has a reason. Al-Ṭabarī records the two readings of the first blindness and holds both. The first: blind to God's blessings and evidences, unable to see the signs that are set before him. The second: blind to the arguments and the guidance, which is the condition described at verses 45–46 with the coverings and the deafness. The two are related as symptom and cause, and the verse treats the blindness as a settled state rather than an event — *kāna fī hādhihī aʿmā*, he *was* blind in this, using the verb of being rather than of becoming. The Qur'an supplies the mechanism elsewhere, and 20:124–126 states it with a dialogue that makes it concrete. The one who turns away from God's remembrance is given a constricted life and raised blind on the Day; he asks, *"My Lord, why hast Thou raised me blind, when I was seeing?"* — and the answer is not a punishment imposed from outside but a disclosure: *"Thus it is — Our signs came to thee and thou didst forget them, and likewise today thou art forgotten."* The blindness in the Hereafter is the worldly blindness, now permanent and now visible to its possessor. This is why it is worse: the man who could not see in a world where seeing was optional cannot see in a world where seeing is the only activity. The theological principle beneath the verse is that the soul's dispositions survive death. The faculties that were atrophied by disuse do not become functional at the resurrection, and the ones that were trained do not become atrophied. 35:43 states the corollary: *"Do they await anything but the way (sunnah) of the former peoples? Thou wilt find no change in the way of God."* And 6:110 describes the process: *"And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time."* The pastoral weight of the verse is therefore on the word *kāna* — was. The condition is acquired over time, in this life, by a series of refusals, none of which seemed decisive. The verse is a warning about accumulation rather than about a single act, and it locates the moment of decision not in the Hereafter but in the ordinary Tuesday on which a person decides not to look.

**The Verse's Practical Force**

The verse makes a claim about the formation of character that is independently verifiable: habits of inattention become capacities of inattention. The person who has spent decades not looking at a certain fact does not simply decline to look; he has lost the ability. The verse extends this to its final term and states the consequence: what becomes fixed in this life is what one arrives with in the next. This is why the Qur'an's spiritual program is largely about *attention* — the fixed prayer times of verse 78, the recitation, the remembrance, the night vigil of verse 79. These are not merely acts of worship; they are the maintenance of the faculty of sight. The verse supplies the reason they are prescribed: the faculty atrophies without use, and the atrophy is not reversible at will.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:73

> **And they were about to tempt thee away from that which We revealed unto thee, that thou mightest falsely ascribe unto Us something other than it, whereupon they would surely have taken thee as a friend.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Attempt and Its Nature**

*Wa in kādū la-yaftinūnaka ʿani alladhī awḥaynā ilayka* — "and they were near to tempting thee away from what We revealed to thee." The construction *in kādū la-* indicates proximity: they came close to succeeding. The verb *fatana* means to test, to tempt, to seduce, to divert. The objective is specified: *li-taftariya ʿalaynā ghayrah* — "that thou mightest fabricate against Us something other than it." The verb *iftarā* means to fabricate, to invent falsely; it is the Qur'an's standard term for false attribution to God. The temptation was not to abandon the revelation but to *modify* it — to add something to it or subtract from it.

**The Reported Occasions**

Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and others record the context. One report concerns the people of al-Ṭāʾif (Thaqīf), who asked the Prophet ﷺ for a year's respite to continue worshipping their deity al-Lāt, or for their land to be made inviolable like Makkah, or both. Another report concerns the Quraysh's request that he approach and touch their idols — in some versions in exchange for permission to touch the Black Stone. The reports indicate that the Prophet ﷺ was inclined toward some accommodation, attracted by the possibility of a peaceful opening. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī note that the verses document the attempt and its defeat. The demand made to the Prophet ﷺ is recorded explicitly elsewhere, in terms that leave its character unmistakable:

> *"And when Our clear signs are recited unto them, those who look not for the meeting with Us say: 'Bring a Qur'an other than this, or change it.'"* (10:15)

**Why the Temptation Was Attractive**

The commentators are careful not to suggest any moral defect in the Prophet ﷺ. The attraction was not to the idols but to the *outcome* — the conversion of a tribe, the end of persecution, the opening of Makkah. The offer was framed as a strategic concession, not as an abandonment of faith. This is what makes the temptation real: it appealed to a legitimate good through an illegitimate means. The verse's lesson is therefore about the nature of compromise in matters of revelation. A concession in the *content* of the message, however small and however well-intentioned, would have altered the thing itself. The Qur'an's position is that the message is not a negotiable instrument; it may be conveyed in different ways, at different times, to different people, but its content is not subject to amendment for the sake of receptivity.

**"Whereupon They Would Surely Have Taken Thee as a Friend"**

*Wa idhan la-ttakhadhūka khalīlā* — *khalīl* is an intimate friend, a bosom companion; the Qur'an uses the term of Abraham at 4:125, *"And God took Abraham as a khalīl."* The verse identifies the price of the concession: friendship with those who had persecuted the believers. The transaction is named plainly — modify the revelation, and the hostility ends. The Qur'an's warning about such friendship is consistent: 3:28, 4:139–140, 5:51, 60:1. The point is not that Muslims may not be on good terms with non-Muslims — 60:8 explicitly commands kindness and justice toward those who do not fight them — but that the *cost* of such friendship must never be paid from the content of the revelation.

****They Very Nearly Made You Lean: The Pressure at the Point of Concession****

The occasions recorded for this verse are among the most historically specific in the Qur'an, and the verse's candid reporting of the Prophet's ﷺ near-inclination is one of the strongest evidences of its own authenticity. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the reports at length. One group of them concerns the delegation of the Thaqīf from Ṭāʾif, who proposed conditions: that the valley of Ṭāʾif be granted to them, that wine and interest be permitted them for a period, that the idol al-Lāt not be broken, and that they be exempt from the prayer's prostration. Another concerns the Quraysh leaders who offered a reciprocal arrangement — that the Prophet ﷺ cease reviling their gods and they would cease opposing him, or that the poor believers be driven from his assembly so that the notables might sit with him. 6:52 records the answer to the second proposal, and 80:1–10 records the rebuke when the Prophet ﷺ turned away from a blind man to address the notables. The grammar of the verse is precise. *Wa in kādū la-yaftinūnaka* — they very nearly tempted thee away from what We revealed to thee — uses the construction of near-occurrence, and al-Qurṭubī notes that *kāda* in Arabic, when followed by the imperfect without an accompanying statement of occurrence, indicates that the thing did not happen. The verse therefore records an approach that was withstood, not a lapse. The phrase *li-taftariya ʿalaynā ghayrah*, that thou shouldst invent something other than it against Us, names the exact demand: not the abandonment of the message but its modification. This distinction is the verse's permanent content. The pressure on a messenger is rarely to abandon the truth outright, since that would be recognized and refused; it is to adjust it — to soften one clause, to grant one exemption, to accommodate one interest. The verse identifies the offer precisely, and identifies the reward offered for it: *wa idhan la-ittakhadhūka khalīlā*, and then they would have taken thee as an intimate friend. The price of the modification is friendship with the people whose demands were modified, which is to say that the accommodation purchases the accommodation's own reward. 68:8–9 states the same offer in its shortest form: *"They wish that thou shouldst be pliant, so that they may be pliant (waddū law tudhinu fa-yudhinūn)."* The verse's theological significance is that *ʿiṣmah*, the protection of the prophets, is protection under pressure rather than immunity from it. The near-inclination was real — the following verse says so explicitly — and what prevented its realization was divine confirmation, which is stated as a fact rather than as a merit of the Prophet ﷺ. The lesson for anyone who carries a message is the one the verse models: the pressure will come in the form of a reasonable proposal, and the test will not feel like a test.

**The Verse's Permanence**

The situation is not historical. Every generation faces its version of the offer: adjust the teaching slightly, and acceptance follows. The verse records that the offer was made to the Prophet ﷺ himself, that it was attractive, and that it was refused. The precedent is the point: if the concession was unavailable to him, it is unavailable to anyone.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:74

> **And had We not made thee firm, thou wouldst certainly have inclined toward them a little.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Firming**

*Wa law lā an thabbatnāka laqad kinta rakinta ilayhim shayʾan qalīlā* — "and had We not made thee firm, thou wouldst have inclined toward them a little." The verb *thabbata* is the intensive of *th-b-t*, to make firm, to establish, to steady; the Qur'an uses it of God's strengthening of the believers at 8:11 and of the strengthening of the heart at 11:120. The verse states a counterfactual: the firmness was God's act, and without it the inclination would have occurred. Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī read this as the clearest statement in the Qur'an of the doctrine of *ʿiṣmah* — the prophets' divinely guaranteed protection from sin. The protection is not a property of the prophet's own strength; it is a divine bestowal, and the verse says so explicitly.

**"A Little"**

*Shayʾan qalīlā* — the qualification is significant and the commentators discuss it at length. The inclination would have been slight, not total. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the phrase indicates the *nearness* of the temptation rather than any actual failing: the verse describes what would have happened, and what happened was that it did not. Some commentators read *a little* as indicating the degree of inclination that the human heart is always capable of, even in the best of people. On this reading the verse is a statement about the human condition as much as about the Prophet ﷺ: no one is so firm that, absent divine support, he would not incline somewhat toward a plausible accommodation.

**The Prayer the Verse Produced**

Al-Zamakhsharī records that when verse 75 was revealed, the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"O God! Do not entrust me to my own soul, even for the blink of an eye."*

This supplication became one of the most cited in the Islamic devotional tradition, precisely because of its origin. The one who was divinely protected asked not to be left to himself for the duration of a blink — which establishes the posture the tradition expects of everyone else.

****Wa Lawlā an Thabbatnāka: The Firmness That Is Not Self-Generated****

The verse completes the preceding one, and its construction is a conditional whose antecedent is negated: *and had We not made thee stand firm, thou wouldst very nearly have inclined toward them a little*. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both note the Arabic principle that *kāda* with the imperfect negates the action — "he very nearly did" means he did not — so the verse asserts both the reality of the pressure and the fact of its resistance, and attributes the resistance to God. The verb *thabbata* is from the intensive form of *th-b-t*, to make firm, to establish, to fix in place, and it is the Qur'an's standard term for the divine confirmation of the believer. 14:27 states the general promise: *"God confirms those who believe with the firm word (bi-l-qawl al-thābit) in the life of this world and in the Hereafter."* 8:11 describes the confirmation at Badr: *"...that He might thereby confirm the hearts."* 3:146 records the prayer of the steadfast: *"Our Lord, pour patience upon us and make our feet firm (wa thabbit aqdāmanā)."* And 22:15 supplies the challenge implicit in all of it: whoever thinks God will not help His Messenger should stretch a rope to the ceiling and cut it off. The clause *wa idhan la-kādū yastafizzūnaka min al-arḍ* — and then they would very nearly have provoked thee to leave the land — records the second stage of the pressure: having failed to modify the message, the opponents move to remove the messenger. The Qur'an states the rule that follows in the next verse, and history supplied the confirmation: the expulsion was attempted, the Hijrah occurred, and the one expelled returned to the land eight years later. The pastoral content of the verse is its account of where steadfastness comes from. It is not a natural virtue that some people possess and others lack; it is a confirmation conferred, and it is conferred in proportion to the pressure rather than in spite of it. The person who concludes from his own wavering that he is unfit has misread the verse, since the verse reports wavering in the most steadfast of human beings and locates the remedy outside him. 3:103 states the means by which the confirmation is normally given: *"And hold fast, all of you together, to the rope of God, and be not divided."* The final clause of the verse is the one that has made it a text of consolation across the tradition. Having recorded that the pressure was severe and the inclination real, the Qur'an adds: *wa idhan lā yalbashūna khilāfaka illā qalīlā* — and then they would not have remained after thee except a little. The threat to the opponents is that the arrangement they sought would have been brief, which is the standard answer to every accommodation: what is purchased by modifying the truth does not last long enough to be worth the modification.

**Firmness Is Not Self-Generated**

The verse's teaching for the ordinary believer is precise: firmness is not self-generated. The person who believes his resistance to compromise is a product of his own strength has misidentified its source, and the misidentification is dangerous, because it produces the confidence that precedes the fall. The correct posture is the one the Prophet ﷺ modeled — to ask, continually, not to be left alone with oneself. This is why the Islamic spiritual tradition treats reliance on one's own state (*al-rukūn ilā al-nafs*) as a specific spiritual disease, and why the standard remedy is the constant invocation. The verse supplies the theological ground: the firmest of human beings needed firming.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:75

> **Then We would have made thee taste double in life and double in death. Then thou wouldst have found for thyself no helper against Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Double Punishment**

*Idhan la-adhaqnāka ḍiʿfa al-ḥayāti wa ḍiʿfa al-mamāt* — "then We would have made thee taste double in life and double in death." The verb *adhāqnā* — to make taste — is used in the Qur'an of both blessing and punishment, and its sensory force is deliberate: the punishment is not abstract but experienced. Al-Zamakhsharī explains that the double punishment in death may refer to the punishment of the grave before the Resurrection and the punishment of the Fire after it. Others read the doubling as the multiplication appropriate to the prophet's rank: the higher the station, the graver the deviation.

**The Principle of Proportional Responsibility**

The verse states a principle that runs through the Qur'an and Islamic ethics: responsibility is proportional to knowledge and station. The wives of the Prophet ﷺ were told at 33:30:

> *"O wives of the Prophet, whoever of you commits a manifest indecency, the punishment for her will be doubled. And that is easy for God."*

And those who lead others astray bear an additional burden: 7:38, 11:19–20, 16:25. The logic is that the same act committed by a person of greater influence causes greater harm and represents a greater betrayal of trust. The teacher who errs misleads his students; the leader who errs misleads his community. The doubling is not arbitrary; it corresponds to the reach of the act.

**"Thou Wouldst Have Found No Helper Against Us"**

*Thumma lā tajidu laka ʿalaynā naṣīrā* — *naṣīr* is a helper, an ally in conflict. The verse states that in such a situation there would be no ally against God. The formula recalls verses 68 and 69's *no guardian* and *no avenger*.

****Double in This Life and Double in Death: The Weight of Station****

The verse states a principle that runs through the Qur'an's treatment of those closest to God: the greater the station, the greater the accountability. *Idhan la-adhaqnāka ḍiʿfa al-ḥayāti wa ḍiʿfa al-mamāt* — then We would have made thee taste double in this life and double in death. The word *ḍiʿf* means a multiple, and the commentators explain the doubling as the punishment of one who had received what no one else received: the revelation, the direct confirmation, and the rank. Al-Qurṭubī connects it to 33:30, addressed to the Prophet's ﷺ wives: *"O wives of the Prophet, whoever of you commits a manifest indecency, the punishment for her will be doubled — and that is easy for God."* The logic is stated most clearly in the same passage of Sūrah al-Aḥzāb, where the doubling is paired with its opposite: *"And whoever of you is obedient to God and His Messenger and does righteousness, We shall give her reward twice over"* (33:31). The multiplication runs in both directions and follows from the same premise — nearness to the source increases the weight of everything done in relation to it. 2:286 states the general rule of proportionality, *"God does not burden a soul beyond its capacity,"* and the verse here is the specific application to the one whose capacity was greatest. The final clause — *thumma lā tajidu laka ʿalaynā naṣīrā* — repeats the formula of verses 68 and 69, where the disbeliever finds no *wakīl* and no *tabīʿ*. Its application to the Prophet ﷺ is not a warning about him but a statement about the structure: no one, at any rank, has a helper against God. The most honored of human beings stands in the same relation to the divine judgment as everyone else, and the honor is precisely what makes the relation weightier. The verse's general application has been drawn by the tradition consistently. The scholar, the judge, the preacher, and the leader carry a doubled accountability, since their error is not merely their own — it is transmitted, imitated, and cited. The Prophetic formulation is at Muslim 1017: *"Whoever sets a good precedent in Islam will have its reward and the reward of all who act upon it after him, without that diminishing their reward in any way; and whoever sets an evil precedent in Islam will bear its burden and the burden of all who act upon it after him, without that diminishing their burden in any way."* The mechanism the verse describes is therefore already familiar in the ethical register: influence multiplies consequences in both directions.

**The Counterfactual as Instruction**

The verse is entirely conditional — it describes what *would* have happened, not what happened. The commentators note that its function is didactic: it teaches the community the gravity of altering the revelation by showing what the cost would have been for the one who was most protected. This is a standard method of Qur'anic instruction: to address the Prophet ﷺ with a warning whose actual referent is the community. 39:65, *"And it was revealed to thee and to those before thee: if thou associatest partners, thy work will surely come to naught, and thou wilt surely be among the losers"* — the Prophet ﷺ never committed shirk, and the warning is for those who might. The method has a specific effect: it removes the excuse of the listener who thinks the standard applies only to others.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:76

> **And they were about to incite thee from the land, in order to expel thee therefrom, whereupon they would not have tarried after thee, save a little—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Plot to Expel**

*Wa in kādū la-yastafizzūnaka mina al-arḍi li-yukhrijūka minhā* — the verb *istafazza* is the same tenth form used of Iblīs's incitement in verse 64: to unsettle, to startle into movement, to drive out. The Quraysh's objective was the Prophet's ﷺ removal from Makkah. Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify the referents as the Quraysh, whose persecution eventually produced the Hijrah. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note the important distinction: the Quraysh did not *forcibly expel* the Prophet ﷺ; he left by divine command. Had they expelled him by force, the immediate punishment would have followed. The plot is described in the Qur'an in a verse that names all three of the options the Quraysh considered:

> *"And [remember] when those who disbelieved plotted against thee, to imprison thee, or to kill thee, or to expel thee. And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (8:30)

**"They Would Not Have Tarried After Thee, Save a Little"**

*Wa idhan lā yalbathūna khilāfaka illā qalīlā* — *khilāfaka* means after thee, behind thee, in thy absence. The verse states that their remaining would have been brief. This is the Qur'an's law of the destroyed peoples: the expulsion or killing of a prophet is followed by the community's annihilation. Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, Lot, and Moses all present the same sequence.

**The Alternative Reading**

Some commentators read *li-yastafizzūnaka* as "to kill thee," in which case the verse refers to the plot on the night of the Hijrah, and *they would not have tarried after thee save a little* means that the Quraysh did not remain in their former power for long — within two years, Badr significantly weakened them. This reading is recorded by al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and others, and it fits the historical outcome.

**The Minority Reading and Its Problem**

A minority view holds that the verse concerns some Jews of Madinah who urged the Prophet ﷺ to leave for Syria, the land of the earlier prophets. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī record this but note the difficulty: the sūrah is Makkan and predates the Madinan period. The objection is textual and is generally accepted.

****The Attempted Expulsion, and the Rule That Would Have Followed It****

The verse records a plot and states the divine practice that governs such plots. *Wa in kādū la-yastafizzūnaka min al-arḍ li-yukhrijūka minhā* — they very nearly provoked thee to leave the land, to expel thee from it. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the reports: the Quraysh convened to decide the Prophet's ﷺ fate and settled on the plan that 8:30 describes — *"And when those who disbelieved plotted against thee to imprison thee, or kill thee, or expel thee; they plot, and God plots, and God is the best of plotters."* The three options in that verse are the historical alternatives, and the third was chosen: the expulsion, which became the Hijrah. The verse's second clause states the *sunnah* that would have applied: *wa idhan lā yalbathūna khilāfaka illā qalīlā*, and then they would not have remained after thee except a little. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as the established practice with the prophets — a people who expel their messenger is destroyed shortly after, and the commentators cite the cases of Ṣāliḥ and Lot. The statement is thus a counterfactual with a known antecedent, and history supplied the confirmation in a form the verse did not predict: the expulsion occurred, the destruction of the expellers did not, and the one expelled returned to the city within eight years. The reason, on the exegetes' account, is the same one given at verse 59 for the withholding of the sign: the reprieve was extended so that most of the expellers might believe, and most of them did. The verse's third clause generalizes the case: *sunnata man qad arsalnā qablaka min rusulinā* — the way of those We sent before thee among Our messengers. The Qur'an treats the history of prophecy as a source of law rather than of anecdote, and the word *sunnah* — practice, established way — is the technical term for it. The following verse makes the constancy explicit, and the pairing produces the Qur'an's philosophy of history: events are not unique but instances, and the instances are governed by rules that can be known. The practical consequence for a reader is that the verse supplies a diagnostic for persecution. A community that expels those who tell it what it does not wish to hear is following a pattern with a known outcome, and the outcome does not depend on the strength of the expellers. 22:40–41 states the same principle from the other side, describing those who were expelled from their homes for saying *our Lord is God*, and promising that God will surely support those who support Him.

**Why the Quraysh Were Spared**

The verse contains an implicit mercy, and the commentators draw it out. The Quraysh plotted, persecuted, and drove the Prophet ﷺ out — but they did not *expel* him in the technical sense, and they did not kill him. Because the specific act that triggers immediate destruction did not occur, the respite continued, and many of them lived to embrace Islam — including Abū Sufyān, Hind, and the very men who had plotted at Dār al-Nadwah. The distinction between what they did and what they nearly did had enormous consequences for the history of Islam. The lesson is that the divine law operates on the act, not on the intention alone — and that the gap between the two is where mercy lives.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:77

> **the wont of those among Our messengers whom We sent before thee. And thou wilt find no change in Our wont.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sunnat Allāh: The Divine Wont**

*Sunnata man qad arsalnā qablaka min rusulinā wa lan tajida li-sunnatinā taḥwīlā* — *sunnah* means a way, a course, an established practice. *Sunnat Allāh* — the wont of God — is a technical Qur'anic term for the consistent manner in which God deals with human communities. It appears at 33:62, 35:43, 40:85, and 48:23, always with the same assertion of invariability. At 48:23: *"The wont of God which has passed before — and thou wilt find no change in the wont of God."* At 35:43:

> *"So do they await aught but the wont of the former peoples? And thou wilt find no change in the wont of God, nor any alteration in the wont of God."*

**The Content of the Wont**

The commentators identify it here as the destruction of communities that expel or kill their messengers. The examples are the peoples of Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, Lot, and Moses — all of whom are named in the Qur'an, and all of whom met the same end. The wont is not arbitrary wrath; it is a rule that has been applied consistently and can therefore be predicted.

**Why the Invariability Matters**

The assertion of invariability has two functions. The first is warning: those who oppose the messenger can look at the record and know the outcome. The second, less obvious, is *reassurance to the messenger*: the pattern means that his suffering is not evidence of abandonment. Every previous messenger was opposed, and every previous opposition ended the same way. The one who knows the wont does not despair in the middle of it. This is why the Qur'an so often addresses the Prophet ﷺ with the histories of the earlier prophets — 11:120, *"And all that We relate to thee of the tidings of the messengers is that whereby We make firm thy heart."* The function of sacred history in the Qur'an is not antiquarian; it is stabilizing.

****Lan Tajida li-Sunnatinā Taḥwīlā: The Constancy of the Divine Way****

The verse is the shortest and the most consequential statement of the Qur'an's philosophy of history. *Sunnata rabbinā* — the way of thy Lord — is described with two negations: no *taḥwīl*, no alteration, and no *tabdīl*, no substitution. The distinction between the two words has been pressed by the commentators. *Taḥwīl* is the transformation of a thing into something else while it remains itself; *tabdīl* is its replacement by another. The verse therefore denies both that the divine practice changes in its operation and that it is superseded by a different practice, and the double denial is exhaustive. The formula appears at four other places in the Qur'an, always in the same sense and always attached to a historical claim. 33:62: *"The way of God with those who passed away before — thou wilt find no change in the way of God."* 35:43: *"...the evil plot encompasses only its makers. Do they await anything but the way of the former peoples? Thou wilt find no change in the way of God, and thou wilt find no alteration in the way of God."* 40:85 uses it of the failure of a faith adopted only at the moment of punishment. 48:23 uses it of the victory granted to the messengers. The consistency of the formula indicates that the Qur'an treats God's dealings with human communities as law-like rather than as a sequence of unrelated interventions. Al-Rāzī draws the epistemological consequence, and it is a strong one: if the divine practice is constant, then the past is evidentially available to the present. This is the ground of the Qur'an's repeated commands to travel and observe — 3:137, *"Say: travel in the earth and see what was the end of those who denied"*; 6:11, 12:109, 22:46, 29:20, 30:42, 40:21, 47:10. The ruins are not decoration but data, and the sūrah made the point concretely at verse 58 with the cities destroyed before their inhabitants' term. The modern reader can state the verse's claim without theological vocabulary: history has regularities, the regularities are moral as well as material, and a society that violates them will experience the consequences regardless of its power. The verse's function in the argument of the sūrah is to close the sequence begun at verse 73. The opponents proposed a modification, then an expulsion; the Qur'an answered that both have been tried before, by others, with known results, and that the results are not negotiable. 21:5 supplies the shortest form of the same point: *"Or has there come to them a reminder which the former peoples did not receive?"* — the answer being that the reminder is the same one they received, and so is the outcome.

**The Concept's Reach**

*Sunnat Allāh* became a foundational concept in Islamic thought precisely because of verses like this one. It asserts that God's dealings with human beings follow a knowable pattern — that moral causation is real, that consequences are consistent, and that history is not a sequence of accidents. Ibn Khaldūn's sociology, with its account of the rise and fall of dynasties according to fixed principles, is explicitly built on this Qur'anic concept. For the ordinary reader the concept is equally useful. It means that the moral order the reader observes — communities that flourish when just and decay when corrupt, individuals who prosper when disciplined and suffer when indulgent — is not a tendency but a law, and laws can be relied upon. The person who acts on the assumption that the pattern will hold for him is acting on knowledge rather than hope.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:78

> **Perform the prayer at the declining of the sun till the darkening of the night. And the recitation at dawn—truly, the recitation at dawn is ever witnessed!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Contains the Five Prayers**

*Aqimi al-ṣalāta li-dulūki al-shamsi ilā ghasaqi al-layli wa qurʾāna al-fajr* — "Establish the prayer from the *dulūk* of the sun until the *ghasaq* of the night, and [establish] the *qurʾān* of the dawn." The commentators read this verse, together with 11:114 and 20:130, as containing the Qur'anic basis of the five daily prayers. The Prophet's ﷺ practice then specified the times and forms precisely.

**Dulūk al-Shams: The Declining of the Sun**

*Dulūk* is from *d-l-k*, to decline, to pass the meridian; the word also carries the sense of rubbing, and some connect it to the rubbing of the eyes at the sun's decline. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Bayḍāwī, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī take it as the moment the sun passes its zenith, which is the time of Ẓuhr. A minority read it as sunset, i.e., Maghrib. If *dulūk* is the zenith, then the span "from the declining of the sun to the darkening of the night" covers four prayers: Ẓuhr, ʿAṣr, Maghrib, and ʿIshāʾ. The dawn prayer is named separately, giving five.

**Ghasaq al-Layl: The Darkening of the Night**

*Ghasaq* is the onset of darkness, the deepening of night. Al-Qurṭubī notes that in some Sunni schools it may mean the disappearance of the sun; the Jaʿfarī school takes it as the disappearance of the sun's rays or the beginning of the middle of the night. This difference underlies the Shīʿite practice of combining the two afternoon prayers (Ẓuhr and ʿAṣr) and the two evening prayers (Maghrib and ʿIshāʾ) — three prayer times rather than five — while Sunnis generally observe five distinct times with permission to combine in specific circumstances such as travel.

**Qurʾān al-Fajr: The Recitation of Dawn**

*Qurʾāna al-fajr* — literally "the recitation of the dawn" — is read by al-Bayḍāwī, al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Qurṭubī as the Fajr prayer, whose defining feature is the lengthened recitation. Some specify that it is the Qur'anic recitation within the prayer that is meant. The choice of the word *qurʾān* rather than *ṣalāh* for this prayer is significant: the dawn prayer is distinguished by its recitation, and the verse names it by its distinguishing feature. The Prophet ﷺ lengthened the recitation in Fajr more than in any other prayer, and the practice is documented in the ḥadīth collections.

**"The Recitation of Dawn Is Ever Witnessed"**

*Inna qurʾāna al-fajri kāna mashhūdā* — *mashhūd* means witnessed, attended. The commentators, citing aḥādīth, explain that the dawn recitation is witnessed by the angels of the night and the angels of the day, who overlap at that hour. Al-Bukhārī (4717) records from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"The angels of the night and the angels of the day gather at the Fajr prayer."* Then Abū Hurayrah said: "Recite, if you wish: *'Truly the recitation at dawn is ever witnessed.'*"

The parallel ḥadīth at al-Bukhārī (1341) and Muslim (632) describes the angels ascending and reporting to God what they found the servants doing. The witnessing has a specific weight: Fajr is the hardest prayer for the one who sleeps, since it requires rising before the day's obligations begin, when no one is watching and no social pressure operates. The verse's assurance that it *is* witnessed addresses exactly that difficulty.

**A Note on Chronology**

The commentators note honestly that this sūrah is Makkan, while the detailed ritual form of the prayers was established through Prophetic practice largely in the Madinan period. The verse gives the times; the Sunnah gives the forms. Some Sufi commentaries, recorded by al-Qushayrī, read the verse additionally as an instruction in continuous inward prayer — the "prayer of the heart," the constant remembrance (*dhikr*) that the Sufi seeks to make uninterrupted.

****Li-Dulūki al-Shams: Legislation at the Heart of the Argument****

The sūrah pauses here, in the middle of its argument with the Quraysh, to legislate the prayer times, and the placement is itself a statement. Al-Ṭabarī records the linguistic dispute over *dulūk*, and the majority position — held by Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿUmar, and most of the jurists — is that it denotes the sun's decline from the meridian, so that the verse's first clause covers the midday and afternoon prayers. The minority reading takes it as the sun's setting, covering the evening prayer. The verse's remaining clauses cover the rest: *ghasaqi al-layl*, the darkening of the night, gives the night prayer, and *qurʾāna al-fajr* gives the dawn prayer. The verse is therefore one of the principal scriptural grounds of the five daily prayers, and the jurists read it together with its parallels: 11:114, *"And establish the prayer at the two ends of the day and in the hours of the night"*; 20:130, *"...and glorify thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting, and in the hours of the night"*; and 30:17–18, which enumerates the times explicitly — evening and morning, and in the afternoon and at noon. The Prophetic elaboration is the ḥadīth of the two times for each prayer, recorded by Muslim (612), in which Gabriel leads the Prophet ﷺ through each prayer at its earliest and its latest time across two consecutive days and says: *"The time is between these two."* The phrase *qurʾāna al-fajr* is read by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr as denoting the dawn prayer itself, the recitation being named for the prayer as a whole by way of its most distinguishing element. The clause *inna qurʾāna al-fajri kāna mashhūdā* — the recitation of the dawn is witnessed — is explained by the commentators with reference to the reports that the angels of the night and the angels of the day attend it together, which is why the congregation at that hour is described as attended by witnesses unseen. 50:40 and 52:49 use the same device of naming the prayer by its recitation. The structural point is what makes the verse matter here. The sūrah has just argued that the opponents' proposals are impossible, that the messenger cannot be modified or expelled without consequence, and that the divine practice is unchanging; and then it turns to the messenger and tells him when to pray. The sequence asserts that the response to opposition is not counter-argument but worship, and 20:130 makes the connection explicit: *"And be patient over what they say, and glorify thy Lord with the praise of thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting."* The legislation is placed inside the polemic because the polemic is not the point — the establishment of worship is.

**Why Prayer Is Timed at All**

The verse's structure — prayer indexed to celestial motion — is not arbitrary. A devotion tied to the sun's position cannot be postponed indefinitely, cannot be performed only when convenient, and cannot be compressed into a single weekly observance. It interrupts the day five times, which means it interrupts the day's preoccupations five times. The verse that follows will add the night, so that no portion of the twenty-four hours is without its claim.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:79

> **And keep vigil in prayer for part of the night, as a supererogatory act for thee. It may be that thy Lord will resurrect thee in a praiseworthy station.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Night Vigil**

*Wa mina al-layli fa-tahajjad bihi nāfilatan laka* — "and from the night, keep vigil with it [the Qur'an] as a *nāfilah* for thee." The verb *tahajjada* means to keep vigil, to stay awake for prayer — from *h-j-d*, which in one of its Arabic senses denotes sleeping and in its tenth form denotes the casting off of sleep. The *tahajjud* is therefore prayer performed after having slept, in the latter part of the night, as al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī specify.

**"A Supererogatory Act for Thee"**

*Nāfilatan laka* — *nāfilah* is an addition, a supererogatory act beyond what is obligatory. The commentators note the apparent difficulty: the night prayer was *obligatory* for the Prophet ﷺ according to most, on the basis of 73:1–4, *"O thou enwrapped in thy robes, keep vigil in the night, save a little — half of it, or diminish from it a little, or add to it — and recite the Qur'an in measured tones."* Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī resolve it in two ways: the term is figurative for the Prophet ﷺ, or it indicates that the night prayer is supererogatory for all other Muslims, which is the established ruling (73:20 relaxes the obligation for the community). The verse's *laka* — "for thee" — is read as singling out the Prophet's ﷺ specific relationship to the practice.

**The Praiseworthy Station**

*ʿAsā an yabʿathaka rabbuka maqāman maḥmūdā* — "it may be that thy Lord will raise thee to a *maqām maḥmūd*." The commentators identify the Praiseworthy Station with the Prophet's ﷺ intercession on the Day of Judgment, and with his being seated near the Throne bearing the Banner of Praise (*liwāʾ al-ḥamd*). Al-Bukhārī (4718) records the ḥadīth of intercession in which, on the Day of Judgment, humanity goes from prophet to prophet seeking relief, each saying "I am not for this," until they come to Muhammad ﷺ, who says:

> *"I am for it. Then I will go and prostrate before my Lord, and He will open for me such praises of Him as He has not opened for anyone before me."* (al-Bukhārī 4718; Muslim 194)

This is the *maqām maḥmūd* — the station of praise — and the tradition connects it to the verse explicitly.

**The Connection Between the Vigil and the Station**

The verse links the night prayer to the exaltation with *ʿasā* — "it may be" — which, as the commentators note at verse 8, indicates near-certainty when used by God. The connection is not mechanical but formative: the one who stands before God in the darkness, alone, without witness, is prepared for the station before God in the light, before all creation. The private discipline produces the public rank. Al-Qurṭubī states the general principle that all supererogatory acts exist for the expansion, joy, and ennoblement of the soul, and that the night prayer is of particular benefit because it is performed in complete solitude with God.

**The Ḥadīth Literature on the Night Prayer**

The practice is among the most heavily attested in the tradition. Al-Bukhārī (1121) and Muslim (758) record:

> *"The best prayer after the obligatory prayers is the prayer in the middle of the night."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1163)

And al-Tirmidhī (3549) records:

> *"Hold to the night prayer, for it was the practice of the righteous before you, and it is a means of nearness to your Lord, an expiation for wrong deeds, a prevention from sin, and a driving away of disease from the body."* (graded ḥasan)

****Al-Maqām al-Maḥmūd, and the Night Prayer as Its Means****

The verse commands the night prayer and promises a station in the same breath, and the connection between the two is the verse's substance. *Wa min al-layli fa-tahajjad bihi nāfilatan laka* — and part of the night, keep vigil with it as an addition for thee. The verb *tahajjad* is from *h-j-d*, which denotes both sleeping and waking at night, and the form used here means to shake off sleep for the sake of prayer — the commentators note that the word contains the effort. *Nāfilah* is an addition, a supererogatory act beyond the obligatory, and al-Qurṭubī records the view that the night prayer was obligatory specifically for the Prophet ﷺ and supererogatory for the community, on the strength of the phrase *laka*, for thee. The promise is *maqāman maḥmūdā*, a praiseworthy station, and the identification of this station with the intercession on the Day of Resurrection is established in the collections. Al-Bukhārī (4718) records the ḥadīth of the gathering:

> *"The people will fall to their knees on the Day of Resurrection, and every nation will follow its prophet saying: O so-and-so, intercede for us — until the intercession comes to me. That is the day on which God will raise me to the Praiseworthy Station."* (al-Bukhārī 4718)

Muslim (194) preserves the longer form in which the people go first to Adam, then to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, each of whom declines and refers them onward, until they come to the Prophet ﷺ who says: *"I am for it, I am for it."* The word *maḥmūd* is from the same root as the name Muḥammad, and the commentators note the correspondence: the one named "the Praised" is raised to "the Praiseworthy Station." The verse's pairing of the means and the end is the part with continuing application. The station promised is not obtained by claim but by the vigil, and the Qur'an states the same relation at 73:6–7: *"Indeed, rising by night is more firm in tread and more upright in speech. Indeed, thou hast by day a long occupation."* The night prayer is described there as the condition of steadiness, and the reasoning is given: the day is full of demands, and the one who has no hour in which he is alone with God has no ground from which to meet them. The verse therefore supplies both a discipline and its rationale. The discipline is an hour of the night given to recitation; the rationale is that the capacity to stand under opposition — the subject of verses 73 to 77 — is produced in that hour and nowhere else. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the report that the night prayer was obligatory on the early community for a year until 73:20 abrogated its obligation, and that the abrogation left it as the highest of the voluntary acts, which is its standing in the tradition to this day.

**The Psychological Function**

The verse's placement after the command to the five prayers is instructive. The five prayers structure the day around its natural divisions — noon, afternoon, sunset, night, dawn. The night vigil adds a prayer that has no place in the day's structure at all: it belongs to the hours when nothing else happens, when no one expects anything, when the person is alone with the fact of his existence. This is why the tradition treats it as the most reliable indicator of sincerity. The five prayers can be performed from habit and social expectation. The night prayer cannot: it costs sleep, it has no audience, and no one will know. The verse attaches to it the highest promise in the sūrah, which is a claim about what private, unobserved devotion is worth.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:80

> **And say, "My Lord! Make me enter in a sincere manner, and make me go forth in a sincere manner, and ordain for me, from Thy Presence, an authority to help me."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Taught Prayer for Every Threshold**

*Wa qul rabbi adkhilnī mudkhala ṣidqin wa akhrijnī mukhraja ṣidqin wa-jʿal lī min ladunka sulṭānan naṣīrā* — the verse supplies a duʿāʾ in the imperative: *say*. The Qur'an frequently teaches the Prophet ﷺ specific words, and the practice of learning Qur'anic supplications verbatim derives from verses of this kind.

**Mudkhal Ṣidq and Makhraj Ṣidq**

*Mudkhal ṣidq* — "an entering of truth"; *makhraj ṣidq* — "a going forth of truth." The construction is the Arabic idiom in which a noun of place is qualified by an abstract quality: an entering characterized by truthfulness, sincerity, and rightness. Al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī read the verse in the context of the Hijrah: the entering is into Madinah, the going forth from Makkah. The connection to verses 76–77 is explicit — the Quraysh were about to expel him, and God commanded the migration before they could carry out the expulsion, thereby averting their destruction. Al-Ṭabarī and others record a second, more universal reading: the entering is into the grave, and the going forth from it at the Resurrection. A third extends it to every situation the Prophet ﷺ entered or left, including his prophetic mission. The three readings are not in competition; the duʿāʾ has been recited for all three purposes across the tradition. Its general form — *make my entering true and my leaving true* — is applicable to any threshold: a house, a job, a marriage, a country, a life.

**Why Truth Is Asked for the Entering and the Leaving**

The request is not for safety, wealth, or success but for *ṣidq* — truth, sincerity, rightness. The commentators explain the reasoning: an entering may be corrupted by a false motive (entering a position for status rather than service), and a leaving may be corrupted by a false manner (leaving with resentment, or leaving a duty undone). The duʿāʾ asks that both transitions be internally sound. This is a prayer of unusual sophistication. Most people pray for favorable outcomes; this prayer asks that whatever the outcome, the *character* of the transition be truthful. The one who enters a position sincerely and leaves it sincerely has not lost anything regardless of what happened in between.

**"An Authority to Help Me, From Thy Presence"**

*Wa-jʿal lī min ladunka sulṭānan naṣīrā* — *sulṭān* is authority, warrant, dominion; *naṣīr* is one who gives victory. The phrase *min ladunka* — "from Thy presence" — is significant: the authority sought is not acquired by maneuvering but granted from God's own side. Compare 18:10, the prayer of the Companions of the Cave:

> *"Our Lord, grant us mercy from Thy presence (min ladunnā) and prepare for us right guidance in our affair."*

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Jaṣṣāṣ read the request as being for the authority to establish the religion and to overcome its opponents — a prayer answered at Madinah, where the Prophet ﷺ acquired the social and political capacity to build a community on Qur'anic principles.

****Mudkhal Ṣidq and Mukhraj Ṣidq: The Prayer for Honest Entrances****

The supplication the verse teaches is among the most used in the Islamic devotional tradition, and its two halves cover the whole of a person's movement through the world: *wa qul rabbi adkhilnī mudkhala ṣidqin wa akhrijnī mukhraja ṣidqin* — my Lord, make me enter an entrance of truth and make me leave an exit of truth. The attachment of *ṣidq* to the entrance and the exit rather than to the person is the grammatical feature that the commentators press: what is requested is not merely that the one entering be truthful but that the entering itself be true — legitimate in its grounds, honorable in its manner, and sound in its end. Al-Ṭabarī catalogues the interpretations and does not exclude any of them. The first: the entrance into Madinah at the Hijrah and the exit from Makkah, which is the reading connected to the occasion of the verse and is supported by 2:286's pairing and by the sūrah's preceding verses about expulsion. The second: the entrance into the grave and the exit from it at the resurrection. The third: the entrance into every affair and the exit from it — a general reading that al-Qurṭubī favors, since it makes the supplication applicable to every day. The fourth: the entrance upon the truth in belief and the exit from the world upon it. The four are not competing but nested, and the general reading contains the others. The ḥadīth literature supplies the practical use. Al-Tirmidhī (3406) records the Prophet's ﷺ form of the same request at the point of entering a place:

> *"Whoever enters a place and says: 'I seek refuge in the perfect words of God from the evil of what He created' — nothing will harm him until he leaves that place."* (Muslim 2708)

The pairing of the two supplications indicates the intention: the entrance is a real exposure, and the exit a real deliverance. The verse's placement after the command to the night prayer is significant. The vigil produces the steadiness, and the steadiness is exercised in the entrances and exits of the day — the meeting, the office, the household, the conversation, the decision. Al-Qurṭubī draws the ethical content of *ṣidq* in this position: an entrance of truth is one whose grounds one would be willing to state aloud, and an exit of truth is one that leaves nothing behind that one would wish to conceal. The supplication is therefore a request for consistency between the private and the public, which is the same consistency the night prayer trains. The closing clause — *wa jʿal lī min ladunka sulṭānan naṣīrā* — asks for an assisting authority from God's own presence. The commentators note the word *ladun*, which denotes proximity, and *sulṭān*, which denotes a proof or an authority that compels assent. The request is not for power over people but for the authority of truth supported by God, which is the only authority that survives the entrances and exits the verse has just described.

**The Practical Use of the Verse**

The duʿāʾ has entered Muslim practice as the prayer recited at departures and arrivals. Its logic is available to anyone facing a transition: the outcome is largely outside one's control, but the manner of the entering and the leaving is not. Asking God for a true entry and a true exit is asking for the one thing that, if granted, makes every outcome bearable.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:81

> **Say, "Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Recited at the Fall of the Idols**

*Wa qul jāʾa al-ḥaqqu wa zahaqa al-bāṭil* — "and say: Truth has come, and falsehood has perished." Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record that the Prophet ﷺ recited this verse continuously as he struck the idols around the Kaʿbah on the day Makkah was opened in 8/630. Al-Bukhārī (4287) records the scene: the Prophet ﷺ entered the sanctuary, and there were three hundred and sixty idols; he began striking them with a stick in his hand, saying:

> *"Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever vanishing." And: "Say: Truth has come, and falsehood neither originates nor returns."* (34:49)

The verse is therefore not only a proposition but a record of an event — and the event is the moment the verse was enacted.

**Zahaqa: The Verb of Perishing**

*Zahaqa* means to perish, to be annihilated, to expire — it is used of a soul departing a body. The Qur'an uses it of the destruction of Pharaoh's people at 21:95 and of the perishing of the disbelievers at 8:42. The choice of verb is exact: falsehood does not merely retreat; it *dies*. Its existence was never substantive.

**"Truly Falsehood Is Ever Vanishing"**

*Inna al-bāṭila kāna zahūqā* — *zahūq* is the intensive form, denoting a thing characterized by perishing. The word *kāna* makes it a permanent description, not an event. Falsehood is *by nature* perishable; perishing is not something that happens to it but what it is. The metaphysical claim behind the verse is developed by al-Rāzī and later by the Sufi commentators: *al-Ḥaqq* — the Truth, the Real — is one of the divine Names (22:6, 62; 20:114). Truth is Being; falsehood is the absence or distortion of Being. Whatever is contingent is in the process of vanishing; only the Necessary is permanent. Falsehood therefore has no ontological standing to compete with truth; it is a shadow, and a shadow does not defeat the light that casts it. Compare 21:18: *"Nay, but We hurl the Truth against falsehood, and it crushes its brain; and behold, it vanishes."* The image is violent and precise: truth does not argue falsehood into submission; it *strikes* it, and the striking is fatal because falsehood has no substance to absorb the blow.

**The Verse's Logical Claim**

The verse makes a claim that requires care. It does not say that falsehood never prevails temporarily — the Qur'an documents its temporary prevalence extensively, including the thirteen years of Makkan persecution during which the Prophet ﷺ and his followers were the weaker party. It says that falsehood is *ever vanishing* — that its nature is impermanent, so that its victories are always temporary and its defeats are final. This is a claim about the structure of reality rather than about any particular moment, and it is the ground of the Qur'anic attitude toward history. The one who believes falsehood is perishable by nature can endure its temporary dominance without concluding that it is real. The one who does not believe this will make terms with it, since he expects it to last.

****Truth Cast and Falsehood Vanquished: A Physics Rather Than a Battle****

The verse states its claim in two verbs, and the choice of the second is what makes the verse remarkable. *Wa qul jāʾa al-ḥaqqu wa zahaqa al-bāṭil* — say: the truth has come and falsehood has vanished. The verb *zahaqa* does not mean merely to depart; it denotes perishing, being extinguished, ceasing to have substance — al-Zamakhsharī distinguishes it from *dhahaba*, to go, precisely because it carries destruction rather than movement. Falsehood does not retreat before the truth; it is undone by it. The Qur'an supplies the mechanism at 21:18: *"Nay, We hurl the truth upon falsehood and it shatters its brain (fa-yadmaghuhu), and behold, it perishes."* The image is not of two combatants but of a solid striking a hollow thing. The reason is ontological rather than rhetorical: truth has being, falsehood does not. Al-Rāzī develops the point — falsehood is parasitic on truth, since every false claim borrows its plausibility from some element of the real, and the exposure of the borrowed element is what destroys it. This is why falsehood requires maintenance and truth does not; a lie must be defended, patched, and remembered, while a fact requires only not being forgotten. The historical fulfillment was recorded within the Prophet's ﷺ lifetime. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note that the verse was revealed in Makkah and that its fulfillment came at the conquest, when the Prophet ﷺ entered the Kaʿbah and struck the idols with a stick while reciting it, the idols falling as he passed them. The report is preserved in al-Bukhārī (4287) in connection with the entry into Makkah. The interval between the statement and its fulfillment — roughly eight years, through exile, defeat at Uḥud, and the siege of the Trench — is the verse's practical content: the truth's victory was announced while the truth looked weakest, and the announcement is what sustained the community through the interval. The second clause generalizes the claim: *inna al-bāṭila kāna zahūqā* — falsehood is by nature vanishing. The intensive form *zahūq* makes perishing a property rather than an event, and 34:49 states the same: *"The truth has come, and falsehood originates nothing and brings nothing back."* The denial of origination and of return is a complete negation: falsehood cannot begin a thing and cannot restore one. The verse's application does not require a battlefield. Every person holds false beliefs that persist because they have not been examined rather than because they have been defended, and the verse describes what happens when the truth arrives: not a debate but a collapse. This is why the Qur'an's method throughout is not argument alone but exposure — the presentation of the fact in a form the hearer cannot hold alongside his error.

**The Verse as a Political and Intellectual Formula**

The commentators note that this is among the most quoted verses in the Islamic tradition, cited in both political and intellectual contexts — in declarations of the triumph of Islam, in refutations of false doctrine, and in moments of collective crisis. Its use is not a claim that victory has already occurred but an assertion about the direction of things: whatever the present appearance, this is where the movement is going.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:82

> **And We send down of the Quran that which is a cure and a mercy for the believers. And it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shifāʾ: The Qur'an as Cure**

*Wa nunazzilu mina al-qurʾāni mā huwa shifāʾun wa raḥmatun li-l-muʾminīn* — "and We send down of the Qur'an that which is a *shifāʾ* and a mercy for the believers." The word *shifāʾ* means healing, cure, remedy. This is the only verse in the Qur'an that describes the revelation explicitly as a cure, though 10:57 implies it:

> *"O mankind, there has come to you an exhortation from your Lord, and a cure (shifāʾ) for what is in the breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers."*

The commentators identify the diseases cured. Al-Rāzī and the Sufi exegetes list them: ignorance, doubt, hypocrisy, rancor, hatred, and enmity. These are diseases of the heart in the Qur'anic sense — not emotions but settled conditions of the soul, and each is a genuine affliction that its sufferer recognizes. Al-Rāzī records the saying: *"Whosoever does not seek a cure from the Qur'an, God will not cure him."* The statement is transmitted as a report rather than as an authenticated ḥadīth, and the commentators cite it as such; its force lies in its meaning — that the cure requires the patient's cooperation.

**Why the Qur'an Is Called a Mercy as Well as a Cure**

The two terms are distinct and both are needed. A cure removes a disease; a mercy bestows a good. Al-Rāzī explains that the Qur'an is a mercy insofar as it produces virtue, perfection of character, wisdom, and knowledge — positive attainments, not merely the absence of ailment. A person could be cured of hatred without thereby acquiring love; the mercy supplies the love.

**"It Increases the Wrongdoers in Naught but Loss"**

*Wa lā yazīdu al-ẓālimīna illā khasārā* — the same medicine produces the opposite effect in a different constitution. The verse states this without explanation, and the mechanism is supplied elsewhere in the Qur'an: 2:26, 9:124–25, 5:64, 17:41, 25:60, 35:42, 71:6. The mechanism is not arbitrary. A cure works on an organism that accepts it; the same agent applied to a hostile organism aggravates the condition. The person who approaches the Qur'an with the intention of finding fault does not receive its content as content; he receives it as material for his existing project, and the project is strengthened. This is a claim about the *reader* as much as about the text — and it explains a phenomenon visible in every age: the same book that transforms one person confirms another in his opposition.

**The Verse's Requirement of the Reader**

The verse specifies *the believers* as those for whom it is cure and mercy, which means the effect is conditional. The condition is not intellectual capacity but *receptivity* — the disposition to be changed rather than to evaluate. The Qur'an states the requirement at 50:37: *"Truly in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart, or gives ear while he is witnessing."* The phrase *or gives ear while witnessing* — that is, listening with full presence — identifies the posture in which the cure operates.

****What Descends as a Healing, and the Loss It Adds****

The verse is the Qur'an's own statement of its function, and its two clauses describe opposite effects from the same source. *Wa nunazzilu min al-Qurʾāni mā huwa shifāʾun wa raḥmatun li-l-muʾminīn* — and We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers. The particle *min* has been read in two ways, and al-Ṭabarī holds both: as partitive, indicating that some of what is revealed is healing, and as explicative, indicating that the Qur'an as a whole is healing — the second being the reading favored by al-Qurṭubī, on the ground that the Qur'an describes itself entirely in these terms at 10:57, *"O mankind, there has come to you an admonition from your Lord, and a healing for what is in the breasts."* The two senses of *shifāʾ* are both attested in the tradition. The primary sense, and the one the commentators give first, is the healing of the diseases of the heart — doubt, hypocrisy, ignorance, and disordered desire. Al-Rāzī explains the mechanism: the diseases of the soul are false beliefs and disordered loves, and the Qur'an addresses the first by argument and the second by re-ordering the objects of hope and fear. The secondary sense is physical healing, and the jurists built on it the legislation of *ruqyah*, recitation over the sick. Al-Bukhārī (5736) records the case of the companion who recited al-Fātiḥah over a tribal chief stung by a scorpion and was given a flock of sheep for it, and the Prophet ﷺ approved the practice and asked for his share. 16:69 uses the same word of honey, *"in which there is healing for mankind,"* which establishes that *shifāʾ* is used in the revelation of bodily cure as well as of spiritual. The second clause is the harder and more instructive: *wa lā yazīdu al-ẓālimīna illā khasārā* — and it increases the wrongdoers only in loss. The same text produces healing in one and loss in another, and the difference is not in the text. Al-Qurṭubī explains the mechanism: the revelation obligates, and the one who is obligated and does not comply has added to his account; the one who complies has been healed. The Qur'an states the same phenomenon at 9:124–125, where a sūrah revealed increases the believers in faith and increases the one in whose heart is a disease in filth. The parallel with verse 60 of this sūrah — the tree of Zaqqūm made a trial — is exact: the same datum, two opposite effects, and the effect determined by the receiver. The verse therefore supplies a warning to the reader about his own reading. Exposure to the revelation is not neutral; it either heals or indicts, and the variable is not the amount heard but the response made. 41:44 states the point with a comparison: had the Qur'an been made a foreign tongue, they would have said, *"Why are its verses not explained in detail? What — a foreign tongue and an Arab?"* The objection was never about comprehension.

**Reading as Cure or as Material**

The verse's division is visible in contemporary reading. Two people read the same passage: one is asking "what does this require of me?" and the other is asking "what can I say about this?" The first may be changed; the second will not be. Neither is unintelligent, and the difference is not in the text. The verse names the difference and locates it in the reader, which is both a warning and an invitation: the cure is available, and its availability depends on a posture one can adopt.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:83

> **And whenever We bestow a blessing upon man, he turns away and withdraws. And whenever evil befalls him, he is in despair.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Failures in One Verse**

*Wa idhā anʿamnā ʿalā al-insāni aʿraḍa wa naʾā bi-jānibih* — "and when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws off at his side." The two verbs describe the same movement: *aʿraḍa* is to turn the face away; *naʾā bi-jānibih* is to turn the side — the posture of one who withdraws his body from a conversation. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī explain the sequence: the blessing produces not gratitude but withdrawal from the Giver. The person who has what he wants no longer needs to attend to the One who gave it. Compare 41:51, *"And when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws off; and when evil touches him, he is full of long supplication"*; 11:9–10; 39:49; 41:50.

**"And Whenever Evil Befalls Him, He Is in Despair"**

*Wa idhā massahu al-sharru kāna yaʾūsā* — *yaʾūs* is the intensive form of one who despairs, from *y-ʾ-s*. The verse pairs the two failures precisely: blessing produces withdrawal, and harm produces despair. Neither is the appropriate response. The Qur'an's diagnosis of the two errors identifies a single underlying defect: the person relates to circumstances rather than to God. When circumstances are good, he forgets the source; when they are bad, he forgets the source's power to change them. In both cases God is absent from the equation, which is why the responses are wrong in the same way.

**The Prescribed Responses**

The Qur'an supplies the alternatives. For blessing: gratitude — 16:53, *"And whatever blessing you have is from God"*; 14:7:

> *"If you are grateful, I will surely increase you."*

For harm: patience — 2:155–157, *"And give glad tidings to the patient, who, when affliction strikes them, say: 'Truly we are God's, and unto Him we return'"*; 31:17; 22:35. The two prescribed responses are symmetrical and equally demanding. Gratitude in prosperity requires acknowledging dependence at the moment one feels independent; patience in adversity requires maintaining hope at the moment one feels abandoned. Both require holding God present against the evidence of the immediate circumstance, which is why the Qur'an legislates *remembrance* as a practice — the fixed prayers, the daily dhikr — rather than relying on feeling.

**The Verse as Psychology**

The description is exact enough to be testable. Observe a person in sudden prosperity: the first thing to change is usually his availability — he becomes harder to reach, less present to those he needed when he had nothing. Observe a person in sudden adversity: the first thing to change is usually his horizon — the future contracts to the size of the present problem. Both movements are involuntary, which is why they require a counter-practice rather than a resolution.

****Despair and Elation: The Two Modes of an Unstable Soul****

The verse describes a psychological pattern rather than a moral failing, and its precision is what makes it useful. *Wa idhā anʿamnā ʿalā al-insān aʿraḍa wa naʾā bi-jānibih* — and when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws aside. The verb *aʿraḍa* is the same one used at verse 67 of the man who turns away after being rescued from the sea, and *naʾā bi-jānibih* — he draws aside with his side — describes the physical gesture of withdrawal, the turning of the shoulder that expresses disengagement. The second mode follows: *wa idhā massahu al-sharru kāna yaʾūsā*, and when evil touches him he is despairing. The two modes are the only two the verse describes, and their pairing is the diagnosis. In favor, the human being is disengaged; in adversity, he is without hope. Neither state is the correct one: the first is ingratitude and the second is despair, and both are failures of the same faculty, which is the capacity to hold a stable relation to God across changing circumstances. 41:51 supplies the fuller version and adds a third detail of clinical accuracy: *"And when We bestow favor upon man, he turns away and draws aside; but when evil touches him, he is full of extensive supplication (fa-dhū duʿāʾin ʿarīḍ)."* The word *ʿarīḍ* — broad, extensive — describes the length and elaborateness of the prayer made in distress, which contrasts sharply with the silence of the prosperous hour. 11:9–10 gives the two character-words that name the modes: in favor after adversity, *la-fariḥun fakhūr*, exultant and boastful; in adversity after favor, *la-yaʾūsanna kafūr*, despairing and ungrateful. Al-Rāzī draws the theological inference: the pattern shows that the untrained soul has no fixed relation to God but a reactive one, determined entirely by circumstance. The person in this condition is not irreligious so much as unstable, and the instability is invisible to him because each mode feels appropriate at the time — the prosperity feels earned and the adversity feels total. The Qur'an's remedy is the discipline of remembrance, and 13:28 states it with the diagnosis attached: *"Those who believe, and whose hearts find rest in the remembrance of God — truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* The verse's practical use is as a self-test with two questions, and both are answerable in a moment. In the last week of ease, did you pray? In the last week of difficulty, did you hope?
