The one who answers no to the first and no to the second has identified precisely the condition the verse describes, and the identification is the beginning of its correction — which is why the verse is addressed to the reader in the second person rather than describing an abstract type.

**Its Place in the Sūrah**

The verse follows the description of the Qur'an as cure and mercy. The diseases it names — withdrawal in prosperity and despair in adversity — are precisely among the conditions the cure addresses. The sequence is: here is the medicine (v. 82); here are two of the illnesses (v. 83). And verse 84 will supply the general principle that governs both.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:84

> **Say, "Each acts according to his disposition, and your Lord knows well who is more rightly guided on the way."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shākilah: The Formed Disposition**

*Qul kullun yaʿmalu ʿalā shākilatih* — "Say: Everyone acts according to his *shākilah*." The word is from *sh-k-l*, meaning form, manner, resemblance; *shākilah* denotes one's formed character, the distinctive way in which a person is constituted. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī read it as one's inward nature or inclination; al-Rāzī as one's spiritual path; others as one's religion and accepted doctrines. Al-Qurṭubī adds that it may mean one's intentions. The commentators converge on a meaning: a person's action flows from his formed interior. He does not perform discrete acts in isolation; he acts out of a character that has been formed by his beliefs, habits, and choices, and the acts are its expression.

**Why the Verse Is Both a Description and a Warning**

In its historical context the verse was a statement to the disbelievers of Makkah, comparable to 11:121:

> *"And say to those who believe not: Act according to your position; we too are acting."*

It carries the tone of a final separation: go your way; we go ours; the outcome will disclose which was right. But the verse also states a general truth with a practical edge. If each acts according to his formed disposition, then the disposition is what must be attended to. A person who tries to change his behavior without changing his formation will fail, because the behavior is the symptom. This is why the Qur'an's program is directed at the heart rather than at the act list — and why verse 82 described the revelation as a *cure* rather than a code.

**"Your Lord Knows Well Who Is More Rightly Guided"**

*Wa rabbukum aʿlamu bi-man huwa ahdā sabīlā* — the verse ends by locating the judgment with God. The comparative *ahdā* — more rightly guided — implies that guidance admits of degrees and that the ranking is not visible from outside. This has two effects. The first is humility: the person cannot know his own rank relative to another's, and the one who claims to know has exceeded his knowledge. The second is seriousness: the ranking exists, and it is known, even though it is not published. The verse therefore forbids both self-satisfaction and despair about one's standing, and replaces both with effort.

****Shākilah: Acting From Disposition, and the Knowledge That Follows****

The command *qul* introduces a general statement about human action, and the word at its center has been read three ways. *Kullun yaʿmalu ʿalā shākilatih* — everyone acts according to his *shākilah*. Al-Ṭabarī records the glosses: *ʿalā ṭarīqatih wa jānibih*, upon his way and his side; *ʿalā dīnih*, upon his religion; and *ʿalā niyyatih*, upon his intention. The root *sh-k-l* denotes form, manner, and resemblance, and the word carries the sense of the shape a thing takes — hence the commentators' consensus that it denotes the disposition from which a person's acts proceed, whether that disposition is a creed, a character, or a settled intention. The verse therefore states a claim about predictability. A person's actions are not a series of unrelated events but the expression of a structure, and the structure can be known. This is why the following clause draws the conclusion it does: *fa-rabbukum aʿlamu bi-man huwa ahdā sabīlā* — your Lord knows best who is best guided in the way. The divine knowledge is stated in the comparative — *aʿlam*, *ahdā* — which is the same comparative construction used at verse 57 of the beings seeking *ayyuhum aqrab*, which of them is nearer. The pairing is deliberate: the human being judges by the act, God judges by the disposition that produced it, and the one who knows the disposition knows the acts before they occur. Al-Rāzī develops the ethical consequence, and it runs in two directions. The first is about others: since acts flow from dispositions, an act performed by a person of settled virtue is more creditable than the same act performed by one who stumbled into it, and the Qur'an weights the two differently. The second is about the self: since your acts reveal your disposition, you can read your own character from what you habitually do rather than from what you intend. The Prophetic formulation of the same principle is at al-Bukhārī (52) and Muslim (1907):

> *"Actions are by intentions, and every person has only what he intended. So whoever's migration was to God and His Messenger, his migration was to God and His Messenger; and whoever's migration was for a worldly thing or a woman he would marry, his migration was to that for which he migrated."* (al-Bukhārī 1)

The verse's placement after the discussion of the Qur'an as a healing is what gives it its function. The revelation is described as producing opposite effects in different receivers, and the verse explains why: each receiver acts from his *shākilah*, so the same text meets a different structure in each. The practical conclusion is the one the sūrah has been building toward — that the work is on the disposition rather than on the individual act, since the disposition is what will produce the acts long after the decision has been forgotten.

**The Verse's Place After Verse 83**

Verse 83 described two failure modes; verse 84 explains their source. The man who withdraws in prosperity and despairs in adversity is not making isolated errors; he is expressing a *shākilah* — a formed disposition in which circumstances, rather than God, are the reference point. Changing the response requires changing the formation, and the formation is changed by what the sūrah has just prescribed: the Qur'an received as a cure, the prayers established at their times, the vigil kept in the night.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:85

> **They ask thee about the Spirit. Say, "The Spirit is from the Command of my Lord, and you have not been given knowledge, save a little."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question and the Answer's Brevity**

*Wa yasʾalūnaka ʿani al-rūḥ* — "and they ask thee about the Spirit." The Qur'an records several occasions on which the Prophet ﷺ was asked about something and the answer given was shorter than the question: about the Hour (7:187), about the mountains (20:105), about the Dhu al-Qarnayn (18:83). This is one of them, and it is the most cited. *Quli al-rūḥu min amri rabbī* — "Say: the Spirit is of the command of my Lord." The answer does not describe; it locates. The Spirit belongs to the *amr* — the domain of divine command — and the verse then closes the subject: *wa mā ūtītum mina al-ʿilmi illā qalīlā*, "and you have not been given of knowledge except a little."

**Why the Question Was Asked**

Al-Ṭabarī and others record that the question came from a group of Madinan Jews or Makkan idolaters, and some reports indicate they posed it as a test: if he answers, he is not a prophet; if he refuses, he is. The answer given — a refusal to describe accompanied by a statement about the limits of human knowledge — is reported to have satisfied neither party's expectation and to have been recognized by some as itself the mark of prophethood, since a fabricator would have produced a description.

**What Rūḥ Means**

The commentators preserve several readings. The *life-principle*: al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Ṭabarī and others read it as the spirit that is the source of human life, which God breathed into Adam (32:9, *"and fashioned him and breathed into him of His Spirit"*; 38:72). On this reading the Spirit is the source of human life, knowledge, perception, and spiritual capacity — and therefore of moral obligation, since only a being with these capacities can be obligated. The *Archangel Gabriel*: al-Zamakhsharī records this, supported by 16:102, *"Say: the Holy Spirit (rūḥ al-qudus) has brought it down from thy Lord in truth,"* and 2:87 and 2:253, where Jesus is strengthened by the Holy Spirit. The *Qur'an*: another recorded opinion, on the basis of 40:15:

> *"He casts the Spirit of His command upon whom He wills of His servants."*

Al-Ṭabarī records from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib a tradition describing the Spirit as an angel with seventy thousand heads, each with seventy thousand tongues praising God — a tradition of the kind the exegetes transmit without endorsing as definitive.

**ʿĀlam al-Amr: The Realm of Command**

The verse became the scriptural basis for a distinction that shaped much of Islamic philosophy and Sufism: the division between *ʿālam al-khalq* — the realm of creation, which unfolds through time, causes, and measurable processes — and *ʿālam al-amr*, the realm of command, which is immediate and beyond such processes. The verse's phrase *min amri rabbī* is the anchor, and it is joined to 42:52: *"And thus We revealed to thee a Spirit of Our command"*; 16:2; 97:4, *"The angels and the Spirit descend therein by the leave of their Lord, with every command."* The distinction has a specific function: it marks the boundary of what can be investigated. The realm of creation is open to examination; the realm of command is not, because its mode is not that of created causation.

**"You Have Not Been Given of Knowledge Except a Little"**

This clause is one of the Qur'an's most important epistemological statements. It is not a discouragement from seeking knowledge — the Qur'an commands it repeatedly — but a calibration of what has been attained. The knowledge given to human beings is *qalīl*: little. Not worthless, not false, but limited in scope, and limited in a way that is not remediable by more effort within the same domain. The verse's placement of this statement immediately after the question about the Spirit makes its function clear: there are matters on which the correct answer is that the question exceeds the asker's capacity, and knowing which matters those are is itself knowledge. The one who cannot distinguish the investigable from the non-investigable will waste his capacity on the second and neglect the first.

****Al-Rūḥ min Amri Rabbī: The Answer That Declines the Question****

This verse is one of the most discussed in the Qur'an, and its method of answering is more significant than its content. Al-Ṭabarī records the occasion: a delegation asked the Prophet ﷺ about the *rūḥ*, and the answer came immediately. The exegetes divided over what was being asked, and al-Ṭabarī catalogues the readings — the soul that gives life to the body, the Qur'an itself, the angel Gabriel, or a class of angels described as an army of God. The majority reading is the soul, and the answer addresses it in a way that changes the subject deliberately. The answer has two parts, and the second is the substantive one. *Qul al-rūḥu min amri rabbī* — say: the spirit is of the command of my Lord. The commentators, following al-Rāzī and al-Ghazālī, distinguished between the *ʿālam al-khalq*, the world of creation, which comprises things brought into being through materials, causes, and intervals, and the *ʿālam al-amr*, the world of command, which comprises things that come into being by the word *kun*, be, without a material antecedent. 7:54 makes the same distinction: *"His is the creation and the command."* The soul is placed in the second category, and the consequence is that the methods appropriate to the first — observation, decomposition, inference from parts — do not apply to it. The second clause is the verse's real answer: *wa mā ūtītum min al-ʿilmi illā qalīlā* — and you have been given of knowledge only a little. Al-Rāzī reads it as the ground of the refusal rather than a rebuke: the questioner is being told that his apparatus is inadequate to the question, not that the answer is being withheld arbitrarily. This is a claim about epistemic scope, and it has an obvious modern parallel — the demand that a thing be explained in the terms of a method that cannot reach it. The verse does not say the soul is unknowable in itself; it says that what has been given to the questioner does not reach it. The verse's placement is significant. It sits between the account of the Qur'an as a healing (v. 82) and the statement of its contingency (v. 86), and the three together make a single claim: the revelation heals, it is a gift that could be withdrawn, and there are questions it will not answer. The refusal is therefore part of the gift's character rather than a limitation of it. 5:101 records the same principle in legislation: *"O you who believe, ask not about things which, if made plain to you, may grieve you."* The Qur'an's method throughout is to answer the question that matters and to decline the one that does not, and this verse is the clearest instance of the second.

**The Discipline of Saying "I Do Not Know"**

The verse establishes a rule for speech about God: when asked what one does not know, the correct response is to say so, and to say why. The Prophet ﷺ — the one who received revelation — answered a direct question with a statement of limits. The precedent governs the tradition's entire approach to speculation: it is not forbidden, but it is bounded, and the boundary is stated in this verse.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:86

> **And if We willed, We could take away that which We revealed unto thee. Then thou wouldst not find, for thyself, any guardian against Us,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Possibility of Withdrawal**

*Wa la-in shiʾnā la-nadhhabanna bi-lladhī awḥaynā ilayka* — "and if We willed, We would surely take away what We revealed to thee." The construction *la-in ... la-* expresses a real possibility with emphatic certainty in the consequence. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī note that although the address is second-person singular — to the Prophet ﷺ — most commentators read it as directed to people generally, or to the Prophet ﷺ and his community together. The final clause of verse 87, however, is widely held to be addressed to the Prophet ﷺ specifically.

**What Removal Would Mean**

Al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī explain the removal as the erasure of the Qur'an from both the memories of human beings and the written copies. The tradition preserves striking statements on this. ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd is reported to have said:

> *"The first thing that will be lost from your religion is trust, and the last that will be lost is prayer, so that people will pray but will have no religion. And you will awaken one day and there will be nothing of the Qur'an with you... It will be taken by night, and people will awake in the morning and all of it will have been lifted from the written copies and removed from the hearts."* (reported by al-Zamakhsharī)

And ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar is reported to have said: *"The Hour will not come until the Qur'an is returned whence it descended"* — and that when it returns, it will complain that the people recited it but did not act on it (al-Qurṭubī).

**"Then Thou Wouldst Not Find Any Guardian Against Us"**

*Thumma lā tajidu laka bihi ʿalaynā wakīlā* — the *wakīl* again, in the same formula as verses 68, 69, and 75. The point is that once the revelation is withdrawn, no one can restore it. Not the scholars, not the community, not the Prophet ﷺ himself. The Qur'an is not a human possession that can be recovered by human effort; it is a gift that exists only as long as it is given.

**The Theological Point**

The verse makes a claim that the Islamic tradition has taken seriously: the preservation of the Qur'an is a divine act, not a human achievement. Compare 15:9, *"Truly We have sent down the Reminder, and truly We are its Guardian"* — the guardianship is God's, and verse 86 states the counterfactual: if He did not will its continuance, it would not continue. This has a practical consequence for the community's attitude toward the text. Preservation is not a task completed by the compilers of the muṣḥaf; it is an ongoing divine action in which human effort participates. The memorization of the Qur'an by generation after generation is understood in the tradition not as a human accomplishment but as the visible form of the divine guardianship — which is why the verse's warning is directed at complacency rather than at anxiety.

****If We Willed, We Would Take It Away: The Contingency of the Revelation****

The verse asks the Prophet ﷺ to consider the absence of what he has been given, and the exercise is the point. *Wa la-in shiʾnā la-nadhhabanna bi-alladhī awḥaynā ilayka* — and if We willed, We would surely take away what We have revealed to thee. The double particle *la-in* with the emphatic *la-nadhhabanna* makes the statement as strong as Arabic allows: not a possibility but a certainty held back by nothing but the divine will. Al-Qurṭubī explains the function: the blessing is not measured while it is held but by the consideration of its removal, and the verse instructs the reader to perform that consideration. The clause that follows — *thumma lā tajidu laka bihi ʿalaynā wakīlā*, then thou wouldst find no trustee for thyself with Us concerning it — is the third use of *wakīl* in this passage, after verses 65 and 68, and the accumulation is deliberate. In verse 65 God is sufficient as trustee for the believers; in verse 68 the disbeliever finds none; here the Prophet ﷺ himself would find none. The verse thereby establishes that the relation of dependence is universal and ungraded — no one holds the revelation as a possession, and no one has standing to claim it. The theological content is the contingency of the revelation. The Qur'an does not exist by necessity; it exists because it was sent, and the sending could have been withheld or withdrawn. Al-Rāzī draws the consequence: a gift that could be withdrawn is a gift, and the recipient's obligation is gratitude rather than entitlement. 2:106 states the same principle in the register of legislation: *"Whatever verse We abrogate or cause to be forgotten, We bring one better than it or like it — knowest thou not that God has power over all things?"* The verse's placement after the statement that the spirit belongs to the world of command produces a striking sequence. The human being is told that he has been given little knowledge, and then that the greatest thing he has been given is held only by another's will. The two statements together are the Qur'an's anthropology in miniature: the creature is dependent in his knowing and dependent in his having, and both dependencies are stated to produce gratitude rather than despair — since the alternative to a contingent gift is not a necessary one but nothing. The devotional tradition read the verse accordingly, and the practice it produced is *dhikr al-niʿmah*, the remembrance of the blessing, which al-Qurṭubī describes as the discipline of recalling what one has been given in the form of what one would lose. 14:7 supplies the motive: *"If you are grateful, I will surely increase you; and if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe."*

**The Warning and the Mercy**

Verse 87 supplies the exception, and the commentators read it in two ways: God may choose not to remove it, or He may return it after removing it. Either way the verse establishes that the Qur'an's presence among human beings is an act of mercy — repeated, moment by moment — and not a permanent endowment.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:87

> **save a mercy from thy Lord. Truly His Bounty toward thee is ever great.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception That Governs the Warning**

*Illā raḥmatan min rabbik* — "except a mercy from thy Lord." The exception is attached to the entire preceding statement: the revelation is not taken away because of a mercy. Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that the exception may mean either that God wills not to remove it, or that He would restore it after removal. The theological significance is that mercy, not necessity, is the reason the Qur'an remains. It is not indestructible in itself; it is *kept*. Compare 2:64, 4:83, 4:113, 4:175, 24:10, 14, 20, where mercy and bounty are paired in the same manner. The Qur'an pairs bounty and mercy in a verse addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in a moment of danger:

> *"And but for the Bounty of God upon thee and His Mercy, a faction of them would have endeavored to lead thee astray; yet they lead none astray but themselves, nor can they harm thee in any way."* (4:113)

**"Truly His Bounty Toward Thee Is Ever Great"**

*Inna faḍlahu kāna ʿalayka kabīrā* — the address returns to the Prophet ﷺ, and al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī read the *faḍl* — bounty, superabundant gift — as referring to his station of excellence among human beings and to his status as the seal of the prophets (33:40). Compare verse 79's *maqām maḥmūd*. The word *kabīr* — great — is the same adjective used at verse 9 of the believers' reward (*ajran kabīrā*). The parallel is deliberate: the reward promised to the community and the bounty given to its Prophet are described in the same terms.

****Illā Raḥmatan min Rabbika: The Exception That States the Whole Ground****

The verse completes the preceding one with the exception that explains it. *Illā raḥmatan min rabbik* — except by mercy from thy Lord. The grammatical construction is an exception from an implied negative: the revelation would have been taken away, and nothing prevented it except mercy. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī both note that the exception removes the possibility of any other ground — not the Prophet's ﷺ merit, not the community's worthiness, not the necessity of the message, but mercy. The verse is therefore a statement of grace in its purest Qur'anic form. The clause that follows makes the point quantitative: *inna faḍlahu kāna ʿalayka kabīrā* — indeed His bounty toward thee is great. The word *kabīr*, great, is the same intensive description used at verse 43 of the divine elevation, *ʿuluwwan kabīrā*, and the parallel is not accidental: the transcendence stated there is exercised here as bounty. Al-Rāzī enumerates what the bounty comprises, and the list is effectively the whole of the sūrah — the Night Journey, the revelation, the confirmation at the point of pressure, the praiseworthy station, and the preservation of the message. The verse's theological significance is that it forecloses a specific error. A messenger who has received what no one else received might conclude that he holds it by right, and the community that receives a revelation might conclude that it possesses it. The Qur'an closes both conclusions in two clauses: the ground of the gift is mercy, and its extent is great. 3:164 records the same description of the sending itself: *"God conferred a favor upon the believers when He raised among them a messenger from themselves."* The practical consequence is the one the tradition drew consistently. A person who understands that his knowledge, his faith, and his access to the revelation are gifts rather than achievements relates to them differently: he cannot be arrogant about them, since they are not his, and he cannot be secure in them, since they are held by another's will. 49:17 states the point bluntly to those who claimed credit for their belief: *"They consider it a favor to thee that they have submitted. Say: count not your submission as a favor to me — nay, God confers the favor upon you that He has guided you to faith, if you are truthful."* The verse thus completes the sequence begun at verse 85 in a single movement. The human being has been given little knowledge; what he has been given is held by another's will; and the reason it is held there is mercy. The three statements together produce the disposition the sūrah has been building toward, which is the gratitude of verse 107 and 109 — prostration, weeping, and the glorification with which the sūrah ends.

**The Verse's Sequence With Verse 85**

Verse 85 told the questioners that they had been given only a little knowledge. Verse 86 warns that even the little can be taken. Verse 87 states that it is not taken, and that the reason is mercy. The three verses together form a complete epistemology: human knowledge is limited; what has been given is held by grace, not by right; and the grace is great. The practical effect is to produce an attitude toward knowledge that is neither proud nor anxious. Not proud, because the little is little. Not anxious, because the One who gave it is described as bountiful. The attitude the verses cultivate is gratitude — which is, as verse 3 established with Noah, the characteristic of the thankful servant.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:88

> **Say, "Surely if mankind and jinn banded together to bring the like of this Quran, they would not bring the like thereof, even if they supported one another."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge in Its Strongest Form**

*Qul la-ini ijtamaʿati al-insu wa-l-jinnu ʿalā an yaʾtū bi-mithli hādhā al-qurʾāni lā yaʾtūna bi-mithlih* — "Say: If mankind and jinn gathered together to bring the like of this Qur'an, they would not bring the like of it." The Qur'an issues the challenge in three escalating forms, and this is the most comprehensive. At 2:23 the challenge is to produce *one sūrah* like it: *"And if you are in doubt concerning what We sent down upon Our servant, bring one sūrah like it, and call your witnesses apart from God, if you are truthful."* At 10:38, *"bring a sūrah like it."* At 11:13, *"bring ten sūrahs like it, invented."* Here the challenge is to produce the *whole* Qur'an, and the collaborators include the jinn.

**Why the Jinn Are Included**

The inclusion of the jinn is significant and the commentators note it. The jinn, in Qur'anic anthropology, possess intelligence, language, and moral responsibility — 55:15, 72:1–15. They are not merely spirits but beings capable of speech and composition. Their inclusion removes the possible objection that the challenge was limited to human eloquence and could be met by a non-human intelligence.

**The Doctrines of Iʿjāz**

The verse is the scriptural basis of *iʿjāz al-Qurʾān* — the doctrine of the Qur'an's inimitability — held universally in the Islamic tradition. The commentators enumerate the dimensions in which imitation is held to be impossible. Al-Zamakhsharī emphasizes *eloquence* (faṣāḥah) and *the beauty of organization and composition* (naẓm) — the specific arrangement of words, which he treats as the locus of the miracle. Al-Ṭabarī and others emphasize the *knowledge* it contains, the *excellence of its content*, and the *refinement of its expression*. Later scholars, notably al-Bāqillānī in *Iʿjāz al-Qurʾān*, added the *absence of internal inconsistency* — a claim the Qur'an makes for itself at 4:82:

> *"Do they not reflect upon the Qur'an? If it had been from other than God, they would have found in it much inconsistency."*

Al-Rummānī and al-Khaṭṭābī, writing in the fourth/tenth century, developed the doctrine systematically, and ʿAbd al-Qāhir al-Jurjānī's theory of *naẓm* — that the miraculous quality lies in the specific syntactic and semantic ordering, not in the vocabulary — became the standard account.

**The Historical Context**

Al-Ṭabarī records that the verse was revealed in response to a request by some Madinan Jews that the Prophet ﷺ produce a new verse of revelation as proof. His response was that they already know the Qur'an is from God, since its teachings accord with the Torah's. The verse then shifts the ground: the proof is not a new verse but the impossibility of producing this one.

****The Challenge, and the Fourfold Failure****

This verse is the Qur'an's fullest statement of its inimitability, and its form is a challenge with a specified condition. *Qul la-ini ijtamaʿat al-insu wa al-jinnu ʿalā an yaʾtū bi-mithli hādhā al-Qurʾāni lā yaʾtūna bi-mithlih* — say: if mankind and the jinn gathered to bring the like of this Qur'an, they would not bring the like of it. The condition combines the two classes of rational beings, and the commentators note that the inclusion of the jinn is significant: the challenge is not merely to human eloquence but to every articulate intelligence, and the Arabs of the seventh century — who believed in jinn and attributed poetry to them — could not dismiss the challenge as addressed to a weaker party. The phrase *wa law kāna baʿḍuhum li-baʿḍin ẓahīrā*, even if some of them were helpers to others, adds the condition of cooperation and closes the last escape. Al-Zamakhsharī observes that the challenge is thus set at its most favorable to the challengers: all rational beings, cooperating fully. The Qur'an issues the same challenge three other times at descending scales — 10:38, bring a sūrah like it; 11:13, ten sūras like it, invented; 2:23, a single sūrah like it, with the invitation to call your witnesses besides God if you are truthful — and the descent is instructive, since the final and smallest demand was never met. The doctrine of *iʿjāz*, inimitability, was developed systematically by al-Bāqillānī and after him by al-Khaṭṭābī, ʿAbd al-Qāhir al-Jurjānī, and al-Rāzī, and they identified three dimensions. The linguistic: the Qur'an's composition (*naẓm*) is unlike any known form — neither poetry, since it does not observe meter, nor prose, since it observes a rhythmic structure of its own. The informational: it contains reports of the unseen, past and future, delivered by a man who could not read and had not studied. The legislative: its law has governed civilizations for fourteen centuries without requiring replacement. Al-Jurjānī's contribution was to locate the inimitability in the *naẓm* — the specific arrangement of words in relation to meaning — rather than in any individual word or figure, which makes the challenge structurally impossible to meet by imitation, since imitation reproduces features rather than the principle of arrangement. The historical attempts are recorded and are instructive in their failure. Musaylimah's compositions were mocked by his own contemporaries; Ibn al-Muqaffaʿ, the greatest prose stylist of the second Islamic century, is reported to have attempted a chapter and abandoned it, saying that this is not the speech of mankind. The failure is the evidence, and the verse states in advance that it was not a matter of effort. The verse's function in the argument is to close the demands of verses 90–93. The opponents asked for a spring, a garden, a house of gold, a ladder to heaven, and a piece of the sky; the Qur'an answers that the sign they already possess is one that all of creation, cooperating, cannot reproduce — which reframes the demand entirely. The problem was never the insufficiency of evidence.

**The Structure of the Argument**

The challenge has a specific logical form that the commentators have analyzed. It is not an assertion that no one has succeeded; it is a prediction that no one will succeed, issued publicly, to a hostile audience with every motive to falsify it, in a culture that prized verbal composition above every other achievement. The Arabs of the seventh century had the motive, the skill, and the opportunity. The challenge has stood for fourteen centuries. The argument's force is that the failure to meet it is itself the evidence. A claim that could be falsified by any competent poet, and has not been falsified, carries a specific evidential weight — and the Qur'an's original audience, who were the best judges of Arabic composition available, recognized it. Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah's reported assessment is the tradition's most cited testimony: he heard the Qur'an and said of it that it was not the speech of mankind, and that it shatters what is below it and rises above it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:89

> **And indeed We have employed every kind of parable for mankind in this Quran. Yet most of mankind refuse aught but disbelief.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Kind of Parable**

*Wa laqad ṣarrafnā li-l-nāsi fī hādhā al-qurʾāni min kulli mathal* — the same verb *ṣarrafa* as in verse 41, indicating deliberate variation. The Qur'an has employed *min kulli mathal* — of every kind of likeness, parable, example. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabarī's successors explain it as God's having provided in the Qur'an every indication, proof, and mode of expression human beings need. Ibn Kathīr states it as every argument, conclusive proof, and explanation required to understand the truth. Compare 18:54, *"And We have employed for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of parable, but man is in most things contentious"*; 30:58; 39:27:

> *"And We have set forth for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of parable, that they may take heed."*

**The Range of the Qur'an's Parables**

The commentators note the variety. There are extended allegories, such as the parable of the two gardens at 18:32–43. There are short metaphorical images: 10:24, the life of this world as water that brings forth the earth's adornment and then the earth becomes dry stubble scattered by the winds; 13:17, the foam that passes away and the metal smelted for adornment; 14:24–26, the good word as a good tree and the evil word as an uprooted tree; 16:75–76 and 16:112; 29:41, the spider's house; 62:5, the donkey carrying books. And there are symbolic images of great metaphysical depth, of which the Light Verse (24:35) is the most famous. The range is the point of the verse: no single mode of presentation has been omitted. Argument, narrative, image, law, warning, promise, dialogue, oath — every register is employed, which is why verse 41's claim of deliberate variation can be made.

**"Yet Most of Mankind Refuse Aught but Disbelief"**

*Fa-abā aktharu al-nāsi illā kafūrā* — the verb *abā* means to refuse, to decline, to be unwilling. It denotes not inability but *refusal*, which is a decisive word. The verse does not say most of mankind *cannot* understand; it says they *refuse*. The same statement recurs at 11:17, 12:103, and 40:59. The Qur'an returns to it because it is the central puzzle of revelation: the guidance is complete and the response is refusal.

**Why Refusal and Not Ignorance**

The distinction matters. If the problem were ignorance, more information would solve it. The verse, following immediately after the claim that every kind of parable has been provided, excludes that explanation. The information is there. The refusal is elsewhere — in the will, in the attachments, in what accepting the message would cost. This connects to verse 84's *shākilah*: each acts according to his formed disposition. The disposition formed around something the Qur'an demands surrendering will refuse the parable however clearly it is stated. The Qur'an's own diagnosis at 74:49–51 is blunt: *"So what is wrong with them, that they turn away from the admonition — as though they were startled asses fleeing from a lion?"*

****Ṣarrafnā: The Variety of Illustration, and the Refusal That Followed It****

The verb *ṣarrafa* is from the intensive form of *ṣ-r-f*, to turn, and in this context it denotes the varying and repeating of a thing in different forms. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it: We have set forth for mankind in this Qur'an, of every kind of likeness and argument, in varied and repeated forms. The verse is therefore a statement about method: the Qur'an does not present a truth once and move on, but returns to it from different angles, in different registers, with different illustrations. The same verb is used of the Qur'an's method at 6:105, 20:113, 25:33, 30:58, and 39:27, and the recurrence indicates a deliberate pedagogy. 39:27 states the reasoning: *"And We have set forth for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of likeness, that they may remember."* The variation is not redundancy; it is the recognition that a single formulation reaches some minds and not others, and that a truth must be met from several directions before it is grasped. 6:65 states the same: *"See how We make the signs clear, that they may understand."* The second clause is the verse's point: *fa-abā aktharu al-nās illā kafūrā* — but most people refuse, except in ingratitude. The construction *abā illā* is emphatic, denoting a refusal that admits nothing else. The verse therefore makes a claim that is central to the Qur'an's diagnosis of unbelief: the obstacle was never the availability of evidence. 6:25 states the mechanism: *"And among them are those who listen to thee, but We have placed veils over their hearts so that they understand it not, and in their ears deafness."* 41:44 supplies the confirmation from the objectors' own mouths: had the Qur'an been revealed in a foreign tongue, they would have objected that its verses were not explained in detail — the objection was therefore ready before the explanation. The word *kafūr*, intensive in form, denotes ingratitude as a settled character rather than a single act, and it echoes verse 67's *wa kāna al-kāfiru kafūrā*. The pairing establishes that the refusal described here is the same disposition described there: the one who turns away after rescue. The verse's argument is thus cumulative — the evidence has been varied and repeated, the refusal has persisted through every variation, and the persistence discloses that the cause of the refusal lies elsewhere. Al-Rāzī draws the practical consequence for the one who conveys the message. Since the variety of illustration has not produced acceptance in most, the measure of success cannot be the hearer's response; it must be the completeness of the delivery. The verse therefore releases the teacher from an impossible standard while raising another: the obligation is to vary the presentation until every available route has been tried, and then to leave the result. 5:92 states the same limit: *"Upon Our Messenger is only the clear conveyance."*

**The Verse's Function**

Placed after the challenge of verse 88, the verse explains why the challenge has not been met. The inimitability of the Qur'an is not a secret that investigation would reveal; it is a fact that a specific disposition refuses to register. The reader is thereby told where to look for the obstacle — not in the text but in himself.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:90

> **And they say, "We shall not believe in you till you make a spring gush forth for us from the earth,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional Refusal**

*Wa qālū lan nuʾmina laka ḥattā tafjura lanā mina al-arḍi yanbūʿā* — "and they said: we will never believe you until you make a spring gush forth for us from the earth." The particle *lan* expresses a strong negation of the future: *we will not, ever*. The *ḥattā* makes belief conditional on the performance. The verb *fajjara* is the intensive of *f-j-r*, to cleave, to cause to gush; *yanbūʿ* is a spring, a source of water. In the desert environment of Makkah, a spring is not a convenience but the foundation of settlement — the difference between a caravan stop and a city. The demand is for the transformation of the economic reality of the Hijaz.

**The Historical Setting**

Al-Ṭabarī and others record that the leaders of Quraysh first tried to persuade the Prophet ﷺ to desist from preaching, then attempted to bribe him with wealth, marriage, and status — offers recorded in the biographical sources, most famously in the negotiation with ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah. When that failed, they escalated to demands for signs. The four verses that follow record the full list. The same conditional demand is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an, in the plainest terms:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'If only a sign were sent down unto him from his Lord!'"* (13:27)

**The Structure of the Demands**

The demands fall into two categories, which the commentators distinguish. The first is *economic and material*: a spring, a garden of palms and vines with streams, a house of gold. The second is *supernatural and evidential*: the sky falling in pieces, God and the angels appearing, an ascent to heaven with a physical book brought down. The mixture is revealing. The demanders do not appear to have a coherent criterion of what would convince them; they list whatever would be impressive. This is the signature of a demand not seriously intended — which is why the Qur'an responds as it does at verse 93.

****Four Demands, and the Diagnosis Beneath Them****

The verse records the conditions the Quraysh set for belief, and the list is specific enough to be historically useful. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the reports: a group of the Quraysh leaders said to the Prophet ﷺ that they would not believe until he produced a spring gushing from the earth for them, or a garden of dates and grapes with rivers flowing through it, or caused the sky to fall upon them in pieces as he had claimed, or brought God and the angels before them face to face, or had a house of gold, or ascended into heaven — and would not believe the ascent either until he brought down a book they could read. The four demands divide into two pairs, and the division is diagnostic. The first pair — a spring, a garden, a house of gold — asks for material proof of prosperity, which is the criterion by which the Quraysh measured standing. The second pair — the ascent, the descending book — asks for a supernatural demonstration. Al-Rāzī observes the structure of the escalation: each demand is framed so that its fulfillment would produce the next. This is the character of a demand made not to obtain evidence but to avoid a conclusion, and the Qur'an identifies it as such at 6:109: *"They swear by God their most solemn oath that if a sign came to them they would believe in it. Say: signs are only with God — and what will make you aware that if it came they would not believe?"* The Qur'an records the same pattern across the prophetic history, which is what makes the verse a general claim rather than a report. 2:55 records the demand to see God openly. 4:153 records the People of the Book asking for a book to come down from heaven, and answers it: *"We sent down to Moses clear signs."* 6:124 records the demand for the prophethood to be given to the notables instead. 74:52 states the underlying disposition: *"Nay, every one of them desires that he be given pages spread open."* The pattern is consistent: the demand is for terms rather than for truth, and the terms are set by the one who has already decided. The verse's answer is not a refusal but a redirection, and it is given in the following verses: the sign is already present in a form that cannot be reproduced (v. 88), the messenger cannot produce what is not his to produce (v. 91), and the objection to his humanity is an objection to the nature of messengership itself (vv. 94–95). The practical lesson is the one every person who has argued with a determined skeptic recognizes: a demand that escalates is not a search for evidence, and the correct response is to name the pattern rather than to meet the next demand.

**What the First Demand Reveals**

The request for a spring is diagnostic. The Quraysh's grievance against the Prophet ﷺ was not primarily theological; it was social and economic. His message threatened the Kaʿbah's status as a pan-Arab sanctuary, and with it the trade and prestige that status produced. The demand for a spring is, in effect, a demand that he demonstrate the capacity to *provide* — to be a source of worldly benefit comparable to what the existing order delivered. The Qur'an's refusal is therefore not a refusal to perform but a refusal of the *terms*. A prophet who produces springs on demand is a functionary of material provision, and the mission is something else. The response at the end of verse 93 — *"Am I aught but a human being, a messenger?"* — is the answer to the entire list.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:91

> **or till you have a garden of date palms and grapevines, and you make streams gush forth in the midst of it,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Escalation to Agricultural Wealth**

*Aw takūna laka jannatun min nakhīlin wa ʿinabin fa-tufajjira al-anhāra khilālahā tafjīrā* — "or that you have a garden of date palms and grapevines, and cause rivers to gush through it, gushing." The specification is precise and locally meaningful. Date palms and grapevines were the two most valuable cultivated crops of the Hijaz — the date palm of the oases and the vines of al-Ṭāʾif. A garden of these two, irrigated by flowing streams rather than by drawn water, represented the highest form of agricultural wealth available in that environment. The cognate adverb *tafjīrā* intensifies the verb: not merely causing streams but causing them to *gush abundantly*.

**Why the Demand Is for Personal Wealth**

Note the construction: *an takūna laka* — "that you have." The demand is not for a public benefit but for the Prophet's ﷺ personal enrichment. This exposes the demand's actual premise: that prophethood is a position from which material advantage flows, and that its authenticity can be verified by the holder's prosperity. The premise is the one the Qur'an has already refuted. At 25:7–8 the disbelievers objected: *"What kind of messenger is this, who eats food and walks in the markets? Why was no treasure sent down to him, or an angel come with him?"* And at 26:111 they said of Noah's followers, *"We will not believe; only the lowest follow you."* The recurring objection is not that the message is false but that the messenger is not *impressive*. The Qur'an's answer is consistent: the messenger's lack of wealth is not a defect but a sign. 6:50:

> *"Say: I do not say to you that I possess the treasuries of God, nor that I know the Unseen, nor that I am an angel. I only follow what is revealed to me."*

****Basharan Rasūlā: The Two Nouns That Answer the Objection****

The verse records the most extreme of the demands and gives the sūrah's shortest and most important answer. *Aw tusqiṭa al-samāʾa kamā zaʿamta ʿalaynā kisafā* — or that thou cause the sky to fall upon us in pieces, as thou hast claimed. The demand invokes the Prophet's ﷺ own warning, turning it into a test: if you say the sky will fall, make it fall. The verb *zaʿama* — to claim, to assert — is used of the warning here by the opponents, and the Qur'an uses the same verb of false claims at 4:117 and 6:148, so the opponents' choice of word is itself a verdict on the warning. The answer is the verse's substance: *qul subḥāna rabbī hal kuntu illā basharan rasūlā* — say: glory be to my Lord; am I anything but a human messenger? The two nouns are the answer, and each does a distinct work. *Bashar* denotes the human being in his physical, mortal, limited aspect — the same word used at 3:47, 19:20, and 12:31, where the women cut their hands and said *this is not a human being*. *Rasūl* denotes the one sent with a message. The two together state the Islamic doctrine of prophethood in four words: the messenger is fully human and fully sent, and neither term cancels the other. The glorification that precedes the answer is significant. Al-Qurṭubī explains it as the response to the implication of the demand, which was that the Prophet ﷺ claimed divine power; *subḥāna rabbī* removes the implication before the answer is given. The construction *hal kuntu illā* — am I anything but — is a rhetorical question that expects the answer *no*, and the verse thereby makes the opponents answer their own objection: he never claimed to be able to bring down the sky, so the demand is not a test of his claim but a substitution of a claim he did not make. The Qur'an insists on the messenger's humanity throughout, and the insistence is a refusal of the two opposite errors. 5:75 states it of Jesus: *"The Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers passed away before him."* 6:50 gives the Prophet ﷺ his own formula: *"Say: I do not say to you that I have the treasures of God, nor that I know the unseen, nor that I am an angel; I follow only what is revealed to me."* 41:6 records the command: *"Say: I am only a human being like you, to whom it is revealed that your God is one God."* The error the verse blocks is the elevation of the messenger to a share in divine power, and the error it blocks at the same time is the reduction of the message to the messenger's own invention. The verse therefore completes the argument of the sūrah's central section. The demands are impossible because the messenger is human; the message is authoritative because the messenger is sent; and the glorification belongs to God alone, which is where the sūrah began.

**The Irony of the Demand**

The demand is ironic in a way the Qur'an exploits. The Garden of the Hereafter is described in exactly these terms — palms, vines, and rivers flowing beneath. 13:35: *"The likeness of the Garden promised to the reverent: beneath it rivers flow."* 18:32–34 describes the two gardens, one of them of palms and vines with a river running through. The demanders are asking for the promised Garden in this world, in material form, as the price of believing in the Garden. The refusal is therefore not a denial that such a Garden exists but a refusal to convert it into currency.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:92

> **or till you make the sky fall upon us in pieces, as you have claimed, or you bring God and the angels before us,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for Their Own Destruction**

*Aw tusqiṭa al-samāʾa kamā zaʿamta ʿalaynā kisafā* — "or that you cause the sky to fall upon us in pieces, as you claimed." The reference is to the Qur'an's warnings of celestial punishment; the same threat appears at 34:9:

> *"If We will, We shall cause the earth to swallow them, or cause a fragment of the sky to fall upon them."*

The demand is extraordinary: they ask for the punishment they have been warned of, as proof. The Qur'an records the same phenomenon at 8:32: *"And [remember] when they said: 'O God, if this is the truth from Thee, then rain down upon us stones from the sky, or bring upon us a painful punishment.'"* Al-Ṭabarī and the commentators note the significance: this is not a request for evidence but an expression of contempt — the posture of one who is certain nothing will happen and wishes to demonstrate the certainty.

**"Or You Bring God and the Angels Before Us"**

*Aw taʾtiya bi-Allāhi wa-l-malāʾikati qabīlā* — *qabīlā* means face to face, in direct confrontation, or as a guarantor. The demand is for the direct vision of God and the assembled angels. Al-Ṭabarī notes the parallel with the Israelites' demand at 2:55: *"And [remember] when you said: 'O Moses, we will not believe thee until we see God plainly!' — and the thunderbolt seized you while you looked on."* The precedent is decisive: the demand was made before, it was granted, and the demanders were destroyed. The Qur'an's position on seeing God in this world is stated at 7:143, where Moses asks: *"My Lord, show me, that I may look upon Thee."* The answer: *"Thou shalt not see Me (lan tarānī), but look upon the mountain; if it remains in its place, then thou shalt see Me."* And when the Lord manifested to the mountain, it crumbled to dust and Moses fell in a swoon.

****The Objection Reversed: What the Demand for a Human Messenger Conceals****

The verse states the objection in its own terms and then exposes what it conceals. *Wa mā manaʿa al-nāsa an yuʾminū idh jāʾahumu al-hudā illā an qālū a-baʿatha Allāhu basharan rasūlā* — and nothing prevented people from believing when the guidance came to them except that they said: has God sent a human being as a messenger? The construction is precise: the obstacle is identified not as a lack of evidence but as an objection to the form of the evidence. Al-Rāzī's analysis of the objection is the sharpest available, and it turns on what the objection does not say. The objectors did not claim that a human messenger is impossible — their own tradition affirmed prophets who were human — but that *this* human was not the one they would have chosen. 43:31 states the objection explicitly in those terms: *"And they said: why was not this Qur'an sent down to a great man of the two cities?"* — meaning al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah of Makkah or ʿUrwah ibn Masʿūd of Ṭāʾif. 6:124 supplies the answer: *"God knows best where to place His message."* The objection is therefore not theological but social: it is a claim about who deserves precedence, and it is made by the people who hold it. The Qur'an records the same objection against every prophet, and the record is what makes the verse a general diagnosis rather than a report. 14:10–11: *"Their messengers said to them: we are only human beings like you, but God confers His favor upon whom He wills of His servants."* 23:33–34 records the objection against a prophet who ate food: *"What is this but a human being like you? He eats what you eat and drinks what you drink."* 25:7 records it against the Prophet ﷺ in the same words, adding the demand for an angel or a treasure. The consistency across the prophetic history is the evidence: an objection that recurs identically against every messenger is not about the messenger. The verse's own answer is given in the next verse, and it is structural rather than defensive: a messenger must be of the kind of those to whom he is sent, or he cannot be received. The demand for a non-human messenger is therefore a demand for a message that could not be understood, and the objectors' position is self-refuting — they ask for what would defeat the purpose of the asking. The practical content of the verse is its account of how objections work. An objection raised against the *form* of a message rather than its content is usually a report about the objector's expectations, and it is answered not by improving the form but by naming the expectation. The Qur'an does exactly that, and does it in a single clause: what prevented them from believing was what they said.

**Why the Demand Cannot Be Met**

The Qur'an's reasoning is not that God is incapable of appearing but that the human constitution cannot receive the manifestation. This is why the Isrāʾ and Miʿrāj — in which the Prophet ﷺ did approach the Divine Presence — is presented as a unique miracle requiring a unique preparation, and why the Qur'an describes the nearness at 53:7–9 rather than narrating a vision. The demand also confuses the categories. Faith is the recognition of the unseen on the basis of signs; a demand for the seen as the condition of faith abolishes faith rather than establishing it. The Qur'an makes this explicit at 6:111: *"And even if We sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them face to face, they would not believe unless God willed."* The verse states that the demand, if granted, would not produce the promised belief — which the demanders' own history confirms.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:93

> **or till you have a house of gold ornament, or you ascend to Heaven. And we shall not believe in your ascension till you bring down unto us a book we can read." Say, "Glory be to my Lord! Am I aught but a human being, a messenger?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Two Demands**

*Aw yakūna laka baytun min zukhrufin aw tarqā fī al-samāʾ* — "or that you have a house of ornament [gold], or that you ascend into the sky." *Zukhruf* means gold, or ornament generally; the Qur'an uses it of worldly adornment at 43:33–35, where God says that were it not that mankind would become a single community of disbelief, He would have made the houses of the disbelievers of silver with stairways of gold. *Au tarqā fī al-samāʾ* — "or that you ascend into the sky." The verb *raqiya* means to climb, to ascend — the same root as *miʿrāj*, the ascension. The demand, coming in a sūrah that opens with the Night Journey and Ascension, is remarkable: they are demanding the very thing that has occurred, in a public and verifiable form.

**"We Shall Not Believe in Your Ascension Till You Bring Down a Book We Can Read"**

*Wa lan nuʾmina li-ruqiyyika ḥattā tunazzila ʿalaynā kitāban naqraʾuh* — the demand adds a condition to the condition. The same demand for a physical book appears at 6:7: *"And if We sent down to thee a book upon parchment, and they touched it with their hands, those who disbelieve would say: 'This is nothing but manifest sorcery'"*; and at 6:124; and at 4:153, where the People of the Book ask Moses for a book from heaven. The verse at 6:7 is the decisive answer, and it should be read alongside this one: *even if the book were delivered physically, they would attribute it to sorcery.* The demand is therefore unfalsifiable by construction — every possible proof has a pre-prepared dismissal. This is what identifies it as insincere.

**The Response: "Glory Be to My Lord!"**

*Qul subḥāna rabbī* — the Prophet ﷺ is instructed to answer with *tasbīḥ*. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain the response as an expression of awe at the enormity of what is being asked: that a human being should be able to command the sky, produce gold, or bring God before an audience. The glorification is a refusal framed as reverence — it declines by pointing to the distance between the Creator and the creature.

**"Am I Aught but a Human Being, a Messenger?"**

*Hal anā illā basharun rasūlā* — the answer is a rhetorical question expecting the reply "no." Two nouns: *bashar* — a human being, a mortal of flesh; *rasūl* — a messenger, one sent with a message. The verse holds both together, and the pairing is the Qur'an's definitive statement of prophetic nature. The same formula appears at 18:110: *"Say: I am only a human being like yourselves; it is revealed to me that your God is one God"*; and 41:6: *"Say: I am only a human being like yourselves; it is revealed to me..."* And the earlier messengers gave the same answer: 14:11:

> *"Their messengers said to them: 'We are nothing but human beings like yourselves, but God bestows favor upon whom He wills of His servants.'"*

**Why This Answer Is Sufficient**

The two terms do specific work. *Bashar* excludes the supernatural powers the demands presuppose: a human being does not cause springs, produce gold, or bring God before an audience. *Rasūl* establishes what he actually is: a conveyor of a message, whose authority is in the message and not in personal capacity. The answer relocates the entire question. The demanders asked what the messenger can *do*; the answer states what he is *for*. A messenger who performed miracles on demand would be a wonder-worker, and wonder-working is not the criterion of a message's truth — Pharaoh's magicians performed wonders. The criterion is the message, and the message is what verse 88 challenged them to imitate and verse 89 declared to be complete.

****Law Kāna fī al-Arḍi Malāʾikah: The Necessity of Congruence****

The verse is the Qur'an's answer to the objection of verse 92, and the answer is a principle rather than a defense. *Qul law kāna fī al-arḍi malāʾikatun yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna la-nazzalnā ʿalayhim mina al-samāʾi malakan rasūlā* — say: if there were on earth angels walking about in settled habitation, We would surely have sent down to them from heaven an angel as a messenger. The clause *yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna* is important: it denotes angels established as inhabitants of the earth, walking upon it in settled life, and al-Qurṭubī glosses it as dwelling and being at ease in it, as human beings do. The principle is that the messenger must be of the kind of the recipients. Al-Rāzī develops the reasoning across four grounds. First, comprehension: a being of another order could not be understood, since communication requires a shared medium. Second, imitation: the messenger is a model, and a model of a different nature cannot be followed — 33:21 describes the Prophet ﷺ as *uswah ḥasanah*, a good example, which requires that his life be reproducible. Third, endurance: the recipients could not bear the presence of a being of another order, and the Qur'an states this at 6:9: *"Had We made him an angel, We would have made him in the form of a man, and We would have covered them with the very thing they cover themselves with."* Fourth, verification: a claim made by a being whose nature cannot be tested cannot be checked, and the human messenger's life is open to inspection. The Qur'an states the same principle elsewhere with equal clarity. 16:43 and 21:7 both command: *"Ask the people of the remembrance if you do not know"* — and the preceding clause in both is *"We sent not before thee except men to whom We revealed."* 25:20 generalizes it: *"And We sent not before thee any messengers but they ate food and walked in the markets."* The eating and the walking are not incidental; they are the evidence of the congruence that makes the message receivable. The verse therefore turns the objection into its own refutation. The demand for an angelic messenger is a demand for a messenger who could not communicate, could not be imitated, could not be endured, and could not be verified — which is a demand for no messenger at all. Al-Zamakhsharī states the conclusion in one line: the objection proves the necessity of what it objects to. The practical consequence is a criterion for evaluating any teacher. The one who claims a standing beyond testing, whose life cannot be inspected and whose example cannot be followed, has removed the very features that make teaching possible — and the Qur'an's insistence on the messenger's humanity is the permanent guard against that removal. 5:75 states it as a general rule: *"The Messiah, son of Mary, was only a messenger; messengers passed away before him."*

**The Formula That Governs All Excess**

The response governs the Islamic understanding of prophethood permanently. The Prophet ﷺ is honored, obeyed, and loved — but he is not a source of independent supernatural power, and requests addressed to him as though he were are refused by this verse. The tradition's consistent position, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah and others against various forms of excess, rests on this formula: *basharun rasūlā* — a human being, a messenger. Nothing more, and nothing less.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:94

> **And nothing hindered men from believing when guidance came unto them, save that they said, "Has God sent a human being as a messenger?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Obstacle Identified**

*Wa mā manaʿa al-nāsa an yuʾminū idh jāʾahumu al-hudā illā an qālū a-baʿatha Allāhu basharan rasūlā* — "and nothing prevented people from believing when guidance came to them, except that they said: Has God sent a human being as a messenger?" The verse states a causal claim with unusual directness: the *only* thing standing between the Quraysh and belief was this objection. Not the content of the message, not its moral demands, not its description of the Hereafter — but the humanity of the messenger.

**The Recurring Objection**

The Qur'an documents the objection across the entire prophetic history. 14:10–11: *"Their messengers said to them: 'We are nothing but human beings like yourselves, but God bestows favor upon whom He wills of His servants... Our people said: you are nothing but human beings like us.'"* And 23:24, 33–34, 47; 26:154, 186; 36:15; 54:24; 64:6; 74:25. The objection is not particular to Makkah; it is the standard human response to prophecy. The parallel expectation of an angel appears at 6:8, 11:12, 15:7, 25:7, 25:21, and 43:53.

****Kafā bi-Allāhi Shahīdā: The Witness That Suffices****

The command *qul* introduces the sūrah's central formula of sufficiency, and the formula appears twice in these verses — here and again at verse 96. *Qul kafā bi-Allāhi shahīdan baynī wa baynakum* — say: sufficient is God as a witness between me and you. The word *shahīd* denotes a witness in the full legal sense: one who is present, who knows, and whose testimony is accepted. The verse therefore transfers the case from the court of public opinion to a court whose witness is God. The clause that follows specifies what God witnesses: *innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihī khabīran baṣīrā* — indeed He is, of His servants, All-Aware, All-Seeing. The two divine names are precise and they divide the knowledge between them. *Khabīr* is from *kh-b-r*, to be acquainted with a thing through testing and experience, and it denotes knowledge of the inner reality, the motive beneath the act. *Baṣīr* denotes the seeing of what is manifest. Al-Rāzī observes that the pairing is complete: nothing is hidden from Him either within or without, which is what makes His testimony sufficient where human testimony is not. The verse's function is consolatory, and its placement makes that clear. It follows the demands of verses 90–93 and the objection to the messenger's humanity, and it answers them not by argument but by referral. The messenger who has been asked to produce springs and gardens and to bring down a book from heaven is told that the case does not need to be won in that forum, because the forum is not competent. 10:29 makes the same move: *"Say: is any of your partners able to guide to the truth? Say: God guides to the truth."* 48:28 states the same sufficiency in the register of the message's destiny: *"He it is Who sent His Messenger with the guidance and the religion of truth, that He may make it prevail over all religion — and sufficient is God as a witness."* The second use of the formula at verse 96 is shorter and sharper — *qul kafā bi-Allāhi shahīdan baynī wa baynakum* repeated — and the repetition indicates that the point needed making twice. 13:43 uses it in the same context of rejection: *"And those who disbelieve say: thou art not a messenger. Say: sufficient is God as a witness between me and you, and whoever has knowledge of the Book."* 29:52 uses it to close an argument: *"Say: sufficient is God as a witness between me and you; He knows what is in the heavens and the earth."* The practical content of the verse is the discipline it supplies to anyone who has been required to prove himself to people who have already decided. The effort is not merely futile but misdirected, since the case is not before them. The verse does not counsel silence — the Prophet ﷺ continued to convey the message for thirteen more years — but it relocates the verdict, and the relocation is what makes sustained effort possible without either coercion or despair.

**Why the Humanity of the Messenger Is Objected To**

The commentators identify several layers. Al-Rāzī notes the surface reason: the messenger eats, walks in the markets, has a family, and is known to his neighbors — and familiarity breeds the inability to see rank. 25:7 records the objection precisely:

> *"What kind of messenger is this, who eats food and walks in the markets?"*

There is a deeper layer, which the tradition identifies as envy. 2:90 records the reaction of those who rejected Muhammad ﷺ: *"How wretched is that for which they sold their souls — that they should disbelieve in what God has sent down, resenting that God should send down of His bounty upon whom He wills of His servants."* The word *baghyan* — resentful envy — names the mechanism. The objection to the messenger's humanity is often a way of objecting to *which* human being was chosen. And there is a third layer, which is the one the Qur'an most often addresses: a human messenger can be *imitated*. An angel cannot be followed in his conduct, because his nature differs; a human messenger's life becomes a model, and a model makes demands. Accepting a human messenger means accepting that his way of eating, speaking, trading, and treating people is a standard one is expected to meet.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:95

> **Say, "Were there angels walking about upon the earth in peace, We would have sent down upon them an angel from Heaven as a messenger."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Principle of Correspondence**

*Qul law kāna fī al-arḍi malāʾikatun yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna la-nazzalnā ʿalayhim mina al-samāʾi malakan rasūlā* — "Say: If there were angels upon the earth, walking about in settled habitation, We would have sent down to them from heaven an angel as a messenger." The argument is one of proportion: the messenger corresponds to the community addressed. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī connect it to the Qur'an's consistent principle that God chooses messengers from among the people to whom they are sent: 3:164, 7:35, 63, 69; 9:128; 10:2; 16:113; 23:32; 38:4; 62:2. Since human beings walk the earth, human messengers are sent to them. The phrase *yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna* — walking about in settled habitation, at ease — describes the ordinary condition of dwelling, moving, living. A messenger must share that condition to address it.

**The Practical Reason**

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī add the practical dimension: angels are not perceptible to most human beings, and a messenger who cannot be perceived cannot convey. Compare 6:8–9:

> *"And they say: If only an angel had been sent down to him! But if We sent down an angel, the matter would be decided, and they would be given no respite. And if We had made him an angel, We would have made him in the form of a man, and We would have confused for them what they are confusing."*

The last clause is decisive: an angel sent as a messenger would have to appear in human form, at which point the objection would recur unchanged. Gabriel appeared to Mary *"in the likeness of a perfect man"* (19:17) precisely because that is the form in which a message can be received.

****The Alternative Universe, and What It Proves****

The verse completes the principle stated at verse 93 by describing the counterfactual explicitly. *Wa law kāna fī al-arḍi malāʾikatun yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna la-nazzalnā ʿalayhim mina al-samāʾi malakan rasūlā* — and had there been on earth angels walking in settled habitation, We would have sent down to them from heaven an angel as a messenger. The verse thus constructs the alternative universe in which the objectors' demand would have been correct, and shows that even there the rule holds: the messenger is of the kind of the recipients. Al-Qurṭubī explains the phrase *yamshūna muṭmaʾinnīna* as denoting settled inhabitants, at ease upon the earth as human beings are — and the specification matters, because it excludes the angels who descend on errands. The verse is not about visiting angels but about a population. The reasoning is then airtight: if the inhabitants were angels, the messenger to them would be an angel, because only an angel could live among them, speak to them, and be imitated by them. The congruence is not a preference but a requirement of communication. Al-Rāzī draws the corollary that closes the objection of verse 92. The demand for an angelic messenger from human beings is a demand for a messenger who would be to them what an angel would be to angels — but the recipients are human, and a messenger of another order cannot be received by them. The demand is therefore not merely unreasonable but incoherent: it asks for the conditions of a different world while remaining in this one. The verse also answers an objection that had not yet been raised explicitly, which is why al-Zamakhsharī treats it as a preemptive answer. The objection would run: if the messenger must be of the recipients' kind, why are the angels messengers at all? The answer is that the angels are messengers *to the prophets*, not to the communities — 22:75, *"God chooses messengers from the angels and from mankind"* — and the communication from angel to prophet is of a different order than the communication from prophet to people, which is why the prophets experience it as a weight and the people receive it as speech. 26:193–194 describes the descent: *"The trustworthy Spirit brought it down upon thy heart, that thou mayest be among the warners."* The practical consequence of the two verses together is a rule for every form of teaching. The teacher who is unlike his students in kind cannot reach them, and the students who demand a teacher of another kind are demanding not to be taught. This is why the Qur'an insists on the Prophet's ﷺ humanity with such persistence — it is not a limitation to be excused but the condition of the message's arrival, and 12:109 states the rule in its general form: *"We sent not before thee except men to whom We revealed, from the people of the towns."*

**The Argument's Completion**

Verses 94–95 answer the objection completely. If the messenger were an angel, he could not be perceived and could not be followed; if he were an angel in human form, the objection would return; and since human beings are what inhabit the earth, a human messenger is the only coherent possibility. The objection therefore proves the opposite of what it intends: the humanity of the messenger is not a defect but a necessity. The Qur'an states the same conclusion at 6:9: the confusion the objectors claim to suffer is one they have created, and the arrangement they demand would not resolve it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:96

> **Say, "God suffices as a Witness between you and me. Verily, of His servants He is Aware, Seeing."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Witness Invoked**

*Qul kafā bi-Allāhi shahīdan baynī wa baynakum* — "Say: God suffices as a witness between me and you." The formula *kafā bi-* — "it suffices" — recurs throughout the sūrah (vv. 14, 17, 25, 65, 96) and expresses the sufficiency of God for a specific function. Here the function is *shahādah*, testimony. The same invocation appears at 6:19: *"Say: What thing is greatest in testimony? Say: God is a witness between me and you"*; and at 4:79, 4:166, 13:43, 29:52, 46:8, 48:28. The same invocation appears in another sūrah, with the addition that states the ground of the testimony:

> *"Say: God suffices as a Witness between me and you. He knows what is in the heavens and the earth."* (29:52)

**Why a Witness Is Needed**

The situation is a dispute in which the two parties disagree about what has been communicated and how it has been received. The Prophet ﷺ says he conveyed; the Quraysh say he fabricated or is deceived. There is no human arbiter — the Quraysh are the judges in their own case. The verse resolves the impasse by invoking a witness who is present to both parties' interiors.

**"Verily, of His Servants He Is Aware, Seeing"**

*Innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihi khabīran baṣīrā* — the same pair that closed verses 17 and 30. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the double function: consolation for the Prophet ﷺ, who is fully known and therefore fully vindicated; and threat for the disbelievers, who are fully known and therefore fully accountable. The two functions are inseparable because they are the same fact seen from two sides. That God is aware of His servants is comfort to the one who has been misunderstood and terror to the one who has misrepresented. The verse does not choose between the two readings; it states the fact and leaves each party to locate itself.

****The Second Sufficiency, and the Knowledge of the Servants****

The verse repeats the formula of sufficiency and adds a specification. *Qul kafā bi-Allāhi shahīdan baynī wa baynakum* — say: sufficient is God as a witness between me and you. The repetition of the formula from verse 94 within two verses is unusual in the Qur'an and indicates emphasis, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the second occurrence is shorter, which sharpens rather than weakens it. The added clause specifies the content of the divine witness: *innahu kāna bi-ʿibādihī khabīran baṣīrā* — He is, of His servants, All-Aware, All-Seeing. The word *ʿibād*, servants, is the term the Qur'an uses of human beings in their relation of dependence, and its appearance here is significant: the ones being witnessed are not described as believers and disbelievers but as servants, which places both parties in the same relation and removes the possibility of one party's claiming a standing the other lacks. The two names divide the knowledge between the hidden and the manifest, and the division is what makes the testimony sufficient. *Khabīr* denotes acquaintance with the inner reality — the motive, the intention, the state of the heart; *baṣīr* denotes the seeing of what is outward. A human witness can testify to the second and not to the first, which is why human testimony requires corroboration and divine testimony does not. 5:117 records the Prophet ʿĪsā making exactly this distinction: *"I was a witness over them while I was among them, but when Thou didst take me, Thou wast the Watcher over them — and Thou art Witness over all things."* The verse's function within the argument is to close the sequence begun at verse 90. The demands have been answered, the objection to the messenger's humanity has been turned into a proof, and the case has been referred to a competent witness. The Qur'an does not leave the messenger to argue the case further, and the referral is not a retreat but a conclusion: there is nothing left to establish, because the establishment is not in the messenger's hands. The practical application of the verse is available to anyone who has been required to prove his sincerity to people who have decided against it. The effort to establish sincerity by demonstration is not merely difficult but structurally unavailable, since sincerity is inward and demonstration is outward — which is why the Qur'an's repeated answer to such demands is not more evidence but the naming of the Witness. 29:52 makes the same move at the close of an argument, and 46:8 poses the question that the verse answers: *"Say: do you see what you call upon besides God? Show me what they have created in the earth."*

**Closing the Debate Before a Witness**

Placed at the end of the exchange about the demands for signs, the verse closes the debate. The demands have been answered (vv. 90–93), the objection to the messenger's humanity has been answered (vv. 94–95), and now the dispute itself is referred to a Witness. There is nothing further to argue. The Qur'an frequently ends a polemical exchange this way — not by producing a knockout argument but by locating the argument before God.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:97

> **Whomsoever God guides, he is rightly guided; and whomsoever He leads astray, thou wilt find no protectors for them apart from Him. And We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces—blind, dumb, and deaf—their refuge shall be Hell. Every time it abates, We shall increase for them a blazing flame.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sovereignty of Guidance**

*Wa man yahdi Allāhu fa-huwa al-muhtad* — "whomsoever God guides, he is the rightly guided." The construction is definitive: the one God guides *is* guided, without qualification, and no one can undo it. The converse follows: *wa man yuḍlil fa-lan tajida lahum awliyāʾa min dūnihi* — "and whomsoever He leads astray, thou wilt find no protectors for them apart from Him." The commentators divide on the interpretation, and the division is one of the oldest in Islamic theology. Some read the verse as indicating that God alone determines guidance and misguidance. Others, following the reading of 7:178, hold that God leads astray only as a consequence of prior disbelief and iniquity.
