The Qur'an supports the second reading at 61:5: *"And when they turned aside, God turned aside their hearts"*; and at 2:26: *"He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby, and He misleads none but the transgressors."* The mainstream Sunni position holds both: God's guidance and misguidance are real and sovereign, and they are exercised in accordance with His knowledge of the human being's own choice. The verse's function here is not to settle the metaphysics but to end the Prophet's ﷺ anxiety: guidance is not his to produce, and its absence in some is not his failure.

**"Upon Their Faces"**

*Wa naḥshuruhum yawma al-qiyāmati ʿalā wujūhihim* — "and We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces." The image is of being dragged face-down. Al-Bukhārī (4760) records the exchange it provoked:

> *"A man said: 'O Messenger of God, how will the disbeliever be gathered upon his face?' He said: 'Is the One who made him walk upon his feet in this world not able to make him walk upon his face on the Day of Resurrection?'"*

The answer is an argument from the greater to the lesser: the One who established upright walking can reverse it. The reversal is also symbolically exact — the one who walked arrogantly upon the earth (v. 37) is gathered upon his face.

**"Blind, Dumb, and Deaf"**

*ʿUmyan wa bukman wa ṣumman* — the three faculties of verse 36 — sight, speech, and hearing — are here removed. The Qur'an uses the same triad of those who refuse guidance at 2:18: *"Deaf, dumb, blind — so they will not return"*; and at 2:171; 6:39; 8:22. The connection to verse 36 is exact and the commentators note it: the faculties that were accountable, and were misused, are withdrawn. The person who would not see, would not listen truthfully, and would not speak truthfully arrives without any of the three.

****Gathered Upon Their Faces: The Completion of the Blindness****

The verse states the outcome and then describes it with an image of unusual severity. *Wa naḥshuruhum yawma al-qiyāmati ʿalā wujūhihim ʿumyan wa bukman wa ṣummā* — and We shall gather them on the Day of Resurrection upon their faces, blind, dumb, and deaf. The commentators offer two readings of *ʿalā wujūhihim* and both are attested. The literal: they will be dragged upon their faces, and al-Ṭabarī preserves the report that a companion asked how the disbeliever would walk upon his face and the Prophet ﷺ answered that the One who made him walk upon his feet in this world is able to make him walk upon his face in the next. The figurative: they will be gathered in humiliation, as one says of a man disgraced that he has fallen on his face. The triple privation — blind, dumb, deaf — completes the sequence begun at verse 72, where the one who is blind in this world is blind in the next, and at verse 46, where the coverings and the deafness are described. The Qur'an's account is therefore continuous rather than punitive: the faculties that were not used are not restored but confirmed in their atrophy. 20:124–126 makes the same point in dialogue, and the answer given to the blind man's question — *thus it is, Our signs came to thee and thou didst forget them* — locates the cause in the worldly life. The word *maʾwāhum*, their abode, denotes a place of dwelling and return rather than a temporary station, and the verse's final clause describes the fire's persistence: *kullamā khabat zidnāhum saʿīrā* — whenever it abates, We increase for them a blaze. The verb *khaba* denotes a fire that dims and loses its flame, and al-Qurṭubī explains the clause as the rekindling of a fire that would otherwise go out — the punishment is not a fixed quantity that is exhausted but a condition that is renewed. 4:56 states the same: *"Whenever their skins are roasted through, We shall change them for other skins, that they may taste the punishment."* The verse's placement after the argument about the messenger's humanity is significant. The objections of verses 90–95 are answered, and the verse supplies the consequence of persisting in them. The sequence is not a threat appended to an argument but the argument's completion: a message refused has an outcome, and the outcome is described in the same terms as the refusal — blindness for those who would not see, deafness for those who would not hear, and silence for those who would not speak the truth they recognized. The pastoral reading of the verse is the one the tradition drew from it. The descriptions of the Hereafter are not decorations but the completion of dispositions formed here, and the completion is what makes the present moment consequential. 39:56 supplies the corresponding appeal, addressed to the one still able to act: *"Lest a soul should say: alas, my grief for what I neglected of my duty toward God, and I was among the mockers."*

**"Every Time It Abates, We Increase for Them a Blaze"**

*Kullamā khabat zidnāhum saʿīrā* — *khabat* means to subside, to die down as a fire loses intensity; *saʿīr* is a blazing flame. The verse describes a fire that is continually replenished. The parallel is 4:56: *"Truly those who disbelieve in Our signs — We shall roast them in a Fire. Every time their skins are burned through, We shall replace them with other skins, that they may taste the punishment."* The mechanism in both verses is the same: the capacity to suffer is renewed precisely when it is exhausted. The punishment is not a finite quantity to be endured but a condition that does not deplete.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:98

> **That is their recompense for having disbelieved in Our signs. And they say, "What! When we are bones and dust, shall we indeed be resurrected as a new creation?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Recompense Stated as Earned**

*Dhālika jazāʾuhum bi-annahum kafarū bi-āyātinā* — "that is their recompense, because they disbelieved in Our signs." The word *jazāʾ* — recompense — is the same used at verse 63 of Hell as Iblīs's and his followers' recompense. The preposition *bi-anna* gives the reason: the punishment is *because of* the disbelief, not in addition to it or independent of it. This is the Qur'an's consistent structure of justice, stated at verse 7 and repeated at 10:44, 16:33, 3:117, and 29:40: the punishment is the consequence of the act, not an arbitrary imposition. The verse's function is to close off the complaint that the punishment is disproportionate — it is measured by the thing that produced it. The Qur'an states the consequence of disbelieving in the signs in a single verse:

> *"Those are they who disbelieve in the signs of their Lord and in the meeting with Him; so their works have come to naught, and We shall assign no weight to them on the Day of Resurrection."* (18:105)

**The Return of the Skeptical Question**

*Wa qālū a-idhā kunnā ʿiẓāman wa rafātan a-innā la-mabʿūthūna khalqan jadīdā* — the identical objection from verse 49 returns, word for word. The repetition is significant and the commentators note it. The Qur'an does not repeat because it has run out of material; it repeats because the objection recurs, and because the answer given at verses 50–51 remains the answer. The recurrence also makes a point about the objectors: they have not advanced. After the argument from stone and iron, after the identification of the Originator, after the discussion of guidance and misguidance, the same question returns in the same words. This is what verse 89 described — *most of mankind refuse aught but disbelief* — and what verse 41 described as being increased in nothing but aversion. The refusal is not an intellectual position that could be updated by evidence; it is a settled state.

****The Objection Repeated Verbatim: The Argument's Structural Closure****

The verse repeats the objection of verse 49 almost word for word, and the repetition is the sūrah's most striking structural device. *Wa qālū a-idhā kunnā ʿiẓāman wa rifātan a-innā la-mabʿūthūna khalqan jadīdā* — and they say: what, when we are bones and dust, shall we indeed be raised up as a new creation? The same three elements return — the bones, the powdered remains, the new creation — and the same incredulous interrogative opens the sentence. The effect of the verbatim repetition is to mark the objection as unanswered by the objector rather than by the Qur'an. Between verse 49 and verse 98 the Qur'an has supplied the argument from origination (vv. 50–51), the collapse of duration (v. 52), the account of the Day (vv. 71–72, 97), and the demonstration from the greater creation. The objection returns unchanged, which is itself the finding: the difficulty was never argumentative. 6:109–110 states the mechanism explicitly: *"And even if We sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them face to face, they would not believe unless God willed — but most of them are ignorant."* The verse's answer is the same one given at verse 51, now with the argument made explicit: *a-wa lam yaraw anna Allāha alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wa al-arḍa qādirun ʿalā an yakhluqa mithlahum* — have they not seen that God, Who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create the like of them? The argument is from the greater to the lesser, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly: 36:81, *"Is not He Who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them? Yes indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator."* 40:57 states the comparative explicitly: *"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most people know not."* 79:27–28 supplies the cosmological premise. The clause *mithlahum*, the like of them, has been read two ways by al-Ṭabarī, and both are held: the recreation of the same individuals, and the creation of others like them. The first is the reading the resurrection requires, and the second strengthens the argument rather than weakening it, since a being able to produce a like from nothing is a fortiori able to restore what He already made. The verse's placement completes the sūrah's argument about the Hereafter, which began at verse 10 with the denial of it and returns at verses 49, 52, 72, 97, and here. The repetition of the objection at both ends of the section, with the argument between them, is the Qur'an's method of demonstrating that the obstacle is not intellectual — and the demonstration is made not by assertion but by the objector's own unchanged words.

**Why the Qur'an Repeats Objections Verbatim**

The practice has a pedagogical function. The reader encounters the objection in its original form and its answer, and then encounters the objection again with the answer already in hand. The second encounter is a test of the reader: will he recognize it, and will he recognize the answer? The Qur'an's repetitions are exercises in recognition, and they train the reader to identify a recurring argument rather than to treat each appearance as new.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:99

> **Have they not considered that God, Who created the heavens and the earth, has the power to create the like of them? And He has ordained for them a term, about which there is no doubt. Yet the wrongdoers refuse aught but disbelief.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From the Greater Creation**

*Awa lam yaraw anna Allāha alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa qādirun ʿalā an yakhluqa mithlahum* — "Have they not seen that God, Who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create the like of them?" The argument is a standard *a fortiori*: if the greater has been accomplished, the lesser follows. The Qur'an states the same argument at 40:57:

> *"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most mankind know not."*

And at 46:33; 21:30; 29:19; 14:19. The heavens and the earth are, by any measure, greater objects than a human body; the One who made them is not limited by the smaller task.

**"And He Has Ordained for Them a Term"**

*Wa jaʿala lahum ajalan lā rayba fīh* — *ajal* is an appointed term, a fixed deadline. The commentators note that everything with God is according to a measure (13:8; 15:21; 25:2), and therefore every creature has a decreed term — its lifespan and its eventual judgment. Terms are decreed for individuals (6:60; 11:3; 19:75), for communities (7:34), and for the world itself (30:8; 46:3). The phrase *lā rayba fīh* — "about which there is no doubt" — asserts the certainty of the term. Compare 3:9, 25; 4:87; 6:12; 22:7; 40:59; 45:6.

**The Function of the Term in the Argument**

The mention of the appointed term completes the resurrection argument. The objectors asked "when?" (v. 51). The answer is that a term has been set, that it is certain, and that its exact moment is not disclosed. The combination is precisely calibrated: certainty removes the excuse of doubt, and non-disclosure removes the possibility of postponement. A person who knows the deadline is certain but not the date cannot plan around it.

****From the Greater to the Lesser: The Argument That Requires Only Sight****

The verse opens with the interrogative that introduces the Qur'an's most frequently used argument, and its form is significant: *a-wa lam yaraw* — have they not seen? The appeal is not to a text or a tradition but to observation, and the thing observed is the one nobody disputes. *Inna Allāha alladhī khalaqa al-samāwāti wa al-arḍa la-qādirun ʿalā an yakhluqa mithlahum* — God, Who created the heavens and the earth, is able to create the like of them. Al-Rāzī states the inference in its logical form: the one who originates the greater is able to originate the lesser, since the lesser requires nothing that the greater did not already require, and more. The Qur'an uses the argument repeatedly and always in the same register. 36:81: *"Is not He Who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them? Yes indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator."* 40:57 states the comparative explicitly: *"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most people know not."* 79:27–28 supplies the premise in the form of a question: *"Are you the harder to create, or the heaven which He built?"* 46:33 poses it as a challenge: *"Do they not see that God, Who created the heavens and the earth and was not wearied by their creation, is able to give life to the dead? Yes indeed — He has power over all things."* The force of the argument lies in what it does not require. It does not require the listener to accept a cosmology, a physics, or a revelation; it requires only that he acknowledge that the heavens and the earth exist and that he did not make them. From that acknowledgment the conclusion follows by the ordinary logic of capacity, and 51:20–21 states the whole method: *"And on the earth are signs for those who are certain, and in yourselves — do you not see?"*

The verse's second clause supplies the term and the refusal. *Wa jaʿala lahum ajalan lā rayba fīhi* — and He appointed for them a term about which there is no doubt. The commentators read the term either as the appointed moment of death, which no one doubts when it arrives, or as the Day of Resurrection, whose certainty the verse asserts against the denial of verses 49 and 98. The clause *fa-abā al-ẓālimūna illā kafūrā* — but the wrongdoers refuse, except in ingratitude — repeats the formula of verse 89 and closes the section with the same diagnosis: the evidence has been sufficient throughout, and the refusal is not a response to it. The verse therefore completes the resurrection argument by relocating it. The question is not whether resurrection is conceivable but whether the Maker of the heavens and the earth is able — and anyone who answers the second question has answered the first. This is why the Qur'an's eschatology is inseparable from its cosmology: the doctrine of the Hereafter stands or falls with the doctrine of creation, and the one who has never resolved the second has no ground on which to deny the first.

**"Yet the Wrongdoers Refuse Aught but Disbelief"**

*Fa-abā al-ẓālimūna illā kafūrā* — the same verb *abā* (to refuse) as in verse 89, but now applied to *al-ẓālimūn* — the wrongdoers — rather than to most of mankind. The narrowing is significant: the refusal is not a general human failing but the specific achievement of those who persist. The verse thus ends the resurrection debate where verse 89 ended the parable debate: not with a further argument but with a diagnosis. The argument has been given in full — from origination, from the greater creation, from the appointed term — and the refusal continues. The Qur'an's conclusion is that the obstacle is not in the reasoning.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:100

> **Say, "Were you to possess the treasuries of my Lord's Mercy, you would surely withhold them, out of fear of spending. Man is ever miserly!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypothetical of Unlimited Wealth**

*Qul law antum tamlikūna khazāʾina raḥmati rabbī idhan la-amsaktum khashyata al-infāq* — "Say: If you possessed the treasuries of my Lord's mercy, then you would withhold, out of fear of spending." The hypothetical is deliberately extreme. The treasuries are God's — infinite by definition — and the verse supposes them transferred to human ownership. The predicted outcome is not generosity but *imsāk*, withholding, driven by *khashyat al-infāq*, the fear of expenditure. Al-Ṭabarī reads the treasuries as worldly goods within the divinely apportioned provision (*rizq*); al-Zamakhsharī extends it to all blessings, worldly and other. Either way the claim is the same: infinite resources in human hands would not produce infinite giving.

**Why the Prediction Holds**

Al-Rāzī gives the reasoning, and it is one of the most penetrating psychological observations in the exegetical tradition. Human beings are, as creatures, in a state of *existential poverty* — they do not possess their own existence, let alone their provision. This ontological need produces an ineradicable fear of want. And the fear of want produces miserliness, because the miserly person is not guarding a quantity but guarding against a possibility he cannot exclude. The reasoning explains a phenomenon everyone has observed: the accumulation of wealth does not reliably reduce the fear of losing it. The person with little fears poverty; the person with much fears poverty differently but not less. If the fear were proportional to the amount, the wealthy would be the most generous; they are frequently the least.

**"Man Is Ever Miserly"**

*Wa kāna al-insānu qatūrā* — *qaṭūr* is the intensive of one who restrains, who holds back; it denotes a settled disposition toward withholding. The Qur'an states the same at 4:128, *"and souls are prone to avarice (ḥuṣṣat al-anfusu al-shuḥḥ)"*; and at 70:19–21:

> *"Truly man was created anxious — when evil touches him, impatient; and when good touches him, withholding (manūʿā)."*

**The Contrast With God**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī draw the contrast that gives the verse its theological weight: God, who has no need, gives without limit — to the obedient and the disobedient alike. The miserliness is not a property of wealth but of the *needy holder* of wealth. Since only God is free of need (47:38, *"God is the Rich, and you are the poor"*), only God gives unconditionally. The verse is therefore not merely a criticism of stinginess but a diagnosis of its root. The remedy is not exhortation but the recognition of the actual ownership: the wealth one holds is not one's own, and the fear of losing what one does not own is a category error. This is the theological ground of zakāt — the compulsory alms — which is not charity but the return of a portion that was never the holder's.

****The Treasuries of Mercy, and the Scarcity Projected Onto God****

The verse is a psychological observation stated as a conditional, and its precision is what makes it memorable. *Qul law antum tamlikūna khazāʾina raḥmati rabbī idhan la-amsaktum khashyata al-infāq* — say: if you possessed the treasuries of my Lord's mercy, you would surely withhold them, for fear of spending. The commentators note the reasoning given: the withholding is not from malice but from the fear of exhaustion, which is the reasoning of a creature who has watched his own stores diminish. Al-Ṭabarī records the occasion: the Quraysh said to the Prophet ﷺ that his Lord was stingy, and the verse answered by locating the stinginess where it belonged. Al-Qurṭubī explains the mechanism the verse exposes: the human being measures God's giving by his own, and since his own giving depletes him, he assumes the same of God. This is projection in the strict sense — the attribution of one's own nature to another — and the Qur'an identifies it as the source of a specific theological error: a God imagined as a limited dispenser who must economize, and whose gifts are therefore competitive, so that a gift to one is a loss to another. The verse's answer is implicit in the word *khazāʾin*, treasuries, and the Qur'an states the inexhaustibility explicitly elsewhere. 18:109 gives the image: *"Say: if the sea were ink for the words of my Lord, the sea would be exhausted before the words of my Lord were exhausted, even if We brought the like of it as a further supply."* 34:39 states the principle in the register of provision: *"And whatever you spend of a thing, He will replace it, and He is the best of providers."* 57:29 supplies the conclusion: *"...that the people of the Book may know that they have no power over anything of God's bounty, and that bounty is in the hand of God; He gives it to whom He wills."* The ethical consequence is direct, and 47:38 draws it: *"Here you are — those called upon to spend in the way of God; and among you are those who withhold. And whoever withholds withholds only from himself, and God is the Rich and you are the poor."* The one who withholds from fear of scarcity has misidentified who is being depleted, since the depletion is his own and the supply is not. The verse's modern application requires no translation. Every system organized on the assumption that provision is finite produces hoarding, and the hoarding is then offered as evidence that provision was finite — the belief generating its own confirmation. The verse identifies the error at its root, which is not economic but theological: the assumption that the source is limited, and therefore that generosity is a loss. 3:180 states the inversion: *"Let not those who withhold what God has given them of His bounty think that it is good for them; nay, it is evil for them."*

**The Verse's Answer to the Demands**

Al-Rāzī notes the verse's context: it answers the demands of verses 90–93. The Quraysh asked for springs, gardens, and gold. The verse's answer is that even unlimited provision would not change them, because the defect is not in the amount. This is the same answer given at verse 89 in a different domain: the insufficiency is not in what is offered but in the one receiving it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:101

> **And We indeed gave unto Moses nine clear signs. So ask the Children of Israel. When he came unto them, Pharaoh said, "Truly I think that you, O Moses, are bewitched!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Nine Signs**

*Wa laqad ātaynā Mūsā tisʿa āyātin bayyināt* — "and We gave Moses nine clear signs." The parallel is 27:12, where Moses is told to cast down his staff and put his hand in his bosom:

> *"among nine signs to Pharaoh and his people."*

The commentators do not agree on the enumeration, and al-Rāzī explicitly holds that the verse should not be read as limiting Moses to nine signs. Combining the lists given by Ibn Kathīr, al-Jaṣṣāṣ, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī, the candidates include: the staff becoming a serpent (7:109; 20:19–21; 26:32); the hand becoming white (7:108; 20:22; 27:12); the untying of the knot from his tongue (20:27–28); the plagues of flood, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood (7:133), preceded by drought and scarcity (7:130); the parting of the sea (2:50; 26:63); the destruction of Pharaoh's wealth in answer to Moses's prayer (10:88); the streams from the rock (2:60; 7:160); the manna and quails (2:57; 20:80); and the mountain raised over the Israelites at Sinai (2:63; 7:171).

**The Alternative Reading: Nine Commandments**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī record a ḥadīth identifying the nine signs with nine prohibitions given to Israel: associating partners with God, theft, adultery, murder, sorcery, usury, slandering chaste women, fleeing a just battle, and violating the Sabbath. Al-Tirmidhī (3144) preserves the report from Ṣafwān ibn ʿAssāl, in which a Jew asks the Prophet ﷺ about the nine signs and he enumerates them. This reading is of considerable interest because it connects the nine signs to the very moral code laid out in verses 22–39 of this sūrah — and the overlap is extensive. The reading makes the verse's placement meaningful: the sūrah has given its own code, and now points to the code given to Moses.

**"So Ask the Children of Israel"**

*Fa-sʾal banī isrāʾīla idh jāʾahum* — the command to ask is an appeal to a corroborating source available to the audience. The People of the Book possessed the account of Moses; the Qur'an invites verification rather than asserting without evidence. The same appeal appears at 10:94: *"And if thou art in doubt concerning what We sent down to thee, ask those who read the Book before thee."*

****Nine Signs to One People, and the Verdict They Returned****

The verse supplies the case study that answers the demands of verses 90–93, and it is the most sign-dense episode in the Qur'an. *Wa laqad ātaynā Mūsā tisʿa āyātin bayyināt* — and We gave Moses nine clear signs. Al-Ṭabarī catalogues the identifications from Ibn ʿAbbās and others: the staff, the white hand, the years of famine, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, and either the parting of the sea or the drowning of Pharaoh. The Qur'an enumerates the plagues at 7:130–133 and the two principal signs at 27:12 and 20:17–23, so the nine are assembled from several passages rather than listed in one. The verse's function is the comparison it forces. The people who received the greatest concentration of clear signs in the Qur'an's recorded history did not believe, and the response they gave is recorded in the same breath: *fa-qāla lahu Firʿawnu innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā masḥūrā* — and Pharaoh said to him: I think thee, O Moses, bewitched. The word is the same one used of the Prophet ﷺ at verse 47, and the identity of the accusation across three thousand years is the verse's finding. 51:52 states the general rule: *"Likewise no messenger came to those before them but they said: a sorcerer, or a madman."* The comparison therefore answers the Quraysh's demands completely. They asked for a spring, a garden, a house of gold, a ladder to heaven, and a piece of the sky; Moses was given a staff that became a serpent, a hand that shone white, and five plagues in succession, and the verdict returned was that he was bewitched. Al-Rāzī draws the conclusion: the quantity of signs does not determine the response, since the response is determined by the disposition that receives them. This is the same finding the sūrah reached at verses 59 and 89, now with a documented case. The verse's placement immediately after the statement about the treasuries of mercy is significant. Having established that God's bounty is not depleted by giving, the sūrah shows that it is not *earned* by demanding either: the most spectacular giving in the record went to the people who most rejected it, and the rejection did not interrupt the giving until the appointed term arrived. The practical consequence for the reader is a caution about the logic of demand. The person who will not believe until a condition is met has usually set a condition he will not honor, and the test of this is the history of his previous conditions. The Qur'an's answer to such a person is not another sign but the record of what happened the last time.

**"Truly I Think That You, O Moses, Are Bewitched"**

*Fa-qāla innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā masḥūrā* — the same accusation recorded against the Prophet ﷺ at verse 47. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī note the readings: Pharaoh may be accusing Moses of being a sorcerer (the sense at 10:76; 20:57; 26:34; 27:13), or of being himself deceived by sorcery — misled and confused. The recurrence is the point. The accusation made in Makkah in the seventh century was made in Egypt centuries earlier, in the same words, by a ruler facing the same demand. The Qur'an's method is to show that the opposition to prophecy is not a reasoned evaluation but a repeating reflex — which means that its content tells one nothing about the truth of the message, and its recurrence should alert the hearer to its character.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:102

> **He said, "You certainly know that no one has sent these down as clear portents, save the Lord of the heavens and the earth. And truly I think that you, O Pharaoh, are doomed."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Moses's Counter-Accusation**

*Qāla laqad ʿalimta mā anzala hāʾulāʾi illā rabbu al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍi baṣāʾir* — "He said: You certainly know that none has sent these down as *baṣāʾir* except the Lord of the heavens and the earth." The verb *ʿalimta* — "you have known" — is decisive. Moses does not argue that Pharaoh *should* recognize the signs; he states that Pharaoh *does*. The knowledge is present and the denial is against it. *Baṣāʾir* is the plural of *baṣīrah*, insight, perception, that by which one sees with the inner eye. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the signs are called *baṣāʾir* because they are meant to produce perception. Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī gloss them as clear proofs; al-Qurṭubī as clear indications of God's power and oneness.

**The Qur'an's Confirmation of Pharaoh's Inner Knowledge**

The claim Moses makes is confirmed elsewhere. 27:14: *"And they denied them, though their souls were convinced of them (wa staqannathā anfusuhum), wrongfully and arrogantly."* The phrase is remarkable: the conviction was in the soul, and the denial was in the speech. This is one of the Qur'an's most serious claims about disbelief: that it is frequently not ignorance but *suppression*. Al-Rāzī develops the point: it should be obvious to anyone using his intellect that such signs could only be divine, and only willful stubbornness prevents the acknowledgment. The person who denies is not concluding; he is deciding.

****Baṣāʾir: The Signs That Give Sight, and the Seeing That Was Refused****

The verse records Moses's reply and in doing so names what the signs were. *Wa laqad ʿalimta mā anzala hāʾulāʾi illā rabbu al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi baṣāʾir* — and thou knowest that none sent down these except the Lord of the heavens and the earth, as clear evidences giving insight. The word *baṣāʾir* is the plural of *baṣīrah*, from the root of sight, and it denotes evidences so clear that they confer insight rather than merely presenting data. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the force of the word: a *baṣīrah* is not a thing to be looked at but a thing that makes one see. The clause *wa laqad ʿalimta* — and thou knowest — is the verse's most striking feature, since it attributes knowledge to Pharaoh. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both hold that Moses is telling Pharaoh what Pharaoh already knows: that the signs came from God. The Qur'an states the same fact about Pharaoh and his people at 27:14, and the statement is a precise psychological claim: *"And they denied them, while their souls were certain of them (wa istayqanat-hā anfusuhum), out of wrongdoing and arrogance."* The certainty was present and the denial was simultaneous with it, and the two causes named — *ẓulm*, wrongdoing, and *ʿulūww*, arrogance — locate the denial in the will rather than in the intellect. The reply Pharaoh gives is the verse's confirmation of its own thesis: *wa innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā mathbūrā* — and I think thee, O Moses, destroyed, ruined. The word *mathbūr* is from *th-b-r*, and the commentators gloss it as one cut off from good, or annihilated, or turned away from the truth. The response to certainty is not argument but a prediction of the certain man's destruction, which is what a person says when he has nothing to say. The verse's function in the sūrah is to complete the case begun at verse 101. The signs were given; they were *baṣāʾir*, evidences that confer sight; they were recognized by the one who rejected them; and the rejection proceeded anyway. The Qur'an therefore establishes that the obstacle to faith is not epistemic, and it establishes this not by assertion but by the testimony of the rejecter himself. 6:110 states the general mechanism: *"And We shall turn their hearts and their sight, as they did not believe in it the first time, and We shall leave them in their insolence, wandering blindly."* The practical content of the verse is its account of what happens when a person recognizes a truth he does not intend to accept. He does not become uncertain; he becomes dismissive, and the dismissal takes the form of a prediction about the one who speaks. Anyone who has watched this exchange recognizes it immediately, and the verse supplies its name: the soul was certain, and the wrongdoing and the arrogance were what spoke.

**"And Truly I Think That You, O Pharaoh, Are Doomed"**

*Wa innī la-aẓunnuka yā Firʿawnu mathbūrā* — the structure mirrors Pharaoh's accusation exactly. Pharaoh said *innī la-aẓunnuka yā Mūsā masḥūrā*; Moses replies *wa innī la-aẓunnuka yā Firʿawnu mathbūrā*. The same formula, the same verb of thinking, the same vocative — and opposite contents. *Mathbūr* means destroyed, ruined; al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī gloss it also as prevented from all goodness, or devoid of reason. The same word appears at 25:13–14 in the address to the inhabitants of Hell:

> *"Call not this Day for one destruction, but call for many destructions."*

The exchange is a formal duel of verdicts, and the Qur'an's narrative will confirm which was correct. The symmetry is rhetorically perfect: each party states what he believes about the other, and the drowning settles it.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:103

> **And he desired to incite them from the land; so We drowned him and those with him all together.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Verb as the Quraysh's Plot**

*Fa-arāda an yastafizzahum mina al-arḍ* — "and he desired to *yastafizza* them from the land." The verb is the same tenth form used at verse 76 of the Quraysh's plot against the Prophet ﷺ, and at verse 64 of Satan's incitement. The Qur'an's use of the identical word binds the three episodes into one pattern: the tyrant's project, the adversary's project, and the ancient precedent. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note the range: to remove them by exile or by killing, or to unsettle them by harassment until they leave.

**The Reversal**

*Fa-aghraqnāhu wa man maʿahu jamīʿā* — "so We drowned him and those with him, all together." The verse is a single sentence of complete reversal: the one who intended to expel was himself destroyed, along with everyone with him. Al-Rāzī states the principle: *evil plotting besets none but its authors*, citing 35:43, *"And they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of plotters"*; and 40:45:

> *"And God guarded him from the evils of what they plotted, and Pharaoh's people were encompassed by an evil punishment."*

The drowning is recorded at 2:50, 7:136, 8:54, 10:90, and 43:55. At 10:90 the Qur'an preserves Pharaoh's final words: *"I believe that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe"* — and the answer: *"Now? And thou didst disobey before, and wast among the corrupters?"* The faith arrived at the moment it ceased to be faith, which is the doctrine verse 52 of this sūrah stated.

****The Attempted Expulsion of Moses, and the Rule It Confirms****

The verse completes the case study, and its verb is the one that ties it to the sūrah's own situation. *Fa-arāda an yastafizzahum min al-arḍ* — and he intended to drive them out of the land. The verb *yastafizz* is the same one used at verse 76 of the Quraysh's intention toward the Prophet ﷺ: *wa in kādū la-yastafizzūnaka min al-arḍ*. The lexical identity is not incidental; it is the sūrah's method of establishing that the two cases are instances of one pattern. The outcome is stated in the same breath: *fa-aghraqnāhu wa man maʿahu jamīʿā* — so We drowned him and all who were with him. The verse does not elaborate, since the story was already known to its audience in full; it supplies only the outcome, because the outcome is what the pattern requires. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the drowning is mentioned here rather than the plagues because it is the completion of the expulsion: the one who attempted to drive out the messenger was removed from the land himself. The verse's function is to confirm the *sunnah* stated at verses 76 and 77. The rule was given first — a people who expels its messenger will not remain after it except a little, and thou wilt find no change in the way of God — and the case is given second, so that the rule is not an assertion but a generalization from a documented instance. This is the Qur'an's historical method throughout: it states the pattern and then supplies the evidence, and it does so in that order because the pattern is what the reader needs in order to read the evidence correctly. The parallel between the two cases is close enough to be itemized. In both, the messenger was told to produce material proofs he could not produce. In both, he was accused of being bewitched. In both, the opponents proposed his expulsion. In both, the expulsion was attempted and the expellers were removed. The one difference is the one the sūrah has already explained at verse 59: the destruction of the Quraysh was withheld so that they might believe, and most of them did. The verse therefore supplies what the argument of verses 90–98 required — not another sign but a precedent. The reader who has followed the demands, the objection to the messenger's humanity, and the denial of the resurrection is given a case in which all three occurred together and the outcome is recorded. 40:21 states the method as an instruction: *"Have they not traveled in the earth and seen what was the end of those who were before them? They were greater than them in strength and in the traces they left in the earth, but God seized them for their sins, and they had no defender against God."*

**The Warning to Quraysh**

The commentators are explicit about the verse's function in a Makkan sūrah: Pharaoh's fate is a warning to the Quraysh, who were persecuting the Prophet ﷺ and his followers in the same manner and with the same intention. The parallel is exact — a ruler seeking to expel a messenger — and the parallel's outcome is what the verse offers. The verse is also a comfort. The persecuted community in Makkah, watching a power far greater than itself, is shown the precedent: the power that intended expulsion was drowned, and the expelled inherited the land.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:104

> **And We said thereafter unto the Children of Israel, "Dwell in the land. And when the promise of the Hereafter comes to pass, We shall bring you as a mixed assembly."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Dwell**

*Wa qulnā min baʿdihi li-banī isrāʾīla uskunū al-arḍ* — "and We said after it to the Children of Israel: dwell in the land." The commentators identify the land variously: parts of Egypt now emptied of Pharaoh's people (al-Zamakhsharī), Palestine/Canaan (al-Ṭabarī), or both (al-Qurṭubī). Compare 7:137:

> *"And We made the people who were oppressed to inherit the eastern and western parts of the land which We had blessed."*

The command is a reversal of the expulsion Pharaoh intended. He wanted them out; they are told to dwell. The verse thus completes the reversal begun in verse 103.

**The Glad Tiding for the Prophet ﷺ**

Ibn Kathīr reads the verse as containing a promise to the Prophet ﷺ of the future conquest of Makkah. The reasoning is typological: as the oppressed community inherited the land after the destruction of the oppressor, so the Muslim community will inherit Makkah. The reading is supported by the verse's placement in a Makkan sūrah addressed to a persecuted community, and it corresponds to what occurred in 8/630.

**"We Shall Bring You as a Mixed Assembly"**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu al-ākhirati jiʾnā bikum lafīfā* — *lafīf* means a mixed multitude, an assembly gathered from various sources. Al-Rāzī explains it as the gathering of all types among them — noble and base, obedient and disobedient, pious and iniquitous, strong and weak. Ibn Kathīr adds the reading that God will bring them together with their enemies for judgment.

****Dwell in the Land, and the Gathering of the Lafīf****

The verse records the promise given after the drowning, and its two clauses describe two returns. *Wa qulnā min baʿdihī li-banī Isrāʾīla skunū al-arḍa* — and We said after him to the Children of Israel: dwell in the land. Al-Ṭabarī explains the command as the settlement in the land from which Pharaoh had attempted to expel them, which completes the reversal the previous verse established: the expellers drowned and the expelled inherited. The second clause is the verse's difficult and much-discussed part: *fa-idhā jāʾa waʿdu al-ākhirati jiʾnā bikum lafīfā* — and when the promise of the Hereafter comes, We shall bring you as a *lafīf*. The word *lafīf* denotes a mingled multitude, groups brought together from different places and mixed, and al-Qurṭubī glosses it as assembled from various lands, none of them remaining where they were. The exegetes divided over which promise is meant. One reading, held by a number of the Salaf, takes *waʿdu al-ākhirah* as the second of the two corruptions and returns described at verses 4–8 of this sūrah, so that the verse describes a historical gathering. The other, favored by Ibn Kathīr, takes it as the Day of Resurrection, in which case the gathering is the general one and the word *lafīf* describes the mixed assembly of all peoples. The two readings are not mutually exclusive, and the Qur'an's use of the phrase *waʿdu al-ākhirah* elsewhere supports both. 17:7 uses *waʿdu al-ākhirah* of a historical event, and 30:43 and 20:15 use the parallel phrase of the Day. Al-Rāzī's resolution is that the verse is deliberately general, and that the mingled gathering described is true of the historical return and of the final one, since both involve the assembling of a dispersed people. The verse's function within the sūrah is to close the account of Moses begun at verse 101. The sequence — the nine signs, the accusation of sorcery, the attempted expulsion, the drowning, the settlement, and the final gathering — is a complete narrative in six verses, and it is given at this point because it is the evidence for the *sunnah* stated at verses 76–77. The reader has been told the rule and shown the instance, and the instance is now brought to its conclusion. The practical content of the verse is its account of reversal. The people driven out of a land were settled in it, and the one who drove them out was removed — which is the pattern the sūrah promised would repeat. 28:5–6 states the same promise in its fullest form: *"And We desired to confer a favor upon those who were oppressed in the earth, and to make them leaders, and to make them the inheritors, and to establish them in the earth."*

**Why the Mixture Matters**

The phrase connects this verse to the Israelite cycle at the beginning of the sūrah. Verses 2–8 described two episodes of corruption and two devastations, with mercy in between. The *lafīf* is the final form of that history: the community will not be gathered as a single moral unit but as a mixture, and the judgment will be individual. This is the same doctrine stated at verse 15 — *"no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another"* — and at verse 71, where every people is called by its imām and every individual reads his own book. The communal history does not determine the individual fate; the community arrives mixed, and each is separated. The verse thus closes the sūrah's treatment of Israel on a note of individual accountability, which is the sūrah's persistent theme from verse 13 onward: the omen fastened to each neck, the book read by each soul, the reckoning borne by no one else.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:105

> **In truth We sent it down, and in truth it descended. And We sent thee not, save as a bearer of glad tidings and as a warner,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Bi-l-Ḥaqq: The Double Assertion**

*Wa bi-l-ḥaqqi anzalnāhu wa bi-l-ḥaqqi nazal* — "and We sent it down in truth, and in truth it descended." The repetition of *bi-l-ḥaqq* with two different verbs — *anzalnā* (We sent down) and *nazala* (it descended) — is deliberate. Al-Ṭabarī explains the first as referring to the content: the Qur'an was sent down commanding justice and virtue and forbidding injustice and iniquity. Ibn Kathīr explains it as the impossibility of alteration — it was sent down in truth and therefore cannot be falsified. Al-Rāzī and others read the pair as covering both the origin and the arrival: it came from the Truth and it arrived as the Truth, unchanged in transit. The word *ḥaqq* carries the full weight of the divine Name *al-Ḥaqq* (22:6, 62; 20:114), which verse 81 invoked:

> *"Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished."*

The Qur'an's claim about itself is that it belongs to the category of the Real rather than the contingent — which is why verse 88 can challenge humanity and jinn to produce its like.

**"Save as a Bearer of Glad Tidings and a Warner"**

*Wa mā arsalnāka illā mubashshiran wa nadhīrā* — the two functions are the standard Qur'anic description of the prophetic office. Compare 2:119, *"Truly We sent thee with the truth, as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner"*; 5:19; 25:56; 34:28. And the same description applies to all previous messengers: 2:213, *"And God sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners"*; 4:165; 6:48; 18:56. *Mubashshir* is from *b-sh-r*, to bring good news that shows on the face — the same root as *bashar*, human being, which appeared at verse 93. *Nadhīr* is a warner, one who alerts to danger in time to avoid it.

****Bi-l-Ḥaqqi Nazalnā wa bi-l-Ḥaqqi Nazal: The Two Truths of Descent****

The verse makes a claim about the revelation in a doubled formula, and the doubling has been read precisely by the grammarians. *Wa bi-l-ḥaqqi anzalnāhu wa bi-l-ḥaqqi nazal* — and with the truth We sent it down, and with the truth it came down. Al-Zamakhsharī distinguishes the two: the first *bi-l-ḥaqq* describes the sending, meaning that it was sent for a true purpose and with true content; the second describes the arrival, meaning that it came down unchanged, having lost nothing and gained nothing in the descent. Al-Qurṭubī adds a third reading: that it descended with truth in the sense that its descent was accompanied by the evidences of its truth — the signs, the angels, and the confirmation in the hearts of the believers. The claim is therefore twofold and both parts are necessary. A revelation could be true in content and corrupted in transmission, or faithful in transmission and empty in content; the verse asserts both, and the second assertion is the one the Qur'an makes most insistently elsewhere. 15:9 states it as a promise: *"Indeed, We sent down the Reminder, and indeed We are its guardians."* 41:42 states it as a description: *"Falsehood approaches it not from before it nor from behind it — a revelation from the Wise, the Praised."* 85:21–22 supplies the shortest form: *"Nay, it is a glorious Qur'an, in a Preserved Tablet."* The second clause limits the office and completes the verse: *wa mā arsalnāka illā mubashshiran wa nadhīrā* — and We sent thee only as a bringer of good news and a warner. The two verbal nouns are the Qur'an's standard pair for the prophetic function, and the particle *illā* restricts the office to them. The verse therefore makes the same delimitation the sūrah made at verse 54 and will make at verse 105's close: the messenger conveys, and the outcome is not his. 34:28 states the same limit: *"And We sent thee not except to all mankind, as a bringer of good news and a warner — but most people know not."* The pairing of the two clauses is what gives the verse its force. The revelation is described as true in its origin and in its arrival, and the one who carries it is described as limited in his function. The two statements together prevent the two opposite errors: the message is not diminished by the messenger's limitation, and the messenger is not elevated by the message's truth. This is the structure the entire sūrah has maintained, from the *ʿabd* of verse 1 to the *basharan rasūlā* of verse 91. The verse's practical consequence is a rule for reading and for teaching. The authority of the message is not derived from the one who conveys it, so a weak conveyor does not weaken it and a strong one does not strengthen it; and the conveyor's duty is defined by the two nouns — to give good news and to warn — neither of which includes compelling the response. 88:21–22 states the limit in its plainest form: *"So remind; thou art only a reminder. Thou art not a dictator over them."*

**Why Both Functions Are Necessary**

The pairing is not a stylistic balance but a requirement. A message of glad tidings alone produces presumption; a message of warning alone produces despair, and both states are named as sins (12:87; 15:56). The prophetic office holds them together, and the proportion between them is itself part of the message. The verse also connects to verse 100 and to the whole preceding debate. The demanders asked for springs and gold — material proofs. The verse states what the messenger actually is: a conveyor of good news and warning. He is not a provider of worldly goods, and the demands were addressed to a function he does not hold.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:106

> **and [We sent it down] as a recitation We have divided in parts, that thou mayest recite it unto men in intervals, and We sent it down in successive revelations.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Qurʾānan Farraqnāhu**

*Wa qurʾānan farraqnāhu li-taqraʾahu ʿalā al-nāsi ʿalā mukthin* — "and a Qur'an We have divided, that thou mayest recite it to mankind at intervals." The verb *farraqnā* is the intensive of *f-r-q*, to separate, to distinguish, to divide; the intensive indicates a thorough, item-by-item division. The noun *mukth* means a pause, a deliberate interval, a lingering.

**The Two Modes of Descent**

Al-Jaṣṣāṣ and al-Ṭabarī explain the division as the successive, gradual revelation over the twenty-three years of the prophetic mission. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī record the tradition that the Qur'an was sent down as a whole to the lower heaven on the Night of Power (*Laylat al-Qadr* — one of the odd-numbered nights in the last ten days of Ramadan; see Sūrah 97), and then revealed to the Prophet ﷺ gradually. Some add that it was sent down as a whole into the Prophet's ﷺ heart, on the basis of 26:192–94, and then disclosed to his consciousness over the period of the mission. A third reading takes the division as the Qur'an's division into distinct sections — verses and sūrahs of unequal length.

**The Stated Reason: Recitation at Intervals**

The verse gives the purpose explicitly: *that thou mayest recite it to men at intervals*. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī explain the wisdom: gradual revelation allowed the community time to learn, memorize, understand, and implement. A book delivered complete would have been a text to be studied; a book delivered over twenty-three years became a life to be lived. The parallel verse at 25:32 answers the same objection from the Quraysh and adds a second reason:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'Why was the Qur'an not sent down to him all at once?' It is so, that We may make firm thy heart thereby. And We have recited it to thee in a measured pace (tartīlā)."*

Two reasons are thus given: the firming of the Prophet's ﷺ heart, and the measured delivery to the community.

**The Historical Effect**

The gradual revelation had consequences the tradition documents. Laws were revealed in stages — the prohibition of intoxicants unfolded over three revelations, from 2:219's acknowledgment of harm and benefit, to 4:43's restriction before prayer, to 5:90–91's prohibition. This staging is a documented feature of the revelation and reflects a pedagogy: a community's habits are not reversed by a single command but by a sequence. The verse's principle extends to the manner of teaching. The one who delivers everything at once produces information; the one who delivers it at intervals produces transformation. The Qur'an's own form is an argument for its method.

****Faradnāhu: The Deliberate Pace of the Revelation****

The verse describes the manner of the revelation, and its two verbs are both technical. *Wa Qurʾānan faradnāhu* — and a Qur'an which We have apportioned, divided. The verb *faraḍa* denotes the apportioning of a thing into determined shares, as in the fixed shares of inheritance, and al-Ṭabarī glosses it as divided into commands and prohibitions, promises and warnings, narratives and parables. The second verb supplies the manner: *wa nazzalnāhu tanzīlā* — and We sent it down by degrees, the doubled form and the cognate accusative both emphasizing the gradualness. The Qur'an states the reason for the gradualness explicitly at 25:32: *"And those who disbelieve say: why was not the Qur'an sent down to him all at once? Thus — that We may thereby make thy heart firm; and We have recited it in measured recitation (wa rattalnāhu tartīlā)."* The reasoning is given in two parts, and both concern formation rather than information. The heart is made firm by what is received in portions, since a revelation delivered all at once would be a weight the recipient could not carry and a text the community could not absorb. 2:106 supplies the second reason: the abrogation and replacement of rulings across the period, which allowed the law to be built in stages — the prohibition of alcohol is the standard case, delivered across three stages in three separate revelations. Al-Rāzī draws the pedagogical principle, and it is a general one: a community is formed by a text delivered over time rather than by a text delivered complete, because formation requires the interval in which the material is practiced. The historical fact is the evidence: the revelation came across twenty-three years, and the community that received it was not the same at the end as at the beginning. The sūrah's own placement of the prayer times at verse 78, in the middle of an argument, is an instance of the method — legislation arrives inside the polemic because the formation of the worshipper is the point of the argument. The verse's practical application extends beyond the revelation. Any body of knowledge that must change a person rather than merely inform him is better delivered in portions with intervals for practice than all at once, and the intervals are not delays but the mechanism. The tradition formalized this in the reports about the Companions learning ten verses at a time and not proceeding until they had learned the knowledge and the action contained in them — a practice that treats the interval as the substance of the learning. The verse therefore supplies the answer to a question the sūrah has already encountered: why the revelation did not come with a single overwhelming sign. It came instead in measured portions across twenty-three years, and the result was a transformation that a sign could not have produced.

**"And We Sent It Down in Successive Revelations"**

*Wa nazzalnāhu tanzīlā* — the cognate accusative intensifies: a true, deliberate, staged sending down. The verse closes by affirming that the gradualness is not an accident of circumstance but a divine intention.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:107

> **Say, "Believe in it, or believe not." Surely those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited unto them, fall down prostrate on their faces.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Indifference of Truth to Acceptance**

*Qul āminū bihi aw lā tuʾminū* — "Say: Believe in it, or do not believe." Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī explain the import: the belief or disbelief of the Quraysh has no effect on God. Compare 3:97, *"And whoever disbelieves — truly God is independent of all beings"*; 3:176; 11:57; 47:32. The formula is not a dismissal but a liberation — of the messenger. Having conveyed, he is instructed to state that the outcome does not depend on him and does not affect the truth of the message. The verse is the rhetorical counterpart to verse 54's:

> *"We have not sent thee as a guardian over them."*

**The Contrast Supplied**

*Inna alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma min qablihi idhā yutlā ʿalayhim yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni sujjadā* — "truly those who were given knowledge before it, when it is recited to them, fall down upon their chins, prostrating." The Quraysh's refusal is set against the response of those already possessed of scriptural knowledge. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Ṭabarī identify them as the People of the Book, or the believing portion of them. The Qur'an states the same recognition at 2:146: *"Those to whom We gave the Book know it as they know their own sons"*; and 6:20. And at 28:52–53: *"Those to whom We gave the Book before it — they believe in it. And when it is recited to them, they say: 'We believe in it; truly it is the truth from our Lord.'"*

**The Physical Response**

*Yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni sujjadā* — "they fall upon their chins, prostrating." The verb *kharra* denotes a sudden, uncontrolled falling; *al-adhqān* is the plural of *dhaqan*, the chin. The image is of a person collapsing forward in prostration, overcome. Ibn Kathīr explains the occasion: they prostrate in gratitude for having lived to see the prophet described in their scriptures. Al-Zamakhsharī describes the effect of the recitation: it softens their hearts and moistens their faces — that is, brings tears. The parallel is 5:83: *"And when they hear what was sent down to the Messenger, thou seest their eyes overflow with tears, because of the truth they recognize. They say: 'Our Lord, we believe; so write us down among the witnesses.'"*

****Falling Upon the Chins: The Response of Those Who Were Given Knowledge****

The verse describes the response of a specific group to the recitation, and the description is physical. *Wa yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni sujjadan* — and they fall down upon their chins, prostrating. The word *adhqān* is the plural of *dhaqan*, the chin, and the image is of a prostration so complete that the face reaches the ground — al-Qurṭubī notes that the chin is mentioned as the part that touches first, indicating the speed and the completeness of the fall. The verb *yakhirrūn* denotes falling with force, not lowering oneself. The two accompanying states complete the description: *wa yabkūn*, and they weep; *wa yazīduhum khushūʿā*, and it increases them in humility. The word *khushūʿ* denotes the humility that combines inward submission with outward stillness, and the Qur'an uses it of the heart, the voice, the eyes, and the posture. The verse's claim is that the recitation *increases* it, which makes humility a quantity that grows with exposure rather than a state one arrives at. The verse specifies who the group is: *alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilm min qablih*, those who were given knowledge before it. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr identify them as the believers among the People of the Book who recognized the Qur'an, and 5:83 describes their response in the same terms: *"And when they hear what was sent down to the Messenger, thou seest their eyes overflow with tears because of the truth they recognize. They say: our Lord, we believe, so write us down among the witnesses."* 26:197–199 makes the same point about knowledge: *"Is it not a sign for them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it? And if We had revealed it to one of the foreigners and he had recited it to them, they would not have believed in it."* The contrast within the sūrah is what gives the verse its force. Verses 41, 45, and 46 described the response of those who turned away — the veil, the coverings, the deafness, the turning of the back. This verse describes the response of those who received: the fall, the weeping, the increase in humility. The two descriptions are of the same recitation, and the difference is in the receiver, which is the finding the sūrah reached at verse 82: the same text is a healing for the believers and an increase in loss for the wrongdoers. The verse's practical content is a diagnostic. The measure of a person's relation to the revelation is not his information about it but his physical and emotional response to hearing it, and the response the verse describes is available to anyone who listens. 39:23 states the same effect: *"God has sent down the best of speech, a Book consistent, repeating, at which the skins of those who fear their Lord shiver; then their skins and their hearts soften to the remembrance of God."*

**Why the Verse Contrasts the Two Responses**

The contrast is not merely comparative; it is diagnostic. The Qur'an argues that the correct response to its recitation is visible and physical — prostration, tears, submission — and that its absence indicates a condition of the hearer. 84:20–21 makes the argument explicitly: *"So what ails them, that they believe not, and when the Qur'an is recited to them, they do not prostrate?"* The verse therefore offers the reader a test he can apply: not "do I understand the arguments?" but "does this move me?" The People of the Book recognized the Qur'an not primarily by argument but by recognition — the sense of encountering something they already knew.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:108

> **And they say, "Glory be to our Lord! The Promise of our Lord is indeed fulfilled."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Subḥāna Rabbinā: The Recognition Formula**

*Wa yaqūlūna subḥāna rabbinā in kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā* — "and they say: Glory be to our Lord! Truly the promise of our Lord was to be fulfilled." The *tasbīḥ* here is not the declarative glorification of verse 43 but the exclamation of one who has just witnessed the confirmation of what he was told. The sequence is: recognition, then prostration (v. 107), then glorification, then the acknowledgment that the promise has been kept. Al-Zamakhsharī identifies the promise as God's commitment to send a future prophet — the promise recorded in their own scriptures. Compare 7:157, where the Prophet ﷺ is described as:

> *"whom they find written with them in the Torah and the Gospel."*

**"In kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā"**

The construction *in ... la-* is emphatic: *truly* the promise of our Lord *was* to be fulfilled. *Mafʿūl* — done, enacted, carried out — is the same word used at verses 5 and 7 of the fulfillment of the promise against the Israelites. The Qur'an uses one term for the fulfillment of promises of mercy and promises of punishment, which is a claim about the reliability of divine speech: what God announces, happens, in both directions.

****The Promise Fulfilled: The Confession That Completes the Prostration****

The verse continues the description of verse 107 and supplies the words the prostrators speak. *Wa yaqūlūna subḥāna rabbinā in kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā* — and they say: glory be to our Lord; truly the promise of our Lord is fulfilled. The sequence is significant: the prostration and the weeping come first, and the confession follows them, which is the Qur'an's characteristic order — the recognition arrives through the body before it is articulated. The formula has two parts, and each does distinct work. *Subḥāna rabbinā* is the glorification with which the sūrah began at verse 1, and its return here closes a structural circle: the sūrah opened with the glorification of the One who carried His servant by night, and it reaches this verse with the glorification of those who received what was carried. The confession *in kāna waʿdu rabbinā la-mafʿūlā* uses the emphatic *in* with the *lām* of affirmation, and the word *mafʿūl* denotes a thing that is done and completed, not merely promised. Al-Qurṭubī explains what the promise refers to: the promise of the revelation's truth, of the resurrection, and of the outcome for those who believed — all of which the hearers had been told and had not seen. The confession is therefore made by people who have now seen the fulfillment, and 5:83 records it in the same situation: *"What is wrong with us that we should not believe in God and in the truth that has come to us, when we hope that our Lord will admit us among the righteous?"* The verse's placement after the account of the gradual revelation at verse 106 is significant. The revelation came in portions across twenty-three years, and those who received it in portions are the ones who weep at its completion — which is the sūrah's answer to the demand for a single overwhelming sign. The sign would have produced submission without transformation; the gradual revelation produced the response this verse describes. The practical content of the verse is the sequence it models. The correct response to the recognition of a fulfilled promise is not argument but glorification, and the glorification is accompanied by the acknowledgment that the promise was sure from the beginning — *in kāna*, truly it was. This is the disposition of one who has waited and been vindicated, and it is available to anyone who has held to a promise through the interval in which it looked unfilled. 30:6 states the principle on which the confidence rests: *"The promise of God — God does not fail in His promise, but most people know not."*

**Testimony From an Uninterested Witness**

The verse supplies testimony from an unexpected quarter. The Quraysh had dismissed the Prophet ﷺ as bewitched, a poet, a man taught by another. The verse brings forward witnesses who possessed prior revelation, who had no reason to flatter him, and who recognized him. Their recognition is offered as evidence precisely because they were not partisans. This is a standard evidential structure, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly: 26:197, *"Is it not a sign for them that the scholars of the Children of Israel know it?"* The testimony of the informed outsider carries weight that the testimony of the follower cannot.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:109

> **And they fall down on their faces, weeping, and it increases them in humility.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Repetition of the Prostration**

*Wa yakhirrūna li-l-adhqāni yabkūna* — "and they fall upon their chins, weeping." The verse returns to the image of verse 107 and adds two elements: the weeping and the increase in humility. Al-Bayḍāwī explains the weeping as arising from being greatly moved by the Qur'an's teachings; Ibn Kathīr as arising from humility toward God and belief in His Book and His Prophet. The parallel is 19:58, describing the prophets and the progeny of Noah and Abraham: *"When the signs of the Compassionate were recited to them, they fell down prostrate and weeping."*

**"And It Increases Them in Humility"**

*Wa yazīduhum khushūʿā* — *khushūʿ* is humility, reverence, the inward softening that produces outward stillness. It is the quality the Qur'an attributes to the successful believers at 23:1–2: *"Successful are the believers, who are humble (khāshiʿūn) in their prayer."* The verb *yazīduhum* — it increases them — indicates that the humility is not produced from nothing but augmented. The one who already has humility receives more from the recitation; the effect is proportional to the prior state. This is the same principle as verse 82's *"a cure and a mercy for the believers"* — the Qur'an's effect depends on the constitution of the hearer.

**The Diagnostic Use of the Verse**

The commentators note that the differing responses to the Qur'an are treated throughout as indicators of inward state. 9:124–25: *"And whenever a sūrah is revealed, some of them say: 'Which of you has it increased in faith?' As for those who believe, it increases them in faith... but as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth."* And 74:49–51; and 17:41. The verse therefore supplies a self-examination available to any reader. The question is not whether one agrees with the text intellectually but what the text does to one. The one who hears the Qur'an and is unmoved has information about himself that the one who weeps does not — and the information is not favorable.

****The Names Are Many and the Essence Is One****

The verse is the Qur'an's clearest statement of the doctrine of the divine names, and the occasion recorded for it is specific. Al-Ṭabarī preserves the report that the Prophet ﷺ was heard calling upon God in prayer by the names *al-Raḥmān* and *al-Raḥīm*, and that some of the polytheists objected that he claimed to worship one God while calling upon two. The verse answers by refusing the premise: *quli dʿū Allāha awi dʿū al-Raḥmān ayyan mā tadʿū fa-lahu al-asmāʾ al-ḥusnā* — say: call upon God or call upon al-Raḥmān; whichever you call, His are the most beautiful names. The grammatical construction *ayyan mā tadʿū* is one of the most compressed in the Qur'an, and al-Zamakhsharī explains it as *whichever of them you call*, the *mā* being an intensifier attached to the interrogative. The clause therefore does not distinguish between the names at all: the invocation is identical whichever name is used, because the named is one. This is the doctrine of *tawḥīd al-asmāʾ wa al-ṣifāt* in a single sentence — the names are many, the named is one, and the multiplicity of the names does not divide the essence. The Qur'an states the same doctrine at 7:180: *"And to God belong the most beautiful names, so call upon Him by them, and leave those who deviate concerning His names — they will be requited for what they did."* 59:22–24 enumerates a series of them and closes with *"He is God, the Creator, the Maker, the Fashioner; His are the most beautiful names."* The ḥadīth literature supplies the enumeration:

> *"God has ninety-nine names, a hundred less one. Whoever preserves them (aḥṣāhā) will enter Paradise."* (al-Bukhārī 2736; Muslim 2677)

The word *aḥṣāhā* has been explained by the scholars as more than memorization: it denotes knowing them, understanding what each denotes, and acting accordingly — calling upon God by the name appropriate to the request, which is the practice the verse commands. The theological content of the verse is what distinguishes Islamic monotheism from the alternatives it addresses. The multiplicity of names is not a multiplicity of deities, since each name denotes an attribute of one essence; and the attributes are not additions to the essence, since they are what the essence is. The verse's practical consequence is a rule for prayer: the supplicant should name what he is asking for by the divine name that answers it, which turns invocation from a general appeal into a specific address. The verse's placement at the close of the sūrah is significant. The final verses are a doxology — glorification, prostration, and the naming of God — and this verse supplies the vocabulary for the naming. The sūrah began with *subḥān* and ends with the names by which the glorified is called.

**Why Weeping Is Treated as Evidence**

The Islamic tradition treats tears in the presence of revelation as a genuine spiritual datum rather than a mere emotional discharge. Al-Bukhārī (715) records the Prophet ﷺ weeping during the recitation of the Qur'an; and al-Tirmidhī (1634) records:

> *"Two eyes the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from the fear of God, and an eye that kept watch in the path of God."* (graded ḥasan)

The weeping of verse 109 is thus not a sentimental detail but the observable sign of the humility the recitation produced — and the humility is the thing the recitation was sent to create.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:110

> **Say, "Call upon God, or call upon the Compassionate. Whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names. And be not loud in your prayer, nor too quiet therein, but seek a way between."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Occasion: An Objection About Names**

*Quli dʿū Allāha awi dʿū al-Raḥmān* — "Say: Call upon God, or call upon the Compassionate." Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr record the occasion: the Makkan idolaters heard the Prophet ﷺ supplicating "O God, O Compassionate" — or, in another report, "O Compassionate, O Merciful" — and accused him of invoking two deities. The verse was revealed to establish that the Names refer to one God. The objection was not frivolous from the objectors' perspective: al-Raḥmān was known to them as a designation used in Yamāmah by the false prophet Musaylimah, and they may have taken it for the name of a distinct deity. The verse answers by declaring the equivalence of the Names.

**Al-Raḥmān as a Name of the Essence**

Al-Raḥmān — the Compassionate, the All-Merciful — is one of the most important divine Names, and the commentators treat it as pertaining to the divine Essence rather than to a specific attribute of action. It occurs as a proper Name throughout Sūrah 19 (19:18, 26, 44, 58, 61) and in the opening formula of every sūrah but one. The verse's theological claim is that the multiplicity of Names does not imply a multiplicity of named. *Ayyan mā tadʿū fa-lahu al-asmāʾu al-ḥusnā* — "whichever you call upon, to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names." The Names are many; the Named is one.

**Al-Asmāʾ al-Ḥusnā**

The phrase "the Most Beautiful Names" appears at 7:180: *"And to God belong the Most Beautiful Names, so call upon Him by them, and leave those who deviate concerning His Names"*; at 20:8: *"God — there is no god but He; to Him belong the Most Beautiful Names"*; and at 59:24. The tradition enumerates ninety-nine Names, on the basis of the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī (2736) and Muslim (2677):

> *"God has ninety-nine Names, one hundred less one; whoever preserves them (aḥṣāhā) will enter the Garden."*

The science of the Names became a major discipline in Islamic theology and spirituality, with al-Ghazālī's *al-Maqṣad al-asnā* among its most influential treatments.

**The Instruction on the Voice in Prayer**

*Wa lā tajhar bi-ṣalātika wa lā tukhāfit bihā wa-btaghi bayna dhālika sabīlā* — "and be not loud in thy prayer, nor too quiet in it, but seek a way between." Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn Kathīr record the occasion: the Prophet ﷺ was reciting aloud in Makkah, and the idolaters responded by cursing the Qur'an and the One who sent it. The instruction to lower the voice was to prevent the sacred act from provoking blasphemy. Ibn Kathīr records the other side: not so quiet that those behind the imām cannot hear. And Ibn ʿAbbās's reading, preserved by Ibn Kathīr, gives the interior reason: not loud out of ostentation, not quiet out of fear of people. Compare 7:205: *"And remember thy Lord within thyself, humbly and in fear, without loudness of speech, in the mornings and the evenings, and be not of the heedless."*

****Neither Loud Nor Low: The Commanded Middle in Recitation****

The verse is a command about the volume of prayer, and the occasion recorded for it is one of the most specific in the sūrah. Al-Bukhārī (4722) and Muslim (446) record from Ibn ʿAbbās that the verse was revealed in Makkah when the Prophet ﷺ would raise his voice in prayer and the polytheists, hearing the Qur'an, would revile it and its revealer and the One who sent it; he then lowered his voice to the point where his Companions behind him could not hear, and the verse commanded the middle.

> *"The verse was revealed concerning the prayer: he would raise his voice in recitation, and when the polytheists heard it they reviled the Qur'an and the One who revealed it; so God said to His Prophet: do not raise your voice in prayer, that they may not revile it, and do not lower it, that your Companions may not be unable to hear — and seek a way between that."* (al-Bukhārī 4722)

The two terms are precise. *Tajhar*, from *j-h-r*, denotes the raising of the voice to full openness; *tukhāfit*, from *kh-f-t*, denotes the lowering of it to the point where it is barely audible. The command is *wa btaghi bayna dhālika sabīlā* — seek a way between the two — and the verb *ibtaghā* denotes the active search for a middle rather than the passive acceptance of a compromise. Al-Ṭabarī records the alternative reading of the word *ṣalāh* here as denoting supplication (*duʿāʾ*) rather than the ritual prayer, on the strength of 40:60, *"Call upon Me and I will answer you,"* and of the Arabic usage in which *ṣalāh* and *duʿāʾ* are interchangeable. On that reading the verse commands the middle in supplication, and the jurists hold both readings, since the ruling applies to the ritual prayer and to the invocation outside it. The verse's significance extends beyond volume. It states the principle of the middle (*wasat*) in a concrete case, and the Qur'an applies the principle to the community as a whole at 2:143: *"And thus We have made you a middle nation."* The specific application here is to a situation in which both extremes were errors with real consequences: the loud recitation produced blasphemy from the hearers, and the quiet one deprived the Companions of hearing. The middle was therefore not a compromise between two preferences but the only option that avoided two harms. The practical application is general and the reasoning transfers directly. A person conveying a message must calibrate not only the content but the volume, and the calibration is determined by the effect on both parties — those who would be provoked by the excess and those who would be deprived by the deficiency. The verse supplies the rule and the reason in a single sentence, which is why the jurists derived from it not merely a ruling on volume but a principle about delivery.

**"Seek a Way Between"**

*Wa-btaghi bayna dhālika sabīlā* — *sabīl* is a path; the instruction is to find the middle way. The verse's closing thus restates the sūrah's governing principle: *aqwam* — the most upright (v. 9); neither wasteful nor miserly (vv. 26–29); neither loud nor silent. The middle is not a compromise between two errors but the correct position, which happens to lie between them. Compare 2:143: *"And thus We have made you a middle community (ummatan wasaṭan), that you may be witnesses over mankind."* The middle is the Qur'an's characteristic location, and this sūrah — which began by describing the Qur'an as guiding to what is most upright — ends its legislative section by legislating it in the matter of the voice.

---

## Sūrah al-Isrāʾ 17:111

> **And say, "Praise be to God, who has no child! He has no partner in sovereignty; nor has He any protector out of lowliness." And proclaim His Greatness!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Closes the Chapter**

*Wa quli al-ḥamdu li-Allāhi alladhī lam yattakhidh waladan* — "and say: Praise be to God, who has not taken a child." The sūrah that opened with *subḥān* — glory be to Him — closes with *ḥamd* — praise be to God. The two are the Qur'an's twin formulae of divine acknowledgment: *tasbīḥ* declaring God free of every deficiency, *ḥamd* attributing every perfection to Him. The sūrah is thus framed by the two acts of speech that constitute worship.

**The Three Negations**

The verse denies three things, and each answers a specific claim current in the sūrah's context. *"Who has no child"* — *lam yattakhidh waladan*.
