The verb *ittakhadha* means to take, to adopt; the negation is of *taking* a child, which is the specific claim made by the three groups the Qur'an addresses: the Christians concerning Jesus (19:35, *"It is not fitting for the Compassionate to take a son"*); the Jews and Christians claiming to be God's children (5:18:

> *"And the Jews and the Christians say: 'We are the children of God and His beloved.' Say: 'Then why does He punish you for your sins?'"*

); and the Makkans concerning the angels as daughters (16:57; 37:149–53; 43:16; 52:39) — the belief verse 40 of this sūrah demolished. Sūrah al-Ikhlāṣ states the same in its shortest form: 112:3, *"He begets not, nor is He begotten."* *"He has no partner in sovereignty"* — *wa lam yakun lahu sharīkun fī al-mulk*. This is the denial of shirk in its political form: no one shares God's rule. The sūrah has argued this at verse 42 (the rival gods would seek a way to the Possessor of the Throne) and at verse 56 (those called upon besides Him have no power). *"Nor has He any protector out of lowliness"* — *wa lam yakun lahu waliyyun mina al-dhull*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr explain: God neither seeks nor needs anyone's aid or protection, as a lowly person does. The negation is not of *walī* in general — the Qur'an describes God as the *walī* of the believers (2:257) and describes believers as having God as their *walī* — but of a *walī* arising from *dhull*, weakness. God has friends by His bounty, not protectors by His need.

**The Three Negations as a Summary of Tawḥīd**

The commentators note that the three denials cover the three forms of shirk: shirk in essence (a child, implying a second of the same nature), shirk in sovereignty (a partner in rule), and shirk in need (a protector, implying deficiency). The verse is therefore a complete statement of divine unity in negative form, and it is for this reason that the tradition regards it as one of the most comprehensive verses in the Qur'an. A ḥadīth recorded by al-Tirmidhī (3401) and others preserves the practice of reciting it, and it has entered Muslim devotion as *āyat al-ʿizz* — the verse of majesty — recited at the conclusion of prayers and gatherings.

**"And Proclaim His Greatness!"**

*Wa kabbirhu takbīrā* — the final command. The verb *kabbir* is the second form of *k-b-r*, to declare great; the cognate adverb *takbīrā* intensifies: proclaim His greatness *repeatedly and fully*. This is the scriptural anchor of the *takbīr*: *Allāhu akbar* — "God is Great." The formula's place in Islamic practice is without parallel: it opens the canonical prayer (*takbīrat al-iḥrām*) and recurs at each transition within it; it opens the call to prayer and is repeated in it; it is recited in the funeral prayer; it is chanted in congregation at both ʿĪd prayers and throughout the days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah; it is uttered at the conclusion of a marriage contract, upon a person's acceptance of Islam, and at moments of collective joy; and it has historically been the cry of armies and of crowds facing danger.

**Why the Chapter Ends With the Takbīr**

The ending is a deliberate conclusion to a sūrah that has argued at length about God's transcendence. The arguments have been made — against divine children (v. 40), against rival gods (vv. 42, 56–57), against the objects of worship that themselves supplicate (v. 57). The final verse does not add an argument; it performs the response. Having established that God has no child, no partner, and no need, the only remaining act is to declare Him great. The *takbīr* is not a description but a declaration, and the distinction matters. "God is great" as a description would imply a comparison — greater than what? — and the comparative form *akbar* is understood in the tradition as absolute: great beyond every comparison, great in a sense that admits no standard. The one who says it is not measuring God against anything; he is removing everything else from consideration.

****The Verse of Might: Four Negations and One Command****

The sūrah closes with a declaration that the tradition calls *āyat al-ʿizz*, the verse of might, and its structure is four negations followed by a single command. *Wa quli al-ḥamdu li-Allāhi alladhī lam yattakhidh waladā* — and say: praise belongs to God, Who has taken no son. The first negation answers the Christian claim and the pre-Islamic Arab claim about the angels, and the Qur'an addresses both at 19:88–92 and 37:149–153. The word *yattakhidh* is from the form denoting the taking of a thing for oneself, so the negation is not merely that God has no son but that He did not take one — the claim is about an act that never occurred. The second negation answers the polytheism of partnership: *wa lam yakun lahu sharīkun fī al-mulk*, and there is no partner with Him in dominion. The specification *fī al-mulk* is significant, since it denies partnership in sovereignty rather than merely in worship — the one who has no share in God's rule cannot be a partner in any sense. The third negation is the subtlest: *wa lam yakun lahu walīyun mina al-dhulli*, and He has no protector from abasement. Al-Ṭabarī explains it as the denial of an ally taken out of need, which is the only reason a being takes an ally; God has allies by His choice and not by His need, and the distinction is what separates the Islamic conception from the pagan one, in which the gods require support. The command that closes the verse is the sūrah's final word and its structural completion: *wa kabbirhu takbīrā* — and magnify Him greatly. The doubled form *kabbir* with the cognate accusative *takbīrā* produces the same intensive construction used at verse 43, *ʿuluwwan kabīrā*, and the circle closes: the sūrah opened with *subḥāna alladhī asrā bi-ʿabdih*, glory to the One who carried His servant, and closes with the command to magnify the One who has no son, no partner, and no need. The verse's placement is therefore not incidental. The entire sūrah has been an argument for *tawḥīd* — against the attribution of children to God (vv. 40–41), against the invocation of intermediaries (vv. 56–57), against the elevation of the messenger to a share in divine power (v. 91), and against the claim that the revelation competes with God (v. 111) — and the final verse states the conclusion in the form in which it is to be held: not as a proposition but as a praise. The practical content of the verse is that the doctrine ends in doxology. The four negations are not a metaphysical system but the removal of four obstacles to the praise, and the command that follows them is the sūrah's actual destination. The tradition read it accordingly, and the verse became part of the daily liturgy: the formula *Allāhu akbar*, the magnification the verse commands, opens the prayer and closes it, and is uttered at every transition in the worshipper's day. The sūrah that began with a journey ends with the word by which the journey into prayer is begun.

**The Reader's Position at the End**

The sūrah began with a journey no one witnessed and ended with a formula every Muslim says daily. Between the two it has passed through the history of Israel, the accountability of the individual soul, the moral code, the arguments against shirk, the demands of the Quraysh, the account of Adam and Iblīs, the establishment of the prayers, and the nature of the Qur'an itself. The final command gathers all of it into a single utterance. And the utterance is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in the imperative — *say* — which means it is addressed to everyone who recites the sūrah. The reader who has followed the chapter from *Subḥāna* to *takbīrā* has been led, verse by verse, to the point where the only adequate response is the one the verse supplies. The sūrah does not conclude with a conclusion; it concludes with an act — and the act is available to anyone who says the words.
